\section*{Editorial Note}

This volume is a focused book built from the existing Yau series. ${ }^{1}$ It keeps only two chapters from the geometry-and-analysis selection:
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Problem Section (With Commentary),
\item[2.] Open Problems in Geometry.
\end{itemize}

The original mathematical content is preserved by importing each selected article in full. The Zhu-Chen commentary has been folded into the first chapter as working comparison notes for later problem-by-problem revision. The two chapter guides are grouped together before the reproduced articles, so the internal arrangement is easier to follow before the original text begins.

\section*{Problem Section}

In March 1980, I gave three lectures on open problems in geometry that I considered to be important. My talks aroused much interest in the differential geometry community, and I was rather excited by this group of problems myself.

I still remember that I got the flu during the second lecture. But I insisted on finishing all three lectures, wearing a heavy jacket while giving them. Many people in the audience contributed their opinions. There were very lively conversations indeed. The whole set of problems should be considered a joint effort, although in the final version printed later, I added many more problems to the original set.

In order to encourage Chinese geometers to work on more modern questions, I gave extensive talks on these problems at the 1980 Beijing DD Symposium on differential geometry and differential equations. The conference was organized by Chern in the summer of 1980 and had a rather deep influence on the development of geometry in China.

Many geometers worked on those problems. There were 120 problems in all, and I am pleased that about a quarter of the problems were solved. In 1986, I was asked by Princeton University Press to update this set of problems. My former student Bennett Chow and I spent a month working on this project. But when the manuscript was sent to the Press, it was vetoed by Wu-Chung Hsiang who was then the mathematics editor.

In this commentary, I made some comments myself and also included comments from many friends and colleagues.
Shing-Tung Yau

\title{
Problem Section ${ }^{\ddagger}$
}

\author{
Shing-Tung Yau
}

\section*{Contents}

During the last part of the geometry year in the Institute, there were a lot of requests for a lecture on open problems. After discussions with the following mathematicians: Bourguignon, Calabi, Cheng, Kazdan, Li, Schoen, Simon, Treibergs and Uhlenbeck, I made up a list of sixty problems and gave two lectures on it. Later I was encouraged to enlarge the problem set somewhat.

I should emphasize that the problem set is by no means exhaustive. The choice of the topics depends a lot on the author's personal taste. Apart from a few exceptions, I do not attempt to mention problems in the other subjects which are closely related to differential geometry or can probably be solved by geometric means.

The difficulty of the problems ranges from "elementary" to "deep." Whereas "deep" problems may be solved by a beginning student within a few months and elementary problems can be open for a long time, I do hope that this problem set will provide a condensed overview of the subject for beginning students.

Most, if not all, of the problems are well known. If a problem has an explicit reference or the problem is explicitly suggested by a single person, it will be mentioned. Otherwise the reader can assume that the problem is well known.

Finally, I would like to thank all the mathematicians who read the original draft and suggested corrections, further discussion and references, including F. J. Almgren, Jr., M. Berger, A. Borel, E. Calabi, J. Cheeger, M. Gromov, and H. B. Lawson. I am also grateful to James Mackraz for his valuable help in organizing the problems and compiling the bibliography, and to Neola Crimmins and Kathy Lunetta for a wonderful job of typing this work.

\section*{I. Curvature and the Topology of Manifolds}
A. Sectional curvature

Problem 1 (The Hopf Conjecture) - Open as of 2026
Does $\mathbb{S}^{2} \times \mathbb{S}^{2}$ admit a metric with positive sectional curvature?

Status. Open. The product metric on $\mathbb{S}^{2} \times \mathbb{S}^{2}$ has nonnegative sectional curvature and many zero two-planes; the question is whether these zero planes can be removed by some global deformation of the metric. No metric of positive sectional curvature is known, and no known topological, analytic, or variational obstruction rules out all such metrics. The available results are therefore genuinely partial: they exclude positive curvature near the standard product metric, under symmetry, under pinching or stronger curvature hypotheses, or under additional analytic assumptions.

It is useful to separate this product problem from the Euler-characteristic form of Hopf's conjecture. The latter predicts that every compact even-dimensional positively curved manifold has positive Euler characteristic; it gives no obstruction here, since $\chi\left(\mathbb{S}^{2} \times \mathbb{S}^{2}\right)=4$. A solution of this problem would require a finer obstruction, one sensitive to the middle-dimensional product structure or to the indefinite intersection form, or else a construction of a new positively curved four-manifold outside the currently known simply connected examples $\mathbb{S}^{4}$ and $\mathbb{C P}^{2}$.

The earliest result in this direction is Berger's local obstruction [Br1], which was strengthened by Bourguignon, Deschamps and Sentenac [BDS]. They proved that, in a neighborhood of the product metric on $\mathbb{S}^{2} \times \mathbb{S}^{2}$, there is no metric with positive sectional curvature. Thus the product metric cannot be perturbed locally to a positively curved metric.

There are also extrinsic obstructions which help explain why the problem is not merely a search for an elementary deformation of the standard product. Hopf's hypersurface theorem rules out a positively curved hypersurface realization in codimension one, and Weinstein's codimension-two theorem [Wei] implies that a positively curved $\mathbb{S}^{2} \times \mathbb{S}^{2}$ could not occur as a submanifold of $\mathbb{R}^{6}$ with the induced metric. These results do not obstruct an abstract intrinsic metric, but they are part of the classical evidence that the product topology is hard to reconcile with strict sectional positivity. Ziller's survey [Zi] places this product problem among the central unresolved examples in positive sectional curvature and is a useful guide to the surrounding construction and obstruction landscape.

The next decisive progress concerns symmetry. Hsiang and Kleiner [HK] proved that a compact simply connected positively curved four-manifold with an isometric circle action is homeomorphic to $\mathbb{S}^{4}$ or $\mathbb{C P}^{2}$. Consequently a hypothetical positively curved metric on $\mathbb{S}^{2} \times \mathbb{S}^{2}$ would have finite isometry group; in particular it cannot carry a nontrivial Killing field. Seaman [S] obtained a different kind of restriction: $\mathbb{S}^{2} \times \mathbb{S}^{2}$ admits no metric whose sectional curvature is pinched between 0.19 and 1 . Motivated partly by the role of Killing fields in positive curvature problems, Li and Liu [LL] introduced a geometric heat flow for vector fields on closed manifolds with positive sectional curvature. That flow is not an obstruction to arbitrary positively curved metrics, but it belongs to the analytic program of detecting hidden symmetry, which is exactly what the Hsiang-Kleiner theorem would then rule out on $\mathbb{S}^{2} \times \mathbb{S}^{2}$.

The four-dimensional topology suggests another analytic route. Liu and Wan [LW] proved that a compact oriented Riemannian four-manifold with positive sectional curvature is definite if its harmonic two-forms satisfy a suitable Kato-type inequality. Since $\mathbb{S}^{2} \times \mathbb{S}^{2}$ has indefinite intersection form, this theorem identifies a precise missing estimate: a general proof of the required
inequality for positively curved four-manifolds would imply the Hopf product conjecture. At present this remains a conditional mechanism rather than a theorem in the unrestricted setting.

Stronger curvature hypotheses give complete classification results, but they also show why the original problem is difficult. Hamilton [Ham1] proved that a compact simply connected four-manifold with positive curvature operator is diffeomorphic to $\mathbb{S}^{4}$. In the setting of positive isotropic curvature, Hamilton [Ham2] initiated the Ricci-flow analysis in dimension four, and Chen, Tang and Zhu [CTZ] later obtained the complete classification of compact four-manifolds with positive isotropic curvature. In higher dimensions, Böhm and Wilking [BW] proved that manifolds with positive curvature operator are space forms. Brendle and Schoen [BS] proved the differentiable sphere theorem for pointwise quarter-pinched curvature. These Ricci-flow and pinching theorems do not rule out an arbitrary positively curved metric on $\mathbb{S}^{2} \times \mathbb{S}^{2}$, but they show that once sectional positivity is strengthened in natural analytic directions, the topology collapses to space-form type behavior.

The higher-dimensional symmetry program gives the broader context. Grove and Searle [GS] classified the maximal symmetry rank case: if a compact simply connected positively curved $n$-manifold has an effective isometric torus action of maximal possible rank, then the equality cases are the sphere and complex projective space. Wilking [Wil2] extended this direction to large-symmetry-rank manifolds; in particular, for $n \geqslant 10$ and symmetry rank at least $\frac{n}{4}+1$, the manifold is homeomorphic to $\mathbb{S}^{n}$ or $\mathbb{H P}^{n / 4}$, or is homotopy equivalent to $\mathbb{C P}^{n / 2}$. Later work on the Hopf conjecture with symmetry gives further evidence in this direction. Püttmann and Searle [PS] proved the Hopf Euler-characteristic conjecture under lowcohomogeneity or high-symmetry-rank assumptions. Kennard [Ke] proved the Hopf conjecture for positively curved manifolds under a logarithmic lower bound on the symmetry rank. Amann and Kennard [AK] proved that, under a logarithmic symmetryrank assumption, products $N \times N$ of simply connected closed manifolds do not admit positively curved metrics; they also gave evidence for the conjecture that compact simply connected symmetric spaces of rank greater than one should not admit positive sectional curvature.

Finally, there are examples illustrating the gap between positive curvature everywhere and positivity on a large set. Petersen and Wilhelm [PW] constructed metrics with positive sectional curvature almost everywhere on the unit tangent bundle of $\mathbb{S}^{4}$ and on a real cohomology $\mathbb{C P}^{3}$. Wilking [Wil1] constructed further almost-positive examples. These examples do not produce positive curvature everywhere on $\mathbb{S}^{2} \times \mathbb{S}^{2}$, but they show that almost positive curvature is a much weaker condition than positive sectional curvature.

Recent work also clarifies how delicate the curvature assumption is. Kennard, Mouillé and Nienhaus [KMN] studied Hopftype Euler-characteristic questions under positive second intermediate Ricci curvature. They note that the Hopf conclusion can fail under such relaxations, while proving a positive Euler-characteristic result under additional torus symmetry. This is not a solution of the sectional-curvature Hopf conjecture, but it helps separate which parts of the problem depend specifically on sectional curvature.

Schoen's recent work [Sch] gives a complementary minimal-surface restriction on the topology of positively curved manifolds. He proved that an odd-dimensional closed positively curved manifold cannot contain an incompressible real projective plane, and that in dimension $4 k$ the fundamental group acts trivially on $\pi_{2}$. In particular, $\mathbb{R} \mathbb{P}^{2} \times \mathbb{S}^{2}$ does not admit a metric of positive sectional curvature. This does not settle the simply connected product $\mathbb{S}^{2} \times \mathbb{S}^{2}$, but it is closely related in method: stable minimal two-spheres detect restrictions on the interaction between low homotopy groups and positive sectional curvature, a theme that any four-dimensional obstruction to the Hopf product conjecture would likely have to confront.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Is every compact simply connected four-manifold with positive sectional curvature homeomorphic, or even diffeomorphic, to $\mathbb{S}^{4}$ or $\mathbb{C P}^{2}$ ? This is the natural four-dimensional classification problem behind the Hopf product question. It is known under circle symmetry by Hsiang-Kleiner [HK], and related high-symmetry results support the same picture in higher dimensions [GS, Wil2, Ke].
\item[2.] Original direction. More generally, can any nontrivial product $M_{1} \times M_{2}$ of compact positive-dimensional manifolds admit a metric with positive sectional curvature? The special case $\mathbb{S}^{2} \times \mathbb{S}^{2}$ is Problem 1; the product version with symmetry is discussed by Amann-Kennard [AK].
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Can a compact simply connected symmetric space of rank greater than one admit a metric with positive sectional curvature? This would include $\mathbb{S}^{2} \times \mathbb{S}^{2}$ as the first rank-two example, and is explicitly discussed as a generalization of Hopf's problem in [AK].
\item[4.] Original direction. Does every compact simply connected manifold with nonnegative sectional curvature admit some metric with positive sectional curvature? Yau emphasized this as a basic surrounding question in the original problem list [Yau]. The product metric on $\mathbb{S}^{2} \times \mathbb{S}^{2}$ is the first test case, and the failure of all known deformation methods there is one reason the question is difficult.
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[5.] Original direction. Does the Hopf Euler-characteristic conjecture hold in all even dimensions: if $M^{2 n}$ is compact and has positive sectional curvature, must $\chi(M)>0$ ? It is known in several symmetric situations, for example [PS, Ke], but remains open in general.
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. What are the strongest possible restrictions on the action of $\pi_{1}(M)$ on $\pi_{2}(M)$ for compact manifolds with positive sectional curvature? Schoen's theorem [Sch] gives strong results in odd dimensions and in dimensions divisible by four, but the full structure of low homotopy groups under positive sectional curvature remains part of the broader topology problem.
\item[7.] Reference-driven formulation. Can the harmonic-form criterion of Liu-Wan [LW] be proved directly from positive sectional curvature in dimension four, or can one construct a positively curved four-manifold for which the required Katotype estimate fails? Either outcome would clarify whether the intersection-form approach can reach the Hopf product conjecture.
\item[8.] AI-generated formulation. Find a curvature or topological obstruction which is strong enough to rule out positive sectional curvature on products, but flexible enough to detect metrics far from the product metric. This question is a synthesis of the local obstruction [Br1, BDS], symmetry obstructions [HK, GS, Wil2, PS, Ke, AK], extrinsic restrictions [Wei], and pinching/curvature-operator classification results [S, Ham1, Ham2, BW, BS, CTZ].
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 2}

Is there any metric with positive curvature on exotic spheres?
Status. Needs verification. The problem is claimed affirmative in dimension seven, pending a fully published verification. Petersen and Wilhelm [PW2] announced a metric of positive sectional curvature on the Gromoll-Meyer exotic 7-sphere. If this construction is accepted in the stated form, it answers Yau's original existence question affirmatively. Since the reference used here is an arXiv preprint and not a published account, the entry records the result as a claimed solution rather than as an unconditional settled theorem. In any case, the broader classification problem-which exotic spheres admit positive sectional curvature-remains open.

Milnor's discovery of exotic 7-spheres [Mil] made it natural to ask whether positive sectional curvature can occur on a manifold homeomorphic but not diffeomorphic to a standard sphere. This is a much sharper question than positive scalar curvature. Hitchin [H1] showed that many exotic spheres, detected by the $\alpha$-invariant, do not admit metrics of positive scalar curvature; hence they cannot admit positive sectional curvature. These index-theoretic obstructions rule out large families in certain dimensions, but they do not rule out all exotic spheres and do not decide the Gromoll-Meyer sphere.

The first major positive construction was the Gromoll-Meyer sphere. Gromoll and Meyer [GM1] realized a Milnor exotic 7-sphere as a biquotient of Sp(2) and constructed on it a metric of nonnegative sectional curvature. Their metric has zerocurvature planes, but the biquotient presentation became the basic model for all later attempts to produce strict positivity. Grove and Ziller [GZ] later constructed nonnegatively curved metrics on many Milnor spheres. This shows that nonnegative sectional curvature is far more flexible than positive sectional curvature in the exotic-sphere setting, just as in the Hopf product problem.

The next stage was almost positive curvature. Petersen and Wilhelm [PW1] constructed examples with positive curvature almost everywhere. Eschenburg and Kerin [EK] then showed that the Gromoll-Meyer sphere admits metrics whose sectional curvature is positive outside four explicit subvarieties of codimension at least one. These results did not solve the original problem, but they reduced it to understanding whether the remaining zero-curvature planes could be removed.

Petersen and Wilhelm [PW2] subsequently announced a deformation of the Gromoll-Meyer construction with positive sectional curvature. Mathematically, this is the decisive point in the problem: the announced result would supply the first exotic sphere with positive sectional curvature and would answer Yau's original question. For the purposes of this commentary, however, the status should keep the publication caveat visible. A later attempted explicit construction by Ge and Tang [GT] was withdrawn; the authors state that the metrics constructed there have positive sectional curvature almost everywhere, but the claimed everywhere-positive curvature statement should not be used as a proof.

The proof architecture in [PW2] is substantially more involved than a single perturbation. Starting from an almost positively curved metric, the authors combine Cheeger deformations with a redistribution perturbation, scale the fibers over $\mathbb{S}^{4}$ to obtain positive integrated curvature on the former flat tori, and use a partial conformal change to turn that gain into pointwise positivity. The main technical burden is then to control a uniform neighborhood of every old zero plane in the Grassmannian. This is encoded in two-variable curvature polynomials for planes of the form $\operatorname{span}\{\zeta+\sigma z, W+\tau V\}$ and completed by further Cheeger deformations. The arXiv record for v3 notes that a referee found an error in the original Theorem 5.3; the corrected result is Lemma 5.3. Since the current manuscript remains a preprint, the status above records the claim and its method without treating it as a fully verified published solution.

Recent work has also returned to the geometry of the Gromoll-Meyer sphere from a more explicit metric viewpoint. Berman, Cederwall and Schettini Gherardini [BCSG] study Kaluza-Klein type metrics on the Gromoll-Meyer sphere using SU (2) instanton data on the base $\mathbb{S}^{4}$. Their construction gives an explicit family in which the Ricci tensor can be computed and positive Ricci curvature can be ensured for a range of the scale parameter. It also permits concrete sectional-curvature calculations. This is useful recent progress for understanding the metric geometry of the exotic 7-sphere, but it should not be confused with a new proof of positive sectional curvature everywhere.

The differentiable sphere theorem of Brendle and Schoen [BS] gives an important complementary restriction: a complete manifold with pointwise 1/4-pinched sectional curvature is diffeomorphic to a spherical space form. Therefore no exotic sphere can admit a pointwise 1/4-pinched metric, even though the Gromoll-Meyer sphere would admit positive sectional curvature if the Petersen-Wilhelm construction is accepted in the announced form.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Which exotic spheres admit positive sectional curvature? The Petersen-Wilhelm preprint claims an affirmative example on the Gromoll-Meyer sphere [PW2], while Hitchin's obstruction rules out many exotic spheres even for positive scalar curvature [H1]. A classification remains open.
\item[2.] Original direction. Which Milnor spheres admit positive sectional curvature? Grove and Ziller proved nonnegative curvature for many Milnor spheres [GZ], but the passage from nonnegative to positive curvature is still poorly understood.
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Can the Petersen-Wilhelm construction [PW2] be written in a fully explicit and independently checkable form, for example by giving curvature formulas that make the removal of all zero-curvature planes transparent? This is motivated by the withdrawal of the attempted explicit construction [GT] and by the need for concrete models.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Which compact manifolds with quasipositive or almost positive sectional curvature can be deformed to metrics with positive sectional curvature? The Gromoll-Meyer sphere is the motivating positive case [GM1, EK, PW2], while Wilking's examples [Wil1] show that deformation questions in positive curvature are delicate.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Can the instanton or Kaluza-Klein ansatz for the Gromoll-Meyer sphere be pushed beyond positive Ricci curvature to produce new explicit metrics with nonnegative, almost positive, or positive sectional curvature? The recent computations in [BCSG] give a concrete testing ground for this question.
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Develop a computer-verifiable curvature package for explicit metrics on exotic spheres: write the curvature operator in invariant coordinates, isolate the zero-plane locus, and test whether Cheeger deformation, partial conformal change, or fiber scaling removes the zero planes without creating new negative directions.
\end{itemize}

[Wil1] B. Wilking, Manifolds with positive sectional curvature almost everywhere, Invent. Math. 148 (2002), no. 1, 117-141.

\section*{Problem 3}

Let $M$ be an $N$-dimensional manifold with nonnegative sectional curvature. Is it true that the $i$ th Betti number of $M$ is not greater than the $i$ th Betti number of $\mathbb{T}^{N}$, the $N$-torus?

Status. Open in this sharp form. The statement is usually read for closed manifolds, or for complete manifolds after using the Cheeger-Gromoll soul theorem [CG] to reduce the homotopy type to a compact totally convex soul. Gromov proved that the total Betti number of a complete $N$-manifold with nonnegative sectional curvature is bounded above by a constant depending only on $N[\mathrm{Gr}]$, and Abresch improved the quantitative form of this estimate [Ab]. This is far weaker than the conjectural sharp torus bound $b_{i}(M) \leqslant b_{i}\left(\mathbb{T}^{N}\right)=\binom{N}{i}$.

The first Betti number case is classical. By the Bochner formula, nonnegative Ricci curvature already implies $b_{1}(M) \leqslant N$, with equality only in the flat torus case after passing to the appropriate cover. Thus Problem 3 begins in earnest in the middle Betti numbers. Recent work on almost nonnegative Ricci curvature gives a related structural refinement of this first-Betti-number picture. Huang, Huang, Wang and Zhu [HHWZ] prove fibration theorems over the $b_{1}(M)$-torus under almost nonnegative Ricci curvature together with a lower sectional-curvature bound, removing an upper sectional curvature hypothesis from earlier Yamaguchi-type fibration theorems. Their proof passes to the torsion-free homology cover, takes an equivariant Gromov-Hausdorff limit with an Abelian action, constructs an equivariant almost submetry to a torus, and regularizes it by a center-of-mass procedure before descending the resulting fibration. This is not a solution of the sharp sectional-curvature Betti-number bound, but it shows that the first Betti number is tied to torus-fibration structure in a robust collapsed or almost nonnegative setting.

Gromov's theorem [Gr] remains the main unconditional theorem in the direction of the problem: for every field $F$,
$$\sum_{i} b_{i}(M ; F) \leqslant C(N)$$
for all complete $N$-manifolds with nonnegative sectional curvature. For closed manifolds, Gromov's proof treats the distance function as a nonsmooth Morse function: Toponogov comparison controls its critical points, an isotopy lemma removes criticalpoint-free annuli, and a quantitative covering argument turns local information into a global homology bound. Abresch [Ab] reorganized this mechanism using ranks of homology maps between nested metric sets, a sharper packing lemma, and metric annuli; this also bounds the topology and number of ends under an integrable asymptotic lower-curvature condition. These results imply, for example, that connected sums of sufficiently many copies of $\mathbb{C P}^{2}$ cannot carry nonnegative sectional curvature. They do not, however, identify the optimal bound, nor do they explain why the flat torus should be extremal for each individual Betti number.

The conjectural structural mechanism behind the sharp bound is the Bott-Grove-Halperin rational ellipticity conjecture: a compact simply connected manifold with nonnegative sectional curvature should be rationally elliptic [FHT, FOT]. For elliptic spaces, the formal dimension controls the rational Betti numbers: in particular, Félix-Halperin-Thomas, Theorem 33.2 [FHT], gives $b_{i}(M ; \mathbb{Q}) \leqslant\binom{ N}{i}$. Thus rational ellipticity would give a conceptual explanation for the expected rational Betti-number restrictions in the simply connected part of the problem. It would not by itself settle every integral or finite-field formulation, and it leaves the fundamental-group contribution to be separated by splitting and covering arguments.

The rational homotopy picture is subtle. Totaro [To] showed that closed simply connected nonnegatively curved manifolds do not fall into finitely many rational homotopy types in each dimension; already in dimension six there are infinitely many rational homotopy types. Thus one cannot hope for a simple finiteness theorem for rational homotopy types to imply Problem 3, even though Gromov's theorem gives a uniform bound on homology ranks.

There is also a noncompact analytic analogue involving harmonic functions of polynomial growth. Colding and Minicozzi [CM] proved finite dimensionality for spaces of polynomial growth harmonic functions on complete manifolds with nonnegative Ricci curvature, and Li [Li] gave a short proof. In the Kähler setting with nonnegative holomorphic bisectional curvature, Ni [Ni] proved sharp dimension estimates under maximal volume growth, and Chen-Fu-Yin-Zhu [CFYZ] proved the sharp dimension estimate with rigidity for holomorphic functions of polynomial growth. These results do not solve the compact Betti-number problem, but they show that Yau's "torus or Euclidean model gives the sharp dimension" philosophy has successful analogues in function theory.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Reference-driven formulation. Prove the Bott-Grove-Halperin conjecture: every compact simply connected nonnegatively curved manifold is rationally elliptic [FOT]. This would give a conceptual explanation for the expected rational Bettinumber restrictions.
\item[2.] Original direction. Determine the optimal constant in Gromov's Betti-number theorem. The conjectural optimal total bound is $2^{N}$, the total Betti number of $\mathbb{T}^{N}$ [Gr, Ab, FHT].
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[3.] AI-generated formulation. Classify the equality case in Problem 3: if $b_{i}(M)=b_{i}\left(\mathbb{T}^{N}\right)$ for some nontrivial range of $i$, must $M$ be closely related to a flat torus? This is modeled on the rigidity phenomena in Bochner's theorem and in polynomial-growth function theory [CM, Li, CFYZ].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Separate the compact simply connected part of the problem from the fundamental-group part: after passing to the soul in the complete noncompact case [CG], and after using the Cheeger-Gromoll splitting theorem for the universal cover, determine how much of the torus bound reduces to the rationally elliptic factor.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend the recent first-Betti-number fibration theorems for almost nonnegative Ricci curvature [HHWZ] toward middle-dimensional cohomology: is there a collapsed-geometry mechanism which explains why the torus should remain extremal for all Betti numbers under nonnegative sectional curvature?
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 4}

Let $M$ be a compact manifold with positive curvature. Does $M$ admit a smooth, effective $\mathbb{S}^{1}$ action?

Status. Open. The question is about the smooth topology of the underlying manifold, not only about the isometry group of a particular positively curved metric. All known compact manifolds with positive sectional curvature admit nontrivial smooth circle actions, and in fact the known metrics usually come with much larger isometry groups. No theorem is known, however, which forces a compact positively curved manifold to admit any effective $\mathbb{S}^{1}$-action, and no positively curved example is known whose smooth manifold is asymmetric in this sense.

The motivation is that all classical and currently known compact examples of positive sectional curvature are highly symmetric. Rank one symmetric spaces, Berger spaces, Wallach spaces, Aloff-Wallach spaces, Eschenburg spaces, Bazaikin spaces and the Gromoll-Meyer exotic sphere all arise from homogeneous, biquotient, or related quotient constructions; see the surveys of Ziller and Wilking [Zi, WilS]. These constructions produce not only a positive curvature metric, but also a visible group action. Yau's question asks whether this is an accident of construction or a genuine topological consequence of positive sectional curvature.

The symmetry assumption has produced many strong classification theorems. Grove and Searle [GS] classified the maximal symmetry-rank case. Wilking [Wil2] obtained strong restrictions for high symmetry rank, including connectedness and periodicity phenomena. Püttmann- Searle [PS] and Kennard [Ke] proved Hopf-type Euler-characteristic results under symmetry hypotheses. These results show that symmetry is a powerful organizing principle for positive curvature. They also make clear the gap in Problem 4: the theorems begin once a torus action is given, while Yau asks whether such an action must exist at all.

Recent work has refined the high-symmetry picture rather than settling the existence of a circle action. Kennard, Wiemeler and Wilking [KWW] found a link between torus representations occurring in positive-curvature arguments and regular matroids. At a torus fixed point, the relevant isotropy representation is encoded by a totally unimodular matrix. Seymour's classification reduces its difficult part to weighted graphs; graph systole and matroid cogirth estimates then produce a circle with a large fixed-point component, to which connectedness and cohomological periodicity can be applied. This leads to new obstructions to positive sectional curvature in the presence of torus symmetry. Kennard, Khalili Samani and Searle [KKS] developed parallel restrictions for discrete elementary abelian symmetry: coding-theoretic estimates select involutions with controlled fixed-set codimension, followed by connectedness, periodicity, and induction. These papers are important for the modern reporting of the problem: they show that once symmetry is assumed, the topology is increasingly rigid and computable. They do not, however, supply the missing first step of proving that a positively curved manifold must have any nontrivial smooth circle action. In particular, finite $(\mathbb{Z} / 2)^{r}$ symmetry does not imply continuous circle symmetry.

There are two possible routes to the problem. A topological route would show that any manifold supporting positive sectional curvature must lie in a class of smooth manifolds admitting effective circle actions. An analytic route would try to produce an isometric circle action from the metric itself, equivalently a nonzero Killing field after changing neither the manifold nor the metric. Li and Liu [LL] proposed an approach of the latter kind: they introduced a geometric heat flow for vector fields on closed manifolds with positive sectional curvature, with the goal of detecting or producing Killing fields. The flow gives criteria for the existence of Killing fields, but it has not yet resolved the general circle-action problem.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Does there exist a compact positively curved manifold which admits no effective smooth $\mathbb{S}^{1}$-action? This is the direct negation of Problem 4 and is stronger than asking for a particular positive-curvature metric with finite isometry group.
\item[2.] Original direction. Does every compact positively curved manifold admit some positive-curvature metric with a nontrivial isometric circle action? This is stronger than Yau's smooth-action question but closer to the structure of all known examples.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Can the Li-Liu heat flow be strengthened to force convergence to a nonzero Killing field under positive sectional curvature [LL]? This is an analytic version of the circle-action problem.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify compact positively curved manifolds with small isometry group. The highsymmetry case is much better understood [GS, Wil2, PS, Ke, KWW], so the low-symmetry regime is the natural frontier.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine which restrictions coming from torus symmetry have analogues for discrete abelian symmetry. The results of Kennard-Khalili Samani-Searle [KKS] suggest that discrete symmetry can sometimes substitute for continuous torus symmetry, but it is not a replacement for an effective circle action on the manifold.
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Develop topological tests, using characteristic classes, fixed-point data, and rational homotopy, which could decide whether a proposed positive-curvature manifold admits any effective circle action before one tries to construct a symmetric metric.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 5}

Is there an example of a compact, simply-connected manifold with nonnegative Ricci curvature that does not admit a metric with nonnegative sectional curvature?

Status. Solved affirmatively by Sha and Yang. Their 1989 paper already constructed compact simply connected manifolds with positive Ricci curvature, hence with nonnegative Ricci curvature, whose Betti numbers are too large to allow any metric with nonnegative sectional curvature [SY1]. Their 1991 theorem supplied the especially transparent connected-sum family used below [SY2]. Thus the problem is not merely solved by weakening sectional curvature to Ricci curvature; it is solved by examples showing that the two conditions have sharply different topological consequences.

The point is that positive Ricci curvature is much more flexible than nonnegative sectional curvature. Sha and Yang first constructed examples of manifolds with positive Ricci curvature [SY1], and then proved that connected sums of products $\mathbb{S}^{n} \times \mathbb{S}^{m}$ admit positive Ricci curvature [SY2], in the relevant simply connected range $n, m \geqslant 2$. For a fixed dimension $n+m$, taking more connected summands increases the middle Betti numbers while preserving positive Ricci curvature. In particular, connected sums of many copies of $\mathbb{S}^{2} \times \mathbb{S}^{2}$ give simply connected four-dimensional examples with positive Ricci curvature.

On the other hand, Gromov's Betti-number theorem [Gr] gives a bound, depending only on the dimension, for the total Betti number of a closed manifold with nonnegative sectional curvature. The Betti numbers of the Sha-Yang connected sums grow with the number of summands, while the dimension is fixed. Hence, for sufficiently many summands, these manifolds cannot admit any metric with nonnegative sectional curvature. This is the clean mechanism resolving Yau's question: Ricci positivity survives connected-sum constructions that sectional nonnegativity cannot tolerate because of Gromov's theorem.

Later work made the positive-Ricci side still more flexible. Burdick [Bu] developed core-metric techniques, based on Perelman's connected-sum ideas, and constructed Ricci-positive metrics on large classes of connected sums of projective spaces. The mechanism builds round convex boundaries on punctured summands, attaches them to a Ricci-positive "docking station," and uses Perelman's second-fundamental-form gluing criterion. Reiser [Re] extended the core-metric method to broad classes of fibre and projective bundles. Reiser and Wraith [RW] proved that in dimension $d \geqslant 5$ a Gromov-type Betti-number bound fails for $\operatorname{Ric}_{k}>0$ whenever $k \geqslant\lfloor d / 2\rfloor+2$, using doubly warped surgery metrics, an algebraic positivity criterion, and a smoothing theorem. These developments sharpen the lesson of Problem 5: the transition from sectional curvature to Ricci-type positivity is not a small technical weakening, but a substantial change in the topology allowed by curvature.

The newest Ricci-positive constructions strengthen this conclusion. Reiser [Re] proves that core metrics can be lifted along broad classes of fibre bundles and applies this to represent all classes in the torsion-free oriented bordism ring by connected manifolds with positive Ricci curvature. In a related paper, Reiser [ReT] proves that positive Ricci curvature can be lifted through twisted suspensions, with applications to simply connected manifolds with large symmetry rank and prescribed homology. Reiser and Wraith [RW2] extend the connected-sum philosophy to positive intermediate Ricci curvature via $k$-core metrics. None of these results weakens the Sha-Yang solution of Yau's question; rather, they show that the positive-Ricci side has become an active construction theory, while the nonnegative-sectional side remains constrained by comparison topology.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Classify which simply connected manifolds with positive Ricci curvature can also carry nonnegative sectional curvature. Problem 5 shows that positive Ricci curvature alone is not enough [SY2, Gr].
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Find sharper topological obstructions to nonnegative sectional curvature beyond Gromov's total Betti-number bound [Gr]. This is closely related to Problem 3.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine the sharp threshold among intermediate Ricci curvature conditions at which Gromov-type Betti-number bounds begin or fail. Reiser-Wraith show failure in a range of positive intermediate Ricci curvatures [RW], while sectional nonnegativity retains Gromov's bound [Gr].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Which positive-Ricci connected-sum, fibre-bundle, twisted-suspension, or bordism constructions can be upgraded to positive intermediate Ricci curvature? Recent work of Reiser and Reiser-Wraith [Re, ReT, RW2] gives a concrete framework for this question.
\item[5.] AI-generated formulation. Determine whether large families produced by modern Ricci-positive connected-sum constructions admit any curvature positivity stronger than Ricci but still weak enough to allow unbounded Betti numbers [Bu, Re, RW, RW2].
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 6}

Do all vector bundles over a manifold with positive curvature admit a complete metric with nonnegative sectional curvature?
Status. Open for the original positive-curvature base question. The problem asks for a converse to the soul theorem in the special case where the soul itself has positive sectional curvature. There are important positive results for bundles over spheres and compact rank-one symmetric spaces, but there are also counterexamples and necessary conditions for natural extensions of the question when the base is merely nonnegatively curved, such as a flat torus.

The problem is rooted in the soul theorem of Cheeger and Gromoll [CG2]. Their theorem says that a complete noncompact manifold with nonnegative sectional curvature is diffeomorphic to the normal bundle of a compact totally convex soul. Problem 6 asks for a converse in a favorable case: if the base already admits positive sectional curvature, must every vector bundle over it occur as the total space of a complete nonnegatively curved metric? The positivity of the base is essential; without it the converse is known to fail in natural families.

The Ricci-curvature analogue is much more flexible. Nash [Na] studied positive Ricci curvature on fibre bundles, and later work on positive Ricci curvature shows that many bundle and connected-sum constructions are possible. Sectional curvature is more rigid because O'Neill's formulas, horizontal zero-curvature planes, and the choice of connection on the bundle all enter the curvature calculation. Thus the vector-bundle question is not just a topological existence problem; it is also a problem about constructing connection metrics or more flexible complete metrics with enough horizontal curvature.

Rigas [Ri] proved the stable sphere theorem: after adding a sufficiently large trivial summand, every real vector bundle over any sphere admits nonnegative sectional curvature. Grove and Ziller [GZ] obtained the decisive unstable low-dimensional results. Every vector bundle and every sphere bundle over $\mathbb{S}^{4}$ or $\mathbb{S}^{5}$ admits a complete metric with nonnegative sectional curvature. Their construction encodes a cohomogeneity-one manifold by the two disk bundles of its singular orbits, builds an invariant nonnegatively curved metric when those orbits have codimension two, and passes to associated bundles by Riemannian submersion. They also constructed such metrics for every three-dimensional and 88 of the 144 four-dimensional vector bundles over $\mathbb{S}^{7}$, together with the corresponding sphere bundles.

The broader nonnegative-base version is false. For flat torus bases, there are counterexamples, and Tapp [Ta] formulated differential conditions involving the normal connection curvature, the horizontal sectional curvature, and the Hessian of the vertical curvature which any vector bundle admitting a complete nonnegatively curved metric must satisfy. The inequality comes from the Hessian of the sectional curvature function at a mixed zero-curvature plane along the soul. A strict form is sufficient for positive curvature on the associated sphere bundle and, in the connection-metric case, for complete nonnegative curvature on the vector bundle. Thus the positivity of the base in the original question is not a harmless hypothesis. González-Álvaro [GA] proved a stable positive result: after adding a sufficiently large trivial bundle, every real or complex vector bundle over a compact rank-one symmetric space admits a complete metric with nonnegative sectional curvature. The proof identifies stable classes with reduced $K$-theory, realizes them by homogeneous bundles through representation rings, and equips those representatives with quotient metrics. This stable theorem is one of the closest modern approximations to Yau's original question.

Thus the present frontier has two different forms. The unstable problem asks whether the original bundle itself carries a complete nonnegatively curved metric. The stable problem asks for the minimal rank of a trivial summand after which such a metric exists. The González-Álvaro theorem answers the second question for compact rank-one symmetric spaces, but leaves the first question open even in several natural cases where the base has positive sectional curvature.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Does every vector bundle over a sphere admit a complete metric with nonnegative sectional curvature? The cases $\mathbb{S}^{4}$ and $\mathbb{S}^{5}$, and many bundles over $\mathbb{S}^{7}$, are known [Ri, GZ].
\item[2.] Original direction. Does every vector bundle over every compact rank-one symmetric space admit such a metric without adding a trivial bundle? González-Álvaro proves this after stabilization [GA].
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Find intrinsic topological obstructions for a vector bundle over a positively curved base to admit complete nonnegative sectional curvature. Tapp's conditions [Ta] are an important step, but a classification is not known.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Compare connection metrics with more general complete metrics on the total space: are Tapp-type differential conditions also close to sufficient after allowing deformations away from connection metrics [Ta]?
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine the minimal stable rank in González-Álvaro's theorem [GA]: for a given real or complex bundle over a compact rank-one symmetric space, how many trivial summands are actually necessary before a complete nonnegatively curved metric exists?
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Develop computable characteristic-class tests predicting the minimal rank of a trivial summand needed before a given vector bundle admits nonnegative sectional curvature, extending the stable estimates in [GA].
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 7}
(Chern). Let $M$ be a compact, positively curved manifold. Is it true that every abelian subgroup of $\pi_{1}(M)$ is cyclic?
Status. False. Shankar gave counterexamples in 1998 [S]: compact positively curved manifolds can have noncyclic abelian subgroups in their fundamental groups. Thus Chern's proposed analogue of the negative-curvature cyclicity theorem fails, although the broader problem of classifying fundamental groups in positive sectional curvature remains open.

Chern's question was motivated by two classical comparison pictures. For compact negatively curved manifolds, Preissmann's theorem [P] implies that every nontrivial abelian subgroup of the fundamental group is infinite cyclic. For constant positive curvature, the spherical space-form problem restricts the possible finite groups acting freely on spheres [W]. Since Myers' theorem already implies that the fundamental group of a compact positively curved manifold is finite, Chern asked whether positive sectional curvature might force the abelian subgroups of this finite group to be cyclic, as happens for spherical space-form groups in many familiar cases.

The answer is negative. Shankar [S] constructed positively curved examples whose finite fundamental groups contain noncyclic abelian subgroups. The construction is related to finite quotients of positively curved homogeneous spaces, including Aloff-Wallach type examples. Grove, Shankar and Ziller [GSZ] later produced further counterexamples through Eschenburg spaces and biquotient constructions, showing that the failure of Chern's proposed cyclicity condition is not isolated and is compatible with the symmetry-rich world of known positive-curvature examples. More precisely, for every finite subgroup $\Gamma \subset \operatorname{SO}(3)$ they construct infinitely many positively curved Eschenburg spaces on which $\Gamma$ acts freely and isometrically. The quotients therefore realize $\Gamma$ as fundamental group; taking $\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}_{2} \oplus \mathbb{Z}_{2}$ gives explicit Abelian counterexamples.

Nevertheless, the broader classification problem for fundamental groups of positively curved manifolds remains very much open. Synge's theorem gives strong restrictions in even dimensions, and Myers' theorem gives finiteness of the fundamental group in all dimensions. Under additional torus symmetry, Kennard [Ke] obtained substantial classification results for fundamental groups of positively curved manifolds. Thus the correct post-Shankar problem is no longer cyclicity of abelian subgroups, but the classification of finite groups which can occur under positive sectional curvature, possibly with extra hypotheses such as dimension, orientability, symmetry rank, or cohomological restrictions. One concrete frontier is already visible in the known examples: $\mathbb{Z}_{3} \oplus \mathbb{Z}_{3}$ can occur, whereas it remains unknown whether $\mathbb{Z}_{p} \oplus \mathbb{Z}_{p}$ can occur for any prime $p \geqslant 5[\mathrm{Ke}]$.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Classify the finite groups which occur as fundamental groups of compact positively curved manifolds in each dimension. Shankar's counterexamples [S] show that the answer is broader than Chern's original conjecture, while Myers' theorem still forces finiteness.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify fundamental groups of positively curved manifolds under symmetry assumptions. Kennard's results [Ke] give a systematic picture in the torus-symmetry setting, but the general case remains open.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine which spherical space-form group restrictions survive for positively curved manifolds that are not space forms, and which fail in the homogeneous and biquotient examples [W, S, GSZ].
\item[4.] AI-generated formulation. Find the best replacement for Chern's cyclicity conjecture: what universal restrictions do positive sectional curvature, dimension, orientability, and symmetry rank impose on abelian subgroups of $\pi_{1}(M)$ ? This is based on [S, GSZ, Ke].
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Decide whether a positively curved manifold can have fundamental group containing $\mathbb{Z}_{p} \oplus \mathbb{Z}_{p}$ for a prime $p \geqslant 5$. The cases visible in the current constructions stop at $p=3[\mathrm{GSZ}, \mathrm{Ke}]$.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 8}

For an even-dimensional compact manifold with positive sectional curvature, prove that the Euler number is positive.

Status. Open as of 2026 in every even dimension at least six. The conjecture is classical in dimensions two and four, and it is known under a number of additional hypotheses, especially large symmetry, suitable fixed-point data, and rational-homotopy restrictions. The main lesson of the subject is that the Euler characteristic should be controlled by the global topology forced by positive curvature, not merely by the sign of a local curvature polynomial: Geroch showed that in dimensions at least six positive sectional curvature does not force the Gauss-Bonnet-Chern integrand to be positive [Ge].

The low-dimensional cases explain both the attraction and the limitation of the Gauss-Bonnet approach. For surfaces the assertion is exactly Gauss-Bonnet. In dimension four there is a very short topological proof. If $M^{4}$ is orientable and has positive sectional curvature, Synge's theorem implies that $M$ is simply connected. Thus $b_{1}(M)=b_{3}(M)=0$, and the Euler-Poincaré formula gives
$$\chi(M)=1-b_{1}(M)+b_{2}(M)-b_{3}(M)+b_{4}(M)=2+b_{2}(M)>0 .$$
If $M$ is nonorientable, the orientable double cover has positive sectional curvature and positive Euler characteristic, and $\chi(\widetilde{M})=2 \chi(M)$, so again $\chi(M)>0$. There is also a local four-dimensional proof: Milnor's unpublished observation, recorded by Chern, shows that the algebraic Hopf statement for the Gauss-Bonnet integrand is valid in dimension four [Ch2]. These two proofs are complementary. Synge's proof uses global topology; the Chern-Milnor proof uses the special four-dimensional curvature decomposition. Neither mechanism extends directly to arbitrary dimension.

Historically, Hopf's work on total curvature of hypersurfaces and Chern's intrinsic Gauss-Bonnet theorem made it natural to ask whether positive sectional curvature should force positivity of the Euler characteristic [Ho], [Ch1]. The strongest local version would assert that every algebraic curvature tensor with positive, or nonnegative, sectional curvature has positive, respectively nonnegative, Pfaffian. That statement is true in dimension four, but Geroch disproved it in every even dimension at least six [Ge]. Hence the remaining conjecture is genuinely global: the topology of a closed positively curved manifold may still force $\chi>0$, but this cannot be proved by pointwise positivity of the Gauss-Bonnet integrand alone.

There are important positive results outside the intrinsic low-dimensional case. Hopf's original extrinsic theorem gives positivity for closed positively curved hypersurfaces in Euclidean space, and Weinstein proved a codimension-two analogue for positively curved submanifolds of $\mathbb{R}^{n+2}$ [Ho], [We]. These results show that the conjectural sign is compatible with strong ambient realization hypotheses, although they do not address arbitrary intrinsic metrics.

The most productive intrinsic progress has come from symmetry. Hopf and Samelson proved that for compact homogeneous spaces $G / H$ the Euler characteristic is nonnegative, and is positive precisely when $\operatorname{rank} G=\operatorname{rank} H$ [HS]. Wallach's classification of compact homogeneous spaces with strictly positive sectional curvature then implies the Hopf conclusion for homogeneous positive-curvature examples [Wa]. Later work extended this philosophy beyond homogeneous spaces. Puttmann and Searle proved the conjecture for compact even-dimensional positively curved manifolds admitting an isometric compact Lie group action with principal isotropy $H$ and cohomogeneity $k$ satisfying
$$k-(\operatorname{rank} G-\operatorname{rank} H) \leqslant 5,$$
and also for certain high-symmetry torus actions [PS]. Kennard proved a logarithmic symmetry-rank version using Steenrod operations, and subsequent work with Amann obtained further restrictions on Euler characteristics under large torus symmetry [Ke], [AK].

A more recent development reduces part of the symmetry problem to representation theory. Kennard, Wiemeler and Wilking proved that the Hopf Euler-characteristic conjecture holds for positively curved even-dimensional manifolds whose isometry group has rank at least five [KWW1]. Their argument uses a splitting theorem for torus representations whose isotropy groups are connected, together with the connectedness and periodicity techniques that come from positive curvature. Nienhaus subsequently improved the conclusion from $T^{5}$ to $T^{4}$ symmetry [Ni]. His new four-periodicity theorem exploits the natural complex structure on the normal bundle of a circle fixed set: the periodicity-inducing Euler class becomes a Chern class, so characteristic classes and Steenrod operations force rational four-periodicity under a weaker codimension bound. A classification of the relevant $T^{4}$ weight systems then gives CROSS-type rational cohomology for every torus fixed-set component, and Berger's fixed-point theorem and Lefschetz yield $\chi(M)>0$. In later work, Kennard-Wiemeler-Wilking linked positive curvature with torus symmetry to regular matroids, obtaining new obstructions to positively curved metrics with torus symmetry [KWW2]. These results do not settle the general conjecture, but they mark a shift: the symmetry approach is no longer only a high-rank classification program; it now gives effective low-rank structural tools.

There are also recent results for discrete symmetry and for curvature conditions adjacent to positive sectional curvature. Bosgraaf, Escher and Searle lowered the rank assumptions in fixed-point-set theorems for $\mathbb{Z}_{2}$-tori acting on positively curved
manifolds, obtaining cohomological classification conclusions for fixed-point components [BES]. Kennard, Mouillé and Nienhaus studied Hopf-type Euler-characteristic questions under positive second intermediate Ricci curvature; they emphasize that the Hopf conclusion can fail under such relaxations, but prove a positive Euler-characteristic theorem in dimensions divisible by four under sufficiently large torus symmetry [KMN]. These papers are not solutions of the sectional curvature Hopf conjecture. They are useful because they show which parts of the symmetry method survive when one changes either the symmetry group or the curvature condition.

A complementary 2025 preprint of Huang and Wang [HW] treats curvature subclasses rather than symmetry. By decomposing the curvature operator into its Schouten-Ricci and Weyl parts, they prove the Hopf sign conclusions for compact locally conformally flat manifolds and under an explicit pointwise Weyl-pinching condition relative to a strict sectional-curvature bound. Integral Weyl bounds give further harmonic-form vanishing. Positive sectional curvature alone does not imply these Weyl smallness assumptions, so this is a new partial result rather than a solution of the general conjecture.

A third line connects the conjecture with the Bott-Grove-Halperin rational-ellipticity philosophy. The Bott-Grove-Halperin conjecture predicts that simply connected closed manifolds with nonnegative sectional curvature are rationally elliptic. Rational ellipticity gives strong constraints on Betti numbers and would provide a conceptual path toward the nonnegative Eulercharacteristic form of Hopf's conjecture [GrH], [FHT]. Amann and Kennard proved versions of Hopf's conjecture under the joint assumptions of rational ellipticity and torus symmetry, including positivity of $\chi(M)$ in broad logarithmic-symmetry regimes [AK2]. The missing bridge is still the curvature-to-rational-homotopy step: positive sectional curvature itself is not yet known to imply enough rational-homotopy structure to force the Euler sign.

The analogy with algebraic geometry is useful but should not be confused with the Riemannian problem. Bloch and Gieseker proved positivity of Chern classes for ample vector bundles [BG]. This supports the general theme that positive geometric curvature should force positivity of characteristic numbers, but it does not settle the Riemannian sectional-curvature conjecture.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Prove the positive-curvature Hopf conjecture in the first open case, real dimension six. A proof in dimension six would have to bypass Geroch's obstruction to pointwise Pfaffian positivity and identify a genuinely global positive-curvature mechanism [Ge].
\item[2.] Original direction. Prove the nonnegative version: if a compact even-dimensional manifold has nonnegative sectional curvature, then $\chi(M) \geqslant 0$. This is the natural weak-curvature companion of Hopf's conjecture and is tied to the Bott-Grove-Halperin rational-ellipticity conjecture [GrH], [FHT].
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Reduce the symmetry hypotheses in the known theorems. In particular, determine whether the Hopf conclusion follows from rank below four, from a single isometric circle action, or from structural restrictions on the fixed point set [PS], [Ke], [AK], [KWW1], [Ni], [BES].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend the Kennard-Wiemeler-Wilking representation-theoretic and matroid methods to torus actions with disconnected isotropy, lower rank, or weaker fixed-point data [KWW1, KWW2].
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether Hopf-type Euler-characteristic positivity under positive intermediate Ricci curvature is possible only with substantial symmetry, and identify the exact point at which sectional-curvature methods break down [KMN].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether the conjecture is true for all positively curved manifolds whose rational cohomology is concentrated in even degrees, and find geometric hypotheses which force that cohomological conclusion [GrH], [FHT], [AK2].
\item[7.] AI-generated formulation. Identify curvature cones stronger than positive sectional curvature but weaker than positive curvature operator for which the Gauss-Bonnet-Chern integrand is pointwise nonnegative in all even dimensions. Geroch's examples show that ordinary positive sectional curvature is insufficient [Ge].
\item[8.] Reference-driven formulation. Remove or weaken the Weyl smallness assumptions in the curvature-operator approach, especially in dimension six, where the conjecture first remains open [HW].
\item[9.] AI-generated formulation. Develop a rational-homotopy criterion, usable directly from positive sectional curvature, which rules out odd rational cohomology and hence forces positive Euler characteristic in the rationally elliptic case [GrH], [FHT], [AK2].
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 9}

Characterize the groups which can appear as the fundamental group of some compact manifold with negative curvature.
Status. Open. There is no known intrinsic group-theoretic characterization of the fundamental groups of closed negatively curved manifolds. If $M^{n}$ is closed and negatively curved, then $\pi_{1}(M)$ is a torsion-free word-hyperbolic Poincaré duality group of dimension $n$, and its Gromov boundary is homeomorphic to $\mathbb{S}^{n-1}$. These are powerful necessary conditions, but they are not a characterization: the converse realization problem for hyperbolic groups with sphere boundary is still open even in closely related forms.

The first restrictions are classical. By the Cartan-Hadamard theorem the universal cover is contractible, so a compact negatively curved manifold is a $K(\pi, 1)$ and its fundamental group is torsion-free. Preissmann proved in 1943 that every nontrivial abelian subgroup is infinite cyclic [P]. Yau strengthened this type of restriction by showing that subgroups containing subnormal abelian subgroups are cyclic; in particular, solvable subgroups are severely constrained [Y]. Milnor proved that the fundamental group has exponential growth [M1].

The next stage is geometric and dynamical. Margulis' thin-thick theory gives quantitative restrictions on small displacement subgroups and leads to strong information about conjugacy classes [Ma], [BGS]. Eberlein showed that such groups contain nontrivial free subgroups [E]. These results already show that the group must be large and nonamenable, while its amenable subgroups are small. In the later language of geometric group theory, the universal cover is a Gromov-hyperbolic space and $\pi_{1}(M)$ is a torsion-free word-hyperbolic group [G], [BH]. Consequently, every amenable subgroup is virtually cyclic; in the torsion-free closed negatively curved case, it is cyclic.

Gromov's 1987 theory of hyperbolic groups changed the form of the problem. It gives a broad necessary framework, but it does not solve the realization question: not every torsion-free hyperbolic group is known, or expected, to be the fundamental group of a smooth closed manifold with a negatively curved metric. The boundary at infinity sharpens the obstruction. Since the universal cover of a closed negatively curved $n$-manifold is diffeomorphic to $\mathbb{R}^{n}$ and has visual boundary $\mathbb{S}^{n-1}$, the Gromov boundary of the group is a sphere. Bestvina-Mess related the cohomology of a hyperbolic group to the topology of its boundary; in particular, Poincaré duality for the group is reflected in homological properties of the boundary [BM]. Thus Yau's problem can be rephrased as a realization problem inside the much smaller class of torsion-free hyperbolic Poincaré duality groups with spherical boundary.

This boundary formulation also explains why the problem is close to major open questions in geometric topology. In dimension three, geometrization gives a sharp answer for genuine 3-manifold groups: closed hyperbolic 3-manifold groups are exactly the fundamental groups of closed 3-manifolds carrying hyperbolic geometry. But the purely group-theoretic recognition problem remains subtler. Cannon's conjecture asks whether a word-hyperbolic group with boundary $\mathbb{S}^{2}$ acts geometrically on hyperbolic 3-space; Markovic reduced this to the existence of enough quasiconvex surface subgroups [CS], [Mar]. In higher dimensions, spherical boundary by itself is still not a known Riemannian negative-curvature realization criterion.

Recent work has clarified how much extra structure must be added to a spherical-boundary hyperbolic group before one can hope for a manifold realization theorem. Bregman and Incerti-Medici [BIM] proved a higher dimensional Cannon-type result for torsion-free hyperbolic groups with boundary $\mathbb{S}^{n-1}$, for $n \neq 5$, assuming the existence of sufficiently many quasiconvex codimension-one subgroups whose locally flat spherical limit sets separate boundary points. Their proof uses this system to cubulate the group, passes to malnormal finite-index data, builds an equivariant boundary cell complex, and invokes generalized Schoenflies and topological rigidity. Their conclusion is a virtual closed orientable aspherical manifold realization, not a smooth Riemannian metric of negative sectional curvature. This distinction is important for Yau's problem: current geometric group theory can sometimes turn boundary and cubulation data into manifold topology, but the additional step from aspherical manifold group to negatively curved Riemannian manifold remains a separate and much stronger requirement.

Locally symmetric examples provide important test cases. For irreducible locally symmetric spaces of noncompact type and dimension greater than two, Borel's theorem and Mostow rigidity imply strong restrictions, for example finiteness of the outer automorphism group in the locally symmetric case [B]. However, Mostow and Siu constructed compact Kähler surfaces of negative sectional curvature not homotopy equivalent to locally symmetric spaces [MS]. Thus locally symmetric groups do not exhaust the negatively curved Kähler or Riemannian possibilities. Hyperbolic arithmetic constructions of Millson and Vinberg show that first Betti number phenomena also vary substantially among constant-curvature examples [Mil], [Vi].

The construction side has also expanded far beyond locally symmetric spaces. Charney-Davis strict hyperbolization produces many locally CAT(-1) aspherical spaces and manifolds [CD]. Ontaneda's Riemannian hyperbolization then showed that, for smooth input in the appropriate dimensions, one can smooth the strict hyperbolization construction to obtain closed smooth manifolds with sectional curvatures pinched in $[-1-\epsilon,-1][\mathrm{O}]$. The construction chooses hyperbolization pieces with large normal widths, uses compatible normal smooth structures on links, and combines hyperbolic extension, warp forcing, and two-variable deformation to obtain uniform $C^{2}$ curvature control. It also produces a degree-one map to the input manifold which injects cohomology and preserves rational Pontryagin classes. These results give large new families of negatively curved
manifold groups and show that locally symmetric lattices are only a small part of the landscape. They do not, however, provide an intrinsic test telling whether a given hyperbolic group is realized by a smooth closed negatively curved manifold.

The Kähler version remains especially delicate. If a compact negatively curved manifold is also Kähler, one can ask whether some finite cover must have nonzero first Betti number. This is not known in general. Results on Kähler groups and negatively pinched groups give obstructions in special settings, but they do not yet amount to a characterization [JY].

Related open problems.
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Characterize which torsion-free word-hyperbolic Poincaré duality groups with boundary $\mathbb{S}^{n-1}$ are realized by closed smooth manifolds with negative sectional curvature [G], [BH], [BM].
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether every compact negatively curved Kähler manifold has a finite cover with $b_{1}>0$, or produce a geometric obstruction to this expectation [MS], [JY].
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Decide whether finiteness of $\operatorname{Out}\left(\pi_{1} M\right)$ holds for all closed negatively curved manifolds in the same generality as in the irreducible locally symmetric case [B], [MS].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. In dimension three, relate the group-recognition problem for negatively curved manifolds to Cannon's conjecture and to the existence of quasiconvex surface subgroups [CS], [Mar].
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. In higher dimensions, determine whether the cubulated spherical-boundary realization results of Bregman-Incerti-Medici [BIM] can be strengthened from virtual aspherical manifold realization to smooth closed manifolds admitting negatively curved metrics, or whether new smooth/geometric obstructions appear.
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Resolve the missing $n=5$ case in the higher-dimensional Cannon-type manifold-realization theorem, and determine whether local flatness of the separating limit spheres follows from more intrinsic cubulation or boundary hypotheses [BIM].
\item[7.] AI-generated formulation. Find a purely group-theoretic criterion, stable under quasi-isometry, which distinguishes fundamental groups of smooth closed negatively curved manifolds from general torsion-free word-hyperbolic groups with sphere boundary [G], [BH], [O].
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 10}

As a continuation of the previous problem, let $M^{2 N}$ be a compact manifold with negative curvature. Is it true that $(-1)^{N} \chi(M)>$ 0?

Status. Open as of 2026 in general, with the first open strictly negative-curvature case in real dimension six. The conjecture is known in real dimensions two and four, for locally symmetric examples, and in several Kähler or pinched settings. It is the negative-curvature half of the Hopf sign problem: positive sectional curvature should force $\chi>0$, while negative sectional curvature on a closed $2 N$-manifold should force $(-1)^{N} \chi>0$.

The two-dimensional case is exactly Gauss-Bonnet. In real dimension four the Chern-Gauss-Bonnet integrand has a special sign structure under nonpositive sectional curvature; consequently a closed nonpositively curved four-manifold satisfies $\chi \geqslant 0$, and the strictly negative case gives $\chi>0[\mathrm{Ch}]$. This is a genuinely low-dimensional phenomenon. Geroch showed that in every even dimension at least six the Gauss-Bonnet-Chern integrand is not controlled pointwise by the sign of sectional curvature [Ge]. Thus, as in the positive-curvature Hopf problem, a proof in higher dimensions must use global geometry or topology rather than only a local Pfaffian sign.

Locally symmetric examples form the basic model class. For compact locally symmetric spaces of noncompact type, Hirzebruch proportionality relates characteristic numbers to those of the compact dual, and gives the expected Euler sign [Hi]. Products of negatively curved surfaces give the simplest non-locally-irreducible examples: the sign follows immediately from multiplicativity of Euler characteristic. These model cases suggest the right answer, but they also hide the main difficulty: variable negative curvature has far less algebraic structure than local symmetry.

The analytic route is the Singer $L^{2}$-cohomology philosophy. Since a closed negatively curved manifold is aspherical by Cartan-Hadamard, its Euler characteristic can be studied through the universal cover. Singer's conjecture predicts that for a closed aspherical manifold,
$$b_{p}^{(2)}(\widetilde{M})=0 \quad \text { if } 2 p \neq \operatorname{dim} M .$$
For a closed negatively curved manifold one expects, in addition, positivity of the middle $L^{2}$-Betti number in even dimensions. If this vanishing and middle-dimensional positivity hold, then Atiyah's $L^{2}$ index theorem for coverings gives
$$\chi(M)=\sum_{p}(-1)^{p} b_{p}^{(2)}(\widetilde{M}),$$
and the desired sign follows [At].
The Kähler case is the most complete analytic success story. Gromov introduced Kähler-hyperbolic manifolds and proved that a compact Kähler-hyperbolic manifold of complex dimension $n$ satisfies $(-1)^{n} \chi(M)>0$ [Gr]. Compact Kähler manifolds with negative sectional curvature are Kähler-hyperbolic, so the conjecture is true in that case. Cao-Xavier and Jost-Zuo extended the $L^{2}$-Hodge method to prove $(-1)^{n} \chi(M) \geqslant 0$ for closed nonpositively curved Kähler manifolds under their hypotheses [CX], [JZ]. In algebraic geometry, Bloch-Gieseker's positivity theorem for ample vector bundles gives a parallel positivity phenomenon for characteristic classes [BG]. The open Riemannian problem is therefore not a failure of the $L^{2}$ idea, but the lack of a Kähler package: without type decomposition, Lefschetz operators, and complex positivity, the middle-degree concentration is much harder to force.

There are also partial Riemannian vanishing theorems. Under suitable pinching or curvature-decay hypotheses, Jost-Xin and Ballmann-Brüning proved vanishing results for $L^{2}$ harmonic forms outside the middle degree [JX], [BB]. These results support Singer's route, but they do not yet cover arbitrary closed negatively curved manifolds. Recent work on $L^{2}$-Betti numbers for groups with hierarchies and for Dehn-filling constructions gives additional evidence for the Hopf-Singer viewpoint in large topological classes, but these methods still do not give a general sectional-curvature theorem [OS], [PSu]. Petrosyan and Sun [PSu] construct a classifying space for group-theoretic Dehn fillings and combine quantitative L"uck approximation, the Cohen-Lyndon property, special-quotient theorems, and Atiyah-conjecture discreteness to prove stability of $L^{2}$-Betti numbers for broad deep fillings. As an application they obtain Anderson-type closed Einstein manifolds satisfying the Singer conjecture. These special Einstein examples need not have negative sectional curvature, so they test the $L^{2}$ mechanism without settling Yau's geometric case.

The topological analogue is the aspherical Euler-sign problem, often called the Hopf-Thurston sign conjecture: if $M^{2 N}$ is closed and aspherical, should $(-1)^{N} \chi(M) \geqslant 0$ hold? Kan-Thurston's theorem shows that aspherical models are extremely flexible homologically [KT], while the Charney-Davis conjecture gives a combinatorial nonpositive-curvature version for flag triangulations and piecewise Euclidean manifolds [CD]. These topological forms are related to, but distinct from, the strict Riemannian negative-curvature problem. A negative-curvature proof would be stronger than a purely topological theorem for that geometric class, but it might use analytic information invisible to the fundamental group alone.

Recent work also clarifies the equality case in low-dimensional nonpositive-curvature geometry. Connell-Ruan-Wang proved that if a closed nonpositively curved Riemannian 4-manifold has $\chi=0$, then the Ricci tensor must degenerate somewhere, and
in the analytic case the universal cover has a nontrivial Euclidean de Rham factor [CRW]. Their proof uses pointwise Pfaffian vanishing to isolate a distinguished Ricci eigenline, varies the metric in that direction to force a totally geodesic flat orthogonal foliation, and finishes with a divergence argument and analytic rank rigidity. Kim-Wan subsequently proved that a closed nonpositively curved four-manifold with nonzero Euler characteristic obeys the quantitative inequality
$$\|M\| \geqslant \frac{1}{11}|\chi(M)|,$$
and in particular obtained a four-dimensional positivity result under negative Ricci curvature [KW]. These theorems do not settle the higher-dimensional negative-curvature problem, but they sharpen the boundary between strict negativity, flat directions, Euler characteristic, and simplicial volume. Their proof straightens a fundamental cycle to geodesic simplices and applies the Allendoerfer-Weil Gauss-Bonnet formula simplex by simplex, bounding all face and angle contributions by the $\ell^{1}$-norm.

Related open problems.
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Prove the conjecture in real dimension six. This is the first dimension in which the four-dimensional Chern-Gauss-Bonnet sign argument and all pointwise Pfaffian strategies fail [Ch], [Ge].
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Prove Singer's $L^{2}$-Betti-number vanishing conjecture for all closed negatively curved manifolds, and prove positivity of the middle $L^{2}$-Betti number in even dimensions [At], [Lu].
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Find a higher-dimensional replacement for the four-dimensional Chern-Gauss-Bonnet sign argument, or prove that no pointwise curvature-polynomial refinement can exist beyond the known low-dimensional cases [Ch], [Ge].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend the Kähler-hyperbolic argument beyond the Kähler setting: find a geometric condition implied by negative sectional curvature that forces middle-dimensional $L^{2}$ cohomology and the Hopf sign [Gr], [CX], [JZ].
\item[5.] Original direction. Determine whether the Hopf-Thurston Euler-sign conjecture holds for all closed aspherical manifolds, or at least for all closed smooth manifolds admitting a nonpositively curved metric [KT], [CD].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. In real dimension four, understand whether the Connell-Ruan-Wang rigidity picture for $\chi=0$ has smooth, nonanalytic, or higher-dimensional analogues; in particular, decide when Euler-characteristic equality forces a Euclidean de Rham factor [CRW], [KW].
\item[7.] Reference-driven formulation. Find a six-dimensional analogue of the Kim-Wan geodesic-simplex estimate which controls the signed Euler form, rather than only an absolute simplicial-volume contribution [KW].
\item[8.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether the $L^{2}$-Betti stability methods for deep Dehn fillings can be combined with pinched negatively curved constructions, producing new geometric test families for the Hopf sign [PSu].
\item[9.] AI-generated formulation. Develop a pinching-free Bochner or coarse-index method which proves the $L^{2}$ vanishing outside the middle degree for universal covers of closed negatively curved manifolds [JX], [BB].
\end{itemize}

[BB] W. Ballmann and J. Brüning, On the spectral theory of manifolds with cusps, J. Math. Pures Appl. (9) 80 (2001), no. 6, 593-625.
