\section*{Problem 11}

The Cohn-Vossen inequality says that the total curvature of a complete surface is dominated by its Euler number. Finn [Fi] and Huber [Hu] studied the difference of these numbers in terms of geometric quantities. The question is how to generalize this inequality to higher dimensions. If $M$ is a complete manifold with finite volume and bounded curvature, when is the Euler number equal to the Gauss-Bonnet-Chern integral? If the manifold is locally symmetric, this is true and due to Harder [Ha]. It can be proved that the assertion is true if the curvature of $M$ is bounded between two negative constants. In a private conversation, Gromov claimed that the Gauss-Bonnet-Chern integral is an integer and is the Euler number if one merely assumes that the curvature is nonpositive and the metric is real analytic.

If $M$ is complete and has nonnegative curvature, Poor [Po], R. Walter [Wa] and Greene and Wu (Theorem 9 of [GW]) proved that the Cohn-Vossen inequality holds true if $\operatorname{dim} M=4$. What is the geometric constraint on $M$ if the equality holds? What happens when $\operatorname{dim} M=2 n$ with $n>2$ ?

Status. Open in the general form. The problem should be separated into two related but different questions: a finite-volume Gauss-Bonnet-Chern equality problem, and a nonnegative-curvature Cohn-Vossen inequality/equality problem. Both are understood in several important model geometries, but there is still no general end-geometry criterion in all even dimensions.

In dimension two, Cohn-Vossen's inequality says that for a complete noncompact surface of finite total curvature the curvature integral is bounded above by the Euler characteristic [CV]. Finn and Huber refined the missing term: the defect between the Euler number and the curvature integral is measured by the geometry of the ends [Fi], [Hu]. The higher-dimensional problem is therefore not merely to integrate the Pfaffian curvature form. One must also identify the boundary transgression terms produced by an exhaustion and prove that their limits are intrinsic invariants of the ends.

For complete finite-volume manifolds with bounded curvature, Cheeger-Gromov gave a powerful open-manifold Gauss-Bonnet theorem in the language of $L^{2}$-cohomology and von Neumann dimension [CG]. This explains why Yau's question is close to, but not identical with, the compact Gauss-Bonnet-Chern theorem: the natural analytic invariant at infinity is an $L^{2}$ Euler characteristic, and the comparison with the ordinary Euler number requires topological and geometric control of the ends. The technical heart of this comparison is the construction of good exhaustions with controlled second fundamental form and curvature transgression terms; the Schoen-Yau exhaustion construction and Dafermos' account are useful references for this point [SY], [D]. Finite-volume locally symmetric spaces form the cleanest positive class, by Harder's arithmetic Gauss-Bonnet formula [Ha].

The negatively curved and nonpositively curved cases should also be kept distinct. Under pinched negative curvature and finite volume, the ends have enough controlled asymptotic geometry for the expected Gauss-Bonnet-Chern identity to hold. Gromov's private real-analytic nonpositive-curvature claim, as quoted by Yau, is stronger and should still be recorded as a problem unless one imposes hypotheses which force sufficiently rigid ends. In the smooth nonpositive-curvature category there are many possible cusp and collapse phenomena, so the integer-valuedness of the Pfaffian integral is itself part of the difficulty.

The nonnegative-curvature direction is a different Cohn-Vossen problem. Poor, Walter, and Greene-Wu proved fourdimensional inequalities under nonnegative curvature hypotheses [Po], [Wa], [GW]. Equality should mean that all boundary defect terms at infinity vanish. In dimension two this is a strong asymptotic statement about the ends; in dimension four and higher its geometric meaning is not yet classified. A natural expectation is that equality forces strong splitting, cone, or flatness behavior at infinity, but there is no general theorem in dimensions $2 n>4$.

Several structured theories now give the best evidence for the correct formulation. Chang-Qing-Yang obtained fourdimensional conformally flat end formulae in which the Chern-Gauss-Bonnet defect is explicitly expressed through asymptotic quantities [CQY1], [CQY2]. Fang proved a conformal Gauss-Bonnet-Chern inequality for complete locally conformally flat manifolds with integrable $Q$-curvature or Pfaffian curvature hypotheses [Fa]. Buzano-Nguyen extended this circle to noncompact four-manifolds with conformally flat ends and isolated singularities, where the error terms are isoperimetric deficits, and then to all even dimensions in the conformally flat singular-end setting [BN], [BN2]. These results strongly support the view that the right high-dimensional replacement of the Finn-Huber defect is an explicit end invariant.

There is also recent progress on scalar-curvature versions of the Cohn-Vossen theme. Deng proved Cohn-Vossen-type scalar-curvature inequalities for complete noncompact manifolds with nonnegative Ricci curvature under additional topological, decay, locally conformally flat, or Bakry-Emery hypotheses [De]. Ma proved a locally conformally flat theory for complete noncompact manifolds with nonnegative Ricci curvature, verifying the scalar-curvature form of Yau's Cohn-Vossen conjecture in that class and proving a sharp gap theorem [Ma]. The mechanisms are particularly instructive. In Deng's topological case, a subgroup $\mathbb{Z}^{n-2} \subset \pi_{1}(M)$ forces the universal cover to split an $\mathbb{R}^{n-2}$ factor, reducing the estimate to the classical surface Cohn-Vossen theorem. In Ma's conformally flat case, a refined singularity theorem for $n$-superharmonic functions recovers the logarithmic mass of the conformal factor and identifies it with the asymptotic volume ratio $v$. For $n>3$ this gives the exact
formula
$$\lim _{r \rightarrow \infty} r^{2-n} \int_{B_{r}(p)} R_{g} d \mu_{g}=(n-1)\left(v^{\frac{n-3}{n-1}}-v\right)\left|S^{n-1}\right|$$
These results are quite close in spirit to the original question, but they are scalar or conformally flat substitutes for the full Gauss-Bonnet-Chern problem. The remaining task is to turn such estimates into a Chern-Weil formula with computable boundary terms in arbitrary even dimension.

Related open problems.
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Give necessary and sufficient end-geometry conditions under which a complete finite-volume boundedcurvature $2 n$-manifold satisfies
$$\chi(M)=\int_{M} e(T M, \nabla),$$
where $e(T M, \nabla)$ is the Gauss-Bonnet-Chern form [CG], [D], [SY].
\item[2.] Original direction. Classify equality cases in the known four-dimensional Cohn-Vossen inequalities under nonnegative curvature [Po], [Wa], [GW].
\item[3.] Original direction. Decide whether equality in the nonnegative-curvature Cohn-Vossen inequality forces splitting, asymptotic flatness, asymptotic conical structure, or vanishing of an explicitly defined end defect.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend the Chang-Qing-Yang conformally flat four-dimensional end-defect formula to higher even dimensions or to broader curvature classes [CQY1], [CQY2], [Fa], [BN], [BN2], [Ma].
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether the recent scalar-curvature Cohn-Vossen inequalities under Ricci, locally conformally flat, or Bakry-Emery hypotheses can be lifted to full Chern-Weil boundary formulae in even dimensions [De], [Ma].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Clarify the relation between Cheeger-Gromov's $L^{2}$ Gauss-Bonnet theorem and the ordinary Euler characteristic for finite-volume bounded-curvature manifolds with collapsed or nonstandard ends [CG].
\item[7.] AI-generated formulation. Formulate an intrinsic "isoperimetric deficit at infinity" in dimension $2 n$ whose sum over ends is exactly the difference between $\chi(M)$ and the Gauss-Bonnet-Chern integral, reducing to Finn's formula in dimension two [Fi], [CQY1], [BN], [BN2].
\end{itemize}

\begin{itemize}
\item[] [BN] R. Buzano and H. T. Nguyen, The Chern-Gauss-Bonnet formula for singular non-compact four-dimensional manifolds, J. Geom. Anal. 29 (2019), no. 2, 1382-1406; arXiv:1503.06602.
\item[] [BN2] R. Buzano and H. T. Nguyen, The higher-dimensional Chern-Gauss-Bonnet formula for singular conformally flat manifolds, J. Geom. Anal. 29 (2019), no. 2, 1043-1074; arXiv:1703.05723.

\section*{Problem 12}

Let $M_{1}$ and $M_{2}$ each have negative curvature. If $\pi_{1}\left(M_{1}\right)=\pi_{1}\left(M_{2}\right)$, prove that $M_{1}$ is diffeomorphic to $M_{2}$.
Status. False in the differentiable category in high dimensions. The correct positive statement is topological rigidity: under suitable dimension hypotheses, the fundamental group determines the manifold up to homeomorphism, but not up to diffeomorphism or PL homeomorphism.

The original motivation is Mostow rigidity. If the negatively curved manifolds are locally symmetric of rank one and dimension at least three, an isomorphism of fundamental groups is induced by an isometry. For general variable negative curvature this is too strong. Early progress of Cheeger-Gromov and Farrell-Hsiang showed that $\pi_{1}(M)$ determines various stabilized or associated objects; for example, Farrell-Hsiang proved stable topological information under nonpositive-curvature hypotheses [FH].

The decisive high-dimensional picture was developed by Farrell and Jones. On the positive side, they proved topological rigidity for compact nonpositively curved manifolds: in dimensions different from 3 and 4, if one manifold is closed nonpositively curved and the other is aspherical, then a homotopy equivalence is homotopic to a homeomorphism [FJ1], [FJ3]. Since closed negatively curved manifolds are aspherical by Cartan-Hadamard, this gives the expected homeomorphism conclusion in the relevant high-dimensional cases. In modern language this is part of the Borel-conjecture and Farrell-Jones assembly-map story. Bartels-Lück prove the required algebraic $K$ - and $L$-theory assembly isomorphisms by controlled methods on hyperbolic and CAT(0) flow spaces; the surgery exact sequence then collapses the topological structure set. In particular, they proved the Borel conjecture for word-hyperbolic groups and for CAT(0) groups [BL], so the homeomorphism-level rigidity expected from the fundamental group is now known for closed negatively curved manifolds in the standard high-dimensional topological category. In dimension three, geometrization and Mostow rigidity settle the closed hyperbolic case and give the corresponding topological rigidity picture for negatively curved 3-manifolds. Dimension four remains the exceptional dimension; even the homeomorphism form is tied to the difficult four-dimensional Borel-conjecture landscape.

On the negative side, Farrell and Jones constructed negatively curved manifolds with exotic smooth structures [FJ2]. Thus two closed negatively curved manifolds can be homeomorphic, and hence have the same fundamental group, without being diffeomorphic. Farrell-Jones-Ontaneda strengthened this phenomenon by constructing examples in dimensions greater than five which are not even PL-homeomorphic, while still carrying negatively curved metrics [FJO]. These examples show that the original differentiable statement of the problem cannot be true.

Farrell-Jones's Theorem 1.1 gives more than a qualitative counterexample. Starting from a closed real-hyperbolic manifold of dimension at least five, they pass to a finite cover and form connected sums with representatives of the relevant homotopysphere classes. Smoothing theory distinguishes the resulting differentiable structures, while a warping deformation inserts the exotic transition into a sufficiently long hyperbolic annulus. Making the annulus long makes every curvature error arbitrarily small, so the exotic smoothings carry metrics with sectional curvature in $(-1-\delta,-1+\delta)$ for any prescribed $\delta>0$ [FJ2]. This explains why neither strong pinching nor the fundamental group detects the smooth structure.

This problem should therefore be separated into three levels: homeomorphic rigidity, PL rigidity, and smooth rigidity. The homeomorphic version is a special case of the Borel conjecture for aspherical manifolds and is positive for the hyperbolic and CAT(0) group classes relevant here [BL]. The smooth and PL versions fail in high dimensions because exotic smooth and triangulation phenomena are compatible with negative curvature [FJ2], [FJO]. Related differentiable rigidity questions under stronger geometric assumptions, such as entropy or locally symmetric hypotheses, are treated by Besson-Courtois-Gallot and related work [BCG].

Later work shows that the failure of smooth rigidity is not just a bare existence phenomenon. Farrell-Ontaneda proved that the space of nonpositively curved metrics on a negatively curved manifold can be highly disconnected, so the moduli of curvature-constrained metrics remembers more than the fundamental group [FO]. Bustamante-Tshishiku studied symmetries of exotic negatively curved manifolds homeomorphic but not diffeomorphic to hyperbolic manifolds, producing examples where the hyperbolic isometry group acts smoothly and examples where large subgroups cannot act smoothly [BT]. Thus the modern refinement of Yau's question asks not only whether $\pi_{1}$ determines the underlying topological manifold, but how much differentiable and symmetry information can survive under negative curvature.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Reference-driven formulation. Prove the homeomorphism version in dimension four for closed negatively curved manifolds, or identify the precise four-dimensional obstruction. This is the remaining low-dimensional edge of the Borel-rigidity picture [FJ3], [BL].
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify which exotic smooth structures on a given closed negatively curved topological manifold admit negatively curved metrics [FJ2], [FJO].
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[3.] Original direction. Determine geometric hypotheses stronger than negative curvature, but weaker than local symmetry, under which an isomorphism of fundamental groups forces diffeomorphism or isometry rather than only homeomorphism [BCG], [BL].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Study the moduli space of negatively curved metrics on a fixed topological manifold and determine whether different smooth structures can lie in naturally comparable geometric components [FJ2], [FJO], [FO].
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine which finite isometry groups or finite group actions on a hyperbolic manifold can be realized smoothly on exotic negatively curved smoothings, and which exotic smoothings force symmetry breaking [BT].
\end{itemize}

References.
[BCG] G. Besson, G. Courtois and S. Gallot, Entropies et rigidités des espaces localement symétriques de courbure strictement négative, Geom. Funct. Anal. 5 (1995), no. 5, 731-799.
\begin{itemize}
\item[] [BL] A. Bartels and W. Lück, The Borel conjecture for hyperbolic and CAT(0)-groups, Ann. of Math. (2) 175 (2012), no. 2, 631-689; arXiv:0901.0442.
\item[] [BT] M. Bustamante and B. Tshishiku, Symmetries of exotic negatively curved manifolds, arXiv:1901.00815.
\item[] [FH] F. T. Farrell and W. C. Hsiang, On Novikov's conjecture for nonpositively curved manifolds. I, Ann. of Math. (2) 113 (1981), no. 1, 199-209.
\item[] [FJ1] F. T. Farrell and L. E. Jones, Compact negatively curved manifolds (of dim $\neq 3,4)$ are topologically rigid, Proc. Natl. Acad. Sci. USA 86 (1989), no. 10, 3461-3463.
\item[] [FJ2] F. T. Farrell and L. E. Jones, Negatively curved manifolds with exotic smooth structures, J. Amer. Math. Soc. 2 (1989), no. 4, 899-908.
\item[] [FJ3] F. T. Farrell and L. E. Jones, Topological rigidity for compact nonpositively curved manifolds, in Differential Geometry: Riemannian Geometry (Los Angeles, CA, 1990), 229-274, Proc. Sympos. Pure Math., 54, Part 3, Amer. Math. Soc., Providence, RI, 1993.
\item[] [FJO] F. T. Farrell, L. E. Jones and P. Ontaneda, Hyperbolic manifolds with negatively curved exotic triangulations in dimensions greater than five, J. Differential Geom. 48 (1998), no. 2, 319-322.

\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 13}

Let $M_{1}$ and $M_{2}$ be compact Einstein manifolds with negative curvature. Suppose $\pi_{1}\left(M_{1}\right) \cong \pi_{1}\left(M_{2}\right)$ and $\operatorname{dim} M_{1} \geqslant 3$. Is $M_{1}$ isometric to $M_{2}$ ?

Status. False as literally stated, because no scale normalization is imposed. If $g$ is a negatively curved Einstein metric, then $c g$ is again negatively curved and Einstein for every $c>0$, while $(M, g)$ and $(M, c g)$ are not isometric in general. The meaningful normalized version-fix the Einstein constant or the volume and ask for uniqueness up to diffeomorphism-is open in general. It is true for important locally symmetric models and locally in the moduli space of negatively curved Einstein metrics, but no general theorem says that the fundamental group determines the normalized Einstein metric.

The locally symmetric case is the model. If both manifolds are compact locally symmetric spaces of negative curvature and dimension at least three, Mostow's strong rigidity theorem shows that an isomorphism of fundamental groups is induced by an isometry after the natural normalization of scale [Mo]. This is stronger than topological rigidity and uses the symmetric-space structure, not merely the Einstein condition.

The harmonic-map approach gives another important class of rigidity theorems. Eells-Sampson proved existence of harmonic representatives for maps into nonpositively curved targets [ES]. Sampson and Siu developed Bochner-type rigidity results for harmonic maps; in particular, for compact Kähler manifolds with sufficiently negative curvature, a homotopy equivalence is forced to be holomorphic or anti-holomorphic, and hence becomes much closer to an isometry after imposing the relevant canonical metric condition [Sa], [Si]. This explains the older comment that strongly negative Kähler curvature is a setting where harmonic maps can prove biholomorphic rigidity.

Entropy rigidity provides a third line of partial progress. Besson, Courtois, and Gallot proved minimal entropy rigidity for negatively curved locally symmetric spaces, giving a powerful extension and new proof of Mostow-type rigidity [BCG]. These results are extremely strong when one of the manifolds is locally symmetric, but they do not settle the general case of two arbitrary negatively curved Einstein manifolds.

There are also four-dimensional and local-rigidity results which show both the strength and the limits of the Einstein condition. LeBrun used Seiberg-Witten theory to prove uniqueness results for Einstein metrics on compact quotients of irreducible four-dimensional symmetric spaces of noncompact type, giving a Riemannian version of Miyaoka-Yau type rigidity [Le]. Koiso's local rigidity theorem shows that compact Einstein metrics with negative sectional curvature have no nontrivial nearby Einstein deformations modulo diffeomorphism and scaling; Fine-Krasnov-Singer later proved a chiral four-dimensional generalization under negativity of the self-dual curvature operator [FKS]. Their proof recasts an oriented Einstein four-metric as a critical point of a pure-connection functional and proves positivity of its Hessian after removing gauge directions. These are local or special-model theorems, not a global classification of all negatively curved Einstein metrics with a fixed fundamental group.

The problem has become more subtle because negatively curved Einstein manifolds need not be locally homogeneous. Fine and Premoselli constructed compact Einstein four-manifolds with negative sectional curvature which are not locally homogeneous [FP]. These examples do not by themselves disprove the problem, but they show that the desired rigidity, if true, would have to apply well beyond the locally symmetric world. Their construction first interpolates between a smooth Einstein model near a codimension-two branch locus and a pulled-back hyperbolic metric, then uses uniform $L^{2}$ coercivity and a parameter-dependent inverse-function theorem to correct the small Einstein defect.

Very recent work strengthens this point in all dimensions at least four. Hamenstädt and Jäckel constructed infinitely many mutually non-homotopic closed $n$-manifolds, for every $n \geqslant 4$, which admit negatively curved Einstein metrics but admit no locally symmetric metric [HJ]. This does not produce two Einstein metrics with the same fundamental group, so it is not a counterexample to the normalized version of Yau's question. It does, however, show that the class of negatively curved Einstein manifolds is much larger than the locally symmetric class even at the level of homotopy types. Any solution of the problem must therefore combine topological rigidity with a genuinely Einstein-specific uniqueness mechanism.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Decide whether a compact negatively curved Einstein manifold is determined up to isometry by its fundamental group after fixing the Einstein constant or volume, under additional pinching assumptions [BCG], [FP].
\item[2.] Original direction. Prove or disprove uniqueness of negatively curved Einstein metrics on a fixed closed aspherical smooth manifold, up to scaling and diffeomorphism [FP].
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend harmonic-map rigidity from strongly negative Kähler curvature to broader Einstein negative-curvature settings [ES], [Sa], [Si].
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Relate local Einstein rigidity under negative sectional curvature to global uniqueness in a fixed aspherical homotopy type [FKS], [Le].
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether the Fine-Premoselli negatively curved Einstein four-manifolds admit any nontrivial Einstein deformations or any distinct Einstein metrics with the same fundamental group [FP], [FKS].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. For the Hamenstädt-Jäckel Gromov-Thurston Einstein manifolds, determine whether the negatively curved Einstein metric is unique up to scaling and diffeomorphism, and whether their fundamental groups can occur for more than one Einstein smooth model [HJ].
\item[7.] Reference-driven formulation. Decide whether every Einstein metric on a closed real-hyperbolic manifold is homothetic to the hyperbolic metric, as asked explicitly by Hamenstädt-Jäckel [HJ].
\end{itemize}

References.
[BCG] G. Besson, G. Courtois and S. Gallot, Entropies et rigidités des espaces localement symétriques de courbure strictement négative, Geom. Funct. Anal. 5 (1995), no. 5, 731-799.
[ES] J. Eells and J. H. Sampson, Harmonic mappings of Riemannian manifolds, Amer. J. Math. 86 (1964), 109-160.
[FKS] J. Fine, K. Krasnov and M. Singer, Local rigidity of Einstein 4-manifolds satisfying a chiral curvature condition, arXiv:1910.09790.
[FP] J. Fine and B. Premoselli, Examples of compact Einstein four-manifolds with negative curvature, J. Amer. Math. Soc. 33 (2020), no. 4, 991-1038; arXiv:1802.00608.
[HJ] U. Hamenstädt and F. Jäckel, Negatively curved Einstein metrics on Gromov-Thurston manifolds, arXiv:2411.12956.
[Le] C. LeBrun, Einstein metrics and Mostow rigidity, Math. Res. Lett. 2 (1995), no. 1, 1-8; arXiv:dg-ga/9411005.
[Mo] G. D. Mostow, Strong Rigidity of Locally Symmetric Spaces, Annals of Mathematics Studies, no. 78, Princeton University Press, 1973.
[Sa] J. H. Sampson, Some properties and applications of harmonic mappings, Ann. Sci. École Norm. Sup. (4) 11 (1978), no. 2, 211-228.
[Si] Y.-T. Siu, The complex-analyticity of harmonic maps and the strong rigidity of compact Kähler manifolds, Ann. of Math. (2) 112 (1980), no. 1, 73-111.

\section*{Problem 14}

Let $M$ be a compact manifold of dimension $N$. Can one find a positive constant $\delta_{N}$ depending only on $N$ so that $M$ is diffeomorphic to a manifold with constant negative curvature whenever the curvature of $M$ lies between $-1-\delta_{N}$ and -1?

Let $M$ be a compact manifold whose sectional curvature lies between -4 and -1. If $M$ is not diffeomorphic to a manifold with constant negative curvature, is $M$ locally symmetric?

Status. False in every dimension $N \geqslant 4$. Gromov-Thurston disproved the proposed dimension-only rigidity constant by constructing nonhyperbolic manifolds with curvature arbitrarily close to -1. Hamenstädt-Jäckel now give, in every dimension at least four, near-hyperbolic manifolds which are not even homeomorphic to a closed locally symmetric space and which nevertheless carry negatively curved Einstein metrics [HJ]. The low dimensions should be kept separate: surfaces are governed by uniformization, and in dimension three geometrization gives a much more rigid topological picture for closed negatively curved manifolds.

The first question asks whether sufficiently strong pinching near constant curvature forces a compact negatively curved manifold to be diffeomorphic to a hyperbolic manifold. Gromov and Thurston constructed, in dimensions at least four, closed manifolds with sectional curvatures arbitrarily close to -1 which are not diffeomorphic to any hyperbolic manifold [GT]. Thus there is no dimension-only pinching constant $\delta_{N}$ with the proposed conclusion once $N \geqslant 4$. This distinguishes the problem sharply from the two- and three-dimensional settings, where uniformization and geometrization leave far less room for exotic smooth or PL behavior.

The smooth and PL failures are even stronger. Farrell, Jones, and Ontaneda constructed hyperbolic manifolds with negatively curved exotic triangulations in dimensions greater than five [FJO]. This is closely related to the exotic smoothstructure phenomena in Problem 12: high dimensional negative curvature is compatible with topology and smooth structures that constant-curvature locally symmetric metrics cannot carry.

The second question is also false. Ontaneda's pinched smooth hyperbolization, later developed as Riemannian hyperbolization, constructs closed Riemannian manifolds with curvatures in intervals arbitrarily close to [-1, -1] which are not homeomorphic to locally symmetric spaces in the relevant high-dimensional range [O1], [O2]. Thus pinching alone does not force local symmetry.

Hamenstädt-Jäckel give a particularly sharp modern form of this failure. For every $n \geqslant 4$ and every $\varepsilon>0$, their Theorem 1 constructs infinitely many pairwise nonhomeomorphic closed $n$-manifolds which admit a metric with sectional curvature in $[-1-\varepsilon,-1+\varepsilon]$ and also an Einstein metric of negative sectional curvature, but are not homeomorphic to any closed locally symmetric space [HJ]. The proof combines arithmetic subgroup separability and codimension-two branched covers with a uniformly invertible gauge-fixed Einstein operator and an implicit-function argument. Thus even adding the Einstein condition does not rescue the second proposed rigidity statement.

The hyperbolization viewpoint has also produced flexible noncompact extensions. Belegradek showed that hyperbolization pulls back regular neighborhoods of PL submanifolds and, using Ontaneda's Riemannian hyperbolization, obtained open complete manifolds of pinched negative curvature which are homotopy equivalent to closed smooth manifolds but have no smooth spines, as well as examples related to nonsmoothable closed manifolds [Be]. These examples are not compact counterexamples to the first sentence of the problem, but they illustrate how little pinched negative curvature alone controls smooth or PL topology once one leaves the locally symmetric category.

There are, however, important positive results once extra structure is imposed. In the Kähler setting, quarter-pinching is far more rigid: Hernandez and Yau-Zheng proved complex hyperbolic rigidity results for negatively quarter-pinched Kähler manifolds [He], [YZ]. Recent work on almost quarter-pinched Kähler examples shows that the exact pinching threshold is a meaningful boundary, not merely a technical artifact [Mi].

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Reference-driven formulation. Identify additional geometric structures, such as Kähler, locally symmetric, or curvatureoperator assumptions, under which pinching near -1 does imply rigidity [He], [YZ].
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify which smooth or PL exotic structures on hyperbolic manifolds admit metrics pinched arbitrarily close to -1 [FJO], [O1].
\item[3.] Original direction. Determine sharp pinching thresholds for rigidity in the Kähler and complex-hyperbolic settings [He], [YZ], [Mi].
\item[4.] AI-generated formulation. Study whether Ontaneda's Riemannian hyperbolization examples can be distinguished from locally symmetric manifolds by quantitative invariants beyond topology, such as entropy, marked length spectrum, or bounded cohomology [O2].
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine which compact phenomena in the Gromov-Thurston and Ontaneda examples persist for complete open pinched negatively curved manifolds with controlled topology at infinity, and whether "no smooth spine" behavior has a compact analogue [Be], [O2].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine which Gromov-Thurston and smooth-hyperbolization manifolds admit negatively curved Einstein metrics, extending the Hamenstädt-Jäckel construction [HJ], [O1], [O2].
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 15}

Develop a useful notion of curvature for PL manifolds so that one could obtain appropriate pinching theorems and formulas for characteristic classes.

Status. Open. There are powerful curvature theories for polyhedral, piecewise-flat, Alexandrov, CAT, and more general singular spaces, but none yet supplies the full package asked for here: a PL notion of sectional curvature strong enough for pinching theorems and, at the same time, a Chern-Weil theory producing characteristic classes. The target is not merely a discretization of one scalar invariant. It is a theory in which PL approximations of positively curved smooth manifolds carry a natural positive-curvature condition, and in which curvature data still remembers Euler and Pontrjagin classes.

The existing theory has developed along several complementary axes. Banchoff proved a polyhedral Gauss-Bonnet theorem for embedded polyhedra [Ba]. Regge introduced a piecewise-flat scalar curvature concentrated on codimension-two faces, now the basic curvature quantity in Regge calculus [R]. Cheeger developed analytic and Hodge-theoretic tools for spaces with cone-like singularities and Riemannian pseudomanifolds [C1], [C2]. Cheeger-Müller- Schrader constructed curvature measures for piecewise-flat spaces and proved convergence results under suitable approximation hypotheses [CMS]. This is one of the closest achievements to the characteristic-class side of Yau's question, but curvature measures are not yet a substitute for a PL sectional curvature theory with sphere, pinching, and rigidity theorems.

A recent advance makes the measure-valued Riemann tensor itself more intrinsic. Gawlik-McKee use compatible moving frames on blown-up polytopes to derive a distributional curvature satisfying the weak Cartan structure equations and the correct gauge-transformation law [GM]. Their formula has three strata: ordinary Riemann curvature in the interiors, jumps of the second fundamental form across codimension-one faces, and angle-defect rotations on codimension-two faces. They also prove a Gauss-Bonnet theorem for compact two-dimensional Regge manifolds, including boundary and corner terms. This solves an important covariance problem, but it does not yet define nonlinear invariant polynomials of singular curvature in higher dimensions; products of measure-valued curvature are precisely where the Euler and Pontrjagin forms become delicate.

Alexandrov and CAT geometry give a different kind of answer. The Burago-Gromov-Perelman theory of Alexandrov spaces with curvature bounded below and the CAT( $\kappa$ ) theory described in Bridson-Haefliger are now indispensable frameworks for singular curvature [BGP], [BH]. They provide compactness, stability, splitting, and comparison theorems, and they are well suited to Gromov-Hausdorff limits. Their curvature bounds, however, are metric-comparison conditions rather than local Chern-Weil representatives of Pontrjagin or Euler classes. This is the essential gap between synthetic curvature and the characteristic-class part of the problem.

More recent work clarifies parts of the differential-geometric dictionary in discrete settings. Glickenstein studied discrete conformal variations and scalar curvature on two- and three-dimensional piecewise-flat manifolds [G1]. McDonald-Miller-Alsing-Gu-Wang-Yau developed an exterior calculus and curvature formalism for piecewise-flat manifolds [McY]. These tools are valuable for discrete differential geometry and numerical geometry, but the original problem remains broader: one needs compatibility among subdivision, smoothing, convergence to smooth curvature, pinching rigidity, and topological characteristic classes.

There is also a computationally effective piecewise-flat curvature program. Conboye and Miller constructed discrete scalar, sectional, and Ricci curvatures on three-dimensional simplicial piecewise-flat triangulations, using dual volumes and parallel transport around hinges, and reported convergence to smooth curvatures and Ricci flow in tested examples [CM]. Conboye later developed piecewise-flat approximations of local extrinsic mean and directed curvatures for non-Euclidean embeddings [Co]. These results are valuable for approximation and numerical geometry. They should nevertheless be distinguished from Yau's requested PL theory: convergence of averaged curvature quantities does not yet supply a subdivision-stable sectional-curvature condition, a PL pinching theorem, or Chern-Weil representatives of Pontrjagin classes.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Formulate a PL sectional curvature condition which is stable under subdivision and converges to smooth sectional curvature under controlled triangulations.
\item[2.] Original direction. Develop a Chern-Weil theory for piecewise-flat manifolds whose characteristic representatives converge to the smooth Pontrjagin and Euler forms [CMS].
\item[3.] Original direction. Determine whether every sufficiently fine PL approximation of a positively curved smooth manifold can be assigned a natural positive PL curvature in such a theory [BGP], [BH].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Compare curvature-measure approaches with Alexandrov lower-curvature and CAT uppercurvature conditions: determine which implications survive under subdivision, Gromov-Hausdorff limits, and smoothing [BGP], [BH], [CMS].
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Test whether the dual-volume discrete scalar, sectional, and Ricci curvatures used in piecewise-flat Ricci-flow computations can be upgraded from numerical approximations to subdivision-stable hypotheses in comparison or pinching theorems [CM], [Co], [GI].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Construct Chern-Weil invariant polynomials for the gauge-covariant Gawlik-McKee curvature measure without undefined products of singular measures, and prove that they represent the PL Euler and Pontrjagin classes [GM], [CMS].
\item[7.] AI-generated formulation. Prove a PL sphere theorem under a natural pointwise pinching condition and identify exactly which additional regularity assumptions on the triangulation are necessary.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 16}

What can one say about the Pontrjagin classes and the Stiefel-Whitney classes of a compact manifold with negative curvature? For example, is it true that a finite covering of such a manifold is spin?

Status. Open in general. Negative sectional curvature does not force the rational Pontrjagin classes to vanish, and no general classification of Pontrjagin or Stiefel-Whitney classes of closed negatively curved manifolds is known. The virtual spin question is also open in this generality: after passing to an orientable finite cover, one asks whether a further finite cover can kill $w_{2}$.

Several distinctions are essential. First, one must distinguish characteristic classes from characteristic numbers. A class may be nonzero even when all corresponding top-dimensional characteristic numbers vanish, and finite covers can change integral and mod-two information in different ways. Second, locally symmetric negative curvature and variable negative curvature have very different flexibility. Classical rigidity gives strong restrictions in locally symmetric cases, but hyperbolization constructions show that variable negative curvature allows much wider topology. Long-Reid used characteristic classes to study geometric boundaries of hyperbolic manifolds [LR]. Lafont-Roy gave conceptual proofs of vanishing and nonvanishing results for compact nonpositively curved locally symmetric spaces, and also proved vanishing results for Stiefel-Whitney numbers of finite covers of the Gromov-Thurston negatively curved examples [LRo]. Thus the picture is neither pure vanishing nor complete freedom.

Ontaneda's hyperbolization program gives decisive evidence against overly optimistic vanishing conjectures. Pinched smooth and Riemannian hyperbolization produce negatively curved closed manifolds in dimensions greater than three with nonzero first rational Pontrjagin class [O1], [O2]. Thus the right question is not whether characteristic classes vanish, but which characteristic-class patterns are compatible with negative curvature, which are stable under finite covers, and which are ruled out by additional pinching or local symmetry.

For the spin question, after passing to the orientation double cover, a finite spin cover exists precisely when $w_{2}(M)$ pulls back to zero on some finite-index subgroup. Since a closed negatively curved manifold is aspherical, this becomes a virtual vanishing problem in the cohomology of $\pi_{1}(M)$. No known mechanism from variable negative curvature alone forces this restriction map to vanish.

The real-hyperbolic case is nevertheless solved affirmatively. A theorem of Deligne-Sullivan says that every finite-volume real-hyperbolic manifold has a finite cover which is stably parallelizable, hence spin. Long-Reid gave a direct spin-group proof [LRS]: lift the lattice to the central double cover $\operatorname{Spin}^{+}(n, 1)$, use linearity and residual finiteness to separate the central element -1 in a finite quotient, and pass to the corresponding finite-index subgroup. Thus Yau's virtual-spin question has a positive answer for constant curvature, while its variable-curvature form remains open.

The flat case is a useful warning: Hamrick-Royster proved that flat manifolds bound, hence all their Stiefel-Whitney numbers vanish [HR], but numerical vanishing is weaker than vanishing of the class $w_{2}$ itself. A virtual spin theorem would therefore be substantially stronger than any statement only about Stiefel-Whitney numbers.

Passing to a cover is essential even in constant curvature. Martelli-Riolo- Slavich constructed compact orientable hyperbolic manifolds without spin structures in every dimension at least four [MRS]. Chen then constructed, in every dimension at least five, infinitely many commensurability classes of closed orientable hyperbolic manifolds without spin ${ }^{c}$ structures, and more generally examples with nonzero $w_{4 k-1}$ in every dimension at least $4 k+1$ [Chn]. The mechanism recursively embeds arithmetic hyperbolic manifolds as geodesic hypersurfaces with controlled normal class and propagates Stiefel-Whitney nonvanishing by the Whitney product formula.

In the cusped category, Reid-Sell showed that every commensurability class of cusped arithmetic hyperbolic manifolds of simplest type in dimensions $2 n+2 \geqslant 6$ contains examples with nonzero even Stiefel-Whitney classes and separate examples without spin ${ }^{c}$ structures [RS]. None of these results contradicts virtual spin: their characteristic-class obstructions disappear on some finite cover. They instead show how much information can be present before that cover is taken.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Characterize the possible rational Pontrjagin classes of closed negatively curved manifolds in each dimension.
\item[2.] Original direction. Decide whether every closed negatively curved manifold has a finite spin cover, equivalently whether $w_{2}$ becomes zero on some finite-index subgroup after orienting.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether strict pinching, for example near -1, imposes stronger restrictions on Stiefel-Whitney classes or numbers than arbitrary negative curvature [LRo],[O1], [O2].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Separate the possible Pontrjagin classes from the possible Pontrjagin numbers for negatively curved manifolds, especially in the locally symmetric, Gromov-Thurston, and Ontaneda-hyperbolized families [LRo], [O2].
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Compare closed negatively curved manifolds with finite-volume cusped hyperbolic manifolds: determine which nonvanishing Stiefel-Whitney phenomena in the arithmetic cusped setting can occur in closed negatively curved manifolds or in their finite cover towers [RS], [Chn].
\item[6.] Author question. Construct closed orientable non-arithmetic hyperbolic manifolds without spin ${ }^{c}$ structures and decide whether they occur in infinitely many commensurability classes [Chn].
\item[7.] Author question. Decide whether a closed orientable hyperbolic manifold can fail to be spin ${ }^{c}$ while $w_{3}=0$, and study analogous obstructions to higher spin structures [Chn].
\item[8.] AI-generated formulation. Find computable obstructions, expressed in the cohomology of the fundamental group, to a closed negatively curved manifold admitting a finite spin cover.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 17}

Prove that the Stiefel-Whitney numbers of a flat manifold are zero.
Status. Solved. Hamrick and Royster proved in 1982 that every compact flat Riemannian manifold is a boundary [HR]. By Thom's description of unoriented cobordism, this is equivalent to the vanishing of all Stiefel-Whitney numbers [Th]. At the time of Yau's list, the problem belonged to the topology of Bieberbach manifolds and compact solvmanifolds; Auslander-Szczarba had already studied characteristic classes in that setting [AS].

The point of the Hamrick-Royster theorem is that it proves a stronger cobordism statement, not merely a numerical cancellation. A compact flat manifold represents zero in the unoriented cobordism ring; Thom's theorem then identifies this with the vanishing of all Stiefel-Whitney numbers [Th]. Thus the original flat problem is completely settled.

The natural continuation is the almost-flat case. By the Gromov-Ruh theory, almost flat manifolds are infranilmanifolds [Gr], [Ru]. For oriented almost flat manifolds, Chern-Weil theory forces rational Pontrjagin numbers to vanish: the curvature can be made arbitrarily small while the diameter is kept bounded. Stiefel-Whitney numbers are subtler because they are mod-two cobordism invariants rather than Chern-Weil integrals. Their vanishing is equivalent to unoriented null-cobordism, and the general almost-flat bounding problem remains open.

There are important positive results in this broader direction. Farrell-Zdravkovska proved bounding theorems for almost flat manifolds under several holonomy hypotheses [FZ]. Davis-Fang later showed that an almost flat manifold bounds when the 2-Sylow subgroup of its holonomy group is cyclic or generalized quaternionic; in particular, cyclic holonomy is covered [DF]. Han-Huang-Zhang proved that every almost-flat spin ${ }^{c}$ manifold bounds an orientable manifold and, in odd dimensions, every chosen spin ${ }^{c}$ structure bounds spin ${ }^{c}$ [HHZ, Theorem 1.1]. Their integral spin ${ }^{c}$ Wu classes, together with Atiyah-Singer divisibility, force the relevant Stiefel-Whitney numbers to vanish. They also prove bounding when the Schur multiplier of the holonomy group has odd order and settle the orientable and spin bordism versions in dimension four. Thus the unresolved part of the almost-flat conjecture is concentrated in non-spin ${ }^{c}$ holonomy extensions.

Spin and spin ${ }^{c}$ structures are not automatic even in the almost-flat world. Gasior-Petrosyan-Szczepański gave necessary and sufficient conditions for almost-flat manifolds with cyclic holonomy to admit spin structures and classified the four-dimensional orientable cyclic-holonomy examples without spin structures [GPS]. Together with the spin ${ }^{c}$ bounding theorem of Han-Huang- Zhang, this makes the remaining problem sharper: the unoriented bounding conjecture for all almost-flat manifolds is not merely a spin-theoretic question, but a question about the full holonomy extension and its mod-two characteristic classes.

There is also a geometric filling version. Ontaneda proved that if an almost flat manifold bounds, then the bounding manifold can be chosen so that its interior carries a complete finite-volume metric with sectional curvatures pinched in $[-1-a,-1]$ for any $a>0[\mathrm{O}]$. Thus the topological bounding problem controls which infranilmanifolds can occur as cusp cross-sections in this negatively curved setting. Long-Reid showed that one cannot generally upgrade such fillings to constant curvature: eta-invariant obstructions prevent some flat manifolds from occurring as totally geodesic boundaries of compact hyperbolic manifolds [LR].

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Reference-driven formulation. Prove or disprove that every almost flat manifold bounds, equivalently that all of its Stiefel-Whitney numbers vanish.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend the known Farrell-Zdravkovska and Davis-Fang holonomy cases to arbitrary finite holonomy groups of infranilmanifolds.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether the spin ${ }^{c}$ bounding theorem for almost-flat manifolds can be upgraded to unoriented bounding without spin ${ }^{c}$ hypotheses, or whether the remaining obstructions are genuinely non-spin ${ }^{c}$ holonomy phenomena [GPS], [HHZ].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify which flat or almost flat manifolds occur as cusp cross-sections of complete finite-volume negatively pinched manifolds.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify which flat manifolds occur as totally geodesic boundary components of compact hyperbolic manifolds.
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine the optimal pinching possible for negatively curved fillings of a given flat or almost flat manifold.
\item[7.] AI-generated formulation. Express the best possible filling pinching constant purely in terms of the Bieberbach group of the flat manifold.
\end{itemize}
8. AI-generated formulation. Describe the almost-flat bounding obstruction as an explicit group-cohomological invariant of the infranil holonomy extension.

References.

\section*{Problem 18}

Given $M$ a complete, noncompact manifold with sectional curvature $K \geqslant 0$; if, at some point $x, K(x)>0$, prove that $M$ is diffeomorphic to $\mathbb{R}^{N}$.

Status. Solved. Perelman proved in 1994 that if a complete open manifold has nonnegative sectional curvature and has positive sectional curvature at one point, then its soul is a point [Pe]. Hence the manifold is diffeomorphic to $\mathbb{R}^{N}$, exactly as Yau asked.

The background is the Cheeger-Gromoll soul theorem: every complete open manifold with nonnegative sectional curvature is diffeomorphic to the normal bundle of a compact totally geodesic soul [CG]. If the soul is a point, the manifold is $\mathbb{R}^{N}$; if the soul has positive dimension, the topology is controlled by its normal bundle. Gromoll-Meyer had already proved the point-soul conclusion under the stronger assumption of positive sectional curvature everywhere [GM]. The Cheeger-Gromoll soul conjecture asked whether positivity at a single point is enough.

Perelman's proof established exactly this one-point version [Pe]. The key geometric background is Sharafutdinov's distance nonincreasing retraction from the open manifold to a soul [Sh]. Perelman proved the additional regularity and submersion property needed to rule out a positive-dimensional soul when the ambient sectional curvature is positive at one point. Thus the topological part of the problem is now a theorem.

A separate nearby direction asks whether zero-curvature directions in a nonnegatively curved metric can be removed while preserving completeness and the underlying manifold. This is not an unresolved clause of Yau's stated Problem 18; it is a local-to-global perturbation problem sensitive to the soul, the Sharafutdinov fibration, and the normal-bundle geometry.

Modern work on the converse soul problem shows how subtle this broader program remains. González-Àlvaro and Zibrowius extended known existence results to simply connected positively curved souls: they constructed pairs of simply connected positively curved manifolds which are tangentially homotopy equivalent but not homeomorphic, and used stable converse-soul results to obtain open manifolds admitting nonhomeomorphic positively curved souls [GZ]. This does not affect Perelman's point-soul theorem under positive curvature at one point. It does show that, once the soul has positive dimension or one asks for deformations and moduli of nonnegatively curved metrics, the topology of possible souls is flexible and not determined only by a coarse homotopy type.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Classify complete open manifolds which admit positive sectional curvature.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine when a nonnegatively curved open manifold whose curvature is positive off a small set admits a deformation to positive sectional curvature.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Study whether the Sharafutdinov fibration and normal-bundle structure give effective obstructions to removing zero-curvature strata from nonnegatively curved open metrics.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine when a vector bundle over a positively curved closed manifold admits a complete metric of nonnegative sectional curvature for which the zero section is a soul, and how many nonhomeomorphic positively curved souls can occur on one open manifold [GZ].
\item[5.] AI-generated formulation. Find quantitative lower-curvature, volume-growth, or convexity assumptions which force the soul to be a point without assuming strict positivity at a point.
\end{itemize}

\section*{B. Ricci curvature}

\section*{Problem 19}

Let $\Omega$ be a closed $4 k$-form defined on a compact manifold $M$ which represents some Pontrjagin class of $M$. Can one find a metric on $M$ so that $\Omega$ is represented by the curvature form according to Chern [Ch]? If there are other topological obstructions, what are they? It is rather clear that such obstructions should exist. The problem is to find a sufficient condition.

One can ask the same question for Chern classes of Kähler manifolds. For the first Chern class, this was conjectured by Calabi and solved in [Y].

A resolution of this question will give a deep understanding of the curvature tensor.

Status. Open. For the first Chern class in the Kähler case, the corresponding prescription of the Ricci form is settled by Yau's solution of the Calabi conjecture [Y]. For higher Pontrjagin forms of a real Riemannian metric, no satisfactory necessary and sufficient condition is known.

Chern-Weil theory associates closed differential forms to invariant polynomials in the curvature of a connection; for the Levi-Civita connection these forms represent the Pontrjagin classes of the tangent bundle [Ch]. The problem asks for a converse prescription theorem: given a closed representative of a Pontrjagin class, can it be realized as the Chern-Weil form of some Riemannian metric? The case of scalar curvature is much more flexible and was developed by Kazdan-Warner [KW], while prescribed Ricci curvature has a local theory due to DeTurck [D]. These results show that curvature prescription problems can have very different answers depending on which curvature contraction is prescribed. The Pontrjagin-form problem remains substantially more rigid because the forms are nonlinear in the full curvature tensor and must satisfy Bianchi-type identities.

It is important to distinguish this question from the corresponding problem for arbitrary connections on a fixed vector bundle. The space of connections is affine and much larger than the space of Levi-Civita connections. Universal-connection theory of Narasimhan-Ramanan [NR] and later work on Chern-Weil representatives show that characteristic forms can often be studied flexibly once the connection is allowed to vary freely. Yau's question asks for a representative coming from the Riemannian metric itself; this imposes torsion-freeness, metric compatibility, and the algebraic symmetries of the Riemann curvature tensor. These extra constraints are the source of the missing "sufficient condition" in the problem.

In complex geometry, the first Chern form is the Ricci form of a Kähler metric; Calabi's conjecture, proved by Yau, gives a precise prescription theorem in a fixed Kähler class [Y]. Higher Chern forms and real Pontrjagin forms are not presently governed by an analogous general theorem. Recent work on representability of Chern-Weil forms for Hermitian metrics on vector bundles gives useful models and examples, but it still does not provide a general Levi-Civita prescription theorem for Pontrjagin forms [P].

A new higher-form Monge-Ampère model gives another useful comparison. George introduced a volume form associated to a positive $(p, p)$-form and proved existence and uniqueness for its prescribed-volume equation on a compact Kähler manifold [Ge]. The associated cohomology-preserving flow extends Kähler-Ricci flow and converges in the fixed positive-form setting. The decisive estimate splits multi-indices relative to the largest Hessian eigenvalue and uses a Kronecker-product structure to control otherwise unmanageable third-order terms. This is a genuine higher-form prescription theorem, but its "Ricci form" is defined from the induced volume of a positive ( $p, p$ )-form; it is not yet a theorem that a prescribed higher Chern-Weil form comes from the curvature of a Kähler or Levi-Civita connection.

There are also newer scalar analogues in Hermitian geometry. Recent work on prescribed Chern scalar curvature, including flow approaches on compact Hermitian manifolds of negative Gauduchon degree and complete noncompact extensions, shows that Chern-connection prescription problems can sometimes be attacked by conformal or parabolic methods [Yu1], [Yu2]. These results concern scalar contractions of Chern curvature, not full Chern-Weil forms and not Levi-Civita Pontrjagin forms. They are nevertheless useful comparisons: they illustrate how extra complex structure and a chosen connection can convert a curvature-prescription question into a tractable PDE, whereas Yau's Pontrjagin-form problem still lacks such a canonical elliptic formulation.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Find local obstructions, beyond de Rham cohomology, for a closed $4 k$-form to be a Pontrjagin form of a Levi-Civita connection.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Develop a prescribed Chern-Weil form theory for Kähler metrics and compare it with complex Monge-Ampère equations.
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Compare the flexible prescription problem for arbitrary connections with the constrained problem for Levi-Civita connections, and isolate the first metric-compatibility obstruction.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether the Hermitian prescribed Chern scalar curvature methods can be lifted from scalar contractions to Chern-Weil representatives in fixed Bott-Chern or de Rham cohomology classes [P], [Yu1], [Yu2].
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Find geometric conditions under which George's positive $(p, p)$-form flow preserves the subclass of forms arising as higher Chern forms of a Kähler metric [Ge].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Determine whether every sufficiently small exact perturbation of a Pontrjagin form of a generic metric can again be realized as a Pontrjagin form after perturbing the metric.
\end{itemize}

\section*{B. Ricci curvature}

\section*{Problem 20}

Find necessary and sufficient conditions on a symmetric tensor $T_{i j}$ on a compact manifold so that one can find a metric $g_{i j}$ to satisfy
$$R_{i j}-R / 2 g_{i j}=T_{i j}$$
whence $R_{i j}$ is the Ricci tensor and $R$ is the scalar curvature of $g_{i j}$. If $g_{i j}$ is the Lorentz metric on a four-dimensional manifold, this is simply the Einstein field equation. If $M$ has boundary, what are suitable boundary conditions to impose?

Status. Open as a global prescription problem. The local prescribed Ricci curvature theory gives important partial results, but a necessary and sufficient global theorem for prescribing the Einstein tensor on a compact manifold is not known.

The contracted Bianchi identity gives an immediate necessary condition: the tensor $T_{i j}$ must be divergence-free with respect to the unknown metric $g$. This makes the problem different from prescribing a fixed tensor with respect to a fixed background metric. In the Riemannian setting, DeTurck proved a local existence theorem for prescribed Ricci curvature [D]. Since the Einstein tensor determines the Ricci tensor algebraically when the dimension is not two, this is closely related to the local form of the present problem, after accounting for gauge and trace issues. Indeed, if $G_{g}=T$, then
$$\operatorname{Ric}_{g}=T-\frac{\operatorname{tr}_{g} T}{n-2} g, \quad n>2,$$
but the trace on the right is taken with respect to the unknown metric. This is why the problem is not merely DeTurck's prescribed-Ricci problem in different notation. Globally, topology, elliptic gauge fixing, and the dependence of the divergence condition on $g$ remain serious obstructions.

In Lorentzian signature the equation is the Einstein field equation, and the tensor $T$ is normally constrained by matter equations and a variational or hyperbolic evolution system. Thus the geometric prescription problem and the physical Cauchy-boundary problem should be separated. The Cauchy problem, beginning with Choquet-Bruhat's work and its later global formulation with Geroch, treats Einstein's equations as a hyperbolic system after gauge choice and requires the initial data to satisfy the Einstein constraint equations [CBG, CBY]. Boundary versions are even more delicate: they must include boundary geometry, gauge, compatibility conditions, and constraints, not only Dirichlet data for the metric.

For Riemannian Einstein metrics with boundary, modern work shows that the naive Dirichlet and Neumann boundary maps are generally not Fredholm. Anderson identified natural mixed boundary data, involving the conformal class of the boundary metric together with mean-curvature-type data, which lead to Fredholm boundary-value problems in the Einstein case [A]. This gives a model for what the boundary part of Yau's problem should look like, but it is still far from a general Einstein-tensor prescription theorem with arbitrary source tensor $T$.

Recent work has pushed this boundary perspective directly to the prescribed Einstein tensor. Delay proved local invertibility near a compact Einstein manifold with boundary for a map recording Ricci curvature, the induced boundary conformal class, and boundary mean curvature, under explicit Hodge- and Lichnerowicz-spectrum nondegeneracy [Del, Theorem 1.1]. His Theorem 6.2 treats an Einstein-type operator whose parameter choice $\kappa=-1 / 2$ is the Einstein tensor itself. Thus small Einstein-tensor data together with nearby mixed boundary data $([\gamma], H)$ determine a unique nearby metric. The proof adds a Bianchi-gauge correction, splits trace and trace-free tensors, uses the spectral exclusions to invert the linearized mixed problem, and finally removes the gauge term using the Bianchi identity. This is perturbative near a nondegenerate Einstein background, not a global characterization of all tensors $T$.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Classify divergence-free symmetric tensors which occur as Einstein tensors of Riemannian metrics on a fixed compact manifold.
\item[2.] Original direction. Formulate well-posed boundary data for the Riemannian Einstein-tensor prescription problem.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether Anderson-type mixed boundary data extend from Einstein metrics to the full prescribed Einstein-tensor equation.
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Separate the Riemannian elliptic prescription problem from the Lorentzian initial-boundary value problem with matter fields and constraint propagation.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend Delay's local Einstein-tensor inversion near a nondegenerate Einstein metric to a global range or degree theorem under geometric compactness hypotheses [A], [Del].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Determine whether the Einstein-tensor map is locally surjective, modulo diffeomorphisms and scaling, at a generic compact Einstein metric.
\end{itemize}

References.
\begin{itemize}

\item[] [CBG] Y. Choquet-Bruhat and R. Geroch, Global aspects of the Cauchy problem in general relativity, Comm. Math. Phys. 14 (1969), 329-335.
\item[] [CBY] Y. Choquet-Bruhat and J. W. York, Jr., The Cauchy problem, in General Relativity and Gravitation, Vol. 1, 99-172, Plenum, New York-London, 1980.
\item[] [Del] E. Delay, Prescribed Ricci curvature near an Einstein manifold with boundary, arXiv:2503.18452.
\item[] [D] Dennis M. DeTurck, Existence of metrics with prescribed Ricci curvature: local theory. Invent. Math. 65 (1981/82), no. 1, 179-207.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 21}

Let $M$ be a complete manifold with positive Ricci curvature. Can $M$ be deformed to a compact manifold with boundary?

Status. False in general. Complete manifolds with positive Ricci curvature need not have finite topological type, hence need not be diffeomorphic to the interior of a compact manifold with boundary.

The question is motivated by the soul theorem for nonnegative sectional curvature, where an open manifold has finite topological type. Ricci curvature is much weaker. Sha-Yang constructed complete metrics with positive Ricci curvature on infinite connected sums, including infinite connected sums of $\mathbb{S}^{2} \times \mathbb{S}^{2}$, giving explicit examples of infinite topological type [SY]. Thus the direct analogue of the soul theorem fails.

Positive results require additional hypotheses. Shen proved finite topological consequences under nonnegative Ricci curvature together with large volume growth assumptions [S]. Later work developed this theme using asymptotic Ricci lower bounds, curvature decay, rewinding volume, and volume-growth hypotheses [H, Z]. These theorems show that finite topology can be recovered when the geometry at infinity is controlled strongly enough.

The boundary of the theory is sharp. Even quite strong asymptotic conditions do not automatically restore finite topological type: Jiang-Yang constructed complete manifolds of positive Ricci curvature with quadratically asymptotically nonnegative sectional curvature and infinite topological type in high dimensions [JY]. Thus the modern form of the problem is not simply "positive Ricci plus mild control at infinity," but rather to identify the exact package of volume, curvature-decay, and local noncollapsing assumptions that prevents topological complexity from escaping to infinity.

The dimensions and topology in these counterexamples are now quite precise. Jiang-Yang first produced a six-dimensional example [JY] and then constructed examples in every dimension at least five with specified infinite intermediate Betti numbers [JY2]. Their mechanism is an infinite sequence of Perelman-type surgeries inside a doubly warped end: punctured $\mathbb{C P}^{2}$ -type cores are attached through positive-Ricci necks, while the warping functions balance Ricci positivity against quadratic sectional-curvature decay.

The negative side has become even sharper. Jiang recently constructed in every dimension at least four complete open manifolds with positive Ricci curvature, sectional curvature bounded from below, and infinite intermediate Betti numbers, with volume growth $O\left(r^{2+\gamma}\right)$ for arbitrarily small $\gamma>0$ [Ji]. In a related but nonnegative-Ricci direction, Brue-Naber-Semola constructed smooth complete examples with Ric $\geqslant 0$ and infinitely generated fundamental group, first in dimension seven and then in dimension six [BNS1], [BNS2]. These latter examples answer Milnor's finite-generation conjecture rather than Yau's finite-topological-type question directly, but they reinforce the same lesson: Ricci lower bounds alone allow topological complexity at infinity that has no analogue under nonnegative sectional curvature.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Find sharp geometric conditions, weaker than sectional curvature bounds, which force finite topological type under positive Ricci curvature.
\item[2.] Original direction. Classify which infinite connected sums admit complete positive Ricci curvature.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine the optimal combination of large volume growth, curvature decay, and local rewinding-volume control that implies finite topological type under nonnegative or positive Ricci curvature.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Decide in which dimensions positive Ricci curvature with quadratically asymptotically nonnegative sectional curvature can still have infinite topological type.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Decide whether the almost quadratic volume-growth examples with positive Ricci curvature and infinite Betti numbers can be improved to exactly quadratic volume growth, or whether quadratic growth is a genuine threshold for finite topology under a lower sectional curvature bound [Ji].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Compare finite topological type with finite generation of the fundamental group under Ricci lower bounds: determine which features of the Brue-Naber-Semola counterexamples can coexist with positive Ricci curvature or with stronger volume-growth assumptions [BNS1], [BNS2].
\item[7.] AI-generated formulation. Determine whether positive Ricci curvature together with Euclidean volume growth and a quantitative lower injectivity-radius condition forces finite topological type.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 22}

Characterize the fundamental group of a complete manifold with positive Ricci curvature.
If the manifold is compact, the splitting theorem of Cheeger-Gromoll [CG] provides a rather satisfactory answer.
The case of noncompact manifolds is more complicated. Recently, P. Nabonnand [N], under the direction of Bérard-Bergery, provided an example of a noncompact, complete manifold with positive Ricci curvature whose fundamental group is infinite cyclic. This example has dimension $\geqslant 4$.

In the case of three dimensions, Schoen and Yau [SY] proved that such manifolds are diffeomorphic to $\mathbb{R}^{3}$. It may be possible that the fundamental group of the manifold is always a finite extension of a polycyclic group.

Status. Open in its full positive Ricci form. The compact case is well understood up to the crystallographic extension given by the Cheeger-Gromoll splitting theorem [CG]. The noncompact case is much subtler: many nilpotent groups occur, dimension three is understood, and the analogous finite-generation expectation for nonnegative Ricci curvature is now known to be false in high dimensions. No audited counterexample upgrades the latter construction to strictly positive Ricci curvature, so that distinction is part of the remaining problem rather than a technicality.

In the compact nonnegative Ricci case, Cheeger and Gromoll proved that the universal cover splits isometrically as $\mathbb{R}^{d} \times N$ with $N$ compact [CG]. Hence the fundamental group is virtually crystallographic. Conversely, Wilking proved that the expected abstract groups occur as fundamental groups of compact manifolds with nonnegative sectional curvature [W].

In dimension three, the picture is much more rigid. Schoen and Yau proved that a complete noncompact three-manifold with positive Ricci curvature is diffeomorphic to $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ [SY]. Hamilton's Ricci-flow classification treats the compact nonnegative Ricci case [Ham]. Liu later completed the noncompact nonnegative Ricci classification in dimension three: either the manifold is diffeomorphic to $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ or its universal cover splits isometrically as $N^{2} \times \mathbb{R}$ [Liu]. Pan gave another proof of Milnor's finite-generation conjecture in dimension three using Cheeger-Colding theory [Pan].

In higher-dimensional noncompact geometry, the original expectation was strongly influenced by Milnor's theorem that every finitely generated subgroup of the fundamental group of a complete manifold with nonnegative Ricci curvature has polynomial growth [Mil]. If the whole group were finitely generated, Gromov's theorem on groups of polynomial growth would imply that it is virtually nilpotent [G]. Several partial results supported this picture: Li proved finiteness under Euclidean volume growth [Li], Anderson proved finite generation under additional volume growth assumptions [A], and Sormani proved finite generation under small linear diameter growth [So]. Kapovitch and Wilking proved a Ricci-lower-bound Margulis lemma, showing that local fundamental groups contain nilpotent subgroups of uniformly bounded index [KW].

The situation changed substantially with the work of Brue-Naber- Semola, who constructed complete manifolds with nonnegative Ricci curvature and infinitely generated fundamental group, disproving Milnor's conjecture in high dimensions [BNS]. This does not by itself classify the positive Ricci case, but it shows that the noncompact Ricci-lower-bound world is broader than the earlier virtually nilpotent picture suggested.

The counterexample side has also been sharpened dimensionally. The Brue-Naber-Semola construction now gives a smooth complete six-dimensional manifold with Ric $\geqslant 0$ and infinitely generated fundamental group exactly $\mathbb{Q} / \mathbb{Z}$ [BNS2]. Its universal cover is diffeomorphic to $\mathbb{S}^{3} \times \mathbb{R}^{3}$; the curvature is bounded and decays polynomially, and the example is uniformly locally noncollapsed. The topology is assembled from nested finite cyclic groups by recursively twisted gluing. In particular, the Milnor finite-generation conjecture is false in all dimensions at least six, while the four- and five-dimensional cases remain natural borderline questions. This is important for Yau's positive-Ricci problem because any attempted classification must now distinguish carefully between positive Ricci curvature, nonnegative Ricci curvature, and the strength of the asymptotic hypotheses imposed at infinity.

After these counterexamples, a more refined positive theory has begun to emerge under volume-growth hypotheses. Navarro-Pan-Zhu proved that for complete manifolds with nonnegative Ricci curvature and linear volume growth, the fundamental group is virtually free abelian, and if the Ricci curvature is positive everywhere then the fundamental group is finite [NPZ1]. More precisely, the nonnegative-Ricci conclusion is that $\pi_{1}(M)$ contains $\mathbb{Z}^{k}$ with finite index. Their subsequent theorem uses the relative volume function $R V(s)$ : under sublinear end diameter, $R V(s)=o\left(s^{2}\right)$ forces virtual abelianness, while a stronger $R V(s)=o\left(s^{1+\delta}\right)$ hypothesis and Ricci positivity at one point force finiteness [NPZ2]. Absolute subquadratic volume growth is not known to give the same conclusion. Huang-Huang further proved that in the linear- growth case the universal cover splits as $\mathbb{R}^{k} \times N$, with a finite cover fibering over $\mathbb{T}^{k}$, and positivity at one point already forces finiteness [HH]. These results suggest that the replacement for Milnor's conjecture should be formulated in terms of asymptotic volume geometry rather than Ricci positivity alone.

There are also positive realization results. Nabonnand constructed a complete noncompact positive Ricci example with fundamental group $\mathbb{Z}[\mathrm{N}]$. Wei proved that every finitely generated torsion-free nilpotent group occurs as the fundamental group of a complete manifold with positive Ricci curvature [Wei]. These examples show that even under positive Ricci curvature, the class of possible noncompact fundamental groups is large.

Related open problems.
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Characterize the groups which occur as fundamental groups of complete noncompact manifolds with positive Ricci curvature.
\item[2.] Original direction. Decide whether every finitely generated virtually nilpotent group occurs as the fundamental group of a complete positive Ricci manifold, allowing torsion.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether the high-dimensional infinitely generated examples with nonnegative Ricci curvature can be modified to have positive Ricci curvature everywhere [BNS], [BNS2].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether positive Ricci curvature plus linear or slow relative volume growth is the optimal regime in which the fundamental group must be finite or virtually abelian [NPZ1], [NPZ2], [HH].
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Decide the Milnor finite-generation conjecture in dimensions four and five, or find a geometric reason why the six-dimensional Brue-Naber-Semola mechanism cannot occur there [BNS2].
\item[6.] Original direction. Find the exact restrictions imposed by positive Ricci curvature on abelian subgroups of the fundamental group in the noncompact case.
\item[7.] Reference-driven formulation. Decide whether absolute subquadratic volume growth, without a sublinear end-diameter hypothesis, forces finite generation or virtual abelianness [NPZ1], [NPZ2].
\item[8.] AI-generated formulation. Formulate a replacement for Milnor's finite-generation conjecture which is true for positive Ricci curvature and stable under measured Gromov-Hausdorff limits.
\end{itemize}
