\section*{Problem 23}

Construct an explicit metric with zero Ricci curvature on the $K 3$ surface. The existence of such a metric was proved in Yau [Y]. Is there any four-dimensional manifold with zero Ricci curvature which is not covered by a torus or a K3 surface? A simpler unsolved question is whether such a manifold can be diffeomorphic to $\mathbb{S}^{4}$ or $\mathbb{S}^{2} \times \mathbb{S}^{2}$.

Status. Open for an explicit closed-form metric on a compact $K 3$ surface. Yau's theorem gives existence and uniqueness of the Ricci-flat Kähler metric in each Kähler class [Y], but no fully explicit formula is known for a compact $K 3$ surface. The broader four-dimensional Ricci-flat classification question remains open as a Riemannian problem, although the compact Kähler surface case is controlled by the Enriques-Kodaira classification and Yau's Calabi-Yau theorem.

Yau's solution of the Calabi conjecture proves that every complex K3 surface carries a Ricci-flat Kähler metric in each Kähler class [Y]. The proof is analytic and solves a complex Monge-Ampère equation; it does not produce an elementary explicit formula. This is why K3 remains a central test case for numerical and asymptotic approaches to Calabi-Yau metrics. Gross and Wilson analyzed collapsing Ricci-flat metrics on elliptic K3 surfaces near the large complex structure limit [GW]. Donaldson proposed and developed numerical approximation methods for Calabi-Yau metrics [D]. Yu-Shen Lin's thesis belongs to this numerical and string- theoretic direction [L].

More recent work gives detailed asymptotic and gluing models for families of K3 Ricci-flat metrics. Foscolo constructed collapsing hyperkähler metrics on $K 3$ using ALF gravitational instantons [F]. Hein-Sun-Viaclovsky-Zhang exhibited collapsing families with nilpotent structures and bubbling models [HSVZ]. Chen-Viaclovsky- Zhang constructed collapsing Ricci-flat Kähler metrics on elliptic K3 surfaces with precise descriptions near singular fibers [CVZ]. These works give powerful models of the metric geometry, but they do not give a single elementary explicit compact $K 3$ metric.

The Kummer construction continues to be one of the closest rigorous approaches to an explicit model. Thomas Jiang gave new analytic proofs of the Kummer construction of Calabi-Yau and hyperkähler metrics on $K 3$, including improved estimates and a description of large families of noncollapsed hyperkähler orbifold limits [J]. A recent preprint of Shackleton revisits the Kummer surface using Donaldson's gluing strategy and ordinary compact Hölder and Sobolev theory, rather than weighted spaces or conformal changes to model spaces [Sh]. It identifies the Eguchi-Hanson and Gibbons-Hawking descriptions, builds an approximate metric on the resolution of $\mathbb{T}^{4} /\{ \pm 1\}$, and solves for the correction by a Banach fixed-point argument. Thus it makes the local ingredients and the analytic perturbation more explicit, but the corrected compact metric is still defined implicitly as a PDE solution, not by a closed formula.

There is also a fast-growing computational direction. Machine-learning and balanced-metric methods approximate Calabi-Yau metrics very accurately, and recent symbolic-approximation work tries to distill neural approximations into compact analytic expressions in highly symmetric Calabi-Yau examples [MM]. This is directly relevant to the "explicit metric" aspect of Yau's question: it suggests a possible route from numerical approximations to human-readable formulae, while also emphasizing that no exact compact $K 3$ formula is presently known.

For the topological question, compact examples of Ricci-flat four-manifolds include flat quotients of tori and Ricci-flat Kähler metrics on $K 3$ surfaces and their finite quotients. In the complex surface category, Kodaira dimension zero gives complex tori, K3 surfaces, Enriques surfaces, and bi-elliptic or hyperelliptic surfaces; after passing to a finite cover, these are covered by a torus or a K3 surface. Yau's theorem supplies the Ricci-flat Kähler metrics in this setting.

The Riemannian question is different. A compact Ricci-flat four-manifold is Einstein, so it satisfies the Hitchin-Thorpe inequality [HT]. Equality is very rigid, but the inequality alone does not rule out $\mathbb{S}^{4}$ or $\mathbb{S}^{2} \times \mathbb{S}^{2}$. No Ricci-flat metric is known on either of these manifolds. The remaining issue is whether a compact Ricci-flat four-manifold can have generic Riemannian holonomy and topology not accounted for by the torus/K3 finite-cover picture.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Construct a closed-form Ricci-flat Kähler metric on a compact $K 3$ surface, even for a highly symmetric algebraic K3 such as the Fermat quartic.
\item[2.] Original direction. Decide whether $\mathbb{S}^{4}$ or $\mathbb{S}^{2} \times \mathbb{S}^{2}$ admits a Ricci-flat metric.
\item[3.] Original direction. Classify compact Ricci-flat four-manifolds not finitely covered by a torus or a K3 surface, or prove that none exist.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether the Hitchin-Thorpe inequality, Seiberg-Witten theory, or collapse theory can rule out Ricci-flat metrics on $\mathbb{S}^{4}$ and $\mathbb{S}^{2} \times \mathbb{S}^{2}$.
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Give effective error estimates for Kummer-type gluing and collapsing approximations to Ricci-flat K3 metrics [J], [Sh].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether symbolic or machine-learned approximations to highly symmetric Calabi-Yau metrics can be converted into certified approximate solutions with explicit residual bounds for the complex Monge-Ampère equation [D], [MM].
\item[7.] AI-generated formulation. Give a rigorous convergence theorem comparing numerical balanced-metric approximations of a K3 Calabi-Yau metric with the analytic estimates in Yau's proof.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 24}

Can every manifold with dimension $\geqslant 3$ admit a metric with negative Ricci curvature?
It is very hard to suggest what the right answer to this question is. One does not know if $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ or $\mathbb{T}^{3}$ admit such a metric. However, there are many examples of simply connected Kähler manifolds with negative Ricci curvature [Y].

Perhaps a compact manifold with nonpositive Ricci curvature does not admit an SU(2) action. This conjecture is partly motivated by Bochner's theorem that SU(2) cannot act isometrically and partly motivated by the theorem of Lawson and Yau [LY] stating that a manifold with effective SU(2) action admits a metric of positive scalar curvature.

If this last conjecture is true, then one can prove that the complex structures over $\mathbb{S}^{2} \times \mathbb{S}^{2}$ are given by the standard Hirzebruch surfaces.

Status. Solved affirmatively as an existence problem. Gao and Yau proved the compact three-dimensional case [GY], and Lohkamp proved that every smooth manifold of dimension at least three admits a Riemannian metric with negative Ricci curvature; in the noncompact case the metric can be chosen complete [L1, L2]. In fact, Lohkamp obtains dimensional constants $a(n)>b(n)>0$ and a complete metric satisfying $-a(n)<\operatorname{Ric}<-b(n)$ [L1].

The result shows a sharp contrast between Ricci curvature and sectional curvature. In dimension two, negative Ricci curvature is the same as negative Gaussian curvature and is strongly constrained by topology. In dimensions at least three, Lohkamp's theorem says that negative Ricci curvature alone imposes no topological restriction on the underlying smooth manifold [L2]. Thus even $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ and $\mathbb{T}^{3}$ admit metrics of negative Ricci curvature.

The symmetry issue has an exact answer in the negative-Ricci category. On a compact manifold the Bochner principle kills every Killing field, so the isometry group must be finite. Conversely, Lohkamp proved that every finite subgroup $G<\operatorname{Diff}(M)$ is the full isometry group of some negative-Ricci metric [L1, Theorem 2]. Thus the finite- group equivariant problem is solved, even though a naive averaging argument would not preserve the Ricci inequality. One must also distinguish an isometric action from a smooth action on the underlying manifold: SU(2) cannot act effectively by isometries of a compact negative-Ricci metric, but the existence of such a metric does not prevent the manifold from carrying an unrelated smooth SU(2) action. In that latter interpretation, Yau's suggested smooth-action obstruction is false.

In the Kähler category, negative Ricci curvature is much more rigid. If the first Chern class is negative, Aubin and Yau produce a Kähler-Einstein metric with negative Ricci curvature [Au, Y]. For general compact complex manifolds, the existence of a Kähler metric with negative Ricci form is governed by positivity of the canonical class rather than by the smooth topology alone. For manifolds with large symmetry, Lawson and Yau proved that many nonabelian group actions imply the existence of positive scalar curvature metrics [LY]; this motivates the question of how negative Ricci curvature interacts with effective group actions once one imposes equivariance, Kählerness, conformal flatness, or sectional-curvature bounds.

Recent work in special geometric categories reinforces this distinction. For example, Payne proved that no closed manifold admits a closed $G_{2}$-structure with negative Ricci curvature, and obtained further noncompact restrictions under pinching assumptions [P]. This does not contradict Lohkamp's theorem, because a closed $G_{2}$-structure is substantial extra structure. It is nevertheless a useful modern model for how Yau's solved smooth existence question can be refined into structured negative-Ricci questions where topology, torsion, and special holonomy data again become visible.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Classify compact Kähler manifolds admitting Kähler metrics with negative Ricci curvature, in terms of the canonical class and algebro-geometric positivity.
\item[2.] Original direction. Determine which closed manifolds admit metrics with negative Ricci curvature together with additional restrictions such as conformal flatness, local symmetry, or sectional-curvature bounds.
\item[3.] Original direction. Study whether effective nonabelian group actions impose meaningful restrictions on negative Ricci curvature when combined with stronger curvature assumptions.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Develop a parametric version of Lohkamp's construction for families of metrics and for quotients by diffeomorphism groups.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Strengthen Lohkamp's exact finite-isometry-group realization theorem by prescribing local geometry, curvature pinching, volume, or diameter while retaining the given group action [L1].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine which special geometric structures, such as closed $G_{2}$-structures, symplectic structures, or calibrated structures, obstruct negative Ricci curvature even though the underlying smooth manifold admits a negative-Ricci metric [P].
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[7.] AI-generated formulation. Find quantitative versions of Lohkamp's construction which control volume, diameter, or a mixed sectional-Ricci curvature bound.
\end{itemize}

Problem 25
Classify four-dimensional compact Einstein manifolds with negative Ricci curvature. Can $\mathbb{S}^{4}$ admit such a metric? The Thorpe-Hitchin inequality [H] gives some relation on the Euler number and the index of these manifolds.

Status. Open. The classification of compact four-dimensional Einstein manifolds with negative Einstein constant is far out of reach. The special question whether $\mathbb{S}^{4}$ admits an Einstein metric with negative Ricci curvature is still open.

The first general restriction is the Hitchin-Thorpe inequality, which relates the Euler characteristic and signature of an oriented compact Einstein four-manifold [H]. This is only a necessary condition and is far from a classification. Even in the Kähler case, the problem contains the diffeomorphism classification of minimal complex surfaces of general type. In practice, one asks geography questions, concerning possible pairs of characteristic numbers, and botany questions, concerning the number of smooth structures for fixed characteristic numbers.

There is a large constructive Kähler source. If a compact complex surface has ample canonical bundle, equivalently negative first Chern class, the Aubin-Yau theorem produces a Kähler-Einstein metric with negative Ricci curvature [Au, Y]. This supplies many examples among surfaces of general type, but it does not classify the underlying smooth four-manifolds and does not address non-Kähler-Einstein metrics.

There are strong rigidity theorems in locally symmetric cases. Besson-Courtois-Gallot proved minimal-entropy rigidity, implying in particular that on a compact real-hyperbolic four-manifold the standard hyperbolic metric is rigid among Einstein metrics up to scale and diffeomorphism [BCG]. In the complex-hyperbolic case, LeBrun used Seiberg-Witten theory to obtain rigidity results for Einstein metrics on complex-hyperbolic surfaces [L1].

Seiberg-Witten theory gives many obstructions to Einstein metrics on four-manifolds. LeBrun derived sharp curvature estimates and nonexistence results for Einstein metrics on manifolds with nontrivial Seiberg-Witten invariants [L2]. These results show that the Hitchin-Thorpe inequality is not sufficient. On the constructive side, Fine and Premoselli recently produced compact negatively curved Einstein four-manifolds which are not locally homogeneous, using branched covers of hyperbolic four-manifolds and perturbation of approximate Einstein metrics [FP]. Thus the class is larger than the locally symmetric examples, but still very far from classified.

There is also progress on rigidity within curvature-sign subclasses. Fine-Krasnov-Singer proved local rigidity for compact oriented Einstein four-manifolds satisfying a chiral curvature condition, namely negativity of the self-dual part of the curvature operator [FKS]. This generalizes the familiar local rigidity of negatively sectionally curved Einstein metrics in a four-dimensional direction. It does not classify negative Einstein four-manifolds, but it gives a useful boundary between flexible construction problems and rigid moduli problems.

The sphere question remains one of the cleanest test cases. The four-sphere satisfies the Hitchin-Thorpe inequality, so the basic topological obstruction gives no information. A negative Einstein metric on $\mathbb{S}^{4}$ would be neither Kähler nor locally symmetric in any standard way; conversely, no known gauge-theoretic or minimal-surface obstruction rules it out.

Related open problems.
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Decide whether $\mathbb{S}^{4}$ admits an Einstein metric with negative Einstein constant.
\item[2.] Original direction. Determine the geography region of pairs $(\chi(M), \tau(M))$ realized by compact Einstein four-manifolds with negative Einstein constant.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine which simply connected smooth four-manifolds in the Hitchin-Thorpe region admit negative Einstein metrics, and compare the answer with complex-surface geography [Au], [H], [L2], [Y].
\item[4.] Original direction. Classify compact negatively curved Einstein four-manifolds up to diffeomorphism in any natural subclass beyond the locally symmetric case.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Understand whether the Fine-Premoselli branched-cover construction can be varied to realize new geography regions or prescribed fundamental groups [FP].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether the Fine-Krasnov-Singer chiral-curvature rigidity hypothesis can be converted into a global compactness or finiteness theorem for negative Einstein four-manifolds [FKS].
\item[7.] AI-generated formulation. Find effective Seiberg-Witten or monopole-type obstructions which separate the topological Hitchin-Thorpe region into realizable and non-realizable Einstein geography regions.
\end{itemize}

\item[] [BCG] G. Besson, G. Courtois and S. Gallot, Minimal entropy and Mostow's rigidity theorems, Ergodic Theory Dynam. Systems 16 (1996), no. 4, 623-649.

\section*{Problem 26}

Find for each $N$ constants $c_{N}<C_{N}$ so that if the Ricci curvature of a compact manifold satisfies $c_{N} \delta_{i j} \leqslant R_{i j} \leqslant C_{N} \delta_{i j}$ then the manifold admits an Einstein metric.

Status. Open in the stated generality. No known universal pair of constants depending only on $N$ gives a theorem of this form from two-sided Ricci pinching alone.

The problem asks whether sufficiently strong Ricci pinching forces the underlying compact manifold to admit some Einstein metric, not necessarily the original one. This is weaker in conclusion than proving that the given metric evolves or deforms to an Einstein metric, but stronger in topology because the hypothesis controls only the Ricci tensor. In dimension three, Hamilton's Ricci flow theorem shows that compact three-manifolds with positive Ricci curvature are spherical space forms and hence admit constant-curvature Einstein metrics [Ham]. This gives a positive result in a special dimension with a stronger sign condition. Catino-Djadli obtained a different three-dimensional partial answer: an explicit integral pinching condition between the $L^{2}$ norms of the Ricci and scalar curvatures, together with positive scalar curvature, forces the manifold to be a spherical space form and hence to admit a positive Einstein metric [CD].

In higher dimensions, the strongest general convergence and classification theorems usually assume pinching of the full curvature operator, isotropic curvature, or sectional curvature, rather than only Ricci curvature. Hamilton's four-dimensional work on positive curvature operator [Ham4], Böhm-Wilking's theorem for positive curvature operator [BW], and Brendle-Schoen's differentiable sphere theorem under pointwise quarter-pinching [BS] show how powerful curvature pinching can be when it controls more than the Ricci tensor. These results are evidence for the philosophy of the problem, but do not settle the Ricci-pinching statement.

There are also analytic stability results near known Einstein metrics or in noncompact settings, where normalized Ricci flow can converge to an Einstein metric under nondegeneracy and sufficiently strong pinching assumptions [Kr, QSW]. Tian-Wang's work on almost-Einstein manifolds shows that, under noncollapsing and flow-type hypotheses, limits retain much of the structure known for Einstein limits [TW]. Such theorems are local, compactness-theoretic, or require additional structure; they do not give the universal compact theorem asked for here.

Anderson proved a precise "almost Einstein implies Einstein" theorem, but only inside a precompact geometric class [An, Corollary B(4)]. In dimension four his hypotheses control | Ric|, a scaled $L^{q / 2}$ norm of $D^{2}$ Ric for some $q>4$, the shortest inessential geodesic loop, volume, diameter, and $b_{2}$; if in addition $\int_{M} \mid$ Ric $-\lambda g \mid$ is sufficiently small, the underlying manifold admits a metric with $\operatorname{Ric}=\lambda g$. In general dimension the $b_{2}$ bound is replaced by an $L^{n / 2}$ bound on the full curvature. This is the closest classical theorem to Yau's formulation, but its extra compactness and derivative hypotheses are essential to the proof and are not consequences of two-sided Ricci pinching alone. Recent pinching results in submanifold geometry similarly show strong topological rigidity from Ricci lower bounds when extrinsic curvature data are present [GTZ]. They are useful analogies, not solutions of Yau's intrinsic problem.

The main difficulty is that two-sided Ricci control does not control the Weyl tensor. A metric may have Ricci tensor close to a constant multiple of the metric while its full curvature tensor, topology, or collapse behavior remains complicated. Thus a plausible modern version of the problem probably needs either a noncollapsing normalization, a curvature-operator hypothesis, or a Ricci-flow stability assumption in addition to pointwise Ricci pinching.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Find a topological obstruction to the existence of universal Ricci-pinching constants $c_{N}<C_{N}$ in this problem.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine which of the derivative, loop-length, volume, diameter, and curvature-energy hypotheses in Anderson's almost-Einstein theorem can be removed when one assumes pointwise two-sided Ricci pinching [An].
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether two-sided Ricci pinching plus noncollapsing and a uniform bound on $\|\mathrm{Rm}\|_{L^{n / 2}}$ suffices, without an a priori $D^{2}$ Ric bound, to force existence of an Einstein metric [An], [TW].
\item[4.] Original direction. Prove or disprove an analogous theorem for compact manifolds with positive Ricci curvature operator pinched in $L^{\infty}$ around a constant multiple of the metric.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Decide whether Ricci-pinching theorems for compact submanifolds have an intrinsic analogue after replacing extrinsic curvature control by harmonic-radius, Weyl-energy, or Sobolev-constant bounds [GTZ].
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Find a Ricci-flow invariant pinching condition involving only the traceless Ricci tensor and the scalar curvature which forces convergence to an Einstein metric.
\end{itemize}

\section*{C. Scalar curvature}

Scalar curvature is the weakest of the classical pointwise curvature conditions, but it is also the one most tightly connected with topology, conformal geometry, and general relativity. Since Yau's 1980 list, three themes have transformed the subject: Ricci flow and geometrization in dimension three, surgery and index theory for positive scalar curvature in high dimensions, and minimal hypersurface or minimal-slicing methods which give non-spin obstructions. The problems below should therefore be read not only as classification questions, but also as requests for the right interface between local scalar lower bounds, global topology, and large-scale geometry.

\section*{Problem 27}

Classify complete, three-dimensional manifolds with nonnegative scalar curvature.
This is considerably interesting for general relativity because the "universe" tends to have such metrics. In fact, under physically reasonable assumptions, Schoen and Yau [SY2] did prove that such metrics always exist on the universe.

Schoen-Yau [SY3] also proved that the fundamental group of such a manifold does not contain a subgroup which is isomorphic to the fundamental group of a compact surface of genus $\geqslant 1$. In the case of a compact manifold, this was proven in [SY4]. For dimension exceeding three, the problem was considered in Schoen-Yau [SY5] and Gromov-Lawson [GL].

Status. Partially resolved. Several sharp three-dimensional regimes are classified, with major recent progress on the open case, but there is not yet an unrestricted classification of all complete noncompact three-manifolds with scalar curvature $R \geqslant 0$. The closed orientable positive-scalar-curvature case is classified by geometrization. The complete open case with uniformly positive scalar curvature is now topologically classified, while the borderline nonnegative case remains more delicate because flat regions, splitting, and degeneration at infinity can occur.

For compact orientable three-manifolds, the positive scalar curvature classification says that the manifold is a connected sum of spherical space forms and copies of $\mathbb{S}^{2} \times \mathbb{S}^{1}$; the nonnegative case also allows flat manifolds. Schoen-Yau's minimal surface arguments supplied early topological restrictions, excluding incompressible higher-genus surface subgroups in the relevant settings [SY3], [SY4]. Perelman's Ricci-flow proof of geometrization then gives the full compact topological classification.

For complete noncompact three-manifolds, the first decisive classification theorem was obtained under both bounded geometry and uniform positivity. Bessières-Besson-Maillot proved that an orientable open three-manifold admits a complete bounded-geometry metric with uniformly positive scalar curvature if and only if it is a possibly infinite connected sum of $\mathbb{S}^{2} \times \mathbb{S}^{1}$ 's and spherical space forms from a finite list [BBM]. This is a genuine noncompact analogue of the compact geometrization classification. Bessières-Besson-Maillot- Marques later proved path connectedness of the moduli space of such bounded-geometry uniformly positive scalar curvature metrics [BBMM].

Recent work has pushed the classification well beyond the original bounded-geometry framework. Jian Wang proved that every complete orientable three-manifold with uniformly positive scalar curvature is homeomorphic to an infinite connected sum of spherical three-manifolds and copies of $\mathbb{S}^{1} \times \mathbb{S}^{2}$ [W3]. His proof uses $\mu$-bubbles to produce a prime decomposition and minimal-surface arguments to identify the prime factors. This removes the bounded-geometry hypothesis from the topological classification in the uniformly positive case and should now be regarded as one of the closest modern answers to Yau's original three-dimensional question.

Other recent results attack the gap between uniform positivity and mere nonnegativity. In a series of papers, Jian Wang studied complete contractible three-manifolds with positive scalar curvature: the Whitehead manifold, and more generally contractible genus-one three-manifolds, admit no complete positive-scalar-curvature metric; moreover, a complete contractible three-manifold with positive scalar curvature and trivial fundamental group at infinity is homeomorphic to $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ [W1], [W2]. Chodosh-Lai-Xu proved that a complete contractible three-manifold with positive scalar curvature and bounded geometry must be $\mathbb{R}^{3}$, and that an open handlebody of genus larger than one admits no complete nonnegative-scalar-curvature metric with bounded geometry [CLX]. Their argument uses inverse mean curvature flow in a form suited to noncompact topology. Yan-Zhu recently obtained borderline nonnegative-scalar-curvature results under additional harmonic function regularity assumptions. More precisely, they assume a global lower Ricci bound and, in the nonparabolic case, decay to zero of the minimal positive Green function at infinity; under these hypotheses contractible examples are forced to be $\mathbb{R}^{3}$, and open handlebodies are forced to have genus at most one [YZ]. The proof replaces inverse mean curvature flow by exhaustions through level sets of proper positive harmonic functions and controls their genus by refined average-gradient estimates.

There are also new obstruction results for open manifolds in the same circle of ideas. Shi-Wang-Wu-Zhu introduced open Schoen-Yau-Schick type conditions and proved nonexistence of complete positive-scalar-curvature metrics for such open manifolds [SWWZ]. Together these papers show a clear modern strategy: combine $\mu$-bubbles, stable minimal surfaces, inverse
mean curvature flow, harmonic functions, and three-dimensional topology to isolate which ends and handlebody structures are compatible with scalar curvature lower bounds.

Two further results clarify how topology at infinity enters. Zhu proved a scalar-curvature analogue of the Calabi-Yau volume theorem for manifolds of dimension at most four which are aspherical at infinity, and showed that a complete contractible threemanifold with positive scalar curvature and sublinear volume growth is $\mathbb{R}^{3}[\mathrm{Z}]$. Chen-Chu-Zhu proved in dimensions three, four, and five that a connected sum of a closed aspherical manifold with an arbitrary noncompact manifold admits no complete metric of positive scalar curvature; their nonnegative version yields a flat rigidity alternative [CCZ].

In the asymptotically flat setting, Haslhofer introduced a mass-decreasing flow built from Ricci flow with surgery and conformal rescaling [Ha]. These results show that scalar curvature classification is accessible when the geometry at infinity is controlled, and increasingly also under topological or analytic conditions at infinity; the unresolved part is what remains true for complete metrics with only $R \geqslant 0$ and no bounded-geometry, uniform positivity, harmonic-function, or asymptotic-flatness assumption.

In higher dimensions, positive scalar curvature behaves quite differently. Schoen-Yau and Gromov-Lawson developed the surgery and minimal-hypersurface theory of positive scalar curvature [SY5], [GL]. The high-dimensional simply connected classification involves spin, index-theoretic, and surgery-theoretic obstructions, rather than a three-dimensional prime-decomposition picture.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Classify complete orientable three-manifolds with $R \geqslant 0$ without assuming bounded geometry, uniform positivity, or asymptotic flatness.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether Jian Wang's infinite-connected-sum classification for uniformly positive scalar curvature admits a borderline analogue for $R \geqslant 0$, possibly after separating flat ends and splitting regions [W3].
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine the weakest geometric or analytic hypotheses under which the Bessières-Besson-Maillot and Wang connected-sum classification remains valid [BBM], [BBMM], [W3].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Develop a borderline theory for complete three-manifolds with $R \geqslant 0$ which separates flat, splitting, asymptotically flat, and uniformly positive scalar curvature ends [Ha], [YZ], [Z].
\item[5.] Original direction. Classify asymptotically flat three-manifolds with nonnegative scalar curvature and minimal boundary in terms of mass, topology, and Ricci-flow surgery.
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Decide whether every complete contractible three-manifold with positive scalar curvature is $\mathbb{R}^{3}$, and identify the minimal hypotheses at infinity needed to rule out Whitehead-type behavior [CLX], [W1], [W2], [YZ].
\item[7.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify open handlebodies admitting complete metrics with $R>0$ or $R \geqslant 0$ under bounded geometry, harmonic-function regularity, or other natural control at infinity [CLX], [YZ].
\item[8.] Reference-driven formulation. Decide whether a complete three-manifold with $R \geqslant 0$ and controlled topology at infinity admits a canonical Ricci-flow or inverse-mean-curvature-flow decomposition [CLX], [Ha].
\item[9.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend the open Schoen-Yau-Schick obstruction framework to borderline nonnegative scalar curvature and to weakly controlled ends [CCZ], [SWWZ].
\item[10.] AI-generated formulation. Formulate a coarse topological invariant of a complete noncompact three-manifold which detects whether it can carry a complete nonnegative scalar curvature metric with controlled geometry at infinity.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 28}

Classify all compact, four-dimensional, Einstein manifolds with positive scalar curvature.

Status. Open in general. There is no classification of compact four-dimensional Einstein manifolds with positive scalar curvature without additional hypotheses. Several important subclasses are understood.

The basic topological restriction is the Hitchin-Thorpe inequality: if $\left(M^{4}, g\right)$ is compact, oriented and Einstein, then $2 \chi(M) \geqslant 3|\tau(M)|$, with equality only in the flat or Ricci-flat Kähler cases and their quotients [H]. This gives a first geography constraint, but it is far from sufficient.

Under extra complex or symplectic structure, much more is known. In the Kähler case, positive Einstein constant is the Fano Kähler-Einstein problem. The modern Yau-Tian-Donaldson theorem identifies the existence of Kähler-Einstein metrics on smooth Fano manifolds with algebro-geometric stability [CDS, Ti]. In complex dimension two this places the positive Kähler-Einstein part inside the well-developed classification of del Pezzo surfaces, while still leaving the purely Riemannian Einstein problem much broader. Chen-LeBrun-Weber classified the conformally Kähler-Einstein case arising in this setting [CLW]. LeBrun then gave a complete diffeomorphism classification for compact four-manifolds which admit both a complex or symplectic structure and an Einstein metric with nonnegative Einstein constant [L1]. The resulting list is governed by del Pezzo surfaces in the positive case, together with the Ricci-flat possibilities in the nonnegative case.

The moduli viewpoint has also become much sharper in the Kähler subclass. Odaka-Spotti-Sun proved that the Gromov-Hausdorff compactification of moduli spaces of Kähler-Einstein del Pezzo surfaces agrees with an algebro-geometric compactification by singular log Fano surfaces [OSS]. This does not classify all positive Einstein four-manifolds, but it gives a model for what a successful classification may need to include: not only diffeomorphism types, but also moduli components, degenerations, and singular compactifications.

There are also rigidity theorems under stronger curvature hypotheses. Gursky and LeBrun proved that a compact oriented Einstein four-manifold with nonnegative sectional curvature and positive intersection form is, up to scaling and isometry, the Fubini-Study metric on $\mathbb{C P}^{2}$ [GL]. LeBrun's Seiberg-Witten estimates give stronger curvature inequalities and nonexistence results in many cases [L2]. More recently, LeBrun classified compact oriented Einstein four-manifolds with $\operatorname{det}\left(W^{+}\right)>0$ : there are exactly fifteen oriented diffeomorphism types, and on each the metrics in this curvature class form exactly one connected component of the Einstein moduli space [L3]. Dameno obtained a complementary twistorial rigidity theorem: if the scalar curvature of the Riemannian twistor space is constant along every twistor fiber, then the base Einstein four-manifold is half conformally flat; in the compact positive case this leaves only the standard $\mathbb{S}^{4}$ and $\mathbb{C P}^{2}$ [D]. These results show substantial progress, but they still fall well short of the unrestricted classification problem.

The known examples already show that the classification is not merely the list of locally symmetric spaces. Besides the round sphere, $\mathbb{C P}^{2}$, and products such as $\mathbb{S}^{2} \times \mathbb{S}^{2}$, there are inhomogeneous positive Einstein metrics such as the Page metric and the Chen-LeBrun-Weber metric. The central difficulty is to understand whether these constructions exhaust natural topological classes or whether there are many undetected positive Einstein smooth four-manifolds.

Even the variational behavior of the known examples contains useful structure. Biquard-Ozuch proved that every compact conformally Kähler Einstein four-manifold with nonnegative scalar curvature which is not half conformally flat is unstable for the Hilbert-Einstein functional; in particular this applies to the Chen-LeBrun-Weber metric [BO]. Thus existence, moduli, degeneration, and dynamical or variational stability are genuinely distinct layers of the classification problem.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Classify compact simply connected Einstein four-manifolds with positive Einstein constant.
\item[2.] Original direction. Decide whether the round metric is the unique positive Einstein metric on $\mathbb{S}^{4}$, up to scaling and diffeomorphism, under progressively weaker curvature assumptions.
\item[3.] Original direction. Decide whether every compact positive Einstein four-manifold with positive intersection form must be $\mathbb{C P}^{2}$ under weaker curvature assumptions than nonnegative sectional curvature.
\item[4.] Original direction. Determine the geography of compact positive Einstein four-manifolds in terms of $(\chi, \tau)$ and smooth structure.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether the moduli space of positive Einstein metrics on a fixed compact four-manifold has finitely many components under natural curvature, volume, or diameter bounds [L3], [OSS].
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether the twistor-fiber scalar-curvature condition in Dameno's theorem can be replaced by an integral pinching or small-oscillation hypothesis [D].
\item[7.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify the stable critical points of the Hilbert-Einstein functional among positive Einstein four-manifolds, beyond the conformally Kähler class [BO].
\item[8.] Reference-driven formulation. Relate the Fano Kähler-Einstein classification in complex dimension two to the larger Riemannian positive Einstein geography problem [CDS], [OSS], [Ti].
\item[9.] AI-generated formulation. Find a curvature condition strictly weaker than $\operatorname{det}\left(W^{+}\right)>0$ but strong enough to yield a finite diffeomorphism classification of positive Einstein four-manifolds.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 29}

Prove that a compact $K(\pi, 1)$ manifold with nonnegative scalar curvature is flat.

Status. Partially resolved. The statement is known in dimensions at most five; the new cases 4 and 5 were proved by Chodosh-Li using generalized soap bubbles [CL]. Gromov gave an independent closely related proof in dimension 5 through stable $\mu$-bubbles and torical symmetrization [Gr2]. In these dimensions a smooth closed aspherical manifold admits no metric of positive scalar curvature, and every metric of nonnegative scalar curvature is flat. The problem remains open in full generality for arbitrary closed aspherical manifolds in higher dimensions, although it is known for important classes, including tori, enlargeable manifolds, many spin manifolds detected by higher index theory, and several classes accessible by minimal-hypersurface methods.

The statement is a rigidity form of the positive scalar curvature problem. If an aspherical manifold admits a metric with scalar curvature nonnegative and positive somewhere, then a conformal deformation often converts it to a positive scalar curvature problem. Thus obstructions to positive scalar curvature become obstructions to the nonnegative case, except for the rigid scalarflat possibility. Bourguignon's deformation theorem shows that a scalar-flat metric which is not Ricci-flat can be deformed to one with positive scalar curvature [B]. Hence, once positive scalar curvature is excluded, any nonnegative-scalar-curvature metric must be Ricci-flat. On a closed aspherical manifold, the Cheeger-Gromoll splitting theorem then forces the universal cover of a Ricci-flat metric to split as Euclidean space; equivalently, the compact quotient is a flat Bieberbach manifold [CG]. This explains why the main difficulty is the exclusion of positive scalar curvature.

The problem originates in the Schoen-Yau program relating stable minimal hypersurfaces to scalar curvature. Schoen-Yau proved strong low-dimensional results, including scalar-flat rigidity phenomena for tori in the dimensions where the regularity theory is classical [SY]. Their strategy is to find a stable hypersurface in a suitable homology class, use the stability inequality and the Gauss equation to descend the scalar-curvature condition by one dimension, and iterate until a topological contradiction appears. Their later minimal-slicing program extends this obstruction mechanism beyond the classical regularity range and is designed precisely to handle singularities of stable hypersurfaces [SY2].

Chodosh-Li completed the Schoen-Yau outline for closed aspherical 4-manifolds and developed the additional slice-anddice and $\mu$-bubble arguments needed in dimension 5 [CL]. The technical issue in dimension 5 is not merely the existence of a stable hypersurface, but the need to localize the argument and control the topology and diameter of the lower-dimensional pieces produced by the descent. Gromov obtained a closely related dimension-5 theorem through stable $\mu$-bubbles and torical symmetrization, proving in particular the nonexistence of positive scalar curvature on closed aspherical manifolds of dimension $\leqslant 5$ [Gr2]. Gromov-Lawson introduced enlargeability and proved that enlargeable spin manifolds, including tori, do not admit positive scalar curvature [GL1], [GL2]. Together with rigidity arguments, this gives the expected flat conclusion for many classical examples. Rosenberg's index-theoretic formulation and the Gromov-Lawson-Rosenberg program connect the general aspherical case with higher index theory and the Novikov/Baum- Connes circle of ideas [R]. Hanke-Schick strengthened the enlargeability obstruction by proving nontriviality of the universal index class for enlargeable spin manifolds [HS].

There is also nearby recent progress for open four-manifolds with uniformly positive scalar curvature. Chodosh-Maximo-Mukherjee proved topological obstructions for complete noncompact four-manifolds, including restrictions for interiors of compact contractible four-manifolds and for exotic $\mathbb{R}^{4}$ 's [CMM]. These results do not solve the closed $K(\pi, 1)$ problem, but they show that the soap-bubble and low-dimensional scalar-curvature methods are now probing genuinely four-dimensional smooth phenomena beyond the closed aspherical setting.

The low-dimensional obstruction has also acquired noncompact and splitting forms. Chen-Chu-Zhu proved that, for dimensions three through five, the connected sum of a closed aspherical manifold with an arbitrary noncompact manifold carries no complete positive-scalar- curvature metric, and their nonnegative theorem has a flat rigidity alternative [CCZ]. He-Zhu proved that a complete orientable aspherical manifold of dimension at most five with two ends and nonnegative scalar curvature splits as a product of a closed flat manifold with $\mathbb{R}[\mathrm{HZ}]$. These results do not settle the closed problem above dimension five, but they identify quantitative filling and homology-vanishing statements which a higher-dimensional proof would have to supply.

A very recent extension concerns symplectic rather than homotopy asphericity. Di Cerbo-Dranishnikov-Jauhari proved that a closed c-symplectically aspherical manifold with a spin finite cover admits no Riemannian metric of positive scalar curvature, proved the unconditional result in real dimensions two and four, and showed that smooth projective varieties mapping birationally to symplectically aspherical projective varieties admit no positive-scalar-curvature Kähler metric [DDJ]. They formulate the broader conjecture that every closed symplectically aspherical manifold is obstructed from positive scalar curvature. This is not a solution of Yau's $K(\pi, 1)$ problem, but it enlarges the class of "aspherical" structures for which index and gauge-theoretic mechanisms can be tested.

The modern perspective separates three layers. First, positive scalar curvature on aspherical manifolds is obstructed by stable minimal hypersurfaces, enlargeability, and higher indices. Second, the scalar-flat borderline requires a rigidity theorem showing that a nonnegative-scalar-curvature metric which cannot be deformed to positive scalar curvature must be Ricci-flat, and then
flat in the aspherical setting. Third, Gromov's macroscopic scalar-curvature program asks for quantitative versions in which lower scalar curvature bounds force restrictions on Urysohn width, macroscopic dimension, filling radius, or enlargeability at large scale [Gr]. The full statement for all compact $K(\pi, 1)$ manifolds remains a major open direction.

Related open problems.
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Prove the nonnegative scalar curvature rigidity statement for all closed aspherical manifolds of dimension at least 6.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Quantify the Bourguignon-Cheeger-Gromoll rigidity mechanism: under which noncol-lapsing and diameter hypotheses does a closed aspherical metric with $R \geqslant-\epsilon$ and small negative part converge to a flat metric as $\epsilon \rightarrow 0[\mathrm{~B}],[\mathrm{CG}]$ ?
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Relate this rigidity problem to the strong Novikov conjecture and the Baum-Connes assembly map for the fundamental group.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether minimal-slicing methods prove the aspherical positive-scalar-curvature obstruction for all closed non-spin $K(\pi, 1)$ manifolds in dimensions at least 6 [SY2].
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether the Chodosh-Li $\mu$-bubble and slice-and-dice argument admits a higher-dimensional replacement for the controlled-diameter decomposition used in dimension 5 [CL], [Gr2].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend scalar-flat rigidity from tori and enlargeable spin manifolds to non-spin aspherical manifolds detected by minimal-slicing or macroscopic scalar-curvature methods.
\item[7.] Reference-driven formulation. Compare the closed aspherical rigidity problem with the new obstruction theory for open four-manifolds and exotic $\mathbb{R}^{4}$ 's with uniformly positive scalar curvature and with noncompact aspherical splitting theorems [CCZ], [CMM], [HZ].
\item[8.] Reference-driven formulation. Decide whether the spin-cover and low-dimensional hypotheses in the symplectically aspherical obstruction can be removed [DDJ].
\item[9.] AI-generated formulation. Establish a quantitative version: if a closed aspherical manifold has scalar curvature $R \geqslant-\epsilon$ together with noncollapsing and diameter or width control, must it be close in a geometric or coarse sense to a flat, enlargeable, or lower-dimensional model when $\epsilon$ is small?
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 30}

Prove that a compact, simply-connected, three-dimensional manifold with positive scalar curvature is homeomorphic to the sphere. It is proved in Meeks-Simon-Yau [MSY] that the connected sum of two fake three-spheres does not admit a metric with positive Ricci curvature.

Status. Solved. The conclusion follows from Perelman's proof of the Poincaré conjecture, which is a consequence of the geometrization theorem and finite-extinction argument [Pe], [Pe2]. The scalar curvature assumption is no longer needed.

Before Perelman, the problem was connected with the interaction between scalar curvature, Ricci curvature, and minimal surface methods in dimension three. Meeks-Simon-Yau proved that the connected sum of two fake three-spheres does not admit a metric of positive Ricci curvature [MSY]. Perelman's Ricci-flow proof shows that a compact simply connected three-manifold is homeomorphic to $\mathbb{S}^{3}$, so the original scalar-curvature version is now a topological corollary.

For the simply connected case, Perelman's finite-extinction theorem provides a particularly direct analytic bridge: on a closed oriented three-manifold whose prime decomposition has no aspherical factor, every Ricci flow with surgery becomes extinct in finite time [Pe2, Theorem 1.1]. Applied together with the surgery and canonical-neighborhood theory, this gives elliptization and hence the Poincaré conclusion.

The modern refinement concerns not only existence but the topology of the space of metrics. Bamler-Kleiner proved that, for any closed three-manifold, the space of positive-scalar-curvature metrics is either empty or contractible [BK]. In particular, on $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ the moduli-level analogue of the solved topological problem is also extremely rigid: once positive-scalar-curvature metrics exist, their space has no higher homotopical ambiguity before quotienting by diffeomorphisms. Their proof evolves continuous families by canonical singular Ricci flows and uses "partial homotopies" to pass through neck singularities while preserving positive scalar curvature. Their Ricci-flow-through-singularities techniques also prove the generalized Smale conjecture for spherical space forms, linking scalar curvature, Ricci flow, and diffeomorphism groups.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Understand which geometric curvature conditions give direct analytic proofs of the three-dimensional sphere theorem without using the full geometrization theorem.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify moduli spaces of positive scalar curvature metrics on $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ and on connected sums of spherical space forms and $\mathbb{S}^{2} \times \mathbb{S}^{1}$ [BK].
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether canonical paths produced by Ricci flow with surgery give natural deformation retractions of spaces of positive scalar curvature metrics in dimension three [BK], [Pe].
\item[4.] AI-generated formulation. Find a min-max or stable minimal surface proof of the scalar-curvature sphere conclusion which recovers the Poincaré conclusion under an explicit geometric normalization.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 31}

Classify compact hypersurfaces in $\mathbb{R}^{N+1}$ which have constant scalar curvature. Are they isometric to $\mathbb{S}^{N}$ ? If they are convex, then the answer is yes and was proved by Cheng-Yau [CY].

Status. Solved for embedded compact hypersurfaces. Ros proved that a compact embedded hypersurface in Euclidean space with constant scalar curvature is a round sphere [R]. Thus the answer to the original compact embedded problem is yes.

Cheng and Yau proved earlier that a complete oriented hypersurface in a real space form, with constant scalar curvature and nonnegative sectional curvature, is totally umbilical, a product of two totally umbilical factors, or flat; in Euclidean space their complete noncompact classification gives the generalized cylinders $\mathbb{S}^{N-k} \times \mathbb{R}^{k}$ [CY]. In the compact convex case this gives the round sphere. Their main analytic device is the self-adjoint second-order operator associated with the first Newton transformation of the shape operator. Ros later removed the convexity assumption in the compact embedded case by combining a Reilly identity on the bounded domain with the Newton inequalities [R], [R2].

The distinction between embedded and immersed hypersurfaces is essential. Alexandrov-type reflection and Reilly identities use the global embedded domain in an important way. For embedded compact hypersurfaces, the constant scalar curvature result fits into a wider family of rigidity theorems for constant higher-order mean curvatures: Ros proved corresponding Alexandrov-type theorems for higher mean curvatures [R2]. The immersed case is more flexible and is not covered by the same argument.

Recent work around adjacent curvature hypotheses clarifies how special the constant-scalar-curvature condition is. Ge-Zhao proved that, for hypersurfaces in real space forms, having constant Ricci eigenvalues is equivalent to curvature homogeneity, and hence falls under the known classification of curvature-homogeneous hypersurfaces [GZ]. In particular, a complete hypersurface with constant Ricci eigenvalues is isoparametric in a sphere and, in Euclidean space, whenever it is nonflat; the hyperbolic statement has explicit exceptional cases. This is a stronger condition than constant scalar curvature alone, but it gives a nearby rigidity model: the Gauss equation turns the fixed Ricci spectrum into algebraic constraints on the principal curvatures, and real-analytic continuation fixes their multiplicities. Freitas- Guimarães recently extended the Reilly-formula congruence mechanism to compact hypersurfaces in a hemisphere [FG]. In a different direction, Fu-Hong-Yang-Zhan studied biconservative hypersurfaces with constant scalar curvature in space forms and obtained low-dimensional classification results [FHYZ]. These developments do not change Ros's solution in the compact embedded Euclidean case, but they identify natural strengthened and generalized settings in which classification remains active.

For complete noncompact hypersurfaces, stability hypotheses lead to different rigidity and nonexistence questions. Alencar-Santos-Zhou obtained nonexistence results for stable constant-scalar-curvature hypersurfaces in Euclidean spaces under curvature and finite total-curvature assumptions [ASZ].

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Classify immersed, rather than embedded, compact hypersurfaces in Euclidean space with constant scalar curvature.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Study analogous rigidity for hypersurfaces with constant higher order mean curvature in space forms [R2].
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify compact hypersurfaces with constant scalar curvature in hyperbolic space or the sphere under the weakest embeddedness, star-shapedness, or curvature-sign assumptions, comparing the result with the stronger constant-Ricci-eigenvalue classification [GZ].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Prove quantitative stability versions of the Cheng-Yau and Ros sphere theorems under small oscillation of scalar curvature or higher mean curvature [CY], [R], [R2].
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine how much of the Ge-Zhao constant-Ricci-eigenvalue classification survives when one assumes only constant scalar curvature plus one additional curvature pinching or multiplicity condition [GZ].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend the spherical hemisphere congruence theorem from comparison of mean-curvature vectors to a natural scalar-curvature comparison [FG].
\item[7.] AI-generated formulation. Determine whether a quantitative almost-constant scalar curvature condition forces a compact embedded hypersurface to be close to a round sphere, with an explicit rate in terms of the scalar-curvature oscillation and a noncollapsing constant.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 32}
(Yamabe) Prove that any metric on a compact manifold can be conformally deformed to a metric of constant scalar curvature. Yamabe published a proof [Ya], but N. Trudinger [Tr] found a gap in the work after Yamabe's death. Nonetheless, Yamabe's original proof can be pushed to cover a large class of metrics, as was made clear by Trudinger (see also Eliasson [E]).

Pushing further, Aubin [Au] solved the problem for an even broader class, in dimension $s \geqslant 6$
However, even for surfaces of genus zero it is nontrivial to find a proof without use of a complex analysis.
One can formulate a similar conjecture in the class of complete noncompact manifolds. Progress has been made recently by W. M. Ni [Ni].

Status. Solved for compact manifolds. The Yamabe problem was completed by the combined work of Yamabe, Trudinger, Aubin, and Schoen, with Schoen's 1984 paper closing the remaining cases [Ya], [Tr], [Au], [S].

Yamabe introduced the variational problem and claimed a proof [Ya]. Trudinger found a gap and established important partial results [Tr]. Aubin developed the sharp Sobolev and test-function estimates which settled many cases, especially in high dimensions or when the Weyl tensor is nonzero somewhere [Au]. Schoen completed the proof by using the positive mass theorem to handle the locally conformally flat and remaining low-dimensional cases [S]. Thus every conformal class on a compact smooth manifold contains a metric of constant scalar curvature.

After existence, the next questions concern uniqueness, compactness, flow convergence, and the structure of the solution set. If the Yamabe constant is nonpositive, the Yamabe metric is unique up to scaling. In the positive case uniqueness can fail; Pollack proved nonuniqueness results [P]. Schoen's compactness conjecture predicted compactness of the set of Yamabe solutions away from the conformal sphere. This is true in several ranges and under additional hypotheses, but false in high dimensions: Brendle constructed counterexamples in dimensions $n \geqslant 52$ [B], and Brendle-Marques extended counterexamples to $25 \leqslant n \leqslant 51[\mathrm{BM}]$. Khuri-Marques- Schoen proved compactness of the full solution set for all smooth compact manifolds of dimension at most 24, away from the conformal round sphere [KMS]. No spin hypothesis occurs in the statement of their main compactness theorem. Their proof combines sharp blow-up expansions, Weyl vanishing through a dimension-dependent quadratic form, and positive mass; the same quadratic form acquires negative directions beginning in dimension 25, which drive the counterexamples [B], [BM], [BMS]. Thus the solved existence theorem opened a finer geometric analysis problem: describe precisely when bubbling occurs and how the conformal geometry detects it.

For complete noncompact manifolds the direct analogue is false in general. Jin constructed counterexamples to the complete noncompact Yamabe problem [J]. The noncompact problem remains active under extra hypotheses; negative scalar curvature is better understood, while the nonnegative and positive cases are more delicate.

Several modern variants show that the Yamabe problem has become a prototype for conformally covariant geometric flows and nonlocal operators. Brendle first proved arbitrary-initial-energy convergence when $3 \leqslant n \leqslant 5$ or the initial metric is locally conformally flat [Br1]. His higher-dimensional paper uses a Weyl-tensor condition and the positive mass theorem; in its original form the corresponding spin or curvature hypotheses must be retained, while the modern all-dimensional positive mass theorem supplies the missing global input [Br2]. More recent work studies weighted and fractional analogues: Yan proved convergence of a weighted Yamabe flow on smooth metric measure spaces [Yan], while An-Chan-Ho proved convergence of the fractional Yamabe flow for arbitrary initial energy under the validity of the corresponding fractional positive mass conjecture [ACH]. These are not new proofs of the classical compact Yamabe theorem, but they show how the Yamabe mechanism continues to organize conformal geometry beyond the original local scalar-curvature equation. In CR geometry, Afeltra-Pinamonti-Ho obtained new compactness and noncompactness results for the CR Yamabe problem [APH], reinforcing the lesson that compactness is a separate and subtler question from existence.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Give a complete classification of conformal classes with unique Yamabe metric up to scaling.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. In dimensions $n \geqslant 25$, classify the conformal classes for which the full Yamabe solution set is nevertheless compact, and isolate geometric conditions that exclude the negative quadratic-form directions used in the counterexamples [KMS], [B], [BM].
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Describe the bubbling set and blow-up profiles for Yamabe metrics in all noncompactness regimes, including the role of Weyl jets and mass-type invariants [B], [BM].
\item[4.] Original direction. Classify complete noncompact manifolds whose conformal class contains a complete constant scalar curvature metric.
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine convergence and singularity formation for the Yamabe flow in conformal classes where the Yamabe solution set is noncompact or nonunique [Br1], [Br2].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Develop relative, boundary, CR, weighted, and fractional Yamabe theories compatible with positive mass, minimal boundary, asymptotically flat geometry, and fractional positive mass hypotheses [Ni], [APH], [Yan], [ACH].
\item[7.] AI-generated formulation. Develop a compactification theory for noncompact Yamabe solutions which detects bubbling and loss of completeness by geometric invariants at infinity, and compare the resulting boundary data with the compactness theory for the compact Yamabe equation.
\end{itemize}

\item[] [Ya] H. Yamabe, On a deformation of Riemannian structures on compact manifolds, Osaka Math. J. 12 (1960), 21-37.
\end{itemize}

\section*{II. Curvature and Complex Structure}

\section*{Problem 33}

Let $M$ be a compact Kähler manifold with nonnegative bisectional curvature. Prove that $M$ is biholomorphic to a locally symmetric Kähler manifold, at least when the Ricci curvature is positive.

If the bisectional curvature is strictly positive, the manifold is in fact biholomorphic to $\mathbb{C P}^{N}$, as was conjectured by Frankel and proved in Mori [Mo] and Siu-Yau [SiY].

Status. Solved. Mok proved the generalized Frankel conjecture: a compact Kähler manifold with nonnegative holomorphic bisectional curvature has universal cover biholomorphic and isometric to a product of complex Euclidean space, complex projective spaces, and irreducible compact Hermitian symmetric spaces [Mok]. More precisely, the projective-space factors may carry nonnegative-bisectional Kähler metrics rather than only their canonical symmetric metrics, whereas the irreducible rank-at-least-two Hermitian symmetric factors carry their canonical metrics. In the compact case with positive Ricci curvature there is no flat factor, giving the biholomorphic locally symmetric conclusion asked for here.

The strictly positive bisectional curvature case was the original Frankel conjecture. Mori proved the algebraic version via the Hartshorne conjecture on ample tangent bundles [Mo], and Siu-Yau gave the differential-geometric proof that a compact Kähler manifold with positive bisectional curvature is biholomorphic to $\mathbb{C P}^{N}[\mathrm{SiY}]$. Bando obtained an important three-dimensional classification under nonnegative bisectional curvature [B]. Mok's uniformization theorem completed the general nonnegative case [Mok].

Gu later gave a short transcendental proof of the irreducible core of Mok's theorem [Gu]. Under Kähler-Ricci flow, nonnegative bisectional curvature is preserved and the strong maximum principle transports its zero set by holonomy. In the nonsymmetric case full $U(n)$ holonomy then forces strict positivity and hence the $\mathbb{C P}^{n}$ conclusion; otherwise Berger holonomy leads to the locally symmetric alternative. The Howard-Smyth-Wu splitting theorem then restores the full universal-cover decomposition.

Later developments study weaker curvature conditions. Nonnegative quadratic bisectional curvature and orthogonal bisectional curvature were introduced and developed as possible extensions of the same classification philosophy [WYZ], [CT], [Z]. These conditions are weaker than full nonnegative bisectional curvature, so the corresponding classification problems remain subtler. Li-Wu-Zheng made this separation concrete: the seven-dimensional Kähler C-space $\left(B_{3}, \alpha_{2}\right)$ has nonnegative quadratic bisectional curvature but admits no Kähler metric with nonnegative orthogonal bisectional curvature [LWZ].

More recent work continues to search for curvature cones weaker than nonnegative bisectional curvature but still strong enough to retain Frankel-type consequences. Ni-Zheng introduced and studied cross quadratic bisectional curvature and its dual; among other results, they showed that positivity or nonnegativity of the first cross-quadratic condition gives Fano-type conclusions under suitable hypotheses and is well behaved with respect to Kähler-Ricci-flow deformation [NZ]. Li has recently classified manifolds admitting a new "2-positive bisectional curvature" condition by using Yang-Mills flow on rational curves [Li]. In complex dimension two the class is exactly the del Pezzo surfaces; in dimensions at least three it consists precisely of $\mathbb{C P}^{n}$ and the smooth complex hyperquadric. The proof turns the desired classification into a lower bound for minimal anticanonical degrees and derives that bound from a Hamilton-type maximum principle for Yang-Mills flow on $\mathbb{C P}^{1}$. These results should be read as extensions of the solved Mok-Siu-Yau picture, not replacements for it: the main open issue is which weakened curvature cones still force a rational homogeneous or locally symmetric classification.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Classify compact simply connected locally irreducible Kähler manifolds with nonnegative quadratic bisectional curvature.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine the sharp classification under nonnegative orthogonal holomorphic bisectional curvature [CT], [GuZ].
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend Mok-type uniformization results to natural Hermitian, Sasaki, or orbifold settings, while preserving a curvature condition that is stable under a geometric flow [Mok], [Gu].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether cross quadratic bisectional curvature or its dual gives a classification of compact Kähler manifolds beyond the Fano conclusion known under first-order positivity assumptions [NZ].
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Test whether Li's "2-positive bisectional curvature" condition is preserved by natural flows and whether it forces rational homogeneity under additional simply connectedness or positivity assumptions [Li].
\end{itemize}
6. AI-generated formulation. Find a curvature cone strictly between nonnegative orthogonal bisectional curvature and nonnegative bisectional curvature which is preserved by Kähler-Ricci flow and still forces a locally symmetric classification.

\section*{Problem 34}

Let $M$ be a complete, noncompact, Kähler manifold with positive bisectional curvature. Prove that $M$ is biholomorphic to $\mathbb{C}^{N}$.
Status. Open in full generality. The strongest known results prove the conclusion under additional hypotheses such as maximal volume growth, bounded curvature, curvature decay, or other quantitative control at infinity.

The first obstruction is already analytic: from positive bisectional curvature alone it is still not known in general that $M$ is Stein. Greene and Wu proved that positive sectional curvature gives strictly plurisubharmonic exhaustion functions in important cases, hence Steinness in that stronger Riemannian curvature setting [GW]. Siu and Yau developed related uniformization criteria for complete Kähler manifolds under curvature and decay assumptions [SiY].

The next stage concerns maximum volume growth and curvature decay. Mok-Siu-Yau used Poincaré-Lelong type techniques on complete Kähler manifolds to obtain uniformization results under strong curvature decay and volume growth assumptions [MSY]. Mok then proved an embedding theorem: a complete Kähler manifold with positive bisectional curvature, quadratic curvature decay, and maximal volume growth is biholomorphic to an affine algebraic variety; in complex dimension two, with positive sectional curvature, this gives $\mathbb{C}^{2}$ [Mok]. Shi's work on Ricci flow for complete noncompact Kähler manifolds gave a major analytic tool for later progress [Shi].

In complex dimension two, the conclusion was strengthened under bounded positive bisectional curvature, Euclidean volume growth, and average scalar curvature decay: the surface is biholomorphic to $\mathbb{C}^{2}[\mathrm{CTZ}]$. Related work also showed that bounded positive sectional curvature together with finite analytic first Chern number forces a complete noncompact Kähler surface to be biholomorphic to $\mathbb{C}^{2}[\mathrm{CZ}]$. In all dimensions, Kähler-Ricci flow methods then yielded the theorem that bounded nonnegative holomorphic bisectional curvature plus maximal volume growth implies biholomorphism to $\mathbb{C}^{n}$ [CT1]. A different partial result shows that, under bounded curvature and uniform linear average scalar curvature decay, the manifold is holomorphically covered by a pseudoconvex domain in $\mathbb{C}^{n}$ homeomorphic to $\mathbb{R}^{2 n}$; in particular the simply connected case is Stein [CT2].

A later decisive development is Gang Liu's proof of Yau's uniformization conjecture under maximal volume growth: every complete noncompact Kähler manifold with nonnegative bisectional curvature and maximal volume growth is biholomorphic to $\mathbb{C}^{n}$ [Liu]. Thus the remaining core of the problem is the case without maximal volume growth or comparable control at infinity. The nonnegative bisectional curvature "complex soul" picture also remains an important related direction: one expects a complete noncompact Kähler manifold with nonnegative bisectional curvature to admit a holomorphic structure analogous to a vector bundle over a compact nonnegatively curved Kähler manifold; partial results are known under bounded curvature assumptions [CCZ], and plurisubharmonic-function methods give structure and splitting theorems [NT].

More recent work has clarified the algebraic and analytic mechanisms behind the maximal-volume-growth case. Liu proved finite generation of the ring of polynomial-growth holomorphic functions on complete noncompact Kähler manifolds with nonnegative bisectional curvature, and showed that in the maximal-volume-growth case the manifold is an affine algebraic variety before proving the full biholomorphism to $\mathbb{C}^{n}$ [Liu2], [Liu]. Lee-Tam gave a Ricci-flow route to the same maximalvolume-growth uniformization by first deforming suitable noncollapsed complete metrics to bounded nonnegative bisectional curvature [LT]. Chen-Zhu's volume-growth and curvature-decay estimates, and Ogaja's work on nonmaximal volume growth, emphasize that the main remaining obstacle is to produce enough holomorphic functions or a Stein exhaustion without assuming the strongest asymptotic volume condition [CZ2], [O].

Shi has now made the maximal-growth mechanism quantitative [Shi2]. Assuming nonnegative bisectional curvature and no flat splitting of the universal cover, maximal volume growth is equivalent to the existence of a nonconstant polynomial-growth holomorphic function and to finite average scalar-curvature decay. Refined minimal degrees of global holomorphic coordinates and of canonical forms determine the asymptotic volume ratio and scalar-curvature decay, and also describe Lyapunov exponents of the Kähler-Ricci flow. This unifies the Liu and flow-based proofs, but it also exposes their limitation: in the nonmaximalgrowth case these polynomial-growth functions do not exist.

A major 2026 advance removes every hypothesis at infinity in a stronger-curvature surface case. Datar-Pingali-Seshadri proved that every complete noncompact Kähler surface with positive real sectional curvature is biholomorphic to $\mathbb{C}^{2}$ [DPS]. Their method replaces polynomial-growth coordinates by uniformly Lipschitz plurisubharmonic weights with finite Monge-Ampère mass, weighted $L^{p}$ holomorphic functions, and new Bézout and multiplicity estimates; finite generation and a two-stage algebraic embedding then allow Ramanujam's surface criterion to finish the proof. Positive real sectional curvature is stronger than positive holomorphic bisectional curvature. Indeed, their bisectional-curvature theorem still assumes strong Steinness and contractibility, followed by simple connectivity at infinity to obtain $\mathbb{C}^{2}$. Thus this breakthrough does not settle Yau's full conjecture, but it provides a genuinely new mechanism which does not start from maximal volume growth.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Prove Steinness for complete noncompact Kähler manifolds with positive holomorphic bisectional curvature, without assuming bounded curvature, maximal volume growth, or scalar-curvature decay.
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[2.] Original direction. Remove maximal volume growth from the known $\mathbb{C}^{n}$ uniformization theorems for nonnegative or positive bisectional curvature.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Develop a precise "complex soul theorem" for complete noncompact Kähler manifolds with nonnegative bisectional curvature, identifying when the manifold is biholomorphic to a holomorphic vector bundle over a compact Kähler manifold with nonnegative bisectional curvature [CCZ], [NT].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether polynomial-growth holomorphic functions, heat-flow deformation of plurisubharmonic functions, or Kähler-Ricci flow asymptotics can replace volume-growth assumptions in the uniformization problem [Liu2], [LT], [Shi2].
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Relate lower bounds on volume growth and average curvature decay to the existence of enough holomorphic functions to prove Steinness [CZ2], [O].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend the finite Monge-Ampère-mass weight and Bézout method from positive sectional curvature to positive bisectional curvature, first for complex surfaces and then in higher dimension [DPS].
\item[7.] Reference-driven formulation. In complex dimension two, decide whether positive bisectional curvature forces contractibility and simple connectivity at infinity, thereby supplying the missing topological hypotheses in the Datar-Pingali-Seshadri criterion [DPS].
\item[8.] AI-generated formulation. Find a curvature-decay condition strictly weaker than maximal volume growth but still strong enough to force Steinness and eventually biholomorphism to $\mathbb{C}^{n}$, perhaps by producing a canonical algebra of polynomialgrowth holomorphic functions.
\end{itemize}
