\section*{Problem 35}

Let $M$ be a complete, simply-connected, Kähler manifold with negative bisectional curvature. Prove that $M$ is Stein.
Status. False as stated. Mohsen constructed positive-dimensional compact simply connected Kähler complete intersections carrying metrics of negative holomorphic bisectional curvature [Moh]; his dimension estimate includes compact simply connected complex surfaces. A compact Kähler metric is complete, so these examples satisfy the completeness and simple-connectedness hypotheses in Yau's question. However, a positive-dimensional compact complex manifold cannot be Stein: every holomorphic function on it is constant by the maximum principle. Thus negative holomorphic bisectional curvature alone does not force noncompactness, and the Stein conclusion cannot be true without an additional hypothesis.

This correction is conceptually important. In complex dimension one, a simply connected complete surface with negative curvature is modeled by the disk in the classical uniformization theory, and is therefore Stein. One might hope for a higherdimensional analogue by analogy with Cartan-Hadamard geometry. That analogy is misleading here: negative holomorphic bisectional curvature is a condition on complex two-plane directions, not a statement that all real sectional curvatures are nonpositive. Mohsen's examples show that the missing real-sectional-curvature input is not a technical gap but a genuine obstruction to the proposed proof strategy.

The compact examples fit naturally with the modern algebraic picture. Negative holomorphic bisectional curvature implies negative holomorphic sectional curvature. By the Wu-Yau theorem, a compact Kähler manifold with negative holomorphic sectional curvature has ample canonical bundle [WY], with extensions to the quasi-negative setting due to Tosatti-Yang and Diverio-Trapani [TY, DT]. These results do not exclude compact examples; rather, they place them among canonically polarized projective manifolds. Mohsen's Donaldson-Auroux complete-intersection construction realizes compact simply connected examples in precisely this general-type world.

The right surviving problem is therefore a noncompact Stein problem. If compact counterexamples are excluded explicitly, one can still ask whether a complete noncompact simply connected Kähler manifold with negative holomorphic bisectional curvature admits a strictly plurisubharmonic exhaustion. That is the analytic heart of the Stein question. Noncompact examples with complete negatively curved Kähler metrics on vector-bundle total spaces show that the noncompact theory is substantial [WY2]. Work on real bisectional curvature and mixed curvature also indicates that related curvature cones can force positivity properties of the canonical bundle, but such algebraic positivity is weaker than the existence of a global Stein exhaustion [YZ, CLT].

One should also separate the compact classification problem from the noncompact analytic problem. Mohsen's theorem gives compact simply connected examples in every complex dimension at least two: take a simply connected projective ambient manifold of dimension at least $4 d-1$ and a sufficiently high-degree complete intersection of dimension $d$; Lefschetz gives simple connectivity [Moh]. Thus compact simply connected surfaces already occur, and complex dimension two is the smallest possible dimension. What remains is to classify their topology and deformation types and to find more explicit or lower-codimension constructions. Conversely, a positive answer to a suitably formulated noncompact Stein theorem would not contradict Mohsen's examples, because those examples are compact.

Recent work also sharpens the two sides of the corrected question. On the positive side, Lee proved that Stein manifolds with negatively pinched holomorphic sectional curvature admit complete negative Kähler-Einstein metrics and that normalized Kähler-Ricci flow can deform such metrics to the complete Kähler-Einstein metric [Lee]. This is a converse-type result: it starts from Steinness and produces canonical complete negative curvature, rather than deriving Steinness from bisectional negativity. On the construction side, Wan produced negative holomorphic sectional and bisectional curvature metrics on compact relative Kähler fibrations under curvature negativity conditions on the base and fibers [Wan]. Such examples reinforce the need to separate compact projective negative-curvature constructions from the noncompact Stein-exhaustion problem.

Bakkacha provides a useful converse model on bounded domains [Bak]. If a bounded domain carries a complete negatively pinched bisectional-curvature metric outside a compact set, the metric can be modified to have the same pinching everywhere. In particular this applies to strictly pseudoconvex bounded domains with $C^{2}$ boundary and to bounded domains whose squeezing function tends to one at every boundary point. The construction adds a large multiple of a ball Bergman metric and controls curvature by a spherical-comparison inequality. Since the domain is already Stein, this constructs curvature from Stein geometry rather than deriving Steinness from curvature.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify the topology and deformation types of Mohsen's compact simply connected negatively bisectional Kähler surfaces, and find explicit low-degree or lower-codimension models [Moh].
\item[2.] Original direction. Prove or disprove the noncompact replacement: if $M$ is complete, noncompact, simply connected, and Kähler with negative holomorphic bisectional curvature, must $M$ be Stein?
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Find geometric hypotheses at infinity, such as bounded geometry, curvature pinching, finite topological type, or controlled Kobayashi geometry, which force a strictly plurisubharmonic exhaustion in the noncompact case [Lee], [WY2], [Bak].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Clarify the relation between algebraic negativity of the tangent bundle, positivity of the cotangent bundle, positivity of the canonical bundle, and the existence of a Kähler metric with negative bisectional curvature [WY], [TY], [DT], [Moh].
\item[5.] AI-generated formulation. Find a curvature condition between negative holomorphic sectional curvature and negative bisectional curvature which is strong enough to imply Steinness for complete simply connected noncompact manifolds and still flexible enough to include natural bounded-domain examples.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 36}

If $M$ is complete, Kähler, of finite volume, and has bounded curvature, is $M$ a Zariski open set of some projective manifold? If $M$ has negative bisectional curvature, does $M$ have a finite automorphism group?

Status. The compactification assertion is false as stated, even if noncompactness is added. The automorphism-group assertion remains open under the stated pointwise negative bisectional-curvature hypothesis, although it is known under stronger uniform pinching and end-control assumptions.

Here is a direct counterexample to the first assertion. Let $T$ be a nonprojective compact complex torus and let $\Sigma=\mathbb{P}^{1} \backslash\{0,1, \infty\}$ carry its complete finite-area hyperbolic metric. The product $T \times \Sigma$, with the product of a flat Kähler metric and the hyperbolic metric, is complete, noncompact, of finite volume, and has bounded curvature. It cannot be quasi-projective: if it were a Zariski open subset of a projective variety, the compact analytic slice $T \times\{p\}$ would be projective by Chow's theorem, contradicting the choice of $T$. Thus finite volume and bounded curvature alone do not detect an algebraic complex structure.

After excluding this elementary nonalgebraic factor obstruction, one can ask which additional geometric hypotheses force a quasi-projective algebraic origin. Siu and Yau proved an affirmative result when the sectional curvature is pinched between two negative constants [SiY]. This places the manifold in a projective compactification framework and is a prototype for the later theory of finite-volume negatively curved Kähler manifolds.

Mok then proved compactification theorems for complete Kähler surfaces of finite volume under curvature assumptions [Mok]. Mok and Zhong extended the method to complete Kähler-Einstein manifolds of finite topological type and bounded curvature [MZ]. These results show that the desired Zariski-open conclusion is available when the geometry at infinity is sufficiently controlled; the unresolved part is whether finite volume plus bounded curvature alone is enough to supply that control.

For the automorphism group, negative Ricci curvature tends to destroy continuous holomorphic automorphisms by Bochnertype arguments; in compact Kähler-Einstein settings this goes back to Matsushima-type structure results [Mat]. In the complete finite-volume setting, Lawson-Yau and Avérous-Kobayashi studied automorphisms and isometries of nonpositively curved manifolds of finite volume [LY, Ko]. Yeung later developed related compactness and bounded-domain techniques, including results under uniform squeezing-type hypotheses [Y]. More recent negatively pinched complete Kähler work relates the background metric, Kähler-Einstein metric, Kobayashi metric, and Bergman metric, giving additional control of automorphisms in strongly negatively curved situations [WY].

Wu-Yau give a precise strong case [WY]. If the holomorphic sectional curvature satisfies $-B \leqslant H \leqslant-A<0$, the manifold has a unique complete negative Kähler-Einstein metric, uniformly equivalent to the original metric, and its curvature tensor has bounded covariant derivatives. Every biholomorphism preserves this canonical metric. In conjunction with finite volume and the finite-volume automorphism results, this yields finiteness in the uniformly pinched setting. The gap in Yau's original formulation is genuine: bounded curvature and strict pointwise negativity do not imply a uniform estimate $H \leqslant-A<0$ at infinity.

Recent quasi-projective work is useful even though it often assumes the compactification rather than proving it. Rochon-Zhang described the precise asymptotic behavior of natural complete finite-volume Kähler metrics on complements of normalcrossing divisors and showed how these asymptotics behave under Ricci flow [RZ]. Guenancia proved that quasi-projective manifolds carrying Kähler metrics with negative holomorphic sectional curvature are of log general type [Gue]. These results suggest a two-step refinement of Yau's question: first prove a quasi-projective compactification from finite volume and bounded curvature, then identify which curvature hypotheses force the boundary divisor and log canonical class to have the expected positivity. Cap-Gover's $c$-projective compactification supplies another useful intermediate notion: suitable complete Kähler ends acquire a smooth CR boundary and a precise asymptotic metric form [CG]. What is not known is how to promote such differential-geometric boundary data to a projective algebraic compactification.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Corrected original direction. Classify the additional hypotheses which eliminate the nonprojective-torus product counterexamples and force a complete finite-volume bounded-curvature Kähler manifold to be quasi-projective.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Remove finite-topological-type assumptions from the Mok-Zhong compactification theorem [MZ].
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Prove finite generation of the fundamental group, or finite topological type, from finite volume and bounded Kähler curvature under natural negative-curvature assumptions [SiY], [Mok], [MZ].
\item[4.] Original direction. Determine whether negative holomorphic bisectional curvature and finite volume imply finiteness of the full holomorphic automorphism group.
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Starting from a quasi-projective compactification, characterize the curvature asymptotics at a normal-crossing boundary which are stable under Ricci flow and compatible with finite volume [RZ].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether negative holomorphic sectional or bisectional curvature on the finite-volume manifold forces the eventual compactification to be of log general type [Gue].
\item[7.] Reference-driven formulation. Characterize when a $c$-projective compactification with CR boundary is induced by a normal-crossing algebraic compactification [CG].
\item[8.] AI-generated formulation. Find an intrinsic curvature-and-volume criterion at the cusps which is equivalent to the existence of a normal-crossing projective compactification and strong enough to imply discreteness, or finiteness, of the automorphism group.
\end{itemize}

[Y] S.-K. Yeung, Geometry of domains with the uniform squeezing property, Adv. Math. 221 (2009), no. 2, 547-569.

\section*{Problem 37}

Let $M$ be a compact Kähler manifold with negative sectional curvature. Prove that if $\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{C}} M>1$, then $M$ is rigid, i.e., there is only one complex structure on $M$.

Status. Solved in complex dimension two; open in complex dimension at least three. The natural conclusion is strong rigidity up to complex conjugation: a homotopy-equivalent compact complex manifold should be biholomorphic or anti-biholomorphic to $M$.

The locally symmetric case is the model. If $M$ is a quotient of the complex ball of complex dimension at least two, Mostow strong rigidity fixes the locally symmetric structure from the fundamental group. Yau's use of Kähler-Einstein metrics connects this Riemannian rigidity with the complex structure of the ball quotient [Y, Mos].

The main analytic method is the harmonic-map method. Sampson and Yau developed the foundational curvature identities for harmonic maps from Kähler manifolds, and Siu proved that harmonic maps are pluriharmonic or holomorphic under suitable strong negativity assumptions on the target curvature [Sam, Si]. This gives strong rigidity theorems for compact Kähler manifolds whose curvature tensor satisfies the stronger complex negativity conditions needed in the Bochner formula.

The difficulty is that negative Riemannian sectional curvature is not the same as Siu's strong negativity condition on the complexified curvature tensor. Thus the known harmonic-map argument does not directly apply to an arbitrary compact Kähler metric with negative sectional curvature. Later work of Jost-Yau and others refined harmonic-map techniques, but the full deformation rigidity of the complex structure in this curvature class remains unsettled [JY].

In complex dimension two, Zheng found the missing curvature bridge [Z]. For a Kähler surface, negative (respectively nonpositive) real sectional curvature is equivalent to negativity (respectively nonpositivity) of the complex curvature operator. Consequently the first Pontrjagin form $C_{1}^{2}-2 C_{2}$ is nonnegative. If its integral vanishes, Zheng's equality analysis forces a bidisc universal cover with a product metric; this is incompatible with strictly negative real sectional curvature because the product has zero mixed sectional planes. Hence $c_{1}^{2}>2 c_{2}$, and Zheng's Proposition 3 uses the unique null direction of the complex curvature operator together with the pluriharmonic part of Siu's Bochner-Kodaira identity to prove strong rigidity. Thus Yau's conjecture is a theorem for Kähler surfaces.

There has also been progress on the algebro-geometric side. Since negative sectional curvature implies negative holomorphic sectional curvature, the canonical bundle is ample by the Wu-Yau theorem [WY]. Chen-Yang proved that compact Kähler manifolds homotopic to negatively curved Riemannian manifolds admit Kähler-Einstein metrics of general type and satisfy hyperbolicity-type conclusions [CY]. These results confirm that the complex structures lie in a very restrictive projective general-type region, but they do not yet give uniqueness of the complex structure on the underlying smooth manifold.

Recent work around nearby curvature cones underlines why Yau's rigidity question remains delicate. Lee-Streets studied compact complex manifolds with negative Chern curvature operator and obtained a dichotomy: either a positive $(1,1)$ current of $d d^{c}$-exact type appears, or the manifold is Kähler with ample canonical bundle; in complex dimension two they obtained a complete classification [LS]. Broder-Gaussier have recently constructed complete Kähler metrics with negative bisectional curvature on new hyperbolic complex manifolds, and also produced projective Kobayashi hyperbolic surfaces with prescribed Chern slope behavior [BG]. These results are not rigidity theorems for compact Kähler manifolds of negative Riemannian sectional curvature, but they show that negative holomorphic and Chern curvature conditions allow a much wider geography than the locally symmetric model. The remaining problem is therefore not only to prove hyperbolicity or ampleness, but to turn the real sectional-curvature hypothesis into a deformation-rigidity statement for the complex structure.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Higher-dimensional original direction. Prove that negative sectional curvature implies the complex curvature negativity needed in Siu's harmonic-map rigidity, perhaps after passing to the Kähler-Einstein metric in the same canonical class.
\item[2.] Original direction. Determine whether a compact smooth manifold can carry two non-biholomorphic Kähler complex structures, both admitting negative sectional curvature metrics.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Establish local deformation rigidity for compact Kähler manifolds with negative sectional curvature, even before global uniqueness of complex structure [Si], [JY].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Relate the Teichmüller space of complex structures on a negatively curved Kähler manifold to harmonic-map energy and Kähler-Einstein metrics [Sam], [Si], [CY].
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Compare negative Riemannian sectional curvature with negative Chern curvature operator, real bisectional curvature, and negative bisectional curvature as possible curvature cones for rigidity [LS], [BG].
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend Zheng's Pontrjagin-form and null-direction argument from complex surfaces to higher-dimensional curvature blocks or Chern-number inequalities [Z].
\item[7.] AI-generated formulation. Find a curvature pinching condition strictly weaker than Siu strong negativity but stronger than negative sectional curvature which still forces complex structure rigidity.
\end{itemize}

References.
[BG] K. Broder and H. Gaussier, Curvature of hyperbolic complex manifolds, arXiv:2606.05452.
[CY] B.-L. Chen and X. Yang, Compact Kähler manifolds homotopic to negatively curved Riemannian manifolds, arXiv:1609.06918.
[JY] J. Jost and S.-T. Yau, Harmonic maps and rigidity theorems for spaces of nonpositive curvature, Comm. Anal. Geom. 7 (1999), no. 4, 681-694.
[LS] M.-C. Lee and J. Streets, Complex manifolds with negative curvature operator, arXiv:1903.12645.
[Mos] G. D. Mostow, Strong rigidity of locally symmetric spaces, Annals of Mathematics Studies, No. 78, Princeton Univ. Press, Princeton, NJ, 1973.
[Sam] J. H. Sampson, Applications of harmonic maps to Kähler geometry, in Complex differential geometry and nonlinear differential equations, 125-134, Contemp. Math., 49, Amer. Math. Soc., Providence, RI, 1986.
[Si] Y. T. Siu, The complex-analyticity of harmonic maps and the strong rigidity of compact Kähler manifolds, Ann. of Math. (2) 112 (1980), no. 1, 73-111.
[WY] D. Wu and S.-T. Yau, Negative holomorphic curvature and positive canonical bundle, Invent. Math. 204 (2016), no. 2, 595-604.

[Z] F. Zheng, First Pontrjagin form, rigidity and strong rigidity of nonpositively curved Kähler surface of general type, Math. Z. 220 (1995), no. 2, 159-169.

\section*{Problem 38}

Given $M$ a simply connected, complete Kähler manifold with sectional curvature less than or equal to -1, prove there exists a nonconstant bounded holomorphic function on $M$.

One would like to even prove that there is a branched immersion of $M$ onto a bounded domain in $\mathbb{C}^{N}$.
Status. Open, including under two-sided negative sectional-curvature pinching in complex dimension at least two. A frequently cited preprint claiming the pinched result in complex dimension at least three was withdrawn after an error was found in its boundary analysis [CS]. The sole upper bound $K \leqslant-1$ and the branched-immersion statement are therefore still further out of reach.

The Riemannian background is the asymptotic Dirichlet problem on Cartan-Hadamard manifolds. Sullivan and Anderson constructed many bounded harmonic functions on negatively curved simply connected manifolds under pinched negative curvature assumptions [Su, An]. These results show that the ideal boundary carries rich potential theory, but harmonic functions are not automatically real parts of holomorphic functions in several complex variables.

The Kähler problem asks for a holomorphic replacement of this potential theory. Schoen and Yau emphasized the role of harmonic functions, $L^{2}$ estimates, and complex analytic methods in the negative-curvature setting [SY]. Cao and Shaw proposed an approach for a simply connected complete Kähler manifold whose sectional curvature satisfies
$$-b^{2} \leqslant K_{M} \leqslant-a^{2}<0,$$
in complex dimension at least three [CS]. Their proposed proof used bounded plurisubharmonic exhaustion functions, the $\bar{\partial}$ equation, CR functions on geodesic spheres, and uniform Hölder estimates as the spheres tend to infinity. The authors withdrew the paper on February 6, 2016, after G. Székelyhidi pointed out an error in Chapter 3. No replacement proof was located in this audit. The failed uniform boundary estimate should be recorded as a concrete gap, not as a theorem.

The stronger desired conclusion would be a supply of enough bounded holomorphic functions to give a branched holomorphic map, or immersion, into a bounded domain of some $\mathbb{C}^{N}$. This is substantially stronger than producing one bounded holomorphic function and would amount to a much more precise complex compactification of the ideal boundary.

There is also a CR-geometric version of the problem at infinity. Cao-Chang constructed a bounded contact form on a simply connected complete Kähler manifold with sectional curvature $\leqslant-1$ and showed that the sphere at infinity carries a bounded contact structure and a bounded Tanaka-Webster pseudo-Hermitian structure [CC]. This does not by itself solve the bounded holomorphic function problem, but it supplies the kind of boundary structure one would need in order to imitate the unit ball: prescribe CR data at infinity, solve an analytic extension problem, and then use the resulting bounded holomorphic functions to separate points or directions. Recent work on hyperbolic complex manifolds and negatively curved Kähler metrics also suggests that the existence of negative curvature alone is not enough; the boundary regularity, CR extension, and separation properties have to be tracked as separate analytic inputs [BG].

Wu-Yau nevertheless obtained a strong invariant-metric theorem under two-sided sectional pinching [WY]. On such a simply connected manifold the Bergman, Kobayashi-Royden, and negative Kähler-Einstein metrics exist and are uniformly equivalent to the background metric. This does not solve Problem 38: the Bergman metric is built from square-integrable canonical forms, whereas the Carathéodory metric and Yau's requested map require bounded scalar holomorphic functions. The missing passage from nondegenerate Bergman geometry to bounded scalar holomorphic separation is one precise formulation of the obstruction.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Prove the existence of nonconstant bounded holomorphic functions under the sole assumption $K \leqslant-1$, without a lower sectional-curvature bound.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Repair or replace the failed uniform CR-boundary estimate in the withdrawn Cao-Shaw approach, and settle first the complex dimension two pinched case [CS].
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Construct enough bounded holomorphic functions to separate points and tangent directions on negatively curved complete simply connected Kähler manifolds [CS].
\item[4.] Original direction. Prove or disprove the existence of a branched holomorphic immersion of such $M$ into a bounded domain in $\mathbb{C}^{N}$.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Develop the Cao-Chang CR boundary at infinity into an extension theorem: identify which CR functions on $S_{\infty}(M)$ extend to bounded holomorphic functions in the interior [CC].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Use the uniformly nondegenerate Bergman geometry of Wu-Yau to produce bounded scalar holomorphic functions, or identify a new obstruction separating the Bergman and Carathéodory theories [WY].
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[7.] AI-generated formulation. Build a boundary Poisson transform for Kähler Hadamard manifolds with $K \leqslant-1$ which sends regular CR boundary data to bounded holomorphic functions and gives explicit separation criteria.
\end{itemize}

References.
\begin{itemize}
\item[] [An] M. T. Anderson, The Dirichlet problem at infinity for manifolds of negative curvature, J. Differential Geom. 18 (1983), no. 4, 701-721.
\item[] [BG] K. Broder and H. Gaussier, Curvature of hyperbolic complex manifolds, arXiv:2606.05452.
\item[] [CC] J. Cao and S.-C. Chang, The modified Calabi-Yau problems for CR-manifolds and applications, arXiv:0801.3431.
\item[] [CS] J. Cao and M.-C. Shaw, Bounded holomorphic functions on negatively curved Kähler manifolds of dimension $\geqslant 3$, arXiv:1512.00368 (withdrawn February 6, 2016, because of an error in Chapter 3).

\item[] [Su] D. Sullivan, The Dirichlet problem at infinity for a negatively curved manifold, J. Differential Geom. 18 (1983), no. 4, 723-732.
\item[] [WY] D. Wu and S.-T. Yau, Invariant metrics on negatively pinched complete Kähler manifolds, J. Amer. Math. Soc. 33 (2020), no. 1, 103-133; arXiv:1711.09475.

\section*{Problem 39}

Let $M$ be a compact Kähler manifold with positive first Chern class. Suppose $M$ admits no holomorphic vector field. Prove that $M$ admits a Kähler-Einstein metric. This was conjectured by Calabi [Ca].

Status. False as stated. The corrected existence theorem is now known: a smooth Fano manifold admits a Kähler-Einstein metric if and only if it is K-polystable.

Calabi's problem asked for canonical metrics in a fixed Kähler class [Ca]. In the Fano case $c_{1}(M)>0$, the absence of holomorphic vector fields is not enough. Classical necessary conditions already came from the automorphism group and Futaki's character: Matsushima's theorem restricts the Lie algebra of holomorphic vector fields, and Futaki constructed an obstruction to Kähler-Einstein metrics [Mat, Fu].

Tian supplied an explicit smooth counterexample [Ti]. For a generic three-plane $P_{a} \subset \Lambda^{2} \mathbb{C}^{7}$, he considered the smooth Fano threefold
$$X_{P_{a}}=\left\{U \in G(4,7): P_{a} \text { projects to zero in } \Lambda^{2}\left(\mathbb{C}^{7} / U\right)\right\} .$$
For generic $a$, this threefold has no nontrivial holomorphic vector field, but a special degeneration has a generalized Futaki invariant which violates Tian's necessary weak K-stability condition. Hence $X_{P_{a}}$ admits no Kähler-Einstein metric. This is the exact status-changing example, rather than only a general appeal to the later stability theory.

The decisive correction was to add an algebro-geometric stability condition. Tian introduced an analytic form of K-stability and proved existence under suitable stability assumptions; his 1997 work also made clear that the naive "no holomorphic vector field" condition is insufficient [Ti]. Donaldson then gave the influential algebraic formulation using test configurations and related scalar curvature to stability of polarized varieties [Do].

The smooth Fano Yau-Tian-Donaldson theorem was proved by Chen-Donaldson-Sun: a smooth Fano manifold admits a Kähler-Einstein metric exactly when it is K-polystable [CDS1, CDS2, CDS3]. Tian also gave a proof of the same correspondence [Ti2]. Berman proved the necessity of K-polystability in a broad possibly singular setting [Ber]. Later variational, continuity method, and Kähler-Ricci flow approaches gave further proofs and extensions [BBJ, DS, CSW].

Thus the original problem should be recorded historically as a false formulation whose corrected version became the Yau-Tian-Donaldson correspondence. The active frontier has moved to singular Fano varieties, log Fano pairs, effective criteria for K-stability, and moduli spaces of K-polystable Fano varieties.

The most active recent developments are algebraic and moduli-theoretic. Valuative criteria of Fujita-Li type, and the related stability threshold or delta invariant, have made K-stability much more computable; Dervan-Legendre's valuative framework for polarized varieties records how far this philosophy extends beyond the Fano case [DL]. Xu's survey gives an algebro-geometric account of the modern Fano theory, including valuations, special test configurations, and the role of singularities [Xu]. On the moduli side, the construction and projectivity of K-moduli spaces have become central: Blum-Halpern-Leistner-Liu-Xu related properness to optimal destabilizing degenerations [BHLX], while Xu-Zhuang proved positivity results for the CM line bundle on K-moduli spaces [XZ]. Recent explicit work, such as Liu-Zhao's K-moduli analysis of a family of Fano threefolds, shows that the corrected problem has become a classification program rather than a single existence theorem [LZ]. DeVleming's 2026 survey is a useful current entry point for the K-stability and K-moduli viewpoint [DeV].

The moduli statement can now be made unconditionally. The finite-generation theorem for valuations computing the stability threshold supplies the optimal destabilizations required in [BHLX]. Consequently the stack of K-semistable Fano varieties of fixed dimension and volume is proper, its good moduli space of K-polystable objects is projective, and the CM line bundle gives the natural polarization [BHLX, XZ, DeV].

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Reference-driven formulation. Develop effective and computable K-stability criteria for explicit Fano manifolds and singular Fano varieties, especially through valuations, beta invariants, and delta invariants [Xu], [DL].
\item[2.] Original direction. Extend the Yau-Tian-Donaldson picture as far as possible from Kähler-Einstein Fano metrics to constant scalar curvature Kähler metrics on general polarized varieties.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Describe the compactified moduli spaces of K-polystable Fano varieties, including boundary singularities, CM-line positivity, and degeneration phenomena [BHLX], [XZ], [DeV].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Relate analytic flows, such as the Kähler-Ricci flow, to optimal destabilizing degenerations in unstable Fano cases [CSW], [BHLX].
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Carry out explicit K-moduli computations for Fano threefolds and higher-dimensional families, following the model of recent concrete K-moduli calculations [LZ].
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Produce an explicit "stability atlas" for low-dimensional Fano manifolds: for each deformation family, record the exact K-stable, strictly semistable, and unstable loci together with the corresponding Gromov-Hausdorff or K-moduli boundary points.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 40}

Let $M$ be a complete Kähler manifold with zero Ricci curvature. Prove that $M$ is a Zariski open set of some compact Kähler manifold. If this is true, we shall have algebraic means to classify these manifolds.

Status. Open in this generality. Many complete Ricci-flat Kähler metrics are known on quasi-projective or otherwise compactifiable complex manifolds, but the converse statement-that an arbitrary complete Ricci-flat Kähler manifold must come from deleting an analytic subset of a compact Kähler manifold-is not known.

The compact case is governed by Yau's solution of the Calabi conjecture: a compact Kähler manifold with vanishing first Chern class carries a unique Ricci-flat Kähler metric in each Kähler class [Y]. The noncompact problem is much less rigid, because completeness allows infinite volume and many possible geometries at infinity.

One strong positive direction starts from algebraic geometry and constructs complete Ricci-flat metrics. Tian and Yau constructed complete Ricci-flat Kähler metrics on complements of suitable divisors in compact Kähler or projective manifolds [TY1, TY2]. Bando-Kobayashi and later van Coevering extended this circle of ideas to further quasi-projective and asymptotically conical settings [BK, vC]. Conlon and Hein developed a systematic existence theory for asymptotically conical Calabi-Yau metrics on quasi-projective varieties, giving sharp analytic control at infinity in many divisor-complement situations [CH]. Santoro studied asymptotic expansions of Tian-Yau type complete Ricci-flat metrics on quasi-projective manifolds [Sa1, Sa2]. These results show that Zariski-open Calabi-Yau manifolds provide a large and natural supply of examples, but they start from an algebraic compactification rather than deriving one from the complete metric.

The reverse direction is harder. Finite-volume negatively curved Kähler manifolds admit compactification results under strong curvature and topology assumptions, as in Problem 36, but Ricci-flat complete manifolds usually have infinite volume and their ends need not resemble finite-volume cusps. In complex dimension two, the theory of gravitational instantons gives detailed classifications in several asymptotic regimes; many examples admit algebraic or elliptic-surface compactifications, but these classifications depend on strong curvature decay and volume-growth hypotheses rather than Ricci-flatness alone [HSVZ]. The $L^{2}$ Hodge theory of gravitational instantons also shows that the asymptotic type controls analytic invariants in a way that should enter any compactification theorem [HHM].

There is also a separate line related to positive bisectional curvature. Chen and Zhu proved compactification-type results for complete noncompact Kähler manifolds under positive bisectional curvature hypotheses [CZ]. Such results are important but do not settle the Ricci-flat case, where the curvature has no definite sign.

Newer constructions further emphasize that the metric-to-algebraic direction is not a formal consequence of Ricci-flatness. Conlon-Rochon constructed warped quasi-asymptotically conical Calabi-Yau metrics on smoothings of products of Calabi-Yau cones and described their asymptotics using compactifications by manifolds with corners and weighted blow-ups [CR]. Their examples show that even the tangent cone at infinity may fail to determine the complete Ricci-flat Kähler metric uniquely. Liang-Zhang constructed complete Calabi-Yau metrics on noncompact abelian-fibered threefolds and supplied compactifications in that setting [LZ]. Liang has also constructed complete Calabi-Yau metrics on noncompact K3-fibered threefolds whose sectional curvature is unbounded at infinity [Liang]. These recent examples are compactifiable or built from algebro-geometric input, but they warn that any converse compactification theorem must specify which asymptotic, curvature, or fibration data are being recovered from the metric.

There is, however, a direct converse theorem under strong asymptotic control. Liu proved that a complete noncompact Ricci-flat Kähler manifold with maximal volume growth and quadratic curvature decay is a crepant resolution of a normal affine algebraic variety; that affine variety degenerates in two steps to the unique metric tangent cone at infinity [Liu]. The proof constructs and finitely generates polynomial-growth holomorphic functions using three-circle estimates, plurisubharmonic approximations to the squared distance, and multiplicity bounds. This is the closest theorem in the present audit to the direction asked by Yau, but both maximal volume growth and quadratic decay are essential hypotheses in the argument.

Liu's later tangent-cone rigidity shows that, under Euclidean volume growth and quadratic curvature or scalar-curvature control, the Ricci-flat Kähler condition can force uniqueness of the tangent cone from much weaker initial information [Liu2]. This helps remove one input in some algebraization arguments, but it still does not treat collapsed ends or arbitrary complete Ricci-flat metrics.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Find natural metric hypotheses at infinity, weaker than known asymptotic models, which imply that a complete Ricci-flat Kähler manifold is Zariski open in a compact Kähler manifold.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify complete Ricci-flat Kähler surfaces under minimal curvature-decay and volumegrowth assumptions, and determine exactly which ones admit algebraic compactifications [HSVZ, HHM].
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether bounded curvature, Euclidean or polynomial volume growth, and finite topological type are sufficient for compactifiability in the Ricci-flat Kähler setting [CH, CR, Liu].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Develop an analytic compactification theory using harmonic functions, holomorphic volume forms, fibration structures, and tangent cones at infinity [CR, LZ, Liang].
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend Liu's finite generation and crepant-resolution theorem from maximal volume growth to collapsed ALG, ALH, and fibered ends [Liu, Liu2, LZ, Liang].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Define a "Calabi-Yau end of algebraic type" purely from metric data and prove that it is equivalent to being a punctured neighborhood of a divisor in a compact Kähler manifold.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 41}

Classify all compact, two-dimensional Kähler surfaces with zero scalar curvature. (See [Y].)
Status. Partially resolved. The possible birational types are known: a compact scalar-flat Kähler surface is either Ricci-flat, hence of Kodaira dimension zero, or is rational or ruled, hence of Kodaira dimension $-\infty$ [KLP, LS]. What remains open is the exact classification of the rational and ruled surfaces, their blow-up configurations, and the Kähler classes which carry scalar-flat metrics.

For a compact Kähler surface ( $X, \omega$ ), scalar-flat Kähler means that the scalar curvature is identically zero. The integrated condition is
$$\int_{X} s_{\omega} \omega^{2}=8 \pi c_{1}(X) \cdot[\omega]=0$$
so the Kähler class must lie on the wall $c_{1}(X) \cdot[\omega]=0$. The Chern-Weil identities sharpen this wall condition. They imply $c_{1}^{2}(X) \leqslant 0$, with equality precisely in the Ricci-flat case. Combining this with the Enriques-Kodaira classification, Kim-LeBrun- Pontecorvo and LeBrun-Singer show that the non-Ricci-flat case must be rational or ruled [KLP, LS]. In particular, surfaces of Kodaira dimension one or two do not admit scalar-flat Kähler metrics. When $c_{1}(X)=0$ in real cohomology, Yau's theorem supplies the Ricci-flat branch: complex tori, K3 surfaces, Enriques surfaces, and bielliptic surfaces [Y2].

The scalar-flat condition is nevertheless broader than Ricci-flatness. In real dimension four it is equivalent to anti-self-duality for the complex orientation. The deformation theory of LeBrun-Singer turns an anti-self-dual conformal construction into scalar-flat Kähler metrics after verifying a cohomological obstruction and then solving the nonlinear deformation problem [LS]. Kim-LeBrun- Pontecorvo prove that every ruled surface becomes scalar-flat after sufficiently many blow-ups unless the metric lies in the Ricci-flat branch [KLP]. Rollin-Singer use parabolic stability, orbifold metrics, and gluing to construct further examples; in particular, $\mathbb{C P}^{2}$ blown up at ten suitable points admits a scalar-flat Kähler metric, and ten is forced by $c_{1}^{2} \leqslant 0[\mathrm{RS}]$.

For a fixed polarized surface, scalar-flat Kähler existence is also a special case of the cscK problem. Arezzo-Pacard show that a cscK metric, including a scalar-flat one, persists under sufficiently small blow-ups when the relevant automorphism obstruction vanishes [AP]. Chen-Cheng identify cscK existence with properness of the Mabuchi energy in the analytic framework [CC1, CC2], while Stoppa shows that cscK existence with discrete automorphism group implies K-stability [St]. These results explain why the remaining classification depends on the polarization and the position of the exceptional curves, not only on the differentiable surface.

Brown proves that Kähler blow-ups admit metrics whose scalar curvature is uniformly close to that of the original metric; as an application, a compact complex surface admits a positive-scalar- curvature Kähler metric exactly when it has Kodaira dimension $-\infty$ [Brown]. This settles the adjacent positive chamber, but does not decide which points on the zero-average wall are represented by scalar-flat metrics. The remaining problem is thus a class-level existence and moduli problem inside the already-known rational/ruled birational types.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Within the known rational/ruled branch, determine exactly which complex surfaces admit scalar-flat Kähler metrics [KLP, LS, RS].
\item[2.] Original direction. Determine, for each complex surface, exactly which Kähler classes contain scalar-flat Kähler metrics.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Relate scalar-flat Kähler existence on surfaces to explicit K-stability or uniform K-stability conditions for the corresponding polarized surface [CC1, CC2, St].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify scalar-flat Kähler metrics on blow-ups of ruled and rational surfaces, including sharp bounds on the number and position of blow-up points [KLP, LS, AP, RS].
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether parabolic stability gives a necessary as well as sufficient criterion for the ruled-surface constructions of Rollin-Singer [RS].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Build a wall-crossing description for scalar-flat Kähler surfaces as the Kähler class crosses the hyperplane $c_{1}(X) \cdot[\omega]=0$.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 42}

Let $M$ be a compact simply-connected symplectic manifold. Does $M$ admit a Kähler structure? M. Berger says that Serre indicated a counterexample to him in 1955, where $\pi_{1}(M) \neq 0$. See [Bs].

For any symplectic structure on a manifold, one can define an almost-complex structure. Conversely, it may be true that for any almost-complex structure, one can also find an associated symplectic structure. Is it true that the almost-complex structure determines the symplectic structure up to conjugation by a diffeomorphism? One does not know the answer of this last question even for $\mathbb{C P}^{N}$. However, Moser [Mo] has proved that all elements of a one-parameter family of symplectic structures are mutually conjugate by diffeomorphisms.

Status. False in each universal formulation. There are compact simply connected symplectic manifolds with no Kähler structure; an almost-complex structure need not admit any compatible symplectic form; and neither cohomology nor the induced canonical class determines a symplectic form up to diffeomorphism. The classification on $\mathbb{C P}^{N}$ remains open beyond low-dimensional and special cases.

The early non-Kähler symplectic examples of Kodaira and Thurston were not simply connected [Th]. McDuff gave the first simply connected example: a symplectic blow-up construction whose third Betti number is odd, hence impossible for a compact Kähler manifold by Hodge theory [Mc]. Gompf's symplectic sums realize every finitely presented group as a symplectic fundamental group and give, in particular, simply connected examples not homotopy equivalent to Kähler manifolds [G]. Babenko-Taimanov constructed simply connected nonformal symplectic manifolds in every even dimension at least ten; formality of compact Kähler manifolds gives a separate rational-homotopy obstruction [BT].

The converse from almost-complex to symplectic is false without extra hypotheses. The simplest obstruction is cohomological: a compact symplectic $2 n$-manifold must contain a class $[\omega]$ with $[\omega]^{n} \neq 0$, so an almost-complex manifold such as $\mathbb{S}^{6}$ cannot be symplectic because $H^{2}\left(\mathbb{S}^{6}\right)=0$. In real dimension four the failure is much more refined. Taubes showed that a symplectic 4-manifold has nontrivial Seiberg-Witten invariants in the canonical spin ${ }^{c}$ class [T1, T2], while Witten's framework implies strong finiteness constraints for basic classes [W]. Thus many orientation-compatible almost-complex structures on a fixed smooth 4-manifold cannot be compatible with any symplectic form.

The uniqueness question is also delicate. Moser's theorem says that a smooth path of cohomologous symplectic forms is induced by an isotopy of diffeomorphisms [Mo]. This is a local-in-moduli statement and does not classify disconnected components of the symplectic cone. For $\mathbb{C P}^{2}$, Gromov's theory of pseudoholomorphic curves gives strong uniqueness of symplectic forms up to deformation and diffeomorphism [Gr], with further four-dimensional refinements by McDuff and others [McS]. In higher dimensional $\mathbb{C P}^{N}$ and on general manifolds, the analogous global classification remains open.

Recent examples show how far the uniqueness question is from the Moser-path situation. McMullen-Taubes constructed a closed simply connected four-manifold with symplectic forms whose canonical classes lie in distinct diffeomorphism orbits [MT]. Ning constructed closed manifolds in every even dimension at least six carrying cohomologous symplectic forms with different Gromov widths and first Chern classes [Ning]. These symplectic invariants show that the cohomology class alone cannot classify symplectic forms. For almost-complex-to-symplectic questions, Lejmi proved that the almost complex structure underlying a strict nearly Kähler six-manifold, including the standard almost complex structure on $\mathbb{S}^{6}$, is not compatible with any symplectic form even locally [Lej]. In dimension four, Draghici-Li-Zhang's decomposition of cohomology associated to an almost complex structure gives a useful framework for the question of which classes are represented by $J$-compatible or $J$-tamed symplectic forms [DLZ].

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Classify symplectic structures on $\mathbb{C P}^{N}$ up to diffeomorphism and deformation for $N \geqslant 3$.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine which almost-complex structures in dimension $2 n \geqslant 6$ are homotopic to ones tamed or compatible with a symplectic form [Lej, DLZ].
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Develop higher-dimensional analogues of Taubes' Seiberg-Witten obstruction, possibly using Gromov-Witten or Floer-theoretic invariants [T1, T2, W].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify simply connected symplectic non-Kähler manifolds within fixed homeomorphism or diffeomorphism types [Mc, G].
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Understand when cohomologous symplectic forms are distinguished by capacities, Gromov-Witten invariants, or first Chern class [Ning].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Define a computable "symplectic realizability cone" for almost-complex Chern data and compare it with the full topological cone of almost-complex structures.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 43}

Let $\Omega$ be a bounded, pseudoconvex domain in $\mathbb{C}^{N}$. Cheng and Yau [CY] have established the existence of a canonical Kähler-Einstein metric on $\Omega$. Under general conditions, e.g. $\partial \Omega$ is $C^{2}$, that metric is complete. Is the metric always complete?*

Status. Solved. Mok and Yau proved the completion of this problem: a bounded domain admits a complete Kähler-Einstein metric of negative Ricci curvature if and only if it is pseudoconvex [MY].

Cheng and Yau first established the existence and regularity theory for complete Kähler-Einstein metrics on broad classes of noncompact complex manifolds and bounded strongly pseudoconvex domains [CY]. Their work is based on the complex Monge-Ampère equation and Fefferman's approximate solution near the boundary. For smooth strongly pseudoconvex domains, the Kähler-Einstein metric is complete and has precise boundary asymptotics.

The remaining issue in the original question was whether pseudoconvexity alone forces completeness of the canonical Kähler-Einstein metric on a bounded domain. Mok-Yau proved this affirmatively and also obtained the converse characterization of domains of holomorphy by curvature conditions [MY]. Thus the existence-completeness question is settled, but the detailed boundary behavior remains an active analytic theme, especially for weakly pseudoconvex or nonsmooth domains.

Recent work has shifted the problem toward local boundary models, Bergman-kernel localization, and rigidity of nearby canonical metrics. Gontard proved local boundary regularity and curvature asymptotics for the complete Kähler-Einstein metric near strictly pseudoconvex boundary points and obtained estimates for tube domains near weakly pseudoconvex points [Go1, Go2]. Hsiao-Huang-Li recently established Bergman-kernel localization near finite-type boundary points for unbounded pseudoconvex domains, a tool designed for problems where the local scaling limit is not itself bounded [HHL]. Sha studied Kähler-Ricci solitons on bounded pseudoconvex domains and proved, under suitable hypotheses, that such solitons reduce to Kähler-Einstein metrics [Sha1]. These results do not change the solved status of the completeness question, but they make the residual problem more precise: understand how the complete Kähler-Einstein metric degenerates, localizes, and compares with the Bergman geometry at weak or singular boundary strata.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Describe sharp boundary asymptotics of the Cheng-Yau Kähler-Einstein metric on weakly pseudoconvex finite-type domains.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine the optimal boundary regularity under which the Kähler-Einstein potential has a full polyhomogeneous expansion near strictly pseudoconvex or finite-type points [CY, Go1, HHL].
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Relate the curvature asymptotics of the complete Kähler-Einstein metric to CR invariants of the boundary and to the model geometry of weakly pseudoconvex points [Go1, Go2].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Study stability of the complete Kähler-Einstein metric under domain degeneration, especially for families of pseudoconvex domains with singular limits [MY, HHL].
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend rigidity results for Kähler-Ricci solitons and cscK perturbations on bounded pseudoconvex domains to weaker boundary regularity [Sha1, Sha2].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Develop a boundary stratification theory for the Cheng-Yau metric on arbitrary bounded pseudoconvex domains, analogous to metric tangent cones in Riemannian convergence theory.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 44}

Describe the Kähler-Einstein metric constructed by Cheng-Yau [CY] on the Teichmüller space. What is its relation to the Bergman metric? In general, if a domain is not biholomorphic to a product domain and covers a compact Kähler manifold, is the Kähler-Einstein metric equal to the Bergman metric?

Status. Partially resolved. The Teichmüller/moduli-space part has been solved at the level of metric equivalence and bounded geometry by Liu-Sun-Yau. The broader question asking for exact equality on a non-product domain covering a compact Kähler manifold remains open in the stated generality. Exact equality is much more rigid than the established quasi-isometry results.

Cheng and Yau constructed complete Kähler-Einstein metrics on bounded pseudoconvex domains and on Teichmüller space in this framework [CY]. Teichmüller space also carries several canonical metrics: the Teichmüller/Kobayashi metric, the Carathéodory metric, the Bergman metric, the Weil-Petersson metric, McMullen's metric, and metrics derived from Ricci curvature.

Liu-Sun-Yau introduced the Ricci and perturbed Ricci metrics and used them to compare these canonical metrics on Teichmüller space and on the moduli space of Riemann surfaces [LSY1, LSY2]. They proved that the Bergman and Carathéodory metrics are uniformly equivalent to the Kobayashi metric, and hence to the Kähler-Einstein, McMullen, Ricci, and perturbed Ricci metrics [LSY1, LSY2]. Here "equivalent" is defined to mean quasi-isometric: there is a uniform $C>1$ with $C^{-1} g_{1} \leqslant g_{2} \leqslant C g_{1}$. Their proof uses Bers embeddings between fixed Euclidean balls, monotonicity of intrinsic metrics, and upper and lower estimates for Bergman peak sections. They also obtained bounded geometry and detailed boundary control for the Kähler-Einstein metric [LSY2]. Yeung independently proved quasi-isometry results in this setting [Ye].

Equivalence should be distinguished from equality. Equality between the Bergman metric and a complete Kähler-Einstein metric is characteristic of highly symmetric situations, such as bounded symmetric domains with their invariant metrics after normalization. The available comparison theorems do not prove or disprove equality on Teichmüller space or on every universal cover in Yau's question. The useful modern question is therefore to identify conditions under which the Bergman, Kobayashi, and Kähler-Einstein metrics are equivalent, have comparable curvature, or share the same boundary asymptotics.

Recent Bergman-Einstein rigidity results sharpen the equality side of the problem. Huang-Xiao proved Cheng's conjecture for smoothly bounded strongly pseudoconvex domains: if the Bergman metric is Kähler-Einstein, then the domain is biholomorphic to the ball [HX]. Savale-Xiao proved the corresponding ball characterization for two-dimensional finite-type pseudoconvex domains [SX]. Hsiao-Huang-Li proved that every bounded real-analytic pseudoconvex domain with Einstein Bergman metric is biholomorphic to the ball, using localization near finite-type boundary points and CR extension across the boundary [HHL]. Sha's rigidity theorem for complete Kähler-Einstein metrics under cscK perturbations gives another nearby test: on a bounded strictly pseudoconvex domain, a Bergman metric with constant scalar curvature is forced to be Kähler-Einstein, and hence falls into the same ball-rigidity circle after the Huang-Xiao theorem [Sha2]. These results support the guiding principle that equality with the Bergman metric is a uniformization phenomenon, whereas quasi-isometry is the flexible Teichmüller/moduli-space statement.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Characterize bounded domains for which the Bergman metric is Kähler-Einstein.
\item[2.] Original direction. Determine whether the normalized Kähler-Einstein and Bergman metrics can be exactly equal on a non-product universal cover of a compact Kähler manifold, and classify all equality cases.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Refine the boundary asymptotics of the Kähler-Einstein and Bergman metrics near the Deligne-Mumford boundary of moduli space [LSY1, LSY2, Chan].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend the Liu-Sun-Yau metric comparison framework to moduli spaces of higherdimensional varieties [LSY1, LSY2].
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend Bergman-Einstein ball-rigidity from strongly pseudoconvex and real-analytic finite-type domains to broader smooth weakly pseudoconvex classes [HX, SX, HHL].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine when cscK or Kähler-Ricci soliton assumptions on the Bergman metric already imply the Kähler-Einstein condition [Sha1, Sha2].
\item[7.] AI-generated formulation. Find a curvature or automorphism-group criterion which detects when metric equivalence between the Bergman and Kähler-Einstein metrics improves to equality after scaling.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 45}

Let $M$ be a compact Kähler-Einstein manifold of complex dimension $N$, with negative scalar curvature. Yau [Y] proved then that
$$(-1)^{N} \frac{2(N+1)}{N} C_{1}^{N-2} C_{2} \geqslant(-1)^{N} C_{1}^{N} .$$
Are there any other inequalities of this sort among the Chern numbers of $M$ ? When $N=4$, Bourguignon asked whether or not $C_{4}(M)$ is positive.

Status. Partially resolved. The fundamental Miyaoka-Yau inequality is proved, including its equality case in the smooth ample-canonical situation. The broader problem of finding all universal Chern-number inequalities for negatively curved Kähler-Einstein manifolds remains open. Bourguignon's positivity question for $C_{4}$ in complex dimension four should therefore still be treated as an open problem unless additional hypotheses are imposed.

Historically, the two-dimensional case is the Bogomolov-Miyaoka-Yau inequality. Yau's proof via the Kähler-Einstein metric gave
$$\left(2(N+1) c_{2}(M)-N c_{1}(M)^{2}\right)\left(-c_{1}(M)\right)^{N-2} \geqslant 0$$
for compact Kähler-Einstein manifolds with negative scalar curvature, and equality characterizes quotients of the unit ball. Miyaoka's algebraic proof in the surface case put the inequality into the birational classification of surfaces of general type. In higher dimension, the same inequality is naturally interpreted through the tangent bundle as a Hermite-Einstein bundle and through Bogomolov-Gieseker-Lübke type inequalities.

Greb-Kebekus-Peternell-Taji extended the Miyaoka-Yau inequality and the ball-quotient equality case to klt minimal models of general type, replacing ordinary Chern classes by orbifold Chern classes [GKPT]. Their mechanism is algebrogeometric: stability of a natural Higgs sheaf, a Bogomolov-Gieseker inequality after restriction to surfaces, and a quasi-étale uniformizing cover in the equality case. This is important for moduli and degeneration questions, but it does not by itself give a complete list of all numerical inequalities among the Chern numbers of smooth compact Kähler-Einstein manifolds with $c_{1}<0$.

Several modern results give related inequalities under additional positivity assumptions. Li-Zheng obtain reverse Miyaoka-Yau-type and Schur-polynomial inequalities for very ample polarizations and nef vector bundles [LZ]. Ping Li obtains $\lfloor N / 2\rfloor+1$ sharp Chern-number inequalities for Kähler-hyperbolic manifolds by combining $L^{2}$ Hodge theory, Atiyah's $L^{2}$ index theorem, and the -1 expansion of the Hirzebruch $\chi_{y}$-genus [Li]. These hypotheses are stronger than the existence of a negative Kähler-Einstein metric. The results suggest that the right answer may not be a short finite list of Chern-number inequalities depending only on $N$. The original problem is therefore best organized as two questions: first, whether there are genuinely new universal inequalities beyond the Miyaoka-Yau inequality; second, whether more refined positivity hypotheses force stronger Chern number positivity, including positivity of $C_{4}$ in complex dimension four.

Recent progress has clarified the curvature meaning of the original Miyaoka-Yau expression. Du wrote the quantity
$$-c_{1}^{N}(M)+\frac{2(N+1)}{N} c_{2}(M) c_{1}(M)^{N-2}$$
as an integral involving the holomorphic sectional curvature and its pointwise average, and derived reverse Yau-type inequalities and improved pinching theorems for compact Kähler-Einstein manifolds [D]. Since $c_{4}(M)[M]=\chi(M)$, Bourguignon's question is an Euler-sign problem. Negative Ricci curvature alone does not supply the sectional- or bisectional-curvature sign used in known Hopf-type results. Du's formula does not settle this question, but it gives a sharper analytic framework for testing which Chern number inequalities are really consequences of curvature pinching, which are consequences of the Hermite-Einstein tangent bundle, and which require new global input.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Determine whether every universal linear Chern-number inequality for compact Kähler-Einstein manifolds with $c_{1}<0$ is generated by the Miyaoka-Yau inequality, the obvious sign of $K_{M}^{N}$, and standard topological constraints.
\item[2.] Original direction. Decide Bourguignon's question: if $M$ is a compact Kähler-Einstein fourfold with negative scalar curvature, must $c_{4}(M)=\chi(M)$ be positive?
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify the equality and near-equality cases in the Miyaoka-Yau inequality beyond the smooth ball quotient case, especially in degenerating families of canonically polarized manifolds [GKPT].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Find sharp Chern-number inequalities under additional curvature assumptions, such as pinched negative holomorphic sectional curvature or negative bisectional curvature [D, Li].
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether reverse Yau-type inequalities can be combined with Bogomolov-Gieseker-Lübke inequalities to produce new independent constraints in dimensions $N \geqslant 4$ [B, Lu, D].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Formulate a cone of "Kähler-Einstein Chern numbers" in each complex dimension $N$ and decide whether its extremal rays are represented by locally symmetric examples, complete intersections, or degenerations of canonical models.
\end{itemize}

References.

\item[] [LZ] P. Li and F. Zheng, Chern class inequalities on polarized manifolds and nef vector bundles, arXiv:2004.09224.
\item[] [Li] P. Li, Kähler hyperbolic manifolds and Chern number inequalities, Math. Z. 295 (2020), no. 1-2, 657-684; arXiv:1805.07877.

\item[] [M] Y. Miyaoka, On the Chern numbers of surfaces of general type, Invent. Math. 42 (1977), 225-237.

\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 46}
(Calabi). Let $u$ be a real-valued function defined on $\mathbb{C}^{N}$ so that $\operatorname{det} \frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial z_{i} \partial \bar{z}_{j}}=1$ and $\sum_{i, j} \frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial z_{i} \partial \bar{z}_{j}} d z^{i} d \bar{z}^{j}$ defines a Kähler metric. Prove that this metric is flat (see [Ca2]).

The difficulty lies in the fact that the automorphism group of $\mathbb{C}^{N}$ is very large.

Status. False in the natural complete-metric formulation. LeBrun pointed out that the Taub-NUT metric gives a complete non-flat Ricci-flat Kähler metric on $\mathbb{C}^{2}$. The surviving problem is the classification of complete Ricci-flat Kähler metrics on $\mathbb{C}^{N}$, especially under specified asymptotic, symmetry, or compactification hypotheses.

Calabi's problem is a complex Monge-Ampère analogue of Bernstein and Liouville-type rigidity questions. If the metric is assumed to be the standard Euclidean metric up to sufficiently strong global control, one expects rigidity; without such asymptotic assumptions, LeBrun's observation shows that completeness alone is not enough. The Taub-NUT example is hyperkähler, complete, Ricci-flat, and defined on the smooth manifold $\mathbb{C}^{2}$, but it is not flat.

The post-LeBrun viewpoint is constructive as well as classificatory. There are now several families of complete non-flat Calabi-Yau metrics on affine space or on spaces very close to affine space: Taub-NUT type models in complex dimension two, higher-dimensional extensions in complex dimension three and above, and explicit Hamiltonian-two-form constructions. These examples make clear that the correct rigidity statement must include geometric conditions at infinity.

One natural way to organize the classification is by asymptotic geometry: ALE, ALF, ALG, ALH, asymptotically conical, or more collapsed Taub-NUT type behavior. Another is by algebraic compactification: determine whether the metric is produced from a compactification of $\mathbb{C}^{N}$ by a divisor, and how the divisor and its normal geometry determine the Ricci-flat metric.

Recent constructions show that the classification problem has at least two genuinely different branches. Székelyhidi and Conlon-Rochon constructed complete Calabi-Yau metrics on $\mathbb{C}^{n}, n \geqslant 3$, with maximal volume growth and singular tangent cones at infinity [Sz, CR]. Firester extended this smoothing picture for Calabi-Yau complete intersections, producing complete metrics whose tangent cone at infinity is $\mathbb{C} \times V_{0}$ for a Calabi-Yau cone $V_{0}$ [Fi]. On the collapsed side, Ma recently constructed complete non-flat Taub-NUT-type Calabi-Yau metrics on $\mathbb{C}^{N+1}$ for every $N \geqslant 3$, with tangent cone at infinity $\mathbb{R}^{N+2}$, nonmaximal volume growth, bounded curvature, and torus-fibration asymptotics controlled by a discriminant locus [Ma]. Thus the failure of Calabi's flatness prediction is not isolated: there are both maximal-growth and non-maximal-growth families, and any remaining rigidity theorem must explicitly exclude one or both asymptotic mechanisms.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Classify complete Ricci-flat Kähler metrics on $\mathbb{C}^{N}$ under natural volume growth and curvature decay assumptions.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine which asymptotic models at infinity can occur for complete Calabi-Yau metrics on $\mathbb{C}^{N}$, including singular Calabi-Yau cones and collapsed Taub-NUT-type fibrations [Sz, CR, Li, Ma].
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Decide whether every complete Ricci-flat Kähler metric on $\mathbb{C}^{N}$ with Euclidean volume growth and sufficiently fast curvature decay must be flat, or whether the known maximal-growth examples exhaust the non-flat possibilities [CH, CR, Sz, Fi].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Relate complete Ricci-flat Kähler metrics on affine space to algebraic compactifications by divisors, in the spirit of the Tian-Yau and Conlon-Hein theories.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify complete Calabi-Yau metrics on $\mathbb{C}^{N}$ with non-maximal volume growth and identify which torus-fibration discriminant loci can occur [AC, Li, Ma].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Build a moduli theory for complete Ricci-flat Kähler metrics on $\mathbb{C}^{N}$ with fixed asymptotic fibration type, and identify the Taub-NUT type metrics as boundary or extremal points of that moduli space.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 47}

Let $M$ be a compact Kähler manifold with positive holomorphic sectional curvature or positive Ricci curvature. Prove that $M$ is rationally connected, i.e. any two points of $M$ can be joined by a chain of rational curves.

Status. Solved. The positive Ricci curvature case follows from the Fano case: Campana and Kollár-Miyaoka-Mori proved that smooth Fano manifolds are rationally connected. The positive holomorphic sectional curvature case was solved later by Yang using RC-positivity; in particular a compact Kähler manifold with positive holomorphic sectional curvature is projective and rationally connected.

The historical point is that positive Ricci curvature and positive holomorphic sectional curvature are different conditions. Positive Ricci form gives positivity of $c_{1}(M)$, hence places the manifold in the Fano world. The algebraic geometry of rational curves then applies. This is the Campana and Kollár-Miyaoka-Mori solution.

The holomorphic sectional curvature direction required a different mechanism. Yang proves that positive holomorphic sectional curvature makes every $\Lambda^{p} T_{M}$ RC-positive [Y, Theorem 1.7]. The resulting vanishing theorem excludes holomorphic tensor powers; it also gives projectivity, after which the Campana-Demailly-Peternell criterion implies rational connectedness. Heier-Wong obtained the projective case through total-scalar-curvature and uniruledness methods [HW1, HW2]. Yang's argument removes the projectivity assumption and separates the problem from the Fano route, since positive holomorphic sectional curvature need not imply positive Ricci curvature.

The present frontier is the semipositive and quasi-positive boundary. Recent work of Zhang-Zhang and Matsumura gives structure theorems for compact Kähler manifolds with nonnegative holomorphic sectional curvature: roughly, rationally connected fibers appear over a torus-type base, and quasi-positive or no-truly-flat hypotheses force projectivity and rational connectedness.

There is also a complementary existence problem: which rationally connected or rational manifolds actually carry positive holomorphic sectional curvature? In complex dimension two, Hitchin proved that a compact complex surface with positive holomorphic sectional curvature is rational and constructed examples on Hirzebruch surfaces. Zhang recently proved the converse for rational surfaces: every rational surface admits a Kähler metric with positive holomorphic sectional curvature [SZ]. More generally, Zhang's construction covers projective manifolds obtained from projective toric manifolds by iterated point blow-ups. This turns the solved direction of Yau's problem into a sharper classification question: among rationally connected manifolds, characterize the ones admitting positive holomorphic sectional curvature.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify compact Kähler manifolds with nonnegative holomorphic sectional curvature, including the precise role of flat tangent directions [ZZ, Ma].
\item[2.] Original direction. Determine optimal curvature positivity conditions between positive holomorphic sectional curvature and positive Ricci curvature which still imply rational connectedness.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Characterize rationally connected manifolds, beyond rational surfaces and iterated point blow-ups of toric manifolds, which admit positive holomorphic sectional curvature [SZ].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Study deformation and moduli behavior of compact Kähler manifolds admitting positive holomorphic sectional curvature, especially under blow-ups and degenerations [SZ].
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Decide whether natural weaker notions, such as real bisectional curvature positivity or RC-positivity of tensor bundles, give sharp birational conclusions [Y].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Develop a curvature "MRC-fibration theorem" which reads off the dimension of the rationally connected fiber directly from the null directions of the holomorphic sectional curvature.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 48}

Let $M$ be a compact Kähler manifold with negative sectional curvature. Prove that $M$ is covered by a bounded domain of $\mathbb{C}^{N}$. One might prove a weaker assertion that the universal cover of $M$ has an abundance of bounded holomorphic functions. (See the example of Mostow-Siu [MS].)

Status. Open both for the bounded-domain statement and, at this level of generality, for the weaker bounded-holomorphicfunction direction. Cao and Shaw's arXiv preprint claimed that a simply connected complete Kähler manifold of complex dimension at least three with pinched negative sectional curvature admits a nonconstant bounded holomorphic function, but the authors withdrew the paper after an error was found in Chapter 3 [CS]. Its first version is therefore retained here only as a failed-proof audit and cannot be cited as progress on the problem.

The problem sits between Riemannian negative curvature and several complex-analytic uniformization questions. By Cartan-Hadamard the universal cover is diffeomorphic to Euclidean space, and by classical work of Wu and Greene-Wu one expects strong Stein and convexity properties under nonpositive or negative curvature. However, producing enough bounded holomorphic functions to embed the universal cover as a bounded domain is much harder than proving the existence of some holomorphic functions.

Mostow and Siu constructed compact Kähler surfaces of negative curvature which are not ball quotients. Their examples show that one cannot expect the universal cover to be the unit ball; a successful uniformization theorem must allow more general bounded domains. Jost-Yau and Bland obtained partial results toward bounded holomorphic functions under additional analytic hypotheses. The withdrawn Cao-Shaw argument proposed to combine bounded plurisubharmonic exhaustions, CR analysis on geodesic spheres, and estimates for the $\bar{\partial}$-equation. Because its crucial Chapter 3 step is invalid, this remains a proposed proof architecture rather than an established theorem.

There is also important metric progress. Wu and Yau proved that on complete Kähler manifolds with negatively pinched holomorphic curvature there is a complete Kähler-Einstein metric, and in the simply connected case with negatively pinched sectional curvature the Kähler-Einstein, Kobayashi-Royden, Bergman, and background metrics are uniformly equivalent. This gives strong invariant-metric evidence for the bounded-domain picture, but does not by itself produce a biholomorphic realization of the universal cover as a bounded domain in $\mathbb{C}^{N}$.

Recent function-theoretic work gives additional tests for any bounded-domain strategy. Bracci-Gaussier-Zimmer studied domains admitting negatively pinched Kähler metrics and proved strong restrictions on convex boundaries: finite type follows from smooth boundary hypotheses, and sufficiently tight negative holomorphic sectional pinching characterizes strong pseudoconvexity among convex domains [BGZ]. Huang-Li proved that for a Stein manifold with well-defined Bergman metric of nonpositive constant holomorphic sectional curvature, the Bergman space separates points; in the negative constant case this leads to a ball-type uniformization outside a possible pluripolar set [HL]. These results are not a solution of Yau's boundeddomain problem, because the universal cover is not initially given as a Euclidean domain with controlled boundary. They do, however, indicate what additional boundary, Bergman-space, or convexity conclusions would be needed after one constructs sufficiently many bounded holomorphic functions.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Prove or disprove that the universal cover of every compact Kähler manifold with negative sectional curvature is biholomorphic to a bounded domain in $\mathbb{C}^{N}$.
\item[2.] Original direction. Settle the bounded-holomorphic-function problem in every complex dimension, including the claimed dimension-at-least-three case whose published preprint was withdrawn.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether the Bergman metric equivalence results imply finite-dimensional bounded-domain embeddings under additional regularity or boundary assumptions [WY, HL].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify the possible bounded domains which can occur as universal covers of compact negatively curved Kähler manifolds, and test whether convexity, finite type, or strong pseudoconvexity can be forced [MS, BGZ].
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Repair or replace the failed Chapter 3 argument of Cao-Shaw, then use $L^{2} \bar{\partial}$ methods and Bergman-space separation to construct enough bounded functions for a proper holomorphic embedding [CS, HL].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Find a curvature-pinning or rank condition which forces the universal cover to be a bounded convex domain, and compare it with the Mostow-Siu examples.
\end{itemize}
