\section*{Problem 49}

Let $M_{t}$ be a holomorphic family of Kähler manifolds. Let $d s_{t}^{2}$ be the canonical Kähler-Einstein metric on $M_{t}$. What is the behavior of $d s_{t}^{2}$ as the family $M_{t}$ degenerates?

Status. Partially resolved. Major special regimes have a well-developed theory, but the fully general degeneration theory of canonical Kähler-Einstein metrics is still open. The best developed cases are Fano Kähler-Einstein metrics and their Gromov-Hausdorff limits, Ricci-flat Calabi-Yau degenerations, and negatively curved canonically polarized degenerations.

In the Fano case, the modern picture is quite complete at the level of metric and algebraic compactness. Donaldson-Sun proved that non-collapsed Gromov-Hausdorff limits of Kähler manifolds with two-sided Ricci control are normal algebraic varieties. For Kähler-Einstein Fano manifolds this identifies the limit as a $\mathbb{Q}$-Fano variety carrying a weak Kähler-Einstein metric. Chen-Donaldson-Sun developed the conic continuity method and treated limits with cone angles; this became one of the analytic inputs in the Yau-Tian-Donaldson theorem. The algebraic meaning of the limit is now expressed through K-stability and K-polystability.

For Ricci-flat compact Kähler manifolds, the behavior depends on whether the degeneration is non-collapsing or collapsing. If the Kähler class approaches the boundary of the Kähler cone with positive limiting volume, Tosatti proved smooth convergence away from the subvarieties whose volume tends to zero, and Rong-Zhang identified the metric completion with the global Gromov-Hausdorff limit. If the limiting class has zero volume, the metrics collapse. For holomorphic fibrations, Tosatti proved adiabatic convergence to a metric on the base whose Ricci curvature is controlled by the Weil-Petersson form. Gross-Tosatti-Zhang proved refined collapse results for abelian-fibered Calabi-Yau manifolds, extending the Gross-Wilson analysis of large complex structure limits of K3 surfaces.

If the complex structure also varies, the singularities of the central fiber become decisive. When the limit has canonical singularities, one expects and in many settings obtains non-collapsing convergence to a singular Ricci-flat metric on the limit variety, smooth away from the singular set. At large complex structure limits the SYZ picture predicts collapse, after diameter normalization, to a real Monge-Ampère space of half real dimension, with singular set of real codimension at least two. This picture is proved in detail for K3 surfaces and in important fibration cases, but is far from complete in higher dimensions.

For canonically polarized manifolds and varieties of general type, Aubin-Yau existence gives the smooth negative Ricci Kähler-Einstein metrics. In degenerating families, Song-Tian and Eyssidieux-Guedj-Zeriahi developed the weak Kähler-Einstein metric theory on singular canonical models. The remaining issues include quantitative asymptotics near the central fiber, metric completion, diameter and curvature control, and compatibility with the algebraic compactification of moduli.

Recent work has made the collapsed Calabi-Yau side much more topological and metric-measure theoretic. Hein-Tosatti proved smooth asymptotic expansions, to all orders away from the singular fibers, for collapsing Calabi-Yau metrics in semiample fibration classes [HT]. Székelyhidi proved that for collapsing Calabi-Yau fibrations the Gromov-Hausdorff limit is homeomorphic to the base and that the discriminant locus has Hausdorff codimension at least two, resolving conjectures of Tosatti in this setting [Sz]. This work also places singular Kähler metrics and canonical metrics naturally in the language of RCD spaces [Sz2]. In complex dimension three, Fu-Guo-Song proved RCD and homeomorphism results for singular Kähler spaces with Ricci lower bounds and bounded Nash entropy [FGS]. For conifold transitions, Benabida obtained polyhomogeneous asymptotics for resolutions and smoothings of Calabi-Yau conifolds [Ben]. These developments make the modern target sharper: one wants not only convergence, but a topological identification of the metric completion, quantitative codimension bounds for the discriminant, and asymptotic expansions near the forming singularities.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Give a unified metric compactification theorem for degenerating families of canonical Kähler-Einstein metrics in the Fano, Calabi-Yau, and general type cases.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Describe the precise Gromov-Hausdorff limits of Ricci-flat Calabi-Yau metrics at higherdimensional large complex structure limits, including the affine Monge-Ampère structure and singular set [GW, GTZ2, SZh].
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Establish sharp curvature, diameter, and local non-collapsing estimates near singular fibers in degenerating families [HT, Ben].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Relate metric tangent cones and singularities of Kähler-Einstein limits to the birational type and stability of the central fiber [DS, CDS1, CDS2, EGZ].
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine when singular Kähler-Einstein metric completions are RCD spaces homeomorphic to the underlying algebraic variety [Sz2, FGS].
\end{itemize}
6. AI-generated formulation. Construct a single "degeneration dictionary" which takes an algebraic degeneration and predicts the metric limit, collapsed dimension, singular set, and canonical measure.

\section*{III. Submanifolds}

\section*{Problem 50}

Prove that a compact surface in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ is rigid, i.e. one cannot find a continuous family of surfaces in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ which are isometric to each other and are not obtained from each other by a rigid motion.

This is a very long-standing problem. If we consider polyhedral surfaces, there is a counterexample due to R. Connelly [Co]. Sullivan asked if the (signed) volume enclosed by the surfaces is invariant under isometric deformation.

For the smooth case, Cohn-Vossen proved the rigidity for convex surfaces. In an attempt to generalize Cohn-Vossen's result, L. Nirenberg [Ni] studied the surfaces with $\int K^{+}=4 \pi$. He generalized Cohn-Vossen's result assuming the nonexistence of more than one closed asymptotic line. The real analytic case was first established by A. D. Alexandrov [A1].

Status. Open. The smooth rigidity problem remains open in this generality. Important classical cases are known: convex closed surfaces are rigid by Cohn-Vossen and Pogorelov-type global rigidity theory, and real analytic closed surfaces satisfy rigidity theorems going back to Alexandrov. The polyhedral analogue is false: Connelly constructed flexible polyhedral spheres. Sullivan's volume-invariance question for flexible polyhedra is solved by the Bellows theorem of Sabitov and Connelly-Sabitov-Walz.

The historical development separates three levels of regularity. For convex smooth or convex polyhedral surfaces, the Cauchy-Alexandrov and Cohn-Vossen traditions give strong rigidity. For real analytic closed surfaces, analytic continuation and the geometry of asymptotic lines give rigidity in settings where the smooth problem is still not understood. Nirenberg's work on surfaces with $\int K^{+}=4 \pi$ is one attempt to push convex-surface methods into the nonconvex smooth setting by controlling asymptotic-line behavior.

Polyhedral geometry shows that rigidity is delicate rather than purely topological. Bricard found self-intersecting flexible octahedra; Connelly later constructed flexible polyhedral spheres, including embedded examples. These examples disprove the naive polyhedral rigidity conjecture, but they led to a new invariant problem. The Bellows theorem says that the volume enclosed by an orientable flexible polyhedron is constant during a flex. More recently, the strong Bellows theorem proves invariance of the Dehn invariant, giving scissors-congruence consequences for flexible polyhedra.

Recent discrete-differential examples sharpen the boundary between rigid and flexible behavior. Izmestiev-Rasoulzadeh-Tervooren studied T-hedra, flexible quadrilateral polyhedral surfaces including Miura folds and discrete surfaces of revolution, and also developed their smooth analogues, T-surfaces, with explicit isometric deformation formulas [IRT]. These examples are not counterexamples to the closed smooth rigidity problem as stated, but they are useful test cases: they show how special curvature-line or grid-like structures can carry actual isometric deformations, and hence what sort of exceptional geometry a smooth rigidity theorem must exclude.

The smooth problem is therefore still a central open question in classical surface theory. One should distinguish global rigidity from infinitesimal rigidity, embedded from immersed surfaces, and smooth from $C^{1}$ regularity. Nash-Kuiper type $C^{1}$ flexibility shows that low regularity behaves very differently, so the intended problem is a $C^{2}$, smooth, or analytic rigidity question. In Lorentzian geometry, related rigidity questions for spacelike two-surfaces in Minkowski or static reference spaces enter the Wang-Yau quasi-local mass theory: vanishing or minimizing of the mass is tied to uniqueness and rigidity of isometric embeddings.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Prove or disprove global rigidity for smooth closed embedded surfaces in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ without convexity assumptions.
\item[2.] Original direction. Classify smooth closed surfaces in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ which admit nontrivial infinitesimal bendings, and determine when such bendings integrate to actual isometric deformations.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Develop a smooth analogue of the Bellows theorem: identify which enclosed-volume, total-mean-curvature, or Dehn-type quantities remain invariant under smooth isometric deformations [CSW, GI].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Study rigidity of spacelike two-surfaces isometrically embedded in Minkowski space, static spacetimes, or cylindrical Lorentzian three-manifolds, with applications to quasi-local mass [CWY].
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Use flexible T-surfaces and related grid-like models as local normal forms for possible smooth flexing mechanisms [IRT].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Find curvature-line or asymptotic-line conditions weaker than convexity and analyticity which still force global rigidity of a smooth closed surface in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 51}

Let $M$ be the space of immersions of a fixed compact surface into $\mathbb{R}^{3}$. Prove that the subspace of $M$ which consists of infinitesimally rigid immersions is "generic" in $M$. How can we describe its complement? Study the same problem in the category of surfaces of rotation.

Status. Open in this smooth infinite-dimensional form. There are strong supporting results in special classes, including convex surfaces and surfaces of revolution, and there is a well-developed finite-dimensional generic rigidity theory for frameworks. A full Baire-generic theorem for smooth immersions of a fixed compact surface in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$, together with a useful description of the exceptional set, does not appear to be available.

An infinitesimal bending of an immersion $F: \Sigma \rightarrow \mathbb{R}^{3}$ is a vector field $V$ along $F$ such that the first variation of the induced metric vanishes:
$$\langle d F(X), d V(Y)\rangle+\langle d F(Y), d V(X)\rangle=0 .$$
The trivial solutions are restrictions of ambient Killing fields. Thus infinitesimal rigidity means that the kernel of this firstorder linear system is exactly the six-dimensional space of Euclidean motions. The problem asks whether this minimal-kernel condition is generic among all immersions.

Classically, smooth closed surfaces of positive Gauss curvature are infinitesimally rigid; a modern proof uses the second variation of the Hilbert-Einstein functional [Iz]. Surfaces of revolution give a more tractable reduction of the bending equations. Minagawa-Rado developed this reduction, and Sabitov later obtained local existence and uniqueness results at low regularity and explicit rigidity criteria for compact analytic surfaces of revolution whose poles have prescribed orders of flattening [MR, Sab]. Sabitov also constructed nonanalytic nonrigid examples with any prescribed pole-flattening order. Thus even in the rotationally symmetric class the exceptional set depends on analyticity and fine jet data, rather than on a single curvature-sign condition. For general smooth immersions, the bending equations may also interact with asymptotic lines, umbilics, and global topology in a much less transparent way.

The finite-framework theory gives a useful analogy but not a direct solution. Asimow-Roth showed that generic rigidity and generic infinitesimal rigidity are closely related for finite bar-and-joint frameworks; there the relevant rank conditions are finite-dimensional algebraic conditions. In the surface problem, the corresponding operator is a differential operator whose kernel can change through analytic and geometric degenerations. Describing the complement should therefore involve both PDE Fredholm theory and the classical geometry of lines of curvature and asymptotic directions.

The recent theory of discrete and smooth T-surfaces provides a concrete testing ground for this complement. In the discrete setting, generic quadrilateral surfaces are rigid, while T-hedra form explicit flexible families; in the smooth setting, Izmestiev-Rasoulzadeh-Tervooren give analytic descriptions and parametrizations of deformations of T-surfaces [IRT]. These models suggest that the exceptional set in the smooth immersion space may not be a single simple codimension-one condition, but a union of geometric strata reflecting special curvature-line coordinates, separation of variables in the bending equations, or hidden integrable-system structure.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Prove a Baire-generic infinitesimal rigidity theorem for smooth immersions of a fixed compact surface into $\mathbb{R}^{3}$.
\item[2.] Original direction. Describe the exceptional set of non-infinitesimally-rigid immersions by geometric conditions on curvature, umbilics, asymptotic-line dynamics, or solvability of the bending equations.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Complete the classification of infinitesimally flexible surfaces of revolution and determine whether their complement is open and dense in the natural function space of profiles [MR, Sab].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Relate infinitesimal rigidity of smooth surfaces to polyhedral generic rigidity through triangulation, approximation, and convergence of bending fields [AR1, AR2, Sa].
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Analyze T-surfaces and other explicit flexible smooth families as model strata in the complement of the generic rigid set [IRT].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Develop a Fredholm package for the infinitesimal bending operator modulo Euclidean Killing fields and identify the geometric strata where the Fredholm index or kernel jumps.
\end{itemize}

[AR2] L. Asimow and B. Roth, The rigidity of graphs. II, J. Math. Anal. Appl. 68 (1979), no. 1, 171-190.
[IRT] I. Izmestiev, A. Rasoulzadeh and J. Tervooren, Isometric deformations of discrete and smooth T-surfaces, arXiv:2302.08925.

\section*{Problem 52}

The Nash embedding theorem insures that every manifold can be isometrically embedded into some Euclidean space, but it does not give us geometric properties of the embedding. For example, one hopes to show that a complete manifold with bounded Ricci curvature and positive injectivity radius can be embedded with bounded mean curvature in a higher dimensional Euclidean space.

Status. Open in the stated form. Nash's smooth embedding theorem and Greene's noncompact extension give high-codimension isometric embeddings, but the construction does not give a mean-curvature bound depending only on $n$, a Ricci bound, and a positive injectivity-radius lower bound. Known quantitative embedding theorems usually require stronger bounded-geometry hypotheses, such as bounds on the full curvature tensor and its derivatives, or they give bi-Lipschitz rather than isometric embeddings.

The geometric issue is that an isometric embedding solves the metric equations
$$g_{i j}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N} \partial_{i} F^{\alpha} \partial_{j} F^{\alpha},$$
while the mean curvature involves second derivatives of $F$. Nash's iteration produces smooth embeddings but does not directly control those second derivatives by the intrinsic Ricci curvature and injectivity radius. The Gauss equation also shows that extrinsic curvature and intrinsic curvature are coupled only after the full second fundamental form is known; a bounded mean curvature estimate is weaker than a bounded second fundamental form estimate, but it is still an extrinsic regularity statement not provided by the general embedding theorem.

There are several nearby positive results. For compact manifolds, smooth Nash embeddings exist in dimensions depending only on $n$. For noncompact manifolds, Greene proved smooth isometric embedding theorems into Euclidean or pseudo-Euclidean spaces. Under stronger bounded geometry assumptions one can construct controlled coordinate systems and obtain quantitative local or bi-Lipschitz embeddings. There are also compactness theorems for immersions whose second fundamental forms are bounded in $L^{p}$ or uniformly bounded. These results are important, but they do not answer the precise question with only $|\operatorname{Ric}| \leqslant C_{0}$ and $\operatorname{inj}(M, g) \geqslant i_{0}$.

One must also distinguish bounded mean curvature from bounded image or bounded ambient radius. Bessa and Montenegro studied restrictions on the mean curvature of Nash-type embeddings into Euclidean balls, showing that intrinsic geometry imposes lower bounds in some settings. Such results indicate that the desired embedding, if it exists, cannot be obtained by arbitrary compression of the Nash embedding without paying extrinsic-curvature cost.

A direct modern comparison is Petrunin's uniform form of Nash's construction [Pe]. If a complete manifold has positive injectivity radius and uniform bounds on the full curvature tensor and all of its covariant derivatives, he constructs a uniformly smooth isometric immersion, hence one with bounded normal curvatures. With an additional uniform polynomial-growth hypothesis he upgrades this to a tubed isometric embedding. The paper explains that finitely many full curvature-derivative bounds suffice for corresponding finite regularity, but it leaves the borderline low-regularity regime open. This is substantially stronger intrinsic input than a Ricci bound and injectivity-radius lower bound; in dimensions at least four a Ricci bound does not even control the full curvature tensor.

Recent work has also developed compactness theories in the reverse direction. Breuning's theorem for immersions with $L^{p}$-bounded or uniformly bounded second fundamental form gives a robust language for controlled extrinsic geometry [Br]. Martino-Rivière treat weak immersions whose second fundamental form lies in a critical Sobolev space and obtain induced differential structures and weak sequential closure results [MRi]. These results assume extrinsic curvature control instead of producing it from Ricci and injectivity-radius bounds. The remaining challenge is therefore to lower the intrinsic regularity required by the uniform Nash construction while retaining control of the mean-curvature vector.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Prove or disprove the stated estimate: for each $n, C_{0}, i_{0}$, find $N(n)$ and $C\left(n, C_{0}, i_{0}\right)$ so that every complete $\left(M^{n}, g\right)$ with $|\operatorname{Ric}| \leqslant C_{0}$ and $\operatorname{inj}(M, g) \geqslant i_{0}$ has an isometric embedding $F: M \rightarrow \mathbb{R}^{N(n)}$ with $\left|H_{F}\right| \leqslant$ $C\left(n, C_{0}, i_{0}\right)$.
\item[2.] Original direction. Determine whether the same hypotheses can give an isometric embedding with bounded second fundamental form, or whether full curvature bounds are necessary.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Establish local versions with uniform mean-curvature control on geodesic balls under Ricci and injectivity-radius bounds.
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Compare isometric embeddings with controlled mean curvature to quantitative bi-Lipschitz embeddings of bounded-geometry manifolds.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether compactness theories for immersions with bounded or Sobolevcontrolled second fundamental form can be reversed or combined with Nash-Moser iteration to produce mean-curvature control [Br, MRi].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Decide how far Petrunin's uniformly smooth immersion theorem can be extended from full bounded geometry to two-sided Ricci bounds and positive injectivity radius [Pe].
\item[7.] AI-generated formulation. Develop a Nash iteration with extrinsic curvature bookkeeping, tracking how each smoothing and correction step changes the mean curvature vector.
\end{itemize}

References.
[BM] G. P. Bessa and J. F. Montenegro, On the mean curvature of Nash isometric embeddings, arXiv:0809.2563.
[Br] P. Breuning, Immersions with bounded second fundamental form, J. Geom. Anal. 25 (2015), no. 2, 1344-1386; arXiv:1201.4562.
[EB] S. Eriksson-Bique, Quantitative bi-Lipschitz embeddings of bounded curvature manifolds and orbifolds, Geom. Topol. 22 (2018), no. 4, 1961-2004; arXiv:1507.08211.
[Gr] R. E. Greene, Isometric embeddings of Riemannian and pseudo-Riemannian manifolds, Mem. Amer. Math. Soc. 97 (1970).

\section*{Problem 53}

Can one generalize Weyl's embedding problem to higher dimensions?
This would be to prove that a compact, N-dimensional manifold with positive sectional curvature can be isometrically immersed into the Euclidean space of $\frac{N(N+1)}{2}$ dimensions.

One difficulty comes from the lack of understanding of the nonuniqueness of the immersion. P. Griffiths has recently obtained some new insight into this problem.

Status. Open in the stated smooth compact global form. The analytic local isometric embedding problem is settled by the Janet-Cartan theorem, and Nash gives global smooth embeddings in higher codimension. There is also strong smooth local progress at the determined dimension: every smooth three-dimensional metric is locally isometrically embeddable in $\mathbb{R}^{6}$ at a point where its Riemann tensor is nonzero. What remains open here is the global positive-sectional-curvature problem in the Weyl dimension; in particular, no theorem is known which embeds every compact positively curved three-manifold isometrically in $\mathbb{R}^{6}$.

The classical Weyl problem asks for an isometric embedding of a positively curved metric on $\mathbb{S}^{2}$ into $\mathbb{R}^{3}$. It was solved by Lewy, Nirenberg, Pogorelov, and others through elliptic Monge-Ampère methods and convex-surface theory. In dimension two, positive Gauss curvature gives a scalar elliptic equation for the support function or embedding. In higher dimensions, the isometric embedding equations form a much larger nonlinear system:
$$g_{i j}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N(N+1) / 2} \partial_{i} F^{\alpha} \partial_{j} F^{\alpha} .$$
At the determined dimension the system has exactly as many unknown functions as equations, so one cannot simply use high-codimension flexibility.

Janet and Cartan proved local analytic embedding into $\mathbb{R}^{N(N+1) / 2}$. Bryant, Griffiths, and Yang studied the smooth determined system using exterior differential systems and characteristic varieties. In dimension three they obtained local existence under a curvature condition; Nakamura-Maeda and Goodman-Yang extended this to every point where the Riemann tensor is nonzero. Chen-Clelland-Slemrod-Wang-Yang later gave a simpler proof by converting the linearized equations into a strongly symmetric positive system and applying Nash-Moser iteration [CCSWY]. This is genuine progress on the smooth local problem, but it neither crosses a zero-curvature point in full generality nor globalizes over a compact manifold.

In dimensions at least five, Han and Khuri proved the precise Bryant-Griffiths-Yang conjecture for sufficiently small generic parameters: the characteristic variety of the reduced linearized system is not smooth [HK]. Their proof first reduces the large linearized embedding system to an equivalent $N$-by- $N$ first-order system and then locates the singular locus algebraically. The smallness and genericity qualifications are essential; the result is a structural warning about higher-dimensional PDE analysis, not a global nonembedding theorem.

There is related progress in codimension one under additional assumptions. Li and Weinstein obtained a priori estimates for codimension-one isometric embeddings of metrics with nonnegative sectional curvature and positive scalar curvature on $\mathbb{S}^{n}$, generalizing estimates in the two-dimensional Weyl problem. These results are valuable but address a different target dimension and require local embeddability or curvature hypotheses tailored to the hypersurface equation.

There is also a new computational and weak-formulation direction around the classical two-dimensional Weyl problem. Gao-Hu-Li-Zhang recently proposed finite element methods for isometric embedding of Riemannian manifolds, beginning with Weyl's problem for positive Gaussian curvature surfaces in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$, and proved well-posedness, convergence, and error estimates for their numerical scheme [GHLZ]. This does not settle the higher-dimensional Weyl problem, but it suggests a practical auxiliary program: develop numerical diagnostics for the determined isometric embedding system, especially near characteristic degeneration and nonuniqueness.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Prove or disprove that every compact $N$-manifold with positive sectional curvature admits a smooth isometric immersion into $\mathbb{R}^{N(N+1) / 2}$.
\item[2.] Original direction. Settle the first open case: smooth compact positively curved three-manifolds into $\mathbb{R}^{6}$.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Develop an elliptic or mixed-type analytic framework for the determined isometric embedding system in positive sectional curvature.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether additional topological, convexity, or curvature-pinching hypotheses force global embeddability in the Weyl dimension.
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Use characteristic-variety analysis to decide whether the Bryant-Griffiths-Yang degeneracy found by Han-Khuri can be converted into either an obstruction or a solvability condition [BGY, HK].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Build reliable numerical tests for the higher-dimensional determined system, using the finite element Weyl program as a two-dimensional model [GHLZ].
\item[7.] AI-generated formulation. Identify a geometric replacement for convexity in dimension $N \geqslant 3$ which turns the determined isometric embedding system into a globally solvable elliptic system.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 54}

Given a smooth metric in a neighborhood of a point $p$ in a 2-dimensional manifold, can one find a neighborhood of $p$ that embeds isometrically into $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ ?

The cases where the metric is either $C^{\omega}$ or of strictly positive or negative curvature are well known. See Pogorelov [Pg] for a possible counterexample in the smooth category.

Status. Open for arbitrary smooth metrics. The analytic case is solved by the Janet-Cartan theorem, and the strictly elliptic or hyperbolic cases $K(p)>0$ and $K(p)<0$ are classical. C. S. Lin proved local embedding for sufficiently smooth metrics with nonnegative Gaussian curvature and for clean sign changes. Later work extended the positive results to several finite-order mixed-type configurations. Published counterexamples of Pogorelov and Nadirashvili-Yuan concern $C^{2,1}$ metrics and failure of a local $C^{2}$ realization; they do not by themselves disprove the $C^{\infty}$-metric question. A 2002 Nadirashvili-Yuan preprint claims smooth nonpositively curved metrics with no local $C^{3}$ embedding, but it remains unpublished and is recorded here as a claim, not as a settled status theorem [NYp].

The local isometric embedding equations for a surface in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ are equivalent, after a choice of coordinates, to a Monge-Ampère type equation. The sign of the Gaussian curvature determines the analytic character of the equation: positive curvature is elliptic, negative curvature is hyperbolic, and curvature vanishing or changing sign produces degenerate or mixed type equations. This explains why the analytic and strictly signed cases behave differently from the general smooth case.

Lin's nonnegative-curvature theorem was a major step because it treated degeneracy at $K=0$. His later work on clean sign changes opened the mixed-type direction. Khuri proved local existence when the Gaussian curvature changes sign to finite order along a smooth curve; Han-Khuri treated transverse intersections of zero curves; and Tsung-Yin Lin treated tangent zero curves forming cusp domains. These results show that many geometrically natural degeneracies are still embeddable.

The negative results are equally important for formulating the frontier. Pogorelov's $C^{2,1}$ construction, later supplied with detailed corrections by Holland, builds successively smaller bad metric disks accumulating at the base point [Ho, Pg]. Nadirashvili-Yuan modified this construction to obtain nonnegative Gauss curvature while retaining the same low metric regularity [NY]. Their separate smooth preprint arranges infinitely many localized bad regions and uses the ruling structure forced on the hypothetical $C^{3}$ image to derive incompatible length estimates [NYp]. Because that smooth claim has not become a published theorem, the original universal smooth assertion and the classification of its possible obstructions should both remain on the open list.

The current frontier is therefore a stratification problem for degenerating Monge-Ampère equations. Clean sign change, transverse zero curves, and cusp domains are positive cases, while published low-regularity examples show that uncontrolled degeneration can fail. The new finite-element work on Weyl's problem for $K>0$ surfaces [GHLZ] is adjacent rather than decisive here, but it suggests computable diagnostics near curvature degeneracy.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Settle the original question: prove that every smooth two-dimensional metric is locally smoothly realizable in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$, or produce a published and independently verifiable smooth counterexample.
\item[2.] Original direction. Classify the curvature-zero-set configurations for which a smooth two-dimensional metric admits a smooth local isometric embedding into $\mathbb{R}^{3}$.
\item[3.] Original direction. Determine sharp regularity thresholds for local embeddability near points where Gaussian curvature vanishes to high or infinite order.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Develop a unified Monge-Ampère theory for the mixed elliptic- hyperbolic equations arising from sign-changing Gaussian curvature.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Verify, repair, or refute the smooth obstruction mechanism claimed in the Nadirashvili-Yuan preprint, and express it in intrinsic geometric terms [NYp].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend the positive finite-order zero-set cases from clean curves, transverse intersections, and cusp domains to more general singular or infinitely flat zero sets [Kh, HK, L3].
\item[7.] Reference-driven formulation. Develop numerical or weak-formulation tests for local embeddability near $K=0$, using the positive-curvature finite element Weyl theory as a starting model [GHLZ].
\item[8.] AI-generated formulation. Formulate a local "curvature phase portrait" criterion which decides embeddability from the Taylor expansion and zero-set geometry of $K$ near $p$.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 55}

Suppose one defines an isometric embedding of a manifold into $\mathbb{R}^{N}$ to be elliptic if the second fundamental form corresponding to each normal has at least two nonzero eigenvalues of the same sign (see Tanaka [Ta]). Suppose, then, that we have two elliptic isometric embeddings of a fixed compact manifold. Are they congruent to each other? What is the correct generalization of the Cohn-Vossen rigidity theorem to higher dimensions? If $M$ is a complete immersed surface in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ with finite area and if $K$ is bounded and nonpositive, then is $M$ rigid?

Status. Open in the full elliptic-embedding generality. There is substantial progress for convex hypersurfaces and for rigidity statements closer to the classical Cohn-Vossen setting, but a general congruence theorem for elliptic isometric embeddings in higher codimension is not known. The finite-area nonpositive-curvature surface question also remains part of a broader open rigidity theory for complete noncompact surfaces in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$.

Tanaka introduced a notion of elliptic isometric embedding for which the infinitesimal deformation operator becomes elliptic [Ta]. His Theorem 2.4 is a local rigidity theorem in the space of embeddings: if a compact reference embedding is elliptic and infinitesimally rigid, then two sufficiently $C^{3}$-near embeddings inducing the same metric are congruent. The infinitesimalrigidity and neighborhood hypotheses are essential, so this theorem does not answer the original question about an arbitrary pair of elliptic embeddings. Its mechanism is upper semicontinuity of the kernel of a family of elliptic operators, followed by identification of the minimal kernel with ambient Euclidean Killing fields.

For hypersurfaces, the high-dimensional Cohn-Vossen picture is much better understood. Guan-Shen prove congruence for isometric compact star-shaped hypersurfaces in a space form when the normalized scalar curvature is strictly larger than the ambient curvature [GS]. Borisenko proves intrinsic-metric rigidity for arbitrary compact convex hypersurfaces in $E^{n+1}, n \geqslant 3$, without a smoothness assumption, and obtains corresponding constant-curvature results [Bo]. He also proves local congruence near corresponding strictly convex points in ambient dimension at least four [Bo2]. These are decisive answers in the convex hypersurface branch, but not in higher codimension, where several normal directions interact and Tanaka's ellipticity condition is not ordinary convexity.

The final surface question is related to the global theory of negatively curved or nonpositively curved complete surfaces in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$. Hilbert and Efimov give nonexistence when negative curvature is bounded away from zero everywhere. Gálvez-Martínez-Teruel prove that if this uniform negativity holds only outside a compact set, then every complete immersion has finite total curvature, finite area, is proper, and has ends asymptotic to half-lines [GMT]. Independently, Mendonça proves that such an end with compact boundary cannot have infinite total absolute curvature [Me]. These results isolate a very restricted finite-total-curvature end geometry, but they do not prove rigidity. They also do not settle Yau's full finite-area problem, where $K$ may tend to zero.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Prove or disprove that two elliptic isometric embeddings of a fixed compact manifold into Euclidean space must be congruent.
\item[2.] Original direction. Identify the correct convexity or ellipticity hypothesis in higher codimension which plays the role of positive Gauss curvature in the Cohn-Vossen theorem.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend high-dimensional Cohn-Vossen rigidity beyond hypersurfaces in space forms to higher-codimension elliptic immersions.
\item[4.] Original direction. Determine whether complete immersed finite-area surfaces in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ with bounded nonpositive Gaussian curvature are rigid.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine how much of Borisenko's global and local convex hypersurface rigidity survives under weaker strict-convexity, low-regularity, or space-form hypotheses [Bo, Bo2].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Use the end-geometry restrictions of Gálvez-Martínez-Teruel and Mendonça to isolate the possible finite-area nonpositive-curvature surfaces which could flex [GMT, Me].
\item[7.] Reference-driven formulation. Analyze the borderline finite-area ends for which $K \rightarrow 0$, precisely the regime not excluded by the Efimov-type end theorems [GMT, Me].
\item[8.] AI-generated formulation. Formulate a normal-bundle positivity condition, invariant under rotations of the normal frame, which makes the higher-codimension isometric embedding system elliptic and globally rigid.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 56}

The famous Efimov theorem [Ef] states that no complete surface with curvature $\leqslant-1$ exists in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$. One may ask whether or not a complete hypersurface with Ricci curvature less than -1 can exist in $\mathbb{R}^{N}$. This was asked in [Y3] and [R].

One may also try to generalize Hilbert's theorem and ask if the hyperbolic space form of dimension $N$ can be isometrically embedded in $\mathbb{R}^{2 N-1}$.

Another problem is the nature of the singularities of a surface with $K=-1$ in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$. (See Hopf [Ho].) Can one give a good definition of weak solution for the $K=-1$ embedding equations, analogous to minimal currents for the zero mean curvature equation? Possibly it would be useful to consider objects in the frame bundle.

Status. Partially resolved, with important remaining analytic and geometric questions. Efimov's theorem gives the classical two-dimensional nonexistence result. Smyth-Xavier completely exclude a uniform negative Ricci bound for three-dimensional Euclidean hypersurfaces and prove the corresponding statement in higher dimension provided the sectional curvature omits at least one real value; the unrestricted higher-dimensional case remains open. The low-codimension hyperbolic-space question has recently been settled in the expected negative direction: Moore proved in 2025 that $\mathbb{H}^{N}$ admits no isometric immersion in $\mathbb{R}^{2 N-1}$. The last part, asking for a good weak theory and a useful description of singularities for $K=-1$ surfaces in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$, remains open in the geometric form stated here, although there is substantial PDE progress through the sine-Gordon equation and weak Gauss-Codazzi compactness.

The historical starting point is Hilbert's theorem for the complete hyperbolic plane in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$, followed by Efimov's much stronger theorem excluding complete immersed surfaces whose Gaussian curvature is bounded above by a negative constant. These theorems show that the local theory of negatively curved surfaces and the global complete theory behave very differently.

The first higher-dimensional question asks whether an analogous obstruction follows from a negative upper bound on Ricci curvature. In codimension one, the Gauss equation expresses the intrinsic Ricci curvature in terms of the principal curvatures, so the problem becomes a global question about their possible asymptotic behavior and sign pattern. Smyth-Xavier proved that a complete immersed hypersurface $M^{3} \subset \mathbb{R}^{4}$ with negative Ricci curvature satisfies $\inf _{M}|\operatorname{Ric}|=0$. Thus the proposed bound Ric $\leqslant-c<0$ is impossible in dimension three. For $n>3$ they proved the same conclusion if the sectional curvature does not take every real value. The remaining exceptional regime-where the sectional curvature is forced to range through all of $\mathbb{R}$-is why the unrestricted higher-dimensional formulation is still open [SX].

The second question is a precise higher-dimensional analogue of Hilbert's theorem: can the complete hyperbolic space $\mathbb{H}^{N}$ be isometrically immersed in the Euclidean space of the critical dimension $2 N-1$ ? The answer is now no. Moore's 2025 proof uses the generalized Gauss-Bonnet formula for Riemannian polyhedra of Allendoerfer-Weil and Chern to rule out such an isometric immersion. This result should be recorded separately from the Ricci-curvature question, because Nash's theorem still gives smooth isometric embeddings in sufficiently high codimension, and Fontenele-Xavier show that even in arbitrary codimension isometric embeddings of hyperbolic space must have complicated asymptotic behavior.

The third question concerns the correct weak replacement for smooth pseudospherical surfaces. In asymptotic coordinates, surfaces with $K=-1$ are governed by the sine-Gordon equation, so the problem is linked to integrable systems and to singularities of pseudospherical surfaces. However, the analogy with minimal currents is not yet a complete geometric theory: one would like an intrinsic weak object, a compactness theorem, and a selection principle for singular limits. Chen-Slemrod-Wang developed a compensated-compactness framework for weak solutions of the Gauss-Codazzi-Ricci system, and later work on negatively curved surfaces has produced weak or low-regularity immersions under additional curvature hypotheses. These results give a serious analytic framework, but they do not yet supply a canonical current-like theory for complete $K=-1$ surfaces with singularities.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. For $n>3$, remove the sectional-curvature-range hypothesis from Smyth-Xavier and exclude complete hypersurfaces $M^{n} \subset \mathbb{R}^{n+1}$ under only Ric $\leqslant-c<0$, or construct an example in the exceptional regime where the sectional curvature takes every real value.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify the additional pinching, finite-total-curvature, or finite-index assumptions under which the Smyth-Xavier type principal-curvature obstruction becomes a full Ricci-curvature obstruction.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Develop a current-like or varifold-like weak theory for pseudospherical surfaces, with a compactness theorem, a boundary operator, and a regularity theory for the singular set.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Relate the frame-bundle formulation of $K=-1$ surfaces to weak solutions of the sine-Gordon equation and to weak Gauss-Codazzi data.
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend Moore's nonimmersion theorem for $\mathbb{H}^{N} \subset \mathbb{R}^{2 N-1}$ to broader complete constantnegative-curvature manifolds, or identify the sharp role of the ambient dimension [Mo].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Find a geometric entropy or defect measure which records the loss of smoothness in a sequence of smooth $K=-1$ immersions and whose vanishing characterizes classical pseudospherical surfaces.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 57}

Find nontrivial sufficient conditions for a complete, negatively curved surface to embed isometrically in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$. Such a condition might be a rate of decay of the curvature. Related to this is the Dirichlet problem for prescribed Gauss curvature.

Status. Open in its broad embedded form, but there is substantial partial progress for isometric immersions. The central obstruction is the Hilbert-Efimov phenomenon: a complete surface whose negative curvature is bounded away from zero cannot be smoothly immersed in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$. Therefore any positive result must use decay, finite total curvature, monotonicity, oscillation control, or appropriate boundary/initial data.

Historically, the problem was first clarified by the contrast between local solvability and global obstruction. Locally the isometric embedding equations for negative curvature are hyperbolic and may be written through the Gauss-Codazzi system. Globally, Hilbert's theorem and Efimov's theorem show that constant negative curvature, or curvature bounded above by a negative constant, is impossible for complete smooth immersions in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$.

Hong gave an important positive result: under suitable hypotheses, including sufficiently fast decay of the negative curvature at infinity, one can solve the global isometric embedding problem. In the formulation recorded in the later comparison notes, faster-than-quadratic curvature decay is one natural sufficient condition. This should be understood as a model theorem: it does not give a final intrinsic characterization, but it shows that decay at infinity can overcome the Hilbert-Efimov obstruction.

The modern PDE approach treats the problem through the Gauss-Codazzi equations as a nonlinear hyperbolic system. Chen-Slemrod-Wang gave a compensated-compactness framework for weak Gauss-Codazzi-Ricci data. Cao-Huang-Wang obtained large $L^{\infty}$ solutions by a Lax-Friedrichs scheme, giving $C^{1,1}$ isometric immersions for a class of negatively curved surfaces. Cao-Huang-Wang then proved global smooth isometric immersion under an integrability condition allowing slowly decaying negative curvature [CHW2]. More recently, Cao-Han-Huang-Wang proved smooth isometric immersion results for complete simply connected negatively curved surfaces with finite total curvature and controlled oscillation. Their proof rewrites the Gauss-Codazzi equations as a symmetric hyperbolic system whose selected combinations of Riemann invariants have different decay rates and a partial damping mechanism. These results enlarge the class far beyond monotone rapid decay, but they do not by themselves prove injectivity or proper embeddedness and still leave open the desired sharp intrinsic criterion.

The Dirichlet problem for prescribed Gauss curvature is closely related but should be kept conceptually separate. It asks for embedded or immersed surfaces realizing prescribed curvature with boundary data, and its elliptic or hyperbolic character depends on the sign of the curvature and the chosen formulation. For negative curvature, boundary value problems remain delicate because characteristic directions and possible loss of regularity interact with global completeness.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Find a sharp intrinsic sufficient condition, stated only in terms of the metric and its curvature at infinity, for a complete negatively curved surface to admit a smooth isometric immersion in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether finite total curvature plus a natural quantitative oscillation condition is close to optimal for global smooth isometric immersion.
\item[3.] Original direction. Classify the decay rates of negative curvature which separate Hilbert-Efimov nonexistence from global embeddability.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Develop a regularity theory which upgrades weak or $C^{1,1}$ Gauss-Codazzi solutions to smooth immersions under checkable geometric hypotheses.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Compare rapid monotone decay, slow integrable decay, finite total curvature, and curvature oscillation hypotheses in the Hong and Cao-Huang-Wang programs [H, CHW2, CHHW].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine which immersion theorems under decay or finite-total-curvature hypotheses can be upgraded to proper isometric embeddings, and identify intrinsic conditions preventing self-intersection.
\item[7.] AI-generated formulation. Formulate a boundary-at-infinity Dirichlet problem for complete negatively curved metrics whose solvability is equivalent to global isometric immersion in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 58}

Recall that a Weingarten surface is a surface where the mean curvature $H$ and the Gaussian curvature $K$ satisfy a suitable functional relation of the form $\phi(K, H)=0$ where $\phi$ is a nonsingular function defined on the plane. It would be interesting to know if the ellipsoid of rotation is characterized among compact surfaces by $\lambda_{1}=c \lambda_{2}^{3}$ where $\lambda_{i}$ are the principal curvatures and $c$ is a constant. In general, Voss was able to establish that a compact real analytic Weingarten surface of genus zero is a surface of revolution (see Hopf [Ho]). What are the compact, real analytic Weingarten surfaces of higher genus? Must they have genus $=1$ and be either a tube surface or a surface of revolution?

Hopf proved that for a closed real analytic Weingarten surface of genus zero, the number $\frac{d k_{2}}{d k_{1}}$ (where $k_{1}$ and $k_{2}$ are the principal curvatures) must take the following discrete values at the umbilic point: $0,-1,(2 k+1)^{ \pm 1}$ for $k \geqslant 1$ and $\infty$. Is the same statement valid for compact smooth Weingarten surfaces of genus zero?

Another problem for surfaces in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ is to give an intrinsic characterization of compact surfaces defined by a real algebraic polynomial. How does one express the degree of the polynomial in terms of the invariants of the metric?

Status. Partially resolved, but the full classification questions remain open. The rotational-ellipsoid subproblem has a sharp regularity split: it is affirmative for closed real analytic surfaces, by Voss together with Kühnel-Steller or Simon, but Fernández-Mira constructed compact $C^{2}$ counterexamples. Thus the unrestricted low-regularity statement is false, while the smooth nonanalytic and strictly convex regimes still require separate analysis. Guan-Lin-Ma developed curvature-equation methods for Weingarten-type Christoffel-Minkowski problems. The higher-genus analytic classification, the smooth replacement for Hopf's discrete umbilic statement, and the intrinsic characterization of real algebraic surfaces are still open in the stated generality.

The natural historical order is the following. Hopf's theorem for constant mean curvature spheres and its Weingarten variants show that genus-zero hypotheses are extremely restrictive when the Weingarten relation is analytic or elliptic. Voss's result, as recorded by Hopf, fits this circle: compact real analytic genus-zero Weingarten surfaces are forced toward rotational symmetry. Modern Codazzi-equation methods, including the work of Aledo-Espinar-Gálvez, give a flexible framework for uniqueness and sphere theorems for elliptic Weingarten surfaces in space forms [AEG]. This reinforces the distinction between genus-zero elliptic theory and the much less settled higher-genus or merely smooth cases.

The ellipsoid question is more specific. It asks whether the relation $\lambda_{1}=c \lambda_{2}^{3}$ characterizes an ellipsoid of revolution among compact surfaces. In the real analytic category the answer is yes. In contrast, Fernández-Mira glue two rotational caps, each containing a flat disk and a nonflat annular part, to obtain a compact $C^{2}$ sphere which satisfies the relation in some ordering but is not an ellipsoid [FM, Theorem 8.1]. Their example also corrects an earlier rotational claim used in the analytic literature. Since this relation is non-elliptic, elliptic Weingarten uniqueness theorems do not apply to it directly. Guan-Lin-Ma's work instead supplies a continuity method and a priori estimates for genuinely elliptic prescribed Weingarten-curvature equations on star-shaped hypersurfaces.

The higher-genus question is substantially less settled. A torus tube or a surface of revolution gives natural model examples, but there is no general classification of compact real analytic Weingarten surfaces of higher genus. In the smooth genus-zero question, Hopf's discrete list for the derivative ratio at an umbilic is tied to analyticity and to the local complex-analytic structure of the curvature-line equation; whether the same discreteness survives in the smooth category is still a delicate regularity question.

Recent work also clarifies how much of the genus-zero theory is really elliptic rather than merely analytic. Fernández-Mira classified the possible phase portraits of rotational elliptic Weingarten surfaces in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$, proved a sharp halfspace theorem for finite-order elliptic Weingarten equations, and showed that outside the elliptic setting Yau's rotational-ellipsoid uniqueness question has a negative answer [FM]. Fernández then proved a Hopf-type uniqueness theorem for uniformly elliptic Weingarten spheres in $\mathbb{S}^{2} \times \mathbb{R}$, using rotational examples and the Gálvez-Mira framework [Fe]. These results suggest that the right modern formulation should separate elliptic Weingarten equations, non-elliptic relations, and singular or peaked examples.

The final algebraic-surface question is also open in a different direction. It asks whether one can recognize compact real algebraic surfaces in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ from the intrinsic metric and whether the degree of the defining polynomial has an intrinsic expression. This is not simply a Weingarten problem: it mixes rigidity, algebraic geometry, and inverse spectral or inverse curvature questions.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Complete the classification of compact real analytic Weingarten surfaces of genus at least one under ellipticity assumptions on the Weingarten relation.
\item[2.] Original direction. Decide whether the analytic rigidity theorem for $\lambda_{1}=c \lambda_{2}^{3}$ extends to $C^{\infty}$ nonanalytic compact surfaces; the $C^{2}$ statement is false [FM].
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether strict convexity or strictly positive Gaussian curvature excludes the flat-core mechanism in the Fernández-Mira counterexample and restores ellipsoid rigidity.
\item[4.] Original direction. Determine whether Hopf's discrete umbilic derivative values for real analytic genus-zero Weingarten surfaces remain valid in the smooth category.
\item[5.] Original direction. Give intrinsic metric criteria for a compact surface in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ to be congruent to a real algebraic surface, and relate those criteria to the algebraic degree.
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify compact smooth elliptic Weingarten spheres with singularities or peaked points, extending the rotational phase-space classification of Fernández-Mira [FM].
\item[7.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine which Hopf-type rotational symmetry theorems for elliptic Weingarten spheres in homogeneous spaces descend to $\mathbb{R}^{3}, \mathbb{S}^{2} \times \mathbb{R}$, and other space forms [GM, Fe].
\item[8.] AI-generated formulation. Find a curvature-line index formula for smooth elliptic Weingarten spheres which specializes to Hopf's analytic discreteness theorem when the relation is real analytic.
\end{itemize}

[Fe] I. Fernández, Rotational elliptic Weingarten surfaces in $\mathbb{S}^{2} \times \mathbb{R}$ and the Hopf problem, arXiv:2211.11373.

\section*{Problem 59}

Let $h$ be a real-valued function on $\mathbb{R}^{3}$. Find (reasonable) conditions on $h$ to insure that one can find a closed surface with prescribed genus in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ whose mean curvature (or curvature) is given by $h$.
F. Almgren made the following comments:

For "suitable" $h$ one can obtain a compact smooth submanifold $\partial A$ in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ having mean curvature $h$ by maximizing over bounded open sets $A \subseteq \mathbb{R}^{3}$ the quantity
$$F(A)=\int_{A} h d \mathcal{L}^{3}-\operatorname{Area}(\partial A)$$

A function $h$ would be suitable, for example, in case it were continuous, bounded, and $\mathcal{L}^{3}$ summable, and $\sup F>0$. However, the relation between $h$ and the genus of the resulting extreme $\partial A$ is not clear.

In fact, the problem in this context is a special case of a variety of minimal partitioning problems. One can see [A] for this type of problem, and there is one of interest in the work of Sir W. Thomson (Lord Kelvin) [Th]. With a suitable restriction on $h$, Bakel'man [Ba] and Treibergs-Wei [TW] have found solutions of this problem for the closed surface of genus zero.

Status. Open in the stated genus-prescribed form. There are strong existence theorems for special topologies, for variational weak solutions, and for prescribed mean curvature hypersurfaces under generic or sign hypotheses, but there is no general intrinsic condition on a function $h$ which determines the existence of a closed surface of each prescribed genus in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ with mean curvature $h$.

The variational starting point is Almgren's observation. If $h$ is continuous, bounded, integrable, and the functional
$$F(A)=\int_{A} h d \mathcal{L}^{3}-\operatorname{Area}(\partial A)$$
has positive supremum, then maximizing over bounded open sets suggests a surface whose prescribed mean curvature is $h$. This is powerful because it allows geometric-measure-theoretic compactness, but it does not by itself control the topology of the resulting boundary. Thus the prescribed-genus part of the problem is the genuinely difficult extra constraint.

For genus zero, Bakel'man and Treibergs-Wei obtained embedded sphere results under suitable assumptions. The work of Spruck, Nirenberg, F. Lin, Walter Wei, Treibergs and others developed the elliptic PDE technology around the prescribed mean curvature equation and its Dirichlet problems. Yau later gave a general condition for a function to be the mean curvature of a closed sphere, using ideas related to the Schoen-Yau general relativity method and the Jang equation.

In 2026 Yin obtained a new embedded-sphere theorem directly in the Euclidean setting. For a positive $C^{2}$ function $f: \mathbb{R}^{n+1} \backslash\{0\} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}$, radial inner and outer barriers together with radial monotonicity on the intervening annulus produce a $C^{2, \alpha}$ embedded sphere which is a radial graph and has mean curvature $f$. The proof derives the quasilinear equation on $\mathbb{S}^{n}$, applies Leray-Schauder continuation, and obtains the needed global gradient estimate by a maximum-principle argument [Yi]. This materially strengthens the explicit sphere branch, but it does not control higher genus.

More recent work clarifies both the strength and the limitation of the classical problem. Anderson proved existence of branched conformal immersions of $\mathbb{S}^{2}$ into $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ with prescribed positive mean curvature, up to a finite-dimensional affine indeterminacy, and obtained partial higher-genus results. In closed ambient manifolds, Zhou-Zhu's min-max theory gives prescribed mean curvature hypersurfaces for generic prescription functions. These results show that prescribed mean curvature is now a robust variational subject, but they do not yet answer the original Euclidean question with prescribed embedded topology. Mazurowski-Zhou recently developed an Almgren-Pitts min-max theory for nonlocal functionals of the form
$$\Omega \mapsto \operatorname{Area}(\partial \Omega)-\int_{\Omega} h+f(\operatorname{Vol}(\Omega)),$$
and used it to construct infinitely many half-volume constant mean curvature hypersurfaces in closed manifolds under generic or positive Ricci assumptions [MZ]. Gaspar-Marx-Kuo proved infinitely many prescribed mean curvature hypersurfaces for broad classes of prescription functions when the ambient closed manifold contains a strictly stable minimal hypersurface or a contracting neighborhood [GMK]. These results are not Euclidean prescribed-genus theorems, but they show that Almgren's variational idea has a modern min-max continuation.

The related prescribed Gaussian curvature problem has a different PDE character, closer to Monge-Ampère type equations and convex geometry in the positive curvature case. For the present problem, the mean curvature case is especially subtle because topology can change under varifold or set-theoretic compactness, and because a closed surface imposes global balance conditions not visible in local elliptic theory.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Find necessary and sufficient conditions on $h: \mathbb{R}^{3} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}$ for the existence of an embedded closed surface of a fixed genus with mean curvature $h$.
\item[2.] Original direction. Determine which additional assumptions on $h$ force Almgren-type maximizers to have prescribed genus, or at least give effective genus bounds.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend the sphere results of Bakel'man, Treibergs-Wei, and Yau to higher genus while preserving embeddedness.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Test whether Yin's radial-barrier and monotonicity hypotheses can be replaced by degreetheoretic or variational conditions that survive for prescribed genus, and determine whether the radial-graph conclusion is unique [Yi].
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Relate min-max prescribed mean curvature hypersurfaces to the Euclidean prescribedgenus problem, especially when $h$ has compact support or prescribed sign.
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Adapt the nonlocal min-max functionals used for half-volume CMC hypersurfaces to Euclidean prescription functions and test whether they can impose topological constraints [MZ].
\item[7.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether the infinitely many PMC constructions in closed manifolds have Euclidean analogues after compactification, barriers, or weighted volume constraints [GMK].
\item[8.] AI-generated formulation. Develop a genus-sensitive replacement for Almgren's functional by adding a controllable topological penalty whose critical points still solve the prescribed mean curvature equation.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 60}
(Willmore [Wi]). Let $M$ be a compact, two-dimensional torus embedded in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$. Let $H$ be its mean curvature. Is it true that $\int_{M} H^{2} \geqslant 2 \pi^{2}$ with equality implying that $M$ is obtained from the circular torus by a Möbius transformation? Recently, Li-Yau [LY] defined the concept of conformal area for a conformal structure on a surface. They prove that $\int_{M} H^{2}$ is not less than this area. Using this, they show that $\int_{R P^{2}} H^{2} \geqslant 6 \pi$ and $\int_{M} H^{2} \geqslant 2 \pi^{2}$ if $M$ is conformally equivalent to the square torus.

Status. Solved. Marques and Neves proved the Willmore conjecture: every embedded torus in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ satisfies
$$\int_{M} H^{2} d A \geqslant 2 \pi^{2}$$
with equality precisely for the stereographic images of the Clifford torus, equivalently the circular torus up to Möbius transformation in the Euclidean formulation.

The historical development is especially important here. Willmore formulated the torus inequality in 1965 after computing the energy of standard tori. Li-Yau then introduced their conformal invariant and proved strong lower bounds for Willmore energy, including the non-embedded case and the square-torus conformal class mentioned in the problem. This reduced the hardest part of the conjecture to the embedded torus case.

The final proof by Marques-Neves used Almgren-Pitts min-max theory in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$. The essential bridge is conformal invariance of the Willmore energy: after stereographic projection, one studies a canonical family of cycles associated with an embedded surface in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ and applies the min-max theory for minimal surfaces. The Clifford torus appears as the min-max surface with area $2 \pi^{2}$, and the equality case follows from the rigidity of the argument.

Although the original problem is solved, it opened a larger Willmore program. For genus $g>1$, the natural analogue asks for the minimizer of Willmore energy among surfaces of that genus in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ or $\mathbb{R}^{3}$. Lawson's minimal surfaces are expected to play the role of the Clifford torus in higher genus, and recent work has proved such statements under additional symmetry assumptions, but the full higher-genus Willmore problem remains open.

Kusner-Lü-Wang have sharpened the symmetry-restricted picture by showing that Lawson surfaces $\xi_{m, k}$ minimize the Willmore energy among competitors sharing suitable ambient symmetry groups, while also noting genus-two phenomena where known methods do not guarantee a minimizer in the symmetric class [KLW]. Their revised paper supersedes a withdrawn 2021 preprint, so only the corrected theorem is used here. Quantitative stability has also begun to catch up with the qualitative theorem: Bi-Zhou proved that an integral two-varifold in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ of genus at least one with Willmore energy at $\operatorname{most} 2 \pi^{2}+\delta^{2}$ is, after conformal normalization, $W^{2,2}$-close to the Clifford torus [BZ]. Thus the original Willmore conjecture is solved, but its stability and higher-genus descendants remain active.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Determine the Willmore minimizer among embedded surfaces of each genus $g>1$ in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ or $\mathbb{R}^{3}$.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Prove or disprove the expectation that Lawson's minimal surface $\xi_{g, 1} \subset \mathbb{S}^{3}$ minimizes Willmore energy among genus $g$ surfaces.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Develop a full Morse-theoretic picture of the Willmore functional near the Clifford torus and higher-genus candidate minimizers.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend the min-max and canonical-family methods to constrained Willmore problems, for example fixed conformal class or imposed symmetry.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Remove or weaken the symmetry assumptions in the Kusner-Lü-Wang higher-genus Willmore minimization theorem [KLW].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Strengthen quantitative Clifford-torus stability from $W^{2,2}$ conformal parametrization to geometric distances suited to embedded smooth tori, and identify sharp exponents [BZ].
\item[7.] AI-generated formulation. Construct a quantitative stability inequality for the Willmore conjecture measuring the distance of an embedded torus from the Möbius orbit of the Clifford torus by the energy gap $\int_{M} H^{2}-2 \pi^{2}$.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 61}
(Alexandrov [A]). Let $S$ be the boundary surface of a convex body in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$. If the intrinsic radius of $S$ is bounded by 1 , what is the largest surface area of $S$ possible?

Status. Solved sharply by Grove and Petersen [GP]. If $S$ is a closed locally convex surface in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ with intrinsic radius at most $r$, then
$$\operatorname{Area}(S) \leqslant \pi r^{2} .$$
Consequently the answer for $r=1$ is the sharp supremum $\pi$. In the smooth nondegenerate class the inequality is strict, but there are smooth strictly convex surfaces with area arbitrarily close to $\pi r^{2}$. Their intrinsic metrics converge to the two-dimensional Euclidean purse: a singular Alexandrov sphere obtained by doubling a half-disc, equivalently by identifying boundary points of a disc by reflection. Thus "largest" is naturally interpreted as a supremum unless degenerate convex surfaces are admitted.

The proof is a direct instance of Grove-Petersen volume comparison à la Alexandrov. For an $n$-manifold with sectional curvature at least $k$ and radius at most $r$, ordinary ball comparison gives $\operatorname{vol} M \leqslant v_{k}^{n}(r)$, the volume of the radius- $r$ ball in the simply connected space form. Grove and Petersen prove that this bound is optimal and analyze almost-equality sequences by Gromov-Hausdorff convergence. In the range containing $k=0$, the only maximal singular limit models are the crosscap and the purse; for a spherical convex hypersurface the topology selects the purse. Setting $n=2, k=0$, and $r=1$ gives $v_{0}^{2}(1)=\pi$. Their Corollary D states explicitly that this supplies a solution to Problem 61 as worded in Yau's list.

The radius problem should not be confused with Alexandrov's older and still open intrinsic-diameter conjecture. That conjecture predicts that a convex surface of intrinsic diameter $D$ satisfies $\operatorname{Area}(S) \leqslant \pi D^{2} / 2$, with the doubled disc as the degenerate extremizer. Freitas-Krejčiřík prove the conjecture for general ellipsoids and obtain a deficit estimate involving the length of a cut locus [FK]. The contrast is instructive: a radius bound places the whole surface inside one comparison ball, whereas a diameter bound does not specify a center and requires additional global control of the cut locus.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Reference-driven formulation. Prove a quantitative stability theorem: control the Gromov-Hausdorff distance to the Euclidean purse by the deficit $\pi r^{2}-\operatorname{Area}(S)$, with a sharp rate.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Convert intrinsic near-maximality into an extrinsic statement for convex bodies, for example Hausdorff closeness after rigid motion and normalization to a thin neighborhood of a half-disc.
\item[3.] Original direction. Determine the optimal upper bound under simultaneous lower-curvature, radius, and noncollapsing constraints which exclude the singular purse limit.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Resolve Alexandrov's fixed intrinsic-diameter conjecture beyond the known special classes, and extend the Freitas-Krejčiřík cut-locus deficit method [FK].
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify the possible near-maximal limits in higher dimensions and determine when topological or extrinsic convexity assumptions rule out the crosscap model [GP].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Find a sharp bridge between radius and diameter deficits: does simultaneous near-equality in the radius bound and the conjectural diameter bound force a unique geometric degeneration?
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 62}
(Milnor [KO, M]). Let $\Sigma$ be a complete noncompact surface immersed in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$, and let $\lambda_{1}, \lambda_{2}$ be its principal curvatures. Prove that either $\left|\lambda_{1} \cdot \lambda_{2}\right|$ is not bounded away from zero on $\Sigma$, or $K$ changes sign, or $K \equiv 0$.

Status. Solved in the smooth complete setting, as a direct consequence of Bonnet-Myers in the positive-curvature case and Efimov's theorem in the negative-curvature case. Since
$$K=\lambda_{1} \lambda_{2},$$
the failure of the first alternative means that $|K| \geqslant c>0$ for some constant $c$. If $K$ does not change sign and $K \not \equiv 0$, then either $K \geqslant c>0$ or $K \leqslant-c<0$.

In the first case, the intrinsic metric on $\Sigma$ has Gaussian curvature bounded below by a positive constant. Bonnet-Myers then gives finite diameter, and Hopf-Rinow implies compactness, contradicting the assumption that $\Sigma$ is complete and noncompact. In the second case, Efimov's theorem rules out a complete immersed surface in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ whose Gaussian curvature is bounded above by a negative constant. Therefore the stated trichotomy follows.

The historical difficulty lies almost entirely in the negative-curvature case. Efimov's proof is deep and technically subtle, and this explains the comment that a transparent proof would be valuable. Klotz-Osserman and Milnor placed the result in the broader study of complete open surfaces in Euclidean three-space. More recent work of Gálvez-Martínez-Teruel gives extensions and partial Milnor-type results for complete surfaces whose ends have nonpositive curvature and whose principal curvatures satisfy lower-bound hypotheses. Mendonça sharpened the end-wise form of Efimov's theorem: under negative curvature bounded away from zero and infinite total curvature, complete immersed ends are excluded, and in particular complete Hadamard immersed surfaces with curvature bounded away from zero outside a compact set are ruled out [Me].

The complementary existence theory clarifies the sharp geometry behind the trichotomy. Cao-Han-Huang-Wang construct smooth isometric immersions of complete simply connected negatively curved surfaces of finite total curvature, allowing controlled angular oscillation and radial decay of the Gauss curvature [CHHW]. Their proof rewrites the Gauss-Codazzi equations in geodesic polar coordinates as a symmetric quasilinear hyperbolic system and obtains global estimates in two overlapping coordinate regions. Thus Efimov's uniform negative upper bound is not a technical artifact: once the curvature decays sufficiently toward zero, complete immersions can reappear.

Thus the original problem should be recorded as solved, but the surrounding program remains active: one wants simpler proofs, sharper end-wise hypotheses, and low-regularity or finite-total-curvature versions.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Reference-driven formulation. Find a short and conceptually transparent proof of Efimov's nonimmersion theorem for complete surfaces with $K \leqslant-c<0$ in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine the weakest assumptions at infinity under which a complete immersed surface with eventually nonpositive curvature must satisfy Milnor-type alternatives.
\item[3.] Original direction. Classify complete noncompact immersed surfaces for which $K$ has one sign outside a compact set and one principal curvature is bounded away from zero.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend the trichotomy to lower regularity immersions or weak Gauss-Codazzi solutions.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Relate finite-total-curvature smooth immersion theorems for negatively curved surfaces to Milnor's alternatives, especially when the principal curvatures have prescribed asymptotic behavior [CHHW].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine the borderline between the Efimov nonimmersion regime and the Cao-Han-Huang-Wang existence regime when $|K|$ has inverse-square decay with unrestricted angular oscillation [CHHW].
\item[7.] AI-generated formulation. Develop an Efimov-type "curvature escape" theorem measuring how fast $|K|$ must approach zero along every end of a complete noncompact surface with nonchanging curvature sign.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 63}
(Hopf). Prove that a closed surface $\Sigma$ immersed in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ with constant mean curvature is a round sphere.
Status. False as stated. The positive genus immersed case was disproved by Wente's constant-mean-curvature torus [W]. The statement remains true under the classical additional hypotheses that $\Sigma$ is a topological sphere, or that $\Sigma$ is embedded.

Hopf proved that a constant-mean-curvature immersion of a closed surface homeomorphic to $\mathbb{S}^{2}$ is totally umbilic, hence a round sphere [H]. Alexandrov's reflection method later proved the stronger embedded "soap bubble" theorem: every compact embedded hypersurface in Euclidean space with constant mean curvature is a sphere [A2]. Reilly gave another proof of the embedded case by integral identities [R].

The immersed problem changed substantially after Wente constructed an immersed constant-mean-curvature torus in $\mathbb{R}^{3}[\mathrm{~W}]$. Thus the original conjecture is not an open rigidity theorem for immersed surfaces, but it left a large classification problem. In the torus case, the subsequent integrable-systems theory gives a powerful algebro-geometric description of constant-mean-curvature tori, through finite type and spectral-curve methods [PS, Hi, Bo]. In higher genus, gluing methods and perturbative constructions produced many compact immersed examples and showed that the landscape is much richer than the sphere theorem suggests [K1, K2]. A complete geometric classification for arbitrary topology is still not known, especially if one asks for geometric invariants rather than an existence theorem alone.

For Alexandrov-embedded complete finite-topology CMC surfaces, the modern picture is also moduli-theoretic rather than rigid. Mazzeo-Pacard-Pollack prove gluing theorems attaching Delaunay ends to nondegenerate CMC surfaces and study the forgetful map from CMC moduli spaces to conformal structures; even in genus zero the resulting moduli spaces have nontrivial topology [MPP]. Breiner-Kapouleas extend graph-to-Delaunay gluing constructions to complete CMC hypersurfaces in higherdimensional Euclidean spaces [BK], and the survey of Kapouleas-Breiner-Kleene emphasizes conservation laws as a central organizing tool for such gluing constructions [KBK]. These works do not classify compact immersed CMC surfaces, but they explain why the post-Wente problem should be formulated in terms of moduli spaces, degenerations, flux/conservation laws, and compactification.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Give a geometric classification of compact immersed constant-mean-curvature surfaces in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ of fixed genus.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Describe the moduli space of compact immersed constant-mean-curvature surfaces and its compactification, including degenerations to bubble trees or Delaunay-type pieces.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Relate the spectral-curve classification of constant-mean-curvature tori to more directly geometric invariants, such as symmetry, area, enclosed algebraic volume, and umbilic data.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine which topological types occur under additional constraints such as Alexandrov embeddedness, finite symmetry group, or fixed Willmore energy.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Compare Alexandrov-embedded CMC moduli spaces and their conformal forgetful maps with the compact immersed CMC moduli problem [MPP].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Develop a unified classification scheme which compares the integrable-systems moduli of tori with the gluing moduli of higher-genus compact constant-mean-curvature surfaces.
\end{itemize}
