\section*{Problem 64}

Prove the Carathéodory conjecture that every closed convex surface in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ has at least two umbilic points. In the real analytic case, this was asserted by Bol [B] and Hamburger [H], but doubts were later expressed about these papers. See Klotz [K] for corrections.

Status. Needs verification. Claimed solved for $C^{3, \alpha}$ surfaces by Guilfoyle and Klingenberg, but the acceptance status remains controversial and should be tracked explicitly rather than recorded as an unconditional settled theorem. Their original proof preprint [GK0] was followed by published components establishing Fredholm regularity, interior mean-curvature-flow estimates, and the boundary/existence step used also for the Toponogov conjecture [GK1, GK2, GK3]. The authors now state that these papers complete the proof [GK5]. On the other hand, Guckenheimer's independent 2024 analysis describes the status as controversial and refers to the existing proofs as purported [Gu]. In the absence of an independent verification located in this audit, the conservative label is therefore claimed solution; needs verification. The borderline $C^{2}$ formulation remains open in any case.

The classical route reduces the global conjecture to a local index bound for isolated umbilic points: on a topological sphere the sum of the indices of the principal curvature line field is 2, so if every isolated umbilic has index at most 1, at least two umbilics must occur. In the analytic case, Hamburger gave a long proof of the index bound, and Bol proposed a shorter proof [B, H]. Klotz later corrected important gaps in Bol's argument [K], while the analytic literature remained delicate because the central issue is the resolution of degenerate umbilics.

Modern approaches reformulate the problem in complex and symplectic geometry. The surface of oriented normal lines of a convex surface is a Lagrangian surface in the neutral Kähler geometry of the space of oriented lines. In this language, umbilics become complex points, and the global conjecture is attacked through a Riemann-Hilbert problem for holomorphic discs with Lagrangian boundary conditions. Guilfoyle and Klingenberg's proof combines Fredholm regularity of these boundary value problems with higher-codimensional mean curvature flow estimates [GK0, GK1, GK2, GK3]. Independently, Ghomi and Howard gave a useful reformulation of the $C^{2}$ global conjecture in terms of asymptotically constant graphs [GH].

The most recent literature also shows that the problem should still be tracked at two levels. Guilfoyle and Klingenberg present the global $C^{3, \alpha}$ claim through the incompatibility between Fredholm regularity and the existence of a holomorphic disc produced by spacelike mean curvature flow [GK0, GK5]. Guckenheimer instead studies analytic degenerate umbilics directly: merging and splitting of singularities give a geometric obstruction to integer indices larger than one and relate index bounds to the order of degeneracy [Gu]. His paper both supplies independent local progress and documents the continuing disagreement about the global status. Neither line settles the sharp $C^{2}$ problem.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Obtain an independent verification or a precise gap analysis of the Guilfoyle-Klingenberg $C^{3, \alpha}$ argument, especially the compactness, bubbling, and boundary-control steps for holomorphic discs [GK0, GK3].
\item[2.] Original direction. Decide the sharp regularity threshold for the global Carathéodory conjecture, especially in the $C^{2}$ category where umbilics are naturally defined.
\item[3.] Original direction. Prove or disprove the sharp smooth local index conjecture: whether every isolated umbilic on a smooth convex surface has index at most 1 .
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify possible degenerate smooth non-analytic umbilics, including whether index 3/2 can occur.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Find a purely real-variable proof of the $C^{3, \alpha}$ theorem, or a proof closer to the classical principal-foliation picture.
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend Guckenheimer's analytic degeneracy analysis to a systematic normal form for smooth finite-type umbilics [Gu].
\item[7.] AI-generated formulation. Build a quantitative Carathéodory theory which gives lower bounds for the separation, stability, or total multiplicity of umbilics under small perturbations of a convex surface.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 65}

Can one define the rank of a compact $C^{\omega}$-manifold $M$ with nonpositive curvature so that if $M$ is a locally symmetric space the definition agrees with the standard one? Suppose there is a totally geodesic, immersed, flat 2-plane in $M$. Can one find an immersed totally geodesic torus in $M$ ? (See Gromoll and Wolf [GW], Lawson and Yau [LY].) If the "rank" of $M$ is greater than one, one expects that $M$ is very rigid metrically. How do we describe this rigidity?

Status. Partially resolved. The standard higher-rank rigidity theorem for compact nonpositively curved manifolds is now a central rigidity theorem [Ba, BBE, BBS, BS, EH]. The flat-closing question has a positive answer in the real analytic setting of the original problem by Bangert-Schroeder [BSc]. In the smooth Riemannian setting, however, whether every flat plane in the universal cover must produce an immersed totally geodesic flat torus in the quotient remains open in this level of generality.

The rank used in modern rigidity theory is the geodesic rank: the dimension of the space of parallel Jacobi fields along a geodesic, with the velocity field included. Locally symmetric spaces of noncompact type have rank in this sense equal to their usual symmetric space rank. For compact manifolds of nonpositive curvature, the higher-rank rigidity theorem says, roughly, that an irreducible manifold whose geodesics all have rank at least two is locally symmetric; otherwise the universal cover splits as a nontrivial Riemannian product [Ba, BBE, BBS, BS, EH]. Later work shows that the same philosophy persists beyond nonpositive curvature: Watkins proved a higher-rank rigidity theorem for manifolds without focal points [Wa]. This gives the expected metric rigidity once "rank greater than one" is interpreted as a global geodesic-rank hypothesis.

The flat-plane-to-flat-torus part is different. The flat torus theorem proves one direction: if the fundamental group contains a free abelian subgroup of rank $k$, then a nonpositively curved compact manifold contains a corresponding flat $k$-torus [BH]. The converse, often called the flat closing problem, asks whether the existence of a geometric $k$-flat forces such a periodic flat. Bangert-Schroeder proved the exact $k$-dimensional statement for compact real analytic manifolds of nonpositive curvature: every $k$-flat is accompanied by a closed $k$-flat [BSc]. Their introduction identifies this theorem explicitly as an answer to Yau's Problem 65. The proof combines three ingredients which explain the analyticity hypothesis. First, maximal flats are organized by flags of singular subspaces. Second, real analyticity makes the resulting set of well-structured flats subanalytic and yields a compact invariant manifold in the frame bundle. Third, after eliminating normal affine Jacobi fields, the geodesic flow is normally hyperbolic on a saturated compact set, and a shadowing argument produces a periodic flat. Thus the analytic version of Yau's flat-plane question is settled. The smooth problem is subtler: in general CAT(0) complexes, flat planes need not behave periodically [Wi], although positive results exist for special classes such as Euclidean buildings [Ci]. Thus the original question has split into a solved higher-rank rigidity theorem, an analytic flat-closing theorem, and an open smooth flat-closing problem.

Recent literature does not appear to have closed the smooth Riemannian flat-closing problem. The most useful progress for this problem is therefore conceptual: the no-focal-points rank-rigidity theorem of Watkins shows that the rank-rigidity part survives beyond sectional curvature $\leqslant 0[\mathrm{Wa}]$, while Ciobotaru's building result and Wise's CAT( 0 ) example show that the flat-closing problem is sensitive to the category in which one works [Ci, Wi]. A modern report of this problem should therefore separate "rank rigidity" from "flat closing" and state clearly which category is being used: analytic Riemannian, smooth Riemannian, CAT(0) complex, Euclidean building, or group action.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Prove or disprove the Riemannian flat closing conjecture for closed nonpositively curved manifolds: does every $k$-flat in the universal cover force a virtually $\mathbb{Z}^{k}$ subgroup?
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Decide the smooth flat closing problem under additional hypotheses, such as finite volume, rank-one/non-rank-one decomposition, or restrictions on the Tits boundary.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Describe quantitative versions of rank rigidity: if most geodesics carry almost-parallel Jacobi fields, must the metric be close to a locally symmetric or product metric?
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend rank-rigidity phenomena to manifolds without focal points, or identify the exact hypotheses under which such extensions hold.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Compare the analytic Bangert-Schroeder proof with the building and CAT(0) literature to isolate which recurrence, regularity, or boundary-at-infinity input creates periodic flats [BSc, Ci, Wi].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Replace the subanalytic compactness step in Bangert-Schroeder by a $C^{\infty}$ tame-geometry or dynamical substitute, while retaining enough normal hyperbolicity for the shadowing construction [BSc].
\item[7.] AI-generated formulation. Find a dynamical criterion on the geodesic flow which detects when an aperiodic flat plane can be approximated by periodic flat tori.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 66}
(Kuiper). Let $M$ be the surface obtained by attaching a handle to $\mathbb{R P}^{2}$. Can $M$ be immersed into $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ with a "two-piece" property, i.e. every plane that cuts the surface divides the image into exactly two components?

See Kuiper's survey paper [K] in the Chern Symposium volume.
Status. Solved negatively in the smooth category. Haab proved that the real projective plane with one handle admits no smooth tight immersion into $\mathbb{R}^{3}[\mathrm{H}]$. The distinction between smooth and piecewise-linear geometry is essential: Cervone later constructed a tight polyhedral immersion of exactly this surface [C2].

The two-piece property is one of the geometric forms of tightness. For a smooth immersion it is equivalent to attaining the Chern-Lashof lower bound for total absolute curvature, and generic height functions are perfect Morse functions [CL, K, CR]. The surface in the question is the nonorientable surface of genus three, with Euler characteristic -1. Haab's theorem says more precisely that its normalized total absolute curvature has infimum 5, but no smooth immersion attains this infimum [H].

The mechanism of Haab's proof is global. A generic planar projection of a hypothetical tight immersion has fold curves with several types of saddle singularities. Cutting the surface into the regions determined by these curves and counting their incidences forces two regions to contain local maxima for one height direction. A plane just below the lower maximum then separates those two caps from the rest of the surface, producing at least three pieces and contradicting tightness [C1, C2]. Smooth fold and profile curves are therefore not a technical convenience but the point at which the obstruction enters.

Cervone's polyhedral construction shows exactly why the category matters. His model has 13 vertices, 42 edges, and 28 triangular faces, hence Euler characteristic-1. It is an immersion because every vertex star is embedded. Its tightness is checked combinatorially: each locally extreme vertex lies on the global convex hull, every other vertex lies inside the convex hull of its neighbors, and the induced map on fundamental groups has the required injectivity property [C2]. The positive curvature that Haab's smooth argument forces can be avoided at a non-extreme polyhedral vertex. Nevertheless, Cervone showed that this is not merely a local smoothing issue [C1], and subsequently proved that every tight polyhedral immersion of this surface is asymmetric [C3].

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction, resolved. Haab's theorem gives a negative answer for smooth immersions; determine the sharp quantitative gap above total absolute curvature 5 under additional geometric bounds [H].
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Explain intrinsically why Cervone's polyhedral immersion cannot be smoothed through tight immersions, despite the absence of a local smoothing obstruction [C1, C2].
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify tight polyhedral immersions of this surface up to image homotopy and determine the minimum number of vertices and faces.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Quantify the instability of the two-piece property under smoothing: how many plane sections must acquire a third component, and at what scale?
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend Cervone's asymmetry theorem to a classification of possible finite symmetry groups and regular homotopy classes [C3].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Construct a computable certificate, from a triangulation and its oriented matroid, which verifies the two-piece property without checking all affine half-spaces.
\end{itemize}

\section*{IV. The Spectrum}

\section*{Problem 67}

Let $\Omega_{1}$ and $\Omega_{2}$ be two bounded smooth domains in $\mathbb{R}^{2}$ so that the eigenvalues (counting multiplicity) of the Laplacian acting on functions defined on $\Omega_{1}$ and $\Omega_{2}$ having zero boundary conditions are the same. Is $\Omega_{1}$ isometric to $\Omega_{2}$ ?

This is an old problem. For closed manifolds one can formulate an analogous problem, however, the answer is negative. This is by virtue of examples of Milnor [M] and Vignéras [V], the latter providing a two-dimensional counterexample with negative curvature.

Status. Open for bounded smooth planar domains in the stated generality. The answer is negative for planar domains with corners, by Gordon-Webb-Wolpert [GWW], and positive results are known under strong analytic, symmetry, or special-shape hypotheses [Z2, HZ1, HZ2].

This is Kac's question, "Can one hear the shape of a drum?" [K]. For closed manifolds, Milnor's flat tori and Vignéras' arithmetic hyperbolic surfaces show that the spectrum does not determine the metric in general [M, V]. In the planar domain setting, the famous counterexamples of Gordon-Webb-Wolpert are isospectral but not isometric, but their boundaries have corners [GWW]. Thus they do not settle the smooth version stated here.

The strongest positive results come from wave trace invariants and billiard dynamics. Zelditch proved spectral determination for certain real analytic plane domains with a reflection symmetry and a nondegenerate bouncing-ball orbit [Z2]. Hezari-Zelditch extended this circle of ideas to analytic domains with higher symmetry and to ellipses of sufficiently small eccentricity, which are spectrally unique among all smooth domains [HZ1, HZ2]. Nevertheless, the general smooth problem, the general real analytic problem without symmetry, and broad convex smooth classes remain open.

Recent work continues to strengthen the billiard-dynamical side of the problem. De Simoi-Kaloshin-Wei proved dynamical spectral rigidity for sufficiently smooth $\mathbb{Z}_{2}$-symmetric strictly convex domains near acircle [DKW]. Kaloshin-Sorrentino proved a local Birkhoff conjecture near ellipses, with applications to spectral rigidity of elliptic domains [KS], and Koval obtained local spectral rigidity for almost every ellipse under a boundary-integrability hypothesis [Ko]. Fierobe-Kaloshin-Sorrentino recently proved deformational length spectral rigidity for dihedrally symmetric domains close to an ellipse [FKS]. These results do not produce smooth isospectral counterexamples or solve Kac's problem, but they indicate that wave-trace and billiard length-spectrum methods are now most effective near highly structured convex tables.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Construct, or rule out, nonisometric isospectral bounded smooth domains in $\mathbb{R}^{2}$.
\item[2.] Original direction. Decide whether every real analytic bounded planar domain is spectrally determined, perhaps under generic billiard nondegeneracy assumptions.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Prove spectral determination or spectral rigidity for broad classes of convex smooth domains.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine which billiard invariants of a smooth planar domain are encoded by the Dirichlet or Neumann spectrum.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Bridge dynamical length spectral rigidity for convex billiards with Laplace spectral rigidity, especially near ellipses and near the circle [DKW, KS, Ko, FKS].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Find a smoothing principle which starts from a polygonal isospectral pair and decides whether the isospectrality can survive any smoothing of the corners.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 68}

In Problem 67, suppose the spectra of $\Omega_{1}$ and $\Omega_{2}$ are equal except for a finite number of exceptions. Are the two spectra in fact identical? One can ask a similar question if the set of exceptions is infinite but has density zero.

Status. Open. No general theorem is known which forces equality of the full Dirichlet spectra from equality outside a finite set, or from equality outside a density-zero exceptional set, in the smooth planar domain setting.

The question is a refinement of the inverse spectral problem in Problem 67. Most standard spectral-geometric tools are asymptotic: Weyl's law, heat trace coefficients, and wave trace singularities measure high-energy distributional information rather than the exact placement of a finite list of eigenvalues [K, Z1, DH]. A finite spectral discrepancy changes either trace by a globally smooth term, so it is invisible to the singular-support information used in most inverse results.

The density-zero formulation is even more delicate. A sparse exceptional subsequence may still contribute to lower-order or oscillatory terms in trace formulae, depending on its growth and on where it lies in the spectrum. For this reason one must specify whether the spectra are compared as sets, ordered sequences with multiplicity, counting functions with an error term, or trace distributions. Under these different interpretations the problem may have different analytic strength.

A useful way to phrase the finite-exception case is through the heat trace. Let $A$ and $B$ be the two finite exceptional multisets. Then
$$D(t)=\operatorname{Tr}\left(e^{-t \Delta_{1}}\right)-\operatorname{Tr}\left(e^{-t \Delta_{2}}\right)=\sum_{a \in A} e^{-a t}-\sum_{b \in B} e^{-b t}$$
is entire in $t$, and
$$D(t)=\sum_{m \geqslant 0} \frac{(-t)^{m}}{m!}\left(\sum_{a \in A} a^{m}-\sum_{b \in B} b^{m}\right) .$$
For a smooth planar domain the Dirichlet heat trace has a complete expansion in powers $t^{-1+j / 2}$ [Gr, Va]. Consequently, all half-integer heat coefficients of the two domains must agree, whereas the differences of the integer coefficients are precisely constrained by the power sums of $A$ and $B$. This is substantially stronger than equality of area and perimeter, but it does not by itself make $D$ vanish. If one could prove equality of all integer heat coefficients, then all power sums would agree and the two finite multisets would be identical.

The same obstruction appears in the other standard transforms. The difference of spectral zeta functions is a finite sum of entire functions $a^{-s}-b^{-s}$, so poles and residues do not see it. The difference of wave traces is a finite trigonometric sum and therefore smooth; hence the length-spectrum singularities also agree. Problem 68 therefore asks for a genuinely global uniqueness principle, not a formal consequence of the local trace expansions [DH, Gr, Va].

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Decide whether two smooth planar domains whose Dirichlet spectra agree outside finitely many eigenvalues must be fully isospectral.
\item[2.] Original direction. Formulate sharp density-zero hypotheses under which missing or altered eigenvalues cannot change the geometric inverse problem.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether equality of all sufficiently large eigenvalues forces equality of heat invariants, wave invariants, and then the missing low spectrum.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Decide whether the integer heat coefficients of two smooth planar domains can differ by the moment sequence of two distinct finite positive multisets while all half-integer coefficients agree [Gr, Va].
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Study the analogous question for closed negatively curved manifolds, where the wave trace is tied to the length spectrum.
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify finite exponential errors in heat traces which are compatible with smooth-domain heat asymptotics and boundary wave invariants [BGM, Gi, Gr, Va].
\item[7.] AI-generated formulation. Develop a "spectral unique-continuation" principle: if two spectral measures agree at high energy up to a finite-rank error, determine when the error must vanish.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 69}

Let $g(t)$ be a one-parameter family of metrics on a compact manifold with the same spectrum for the Laplacian. Prove that the metrics $g(t)$ are isometric to each other.

Guillemin and Kazhdan [GK] proved that this is the case if the manifold is a surface of negative curvature, or if the manifold is suitably negatively pinched when the dimension of the manifold is greater than two.

Status. False in this generality. There are nontrivial continuous isospectral deformations of compact Riemannian metrics [GW, Sc1, Sc2]. The statement is true in important rigid classes, especially under negative-curvature hypotheses [GK, CS].

The problem asks for spectral rigidity of a one-parameter isospectral family. Guillemin-Kazhdan proved such rigidity for negatively curved surfaces and for suitably pinched higher-dimensional negatively curved manifolds [GK]. Croke-Sharafutdinov later proved spectral rigidity for compact negatively curved manifolds in all dimensions [CS], and Sharafutdinov obtained related local audibility results for hyperbolic metrics [Sh]. These results use the relation between the Laplace spectrum, the wave trace, the marked length spectrum, and tensor tomography on negatively curved manifolds.

Without curvature or dynamical assumptions, the conclusion fails. Gordon-Wilson constructed continuous families of Dirichlet- and Neumann-isospectral metrics with boundary whose local geometry varies [GW]. Schueth then constructed continuous isospectral families on closed simply connected manifolds, including products of spheres, and on $S^{7}$ [Sc1, Sc2]. In her constructions changes in scalar-curvature critical values prove nonisometry, while a torus-action transplantation criterion preserves the function spectrum. Thus the modern problem is not to prove rigidity for all compact manifolds, but to identify the geometric classes in which isospectral deformations are forced to be trivial. In the planar-domain direction, the ellipse is $C^{\infty}$ - spectrally rigid in the appropriate setting [HZ], building on earlier work of Guillemin-Melrose [GM] and later wave-invariant methods [PT].

Recent rigidity work continues this deformation-theoretic viewpoint in more specialized settings. The billiard literature has obtained local and deformational length-spectral rigidity near circles and ellipses [DKW, KS, Ko, FKS]. On closed manifolds, Henheik-Kaloshin-Li-Vig proved rigidity for certain Laplace-isospectral deformations inside a generic Liouville conformal class on the two-torus, assuming linear dependence on the deformation parameter [HKLV]. Their proof first uses a wave-trace noncancellation theorem to recover rational invariant tori of the geodesic flow, and then applies the second variation of the closed-geodesic energy: preservation of one rational torus forces the linear conformal variation to vanish. These results confirm that the right modern replacement for the false global statement is a collection of rigidity theorems in dynamically controlled or integrable-nearby classes.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Characterize geometric or dynamical hypotheses under which every smooth isospectral deformation is trivial.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend negative-curvature spectral rigidity to broader classes, such as manifolds with no conjugate points or rank-one nonpositive curvature.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Decide which convex planar domains are $C^{\infty}$-spectrally rigid under smooth domain deformations.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Understand whether known isospectral deformation mechanisms can occur under curvature sign restrictions, symmetry restrictions, or simple topology.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Remove linearity, genericity, or conformal-class restrictions from Liouville-torus spectral rigidity [HKLV].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Develop a deformation theory whose infinitesimal kernel separates genuine geometric symmetries from Sunada-type or torus-action isospectral mechanisms.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 70}

Let $\Omega$ be a bounded domain in $\mathbb{R}^{2}$. Let $\lambda_{i}$ be the spectrum of the Laplacian acting on functions with zero boundary data (again, and henceforth, counting with multiplicity). Prove that
$$\lambda_{i} \geqslant \frac{4 \pi i}{\operatorname{area}(\Omega)}$$

This was conjectured by Pólya [Pól] and was proved by him in the case that $\Omega$ can tile $\mathbb{R}^{2}$. One can formulate a similar question for eigenvalues of the Neumann problem (with the inequality in the opposite direction).

Status. Open in general. Pólya proved the conjecture for domains which tile the Euclidean plane [Pól], and Li-Yau proved the corresponding sharp lower bound for averages of Dirichlet eigenvalues [LY]. These average estimates do not imply the individual estimate above.

Historically, Pólya's theorem for tiling domains is the first main case where the Weyl constant gives a term-by-term bound. The Li-Yau inequality gives, in dimension two,
$$\sum_{j=1}^{k} \lambda_{j} \geqslant \frac{2 \pi}{\operatorname{area}(\Omega)} k^{2}$$
which is the sharp Weyl-order lower bound for the sum of the first $k$ eigenvalues. This is often described as the averaged form of Pólya's conjecture.

More recent work has proved the conjecture for additional special classes and has produced stronger universal inequalities short of the full termwise conjecture. Filonov-Levitin-Polterovich-Sher proved the conjecture for the disk, making it the first non-tiling planar domain known to satisfy it, as well as for arbitrary planar sectors and, in the Dirichlet case, Euclidean balls in every dimension [FLPS1]. Their proof converts Bessel-zero counts into uniform weighted lattice-point estimates; only a short Neumann-disk interval requires a rigorous computer-assisted verification. The same authors then proved the Dirichlet conjecture for annuli by combining variational comparison, Bessel phase functions, refined trapezoidal lattice sums, and another certified finite computation [FLPS2]. Steinerberger obtained new two-point lower bounds for Dirichlet eigenvalues; for instance, in dimension two one has
$$2 \lambda_{n}+\lambda_{2 n} \geqslant \frac{10 \pi n}{|\Omega|},$$
which strengthens the information supplied by the Berezin-Li-Yau method but still does not settle the individual Pólya inequality [S].

The Neumann analogue, with the reverse inequality for the counting function or for the individual eigenvalues, is a parallel open problem in general. For planar convex domains, Filonov obtained the improved universal lower estimate
$$N_{N}(\Omega, \lambda) \geqslant \frac{|\Omega| \lambda}{2 \sqrt{3} j_{0}^{2}},$$
where $j_{0}$ is the first positive zero of $J_{0}[\mathrm{Fi}]$. This improves Kröger's general constant but remains below the conjectural $|\Omega| \lambda /(4 \pi)$. Guo-Miao-Wang-Zhan subsequently sharpened the ball estimates and verified the Neumann conjecture for circular cylinders in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ [GMWZ].

Two recent approaches clarify how close present methods come to the termwise conjecture. Jiang-Lin proved an explicit $\varepsilon$-loss version for every bounded Lipschitz domain: above a computable geometric threshold,
$$k \leqslant(1+\varepsilon) \frac{|\Omega| \omega_{n}}{(2 \pi)^{n}} \lambda_{k}^{n / 2} .$$
Their proof combines a minimal containing rectangle, Whitney decomposition, domain monotonicity, and an explicit Weylremainder estimate [JL1]. Ji-Luo obtain improved Berezin-Li-Yau type lower bounds for averaged sums of Dirichlet eigenvalues of the Laplacian and poly-Laplacian on bounded Euclidean domains [JL2]. Such results sharpen the analytic information available below Pólya's conjecture, but they also reinforce the gap between sharp averaged bounds and the individual inequality $\lambda_{i} \geqslant 4 \pi i /|\Omega|$.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Prove Pólya's Dirichlet conjecture for all bounded planar domains, preferably under minimal boundary regularity assumptions.
\item[2.] Original direction. Prove or disprove the Neumann analogue in the same generality.
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend the known positive cases from tiling domains, balls, circular sectors, and annuli to broader families such as convex domains or simply connected smooth domains.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Find a mechanism which upgrades sharp averaged estimates, such as the Li-Yau inequality, to term-by-term estimates under additional geometric hypotheses.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Compare the Filonov-Levitin-Polterovich-Sher annulus method with refined lattice-counting and Bessel-phase estimates for other separable planar domains [FLPS1, FLPS2].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether the explicit $\varepsilon$-loss threshold of Jiang-Lin can be combined with a finite verified computation for broad geometric classes, as in the disk and annulus proofs [JL1, FLPS1, FLPS2].
\item[7.] AI-generated formulation. Identify a geometric correction term which interpolates between the Berezin-Li-Yau average inequality and the individual Pólya bound, and determine whether its nonnegativity is equivalent to Pólya's conjecture.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 71}

Let $M$ be a two-dimensional, closed, compact surface. Can one find a universal constant $C$ so that
$$\frac{\lambda_{i}}{i} \leqslant \frac{C(g+1)}{\operatorname{area}(M)} ?$$
Here, $g$ is the genus of $M$. If $M$ is diffeomorphic to $\mathbb{S}^{2}$, is $\lambda_{i}(M)$ no greater than $\lambda_{i}\left(\mathbb{S}^{2}\right)$ where $\mathbb{S}^{2}$ is equipped with a metric of constant curvature $4 \pi / \operatorname{area}(M)$ ?

In the case $i=1$, this is known to be true. The case when $M$ is diffeomorphic to $\mathbb{S}^{2}$ was proved by Hersch [He]. For $M$ orientable and $g>0$, this was proved by Yang and Yau [YY]. Recently, Li and Yau [LY] were able to find similar bounds for nonorientable surfaces.

Status. Partially resolved. The existence of a universal upper bound of the stated form was solved by Korevaar [K]. The optimal constants, and especially the sharp dependence on the genus and on the index, remain active questions. The proposed higher-eigenvalue comparison with the round metric on $\mathbb{S}^{2}$ is true for the first positive eigenvalue by Hersch [He], but is false as a literal statement for higher eigenvalues; the sharp maximizers on the two-sphere are obtained only in a limiting sense by degeneration to several touching round spheres [KNPP].

The historical line begins with Hersch's sharp bound $\lambda_{1}$ area $\leqslant 8 \pi$ on the two-sphere [He]. Yang-Yau then proved the orientable genus- $g$ first-eigenvalue bound using meromorphic maps to the sphere [YY], and Li-Yau introduced conformal volume, giving a flexible framework which also applies to nonorientable surfaces [LY]. Korevaar's theorem extended the picture from the first eigenvalue to all eigenvalues, proving a bound of the form
$$\lambda_{i}(M) \text { area }(M) \leqslant C(g+1) i$$
for a universal constant $C[\mathrm{~K}]$. Grigoryan-Yau later gave an efficient capacitary decomposition method for proving such inequalities [GY].

The sharp theory is more subtle. On $\mathbb{S}^{2}$, the round metric maximizes only the first positive eigenvalue. For the $k$-th nonzero eigenvalue, Karpukhin-Nadirashvili-Penskoi-Polterovich proved the sharp isoperimetric inequality; the supremum is approached by metrics degenerating to a union of $k$ identical round spheres, and for $k \geqslant 2$ it is not attained by a smooth round metric [KNPP]. Thus the original sphere-comparison question should be recorded as solved positively only for $i=1$ and negatively for higher eigenvalues in the literal form stated.

Recent work has also clarified the variational side of the problem. For a fixed conformal class, Karpukhin-Nadirashvili-Penskoi-Polterovich show that conformal maximizers are either conical extremal metrics or limits with bubble trees [KNPP2]. Karpukhin-Kusner-McGrath-Stern developed equivariant eigenvalue-optimization methods which produce minimal surfaces in balls and spheres and give new existence tools for maximizing metrics under symmetry [KKMS]. Petrides recently proved existence of optimal metrics for a wide class of combinations of Laplace eigenvalues on closed orientable surfaces, relating them to minimal eigenmaps into ellipsoids [Pe]. Karpukhin-Petrides-Stern then completed the first-eigenvalue existence theory: every closed surface, orientable or nonorientable, admits a metric maximizing $\lambda_{1}$ area, smooth except at finitely many conical points [KPS]. Their key step is the strict topological monotonicity
$$\Lambda_{1}\left(M \# \mathbb{R} \mathbb{P}^{2}\right)>\Lambda_{1}(M), \quad \Lambda_{1}\left(M \# T^{2}\right)>\Lambda_{1}(M) .$$
Refined extension operators on shrinking cross-caps and handles force a limiting first-eigenfunction map with vanishing differential at the attachment point, contradicting the constant-energy identity for such an eigenmap. This theorem settles existence for $i=1$, but it does not give the sharp value, uniqueness, or a corresponding result for all higher eigenvalues. These results do not replace Korevaar's universal bound, but they make the sharp version of the problem a question about compactness, bubbling, conical singularities, and eigenmap geometry.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Determine the optimal universal constant in Korevaar's inequality, or the optimal asymptotic constant, for each fixed genus.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. For each closed surface and each $k$, describe whether the sharp supremum of $\lambda_{k}$ area is attained by a smooth metric, by a metric with conical singularities, or only by a degenerating sequence. For $k=1$, existence of a conical maximizer is now known for every closed surface [KPS].
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend the sharp higher-eigenvalue theory from $\mathbb{S}^{2}$ to surfaces of positive genus and to nonorientable surfaces.
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Clarify the best possible linear dependence on $g$ and $k$ in upper bounds for $\lambda_{k}$ area.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Relate optimal metrics for combinations of eigenvalues to minimal eigenmaps into spheres or ellipsoids, and determine which topological types admit smooth maximizers [Pe, KKMS].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Develop a compactification of the space of extremal metrics for surface eigenvalues in which bubbling limits and smooth extremal metrics appear as different boundary strata.
\end{itemize}

Problem 72
Study the discrete spectrum of a complete manifold whose curvature is bounded and negative and whose volume is finite. When is it nonempty? What is its asymptotic behavior and relation to the closed geodesics?

Let $M=\left\{(x, y) \in \mathbb{R}^{2} \mid y>0\right\} / \Gamma$, where $\Gamma$ is a congruence subgroup of $\operatorname{SL}(2, \mathbb{Z})$. It is an old conjecture that $\lambda_{1}$ for $M$ is at least $\frac{1}{4}$. Selberg [Se] proved that $\lambda_{1} \geqslant \frac{3}{16}$.

It will also be important to study the continuous spectrum of a general complete manifold with finite volume. Hopefully, one can obtain some kind of $L^{2}$ index theorem for elliptic operators for these manifolds.

Status. Open in this generality, with substantial partial results in locally symmetric and arithmetic cases. For congruence quotients of the upper half-plane, Selberg's $\lambda_{1} \geqslant 1 / 4$ conjecture remains open. Selberg proved the classical lower bound $\lambda_{1} \geqslant 3 / 16$ [Se], and the best known general bound toward the conjecture is the Kim-Sarnak bound $\lambda_{1} \geqslant 975 / 4096$ [KS], following earlier progress of Luo-Rudnick-Sarnak [LRS].

There are two intertwined issues. First, in finite-volume hyperbolic surfaces and more general cusp manifolds, the Laplacian has continuous spectrum as well as possible discrete spectrum. Thus one cannot usually study the asymptotics of the discrete spectrum in isolation from the continuous spectrum and the scattering matrix. Second, embedded eigenvalues can be unstable under perturbation and may deform into resonances; this phenomenon is already visible for cofinite hyperbolic surfaces in the work of Phillips-Sarnak [PhS].

For this reason, resonances are often the more stable replacement for the discrete spectrum in noncompact finite-volume spectral geometry. They encode scattering poles and are tied to trace formulae, closed geodesics, and the analytic continuation of the resolvent. General finite-volume negatively curved manifolds with bounded curvature do not yet have a single clean theory parallel to the compact Weyl law; the correct formulation depends on cusp geometry, scattering theory, and the choice
of operator. The hoped-for $L^{2}$ index theorem is also part of a broader program: strong results are known in special settings, but the fully geometric variable-curvature finite-volume formulation remains a natural open direction.

For general finite-volume negative curvature, Lott's analysis remains one of the important geometric references: for pinched finite-volume manifolds he proves that every space of $L^{2}$ harmonic $p$-forms is finite dimensional, and the essential spectrum of the $p$-form Laplacian can be described through ordinary differential operators associated to the ends, and examples show that gaps can occur even for metrics with curvature pinched close to -1 [Lo]. On the geometric side, Nguyen Phan constructed finite-volume negatively curved manifolds with unexpected cusp and fundamental-group behavior, illustrating why a single locally symmetric model is too restrictive for the general problem [NP]. On the index-theory side, Albin-Rochon proved families index formulae for manifolds with hyperbolic cusp singularities, extending earlier fibered cusp methods and giving a natural framework for the $L^{2}$ index direction in cusp geometries [AR].

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Prove Selberg's eigenvalue conjecture $\lambda_{1} \geqslant 1 / 4$ for congruence subgroups of $\operatorname{SL}(2, \mathbb{Z})$.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Develop a Weyl-type asymptotic or trace formula for resonances on finite-volume negatively curved manifolds with variable curvature and controlled cusp geometry.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine which embedded eigenvalues of finite-volume negatively curved manifolds are stable under geometric perturbation, and describe precisely how unstable eigenvalues become resonances.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Formulate and prove an $L^{2}$ index theorem for broad classes of finite-volume complete manifolds with bounded negative curvature.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify how cusp geometry controls essential spectrum and spectral gaps for the $p$-form Laplacian in variable negative curvature [Lo, NP].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Find a geometric condition on the cusps under which the resonance counting function, the closed geodesic data, and the $L^{2}$ index contribution fit into one uniform trace formula.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 73}

The behavior of the spectrum of a compact manifold of negative curvature is quite different for dimension two and dimension three. For example, R. Schoen $[\mathrm{S}]$ proved that for a three-dimensional hyperbolic space form (with curvature -1 ), $\lambda_{1} \geqslant \frac{c}{\operatorname{vol}(M)^{2}}$ where $c$ is a universal constant. This is certainly false for surfaces (See Schoen-Wolpert-Yau [SWY]).

Is it true that $\lambda_{1} \operatorname{vol}(M)^{2}$ has an upper bound if $M$ is a three-dimensional hyperbolic space?
Status. False in general for compact hyperbolic three-manifolds. Schoen's estimate gives a universal lower bound of order $\operatorname{vol}(M)^{-2}$ [S], but there are towers of compact arithmetic hyperbolic three-manifolds with volume tending to infinity and a uniform positive spectral gap. Along such a tower, $\lambda_{1} \operatorname{vol}(M)^{2}$ tends to infinity, so no universal upper bound of the proposed form can hold.

The contrast with surfaces is important. For compact hyperbolic surfaces, degeneration in moduli space can force many small eigenvalues, and Schoen-Wolpert-Yau gave geometric bounds for low eigenvalues in terms of the topology and short geodesics [SWY]. In dimension three, Mostow rigidity removes Teichmüller-type deformations of a fixed finite-volume hyperbolic structure, and arithmetic congruence towers give a different phenomenon: the volume can grow while the first positive eigenvalue stays uniformly bounded away from zero. Uniform spectral gaps for congruence covers follow from the representation-theoretic theory of automorphic forms and property $\tau$; see Burger-Sarnak [BS], Clozel [Cl], and the effective treatment of Kelmer-Silberman [KS].

The nonarithmetic case remains much less rigid from the viewpoint of available estimates. It is still natural to ask for geometric upper and lower bounds for $\lambda_{1}$ in terms of volume, diameter, Cheeger constants, Heegaard or congruence data, or the topology of finite covers. The Schoen-Yau scalar-curvature estimate [SY] belongs to a related but different circle of questions, where lower scalar curvature and minimal-surface methods control geometric concentration.

Recent work suggests two useful refinements of this discussion. First, Benjamini-Schramm convergence has become a natural language for large volume locally symmetric spaces: Abert-Bergeron-Biringer-Gelander- Nikolov-Raimbault-Samet developed the framework for asymptotic $L^{2}$-invariants [ABBGNRS], and Fraczyk-Hurtado-Raimbault recently proved Benjamini-Schramm convergence for arithmetic locally symmetric spaces in broad generality [FHR]. Second, spectral gaps in dimension three also interact with topology beyond functions: Abdurrahman-Adve- Giri-Lowe-Zung constructed closed hyperbolic integer homology spheres with volume tending to infinity and a uniform spectral gap for coclosed one-forms [AAGLZ]. These results do not restore the false upper bound for $\lambda_{1} \operatorname{vol}(M)^{2}$, but they point to refined questions involving towers, local convergence, homology, and spectra of differential forms.

A complementary result gives an upper bound of a quite different kind. Bonifacio-Mazáč-Pal combined associativity identities for products of Laplace eigensections with the Selberg trace formula and rigorous linear programming to prove
$$\lambda_{1}(M)<47.32$$
for every closed hyperbolic three-manifold [BMP]. This universal bound on $\lambda_{1}$ is compatible with the negative answer: after multiplication by $\operatorname{vol}(M)^{2}$, congruence towers with a uniform positive gap still make the original quantity diverge.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Characterize which towers of closed hyperbolic three-manifolds have a uniform positive lower bound for $\lambda_{1}$.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine sharp upper and lower bounds for $\lambda_{1}$ in nonarithmetic towers, especially under geometric convergence or Benjamini-Schramm convergence assumptions.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine the optimal universal upper bound for $\lambda_{1}$ on closed hyperbolic three-manifolds and sharpen the trace-formula bound 47.32 [BMP].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Relate spectral gap quantitatively to Heegaard genus, Cheeger constant, injectivity radius distribution, and the topology of covers.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Find analogues of the Schoen-Wolpert-Yau low-eigenvalue description for degenerating sequences of hyperbolic three-manifolds.
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Compare function spectral gaps with coclosed one-form spectral gaps and torsionhomology growth in large-volume hyperbolic three-manifold towers [AAGLZ].
\item[7.] AI-generated formulation. Separate the possible growth rates of $\lambda_{1} \operatorname{vol}(M)^{2}$ into arithmetic, congruence, and nonarithmetic classes, and decide which rates are realizable.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 74}

Let $M$ be a compact surface. Let $\lambda_{1} \leqslant \lambda_{2} \leqslant \cdots$ be the spectrum of $M$ and $\left\{\phi_{i}\right\}$ be the corresponding eigenfunctions. For each $i$, the nodal set $\left\{x \mid \phi_{i}(x)=0\right\}$ is a one-dimensional rectifiable simplicial complex. Let $L_{i}$ be the length of such a set. It is not difficult to prove that $\liminf _{i \rightarrow \infty} L_{i} / \sqrt{\lambda_{i}}$ has a positive lower bound depending only on the area of $M$. (This was independently observed by Brüning [B].)

It seems more difficult to find an upper bound of $\limsup _{i \rightarrow \infty} L_{i} / \sqrt{\lambda_{i}}$.
Status. Partially solved. The lower bound $L_{i} \geqslant c \sqrt{\lambda_{i}}$ is now known for smooth compact manifolds in all dimensions by Logunov [Lo2]. The corresponding sharp upper bound $L_{i} \leqslant C \sqrt{\lambda_{i}}$ is solved for real analytic metrics by Donnelly-Fefferman [DF1], but remains open for general smooth surfaces. Thus the stated upper-bound problem for smooth compact surfaces is still open.

The early surface theory begins with Brüning's lower bound [B] and Dong's analysis of nodal sets on Riemann surfaces [D]. Donnelly and Fefferman proved Yau's conjectural upper and lower bounds in the real analytic case [DF1], and obtained general smooth-surface upper bounds which are weaker than the conjectural order [DF2]. In the convention where the eigenvalue is written as $\lambda^{2}$, the conjectural length bound is $O(\lambda)$, while the classical smooth bound was of larger order.

The modern breakthrough concerns lower bounds and quantitative unique continuation. Colding-Minicozzi and Sogge-Zelditch obtained important lower bounds in higher dimensions [CM, SoZ]. Logunov then proved Nadirashvili's conjecture and the lower bound in Yau's conjecture for smooth compact manifolds [Lo2], and also established polynomial upper bounds for smooth nodal sets [Lo1]. In dimensions two and three, Logunov-Malinnikova obtained refined estimates; in dimension two they improved the known smooth upper exponent to $H^{1}(\{u=0\}) \leqslant C \lambda^{3 / 4-\beta}$ when $\Delta u+\lambda u=0$ [LM]. This is still not the conjectural $C \lambda^{1 / 2}$ upper bound.

Two related literatures help clarify what the sharp bound should mean. Logunov-Malinnikova's review emphasizes that the smooth upper bound is still the missing half of Yau's conjecture and that two-dimensional methods are tied to doubling indices and propagation of smallness [LM2]. Random-wave models and arithmetic random waves typically have nodal length of order $\sqrt{\lambda}$, with refined fluctuation theory surveyed by Rossi [Ro]; this supports the expected order but does not provide a deterministic upper bound. Results on quantum ergodic sequences, such as Hezari's improvements for inner radii of nodal domains under small-scale equidistribution [He], show that dynamical assumptions can improve some nodal-domain geometry along density-one subsequences, but they do not yet give the full sharp nodal-length upper bound.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Prove the sharp upper bound $H^{1}\left(\left\{\phi_{i}=0\right\}\right) \leqslant C \sqrt{\lambda_{i}}$ for smooth compact surfaces.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether additional geometric assumptions, such as negative curvature or quantum ergodicity, imply sharp upper bounds for a full-density subsequence of eigenfunctions.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Find the optimal smooth upper exponent for nodal length on a compact surface if the full Yau upper bound remains out of reach.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Understand the distributional limits of nodal length measures for special eigenfunction sequences on smooth surfaces.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Compare deterministic doubling-index methods with random-wave predictions and quantum ergodic subsequence estimates [LM2, Ro, He].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Develop a scale-by-scale doubling-index criterion which is both necessary and sufficient for the conjectural upper nodal-length bound on smooth surfaces.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 75}
S.-Y. Cheng [C] proved that for a compact surface, the multiplicity of $\lambda_{i}$ has an upper bound depending only on the genus of the surface. Can one generalize this to higher dimensions? Most likely this is not true without modification. What is the correct statement? For a compact surface with fixed genus $g$, can one exhibit explicitly a metric for which $\lambda_{i}$ has maximal multiplicity?

Status. Partially resolved and partly false in the naive higher-dimensional form. On compact surfaces, Cheng's nodal set argument gives multiplicity bounds in terms of the genus and the eigenvalue index [C], with later refinements and extensions to Schrödinger operators and singular or bordered surfaces [Ko, Be]. In dimensions at least three, Colin de Verdière's prescribed finite spectrum theorem shows that no bound for the multiplicity of the $j$-th eigenvalue can depend only on topology and $j$ [CdV].

The two-dimensional result is special because the nodal set of an eigenfunction is a graph on the surface. Euler characteristic and Courant's nodal domain theorem then constrain the possible order of vanishing and hence the dimension of an eigenspace. This is the mechanism behind Cheng's theorem and its later improvements. For example, Berdnikov-Nadirashvili-Penskoi proved the refined estimate
$$m\left(\mathbb{R} \mathbb{P}^{2}, g, \lambda_{2 \ell}\right) \leqslant 4 \ell+1$$
and analogous bounds for bordered and Steklov problems [BNP].
In higher dimensions the topology of nodal sets is too flexible for this argument to survive. Colin de Verdière proved that on any closed manifold of dimension at least three one can prescribe an arbitrary finite part of the Laplace spectrum, with multiplicities, by choosing a suitable smooth metric [CdV]. Thus the correct higher-dimensional replacement must impose extra geometric conditions such as curvature bounds, diameter or volume normalization, analytic metrics, locally symmetric structure, or restrictions to a fixed geometric class.

For fixed surfaces, the explicit extremal-metric problem remains interesting. It is related to the theory of extremal metrics for Laplace eigenvalues, minimal immersions by eigenfunctions, and degeneration by bubbling. The modern variational theory for maximizing eigenvalues on surfaces, including work of Nadirashvili, Sire, Petrides, and Karpukhin-Nadirashvili-Penskoi-Polterovich, gives a natural setting in which high multiplicities can occur [KNPP]. There is a useful logical distinction here. For a fixed surface and index, Cheng-type bounds imply that the possible multiplicities form a nonempty bounded set of integers, so some smooth metric realizes the largest integer. What remains difficult in Yau's question is to find such a metric explicitly and to determine the sharp integer.

Recent spectral-optimization work adds two useful refinements. Stability results of Karpukhin-Nahon-Polterovich-Stern show that metrics nearly maximizing the first or second normalized eigenvalue on several surfaces must be close, in a weak Sobolev sense, to the corresponding maximizing measures [KNPS]. The existence theory of Petrides for broad combinations of eigenvalues on orientable surfaces relates optimal metrics to minimal eigenmaps into ellipsoids [Pe]. These results suggest that maximal multiplicity should be studied together with extremal metrics, stability, and the representation theory of the eigenmap target, not only through nodal graphs. Karpukhin-Petrides-Stern have now proved that every closed surface has a metric maximizing the normalized first eigenvalue, smooth up to finitely many conical points [KPS]. This supplies a canonical extremal eigenmap on every topology, but it does not assert that the first-eigenvalue multiplicity is maximal, nor does it treat arbitrary index.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Determine sharp multiplicity bounds for the $k$-th Laplace eigenvalue on a closed surface of fixed genus.
\item[2.] Original direction. For each genus and index, classify the smooth metrics which achieve maximal multiplicity, and determine whether extremal-eigenvalue or eigenmap methods select any of them.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Find explicit metrics realizing maximal multiplicity when such maximizers exist.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Formulate higher-dimensional multiplicity bounds under natural geometric hypotheses, for example bounded curvature, bounded diameter, or locally symmetric structure.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Relate maximal multiplicity to extremal eigenvalue metrics, minimal eigenmaps, and stability of maximizers [KNPP, KNPS, Pe].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Classify the geometric mechanisms which force high multiplicity on surfaces, separating symmetry, minimal-immersion, and degeneration mechanisms.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 76}

Let $M$ be a compact manifold and denote by $f_{i}, i=1,2, \ldots$, the eigenfunctions for the Laplacian on $M$. Show that the number of critical points of $f_{i}$ is increasing with $i$.

Status. False in this generality. The literal monotonicity statement is not a valid general theorem. The number of critical points depends on the choice of eigenbasis inside eigenspaces, on degeneracy of critical points, and on the regularity of the metric. Moreover, Jakobson-Nadirashvili constructed metrics on $\mathbb{T}^{2}$ with a sequence of eigenfunctions whose eigenvalues tend to infinity but which have only 16 critical points [JN]. Thus no general theorem can say that the number of critical points must increase to infinity along every high-energy sequence.

The problem should therefore be reformulated. A first issue is regularity: for smooth metrics, critical sets can behave pathologically unless one imposes nondegeneracy or analyticity. Buhovsky-Logunov- Sodin constructed a metric on the twotorus for which infinitely many eigenfunctions have infinitely many isolated critical points [BLS]. For real analytic metrics, critical sets are more rigid, and Bakri proved an upper bound for the Hausdorff measure of critical sets of eigenfunctions on compact analytic manifolds [Ba]. This is closer in spirit to Donnelly-Fefferman type estimates for nodal sets.

Another related circle concerns eigenfunction growth. Sogge's $L^{p}$ and sup-norm estimates give general upper bounds for eigenfunctions [So], and the sharpness of maximal growth is tied to geodesic recurrence and focal behavior. Sogge-Zelditch and later work show that saturation of maximal sup-norm growth is geometrically restricted [SoZ]. Toth-Zelditch studied uniformly bounded eigenfunctions in completely integrable settings and showed strong rigidity for flat tori under additional hypotheses [TZ]. These results do not count critical points directly, but they help identify geometric situations where eigenfunctions are unusually concentrated or unusually regular.

Two more recent viewpoints sharpen the reformulated problem. Hofmann and Täufer use the graph structure of nodal sets on compact orientable surfaces to bound critical points lying on the nodal set and sums of vanishing orders in terms of the eigenvalue index and the topology [HT]. This does not count all critical points of an eigenfunction, but it gives a concrete topological replacement for the false monotonicity statement. A complementary probabilistic model is provided by random plane waves: Beliaev-Cammarota-Wigman computed one- and two-point statistics for critical points of the random plane wave [BCW]. These results suggest that for generic or chaotic systems one should ask for asymptotic laws, local statistics, and fluctuation estimates rather than deterministic monotonicity.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. For real analytic metrics, obtain sharp upper bounds for the number of isolated critical points, or for the complexity of the critical set, in terms of the eigenvalue.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Under generic metric assumptions, determine whether eigenfunctions have nondegenerate critical points and whether their number satisfies almost-sure or density-one asymptotic laws.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify compact manifolds or metrics which admit infinitely many eigenfunctions with uniformly bounded numbers of critical points.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Relate critical-point counts to nodal geometry, doubling indices, and eigenfunction concentration.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Bound critical points on the nodal set, and the total vanishing order there, using nodal-graph methods on surfaces [HT].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Replace the false monotonicity question by a stable invariant: define a regularized critical complexity of an eigenspace and ask whether its average has a Weyl-law asymptotic.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 77}

Let $\Omega$ be a bounded domain in $\mathbb{R}^{2}$. Denote by $\lambda_{1}(\Omega)$ and $\lambda_{2}(\Omega)$ the first and second (nonzero) eigenvalues of the Laplacian for functions with zero boundary values. Show
$$\frac{\lambda_{2}(\Omega)}{\lambda_{1}(\Omega)} \leqslant \frac{\lambda_{2}(D)}{\lambda_{1}(D)}$$
where $D$ is the disk in $\mathbb{R}^{2}$, and that equality implies $\Omega$ is a disk.
This will mean that one can determine whether the drum is circular or not by knowing the first two tones of the drum. For more details, see [PPW].

Status. Solved. This is the Payne-Pólya-Weinberger conjecture for the first two Dirichlet eigenvalues, and it was proved sharply by Ashbaugh-Benguria [AB]. The equality case is rigid: equality holds only for the disk in the planar case, and more generally for the ball in Euclidean space.

The historical development begins with the universal eigenvalue inequalities of Payne-Pólya-Weinberger [PPW], who conjectured that the ratio $\lambda_{2} / \lambda_{1}$ for the Dirichlet Laplacian is maximized by the disk. Ashbaugh-Benguria proved the conjecture by a symmetrization argument combined with delicate Bessel-function monotonicity estimates [AB]. Their result is sharp and gives a spectral way to recognize circularity from the first two Dirichlet tones.

The theorem belongs to the broader family of spectral isoperimetric inequalities, alongside the Faber-Krahn theorem for $\lambda_{1}$ and the Szegó-Weinberger theorem for the first nonzero Neumann eigenvalue. Ashbaugh-Benguria also proved a spherical analogue for domains contained in a hemisphere [AB2]. Variants for other boundary conditions, other ambient spaces, and higher eigenvalue ratios remain active sources of problems.

Recent work illustrates how the original solved theorem continues to generate new problems. In the Robin setting, Dai-Sun recently proved a sharp comparison for the ratio of the first two Robin eigenvalues under an interior-sphere hypothesis and an additional distribution-radius condition on the first eigenfunction [DS]. Equality is rigid and their theorem applies for every positive Robin parameter, but these extra hypotheses mean that the unrestricted Payne-Schaefer analogue is not settled by that result. In a different direction, Eddaoudi studied PPW-type inequalities on closed Riemannian manifolds of dimension at least three, with bounds involving scalar curvature, the Yamabe constant, isoperimetric constants, and conformal volume [E]. These developments suggest that the original disk theorem is best viewed as the model case for a family of ratio inequalities whose sharp form depends strongly on boundary condition and ambient geometry.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Find sharp analogues of the PPW ratio inequality for other boundary conditions, such as Robin or mixed boundary conditions, with full equality and stability statements.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine optimal spectral-isoperimetric inequalities for higher ratios $\lambda_{k+1} / \lambda_{k}$ or for finite collections of low Dirichlet eigenvalues.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Prove quantitative stability: if $\lambda_{2}(\Omega) / \lambda_{1}(\Omega)$ is close to the disk value, how close is $\Omega$ to a disk in a geometric metric?
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend sharp ratio inequalities to domains in curved ambient surfaces or manifolds under natural curvature hypotheses.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine the sharp Robin, mixed, and curved-space analogues of the Ashbaugh-Benguria comparison, including removal of the extra geometric and first-eigenfunction hypotheses in the current Robin theorem [DS].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Develop a unified "two-tone rigidity" theory comparing how much of the shape of a domain is detected by the first two eigenvalues under different boundary conditions.
\end{itemize}

\section*{References.}

[AB2] M. S. Ashbaugh and R. D. Benguria, A sharp bound for the ratio of the first two Dirichlet eigenvalues of a domain in a hemisphere of $\mathbb{S}^{n}$, arXiv:math/0008088.
[DS] G. Dai and Y. Sun, A sharp bound for the ratio of the first two eigenvalues of Robin Laplacian, arXiv:2511.20988.
[E] M. Eddaoudi, Payne-Pólya-Weinberger inequalities on closed Riemannian manifolds, arXiv:2503.20181.

\section*{Problem 78}

Let $\Omega$ be a bounded convex domain in $\mathbb{R}^{2}$. Let $f_{2}$ be the second eigenfunction for the Laplacian with zero boundary conditions. Show that the nodal line of $f_{2}$ cannot enclose a compact sub-region of $\Omega$.

In general, one likes to know the qualitative behavior of the nodal line. This conjecture has been around for a long time.
Status. Solved for bounded convex planar domains. This is the convex-domain case of Payne's nodal line conjecture for the second Dirichlet eigenfunction. Lin proved important special cases and structural results [L], and Melas proved that for a bounded convex domain in $\mathbb{R}^{2}$ the nodal line of a second Dirichlet eigenfunction meets the boundary and hence cannot enclose a compact subdomain [M].

The convexity assumption is essential for the known positive theorem. The broader form of Payne's conjecture, asserting that the nodal line of the second Dirichlet eigenfunction must meet the boundary for every bounded planar domain, is false. Hoffmann-Ostenhof-Hoffmann-Ostenhof- Nadirashvili constructed planar domains with holes for which the second nodal line is closed [HHN]. More recent work has reduced the number of holes needed for such counterexamples [DGH].

The counterexample side has recently become sharper. Freitas and Leylekian constructed bounded planar domains with a single hole for which the nodal line of a second Dirichlet eigenfunction is closed and does not touch the boundary [FL]. This shows that if Payne's conjecture has a surviving general form, it must live at the level of simply connected or quantitatively near-convex domains. On the positive and quantitative side, Beck-Canzani-Marzuola studied nodal curves of low Dirichlet eigenfunctions in generalized curvilinear quadrilaterals, proving curvature and boundary-intersection estimates for the second nodal curve in model geometries [BCM]. These results move the problem from mere boundary-touching toward quantitative control of the nodal line.

Bobkov extended a complementary positive direction to the nonlinear $p$-Laplacian: on circularly symmetric domains in arbitrary dimension, moving polarization forces the nodal set of a second eigenfunction to meet the boundary under explicit symmetry and regularity assumptions [Bo]. This does not settle the remaining simply connected planar case, but it identifies a mechanism that does not depend on linearity.

Thus the qualitative picture is now split into three directions: convex domains, where the theorem is true; general multiply connected domains, where it is false; and intermediate geometric classes, where one asks how much convexity, symmetry, or simple connectivity is needed to force the nodal line to hit the boundary.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Determine the weakest geometric assumptions on a simply connected planar domain which force the nodal line of the second Dirichlet eigenfunction to meet the boundary.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify multiply connected planar domains which admit a closed second nodal line.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Prove quantitative versions for convex domains: estimate where the second nodal line hits the boundary and how its geometry depends on the shape of the domain.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend the convex-domain theorem, or identify counterexamples, for higher-dimensional convex domains and for other boundary conditions.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Decide whether a simply connected counterexample exists, or prove that simple connectivity plus a quantitative geometric hypothesis rules out closed second nodal lines [FL].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Define a convexity defect which controls whether Payne's nodal-line conclusion remains true, and seek a sharp threshold for this defect.
\end{itemize}

\section*{References.}

[Bo] V. Bobkov, On the nodal set conjecture for the p-Laplacian in circularly symmetric domains, arXiv:2602.01210.
[DGH] J. Dahne, J. Gómez-Serrano and K. Hou, A counterexample to Payne's nodal line conjecture with few holes, arXiv:2103.02276.

\section*{Problem 79}

Let $M$ be a compact manifold without boundary. Then we can define the eigenvalues for the Laplacian acting on the differential forms. How can we estimate the first nonzero eigenvalue in terms of computable geometric quantities? See Li [L], Li-Yau [LY] and the recent work of Gromov $[\mathrm{G}]$ where the estimates of $\lambda_{n}$ on functions depend on the diameter of $M$ and a lower bound for the Ricci curvature. See also the paper of Uhlenbrock [U].

Status. Partially solved. For functions, the problem is now well understood under the standard assumptions of a lower Riccicurvature bound and a diameter bound. For the Hodge Laplacian on differential forms, the corresponding sharp theory is not complete in this generality; the estimates depend on the degree of the form, on the harmonic part determined by cohomology, and on curvature terms which are stronger than scalar function estimates.

For functions, Cheng's eigenvalue comparison gave the fundamental upper comparison estimates, while Li [L] and Li-Yau [LY] developed lower estimates for the first nonzero eigenvalue in terms of lower Ricci curvature and diameter. Gromov's work on Lévy-Gromov isoperimetry [G] placed these estimates in the broader comparison geometry framework. In the case Ric $\geqslant 0$, Zhong and Yang [ZY] proved the sharp lower bound
$$\lambda_{1}(M) \geqslant \frac{\pi^{2}}{D^{2}},$$
where $D$ is the diameter. Later work on sharp moduli of continuity and the fundamental gap, especially Andrews-Clutterbuck [AC, AC2], clarified the sharp one-dimensional model mechanism behind these estimates.

For differential forms, the first point is that the zero eigenspace of the Hodge Laplacian consists of harmonic forms, hence is governed by de Rham cohomology. Thus one must estimate the first positive eigenvalue after removing the harmonic forms. The Bochner-Weitzenbock formula then involves curvature operators acting on $\Lambda^{p} T^{*} M$, not only the Ricci tensor appearing in the function case. Classical work of Gallot-Meyer [GM] gives estimates under curvature-operator positivity assumptions, and Uhlenbrock's work [U] belongs to the same analytic circle through Kato-type inequalities and spectral distribution estimates. On the other hand, spectral prescription results for Hodge Laplacians, such as those of Jammes [J], show that without appropriate geometric hypotheses one should not expect a simple topological or dimensional bound.

Recent work makes this boundary more precise. Bhattacharya-Maity- Tiwari announced uniform Hodge-eigenvalue bounds under lower Ricci curvature, positive injectivity radius, and bounded diameter, but that preprint was withdrawn after an error was found in Proposition 3.1 and is therefore not used as evidence here. Huang-Wang treat the rough Laplacian on 1-forms and show both why a direct Li-Yau type estimate can fail and how lower bounds can be recovered after adding curvature-integrability and topological assumptions such as nonzero Euler characteristic [HW]. Huang-Zhang then obtained explicit rough and Hodge 1-form estimates under almost-nonnegative curvature-operator hypotheses and nonvanishing Euler characteristic [HZ]. Their argument combines Schrödinger operator estimates with a twisted Hodge-Dirac operator whose index is $\chi(M)$. For coexact 1-forms, Rudd proved Cheeger-type lower bounds in terms of stable filling or stable commutator-length constants by studying the magnetic geodesic flow of an eigenform [Ru]. These results support the view that the form-valued problem should be split into Hodge, rough, exact, coexact, and connection-Laplacian versions, each with its own natural geometric hypotheses.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Find sharp lower bounds for the first positive Hodge Laplacian eigenvalue on $p$-forms under natural lower curvature and diameter assumptions.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine precisely which estimates for forms require curvature-operator bounds and which can be obtained from lower Ricci curvature plus non-collapsing hypotheses.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Separate the estimates for exact and coexact forms, especially in degrees 1 and $n-1$, where comparison with the scalar Laplacian and the Hodge star gives extra structure.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Study rigidity and stability: if equality, or near equality, holds in a sharp lower bound for forms, must the manifold be close to a one-dimensional or symmetric model?
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Find the weakest noncollapsing and curvature-integrability assumptions under which uniform estimates for Hodge and rough Laplacians on 1-forms hold [HW, HZ], and relate the coexact gap to computable filling invariants [Ru].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Is there a Zhong-Yang type sharp diameter lower bound for the first positive exact 1-form eigenvalue under Ric $\geqslant 0$, or can one construct a counterexample showing that such a bound is not the right formulation?
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 80}
(The Schiffer conjecture, or Pompeiu problem). Let $\Omega$ be a smooth bounded domain in $\mathbb{R}^{2}$ with compact closure. Suppose there exists an $f$ which is an eigenfunction for the Laplacian with Neumann boundary conditions. If also $f$ is constant on the boundary of $\Omega$, prove that $\Omega$ is a disk.

This problem is relevant to the following classical problem:
Given a function $f$ defined on $\mathbb{R}^{2}$, and a bounded domain $\Omega$, if one knows the value of the integral of $f$ over all images of $\Omega$ under Euclidean motions of the plane, can the function $f$ be recovered?

Status. Open for smooth bounded simply connected planar domains in the stated Schiffer form. The disk is the model case. The full Pompeiu-Schiffer conjecture is still unresolved, although many special cases and related variants are known.

Pompeiu's original problem is an integral geometry problem: if a nonzero function has zero integral over every rigid motion of a fixed domain, must the domain be a ball? The Schiffer formulation is the corresponding overdetermined Neumann eigenvalue problem: a nonconstant Neumann eigenfunction is also constant on the boundary. Fourier transform methods relate the failure of the Pompeiu property to the zero set of the Fourier transform of the indicator function of the domain; in the smooth contractible setting this is equivalent to the Schiffer conjecture. The work of Brown-Schreiber-Taylor [BST] and Williams [W] established important early structural results and proved the Pompeiu property for large non-spherical classes such as polytopal regions.

In the planar smooth case, the problem remains difficult because the boundary condition is overdetermined but the spectral parameter is not fixed in advance. Deng [De] proved disk rigidity under low-eigenvalue or symmetry assumptions. Ramm [Ra] gives a useful modern account of the equivalent formulations and known reductions. Liu [Li] connects Schiffer's conjecture with interior transmission eigenvalues and invisibility cloaking. Recent work continues to clarify the boundary between the original conjecture and nearby problems: for example, polytopal regions have the Pompeiu property by Fourier-zero arguments [MR, KS], while the classical simply connected hypothesis cannot be discarded: Enciso-Fernández-Ruiz-Sicbaldi constructed analytic noncircular doubly connected domains with a nonradial Neumann eigenfunction locally constant on the boundary [EFRS]. Their local bifurcation starts from radial eigenfunctions on annuli and separates this Schiffer-type counterexample from the still-open simply connected conjecture.

One should also distinguish this classical overdetermined-eigenvalue problem from the Schiffer problem in inverse scattering. In that nearby setting, Hongyu Liu recently proved an almost-sure uniqueness result for determining an obstacle from a single far-field measurement [HL]. This does not solve the deterministic Pompeiu-Schiffer conjecture stated here, but it shows that probabilistic or generic versions of Schiffer-type inverse problems may be more accessible than the full rigidity problem.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Prove Schiffer's conjecture for smooth simply connected planar domains without extra symmetry or eigenvalue-position assumptions.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether analyticity, convexity, or finite-type assumptions on the boundary are sufficient to force disk rigidity.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify smooth bounded domains whose Fourier transform of the indicator function vanishes on a complex sphere centered at the origin.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Understand which multiply connected Schiffer-type formulations should have disks and annuli as the only solutions, and which admit genuine counterexamples.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Compare the deterministic Pompeiu-Schiffer conjecture with almost-sure or generic uniqueness versions in inverse scattering [HL].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Develop a quantitative stability theorem: if a Neumann eigenfunction is nearly constant on the boundary, is the domain close to a disk in a geometric norm?
\end{itemize}
