\section*{V. Problems Related to Geodesics}

\section*{Problem 81}

Prove that every compact manifold $M$ has an infinite number of closed geodesics.
This is an old problem. Klingenberg has studied this extensively, and has obtained many deep results. See his book [Kl] for the case where $\pi_{1}(M)$ is finite.

Status. Open in full generality, if "infinite" means infinitely many geometrically distinct closed geodesics. The existence of at least one closed geodesic on every compact Riemannian manifold was proved by Lyusternik-Fet [LF].

The variational formulation regards closed geodesics as critical points of the energy functional on the free loop space. Gromoll and Meyer [GM] proved that a closed Riemannian manifold has infinitely many geometrically distinct closed geodesics if the Betti numbers of its free loop space are unbounded. Vigué-Poirrier and Sullivan [VPS], using Sullivan's minimal model theory [S], gave a powerful topological criterion: for simply connected closed manifolds whose rational cohomology algebra is not generated by one element, the free-loop-space homology is large enough to force infinitely many closed geodesics.

Other major cases were settled by dynamical and low-dimensional methods. Bangert-Hingston [BH] proved strong results for manifolds with infinite abelian fundamental group. For $\mathbb{S}^{2}$, the classical three-geodesics theorem gives at least three simple closed geodesics, and the combined work of Franks, Bangert, and Hingston [B, F, H] implies infinitely many closed geodesics for every Riemannian 2-sphere. Nevertheless, the general case of compact manifolds, especially rationally elliptic spaces with cohomology generated by one element such as spheres and projective spaces in higher dimensions, remains a central open problem.

Recent progress continues to enlarge the solved classes rather than closing the general problem. Rademacher-Taimanov proved growth results for closed geodesics on connected sums and applied them to compact 3-manifolds with infinite fundamental group; in particular, for generic Riemannian metrics on compact 3-manifolds in that setting one obtains infinitely many geometrically distinct closed geodesics [RT]. De Philippis-Marini-Mazzucchelli-Suhr extended both the three-geodesics theorem and the Bangert-Franks-Hingston infinite-geodesics theorem from Riemannian 2-spheres to reversible Finsler 2-spheres [DMMS]. These results emphasize that the remaining hard cases are concentrated in simply connected or rationally elliptic higher-dimensional manifolds and in nonreversible Finsler settings.

Contreras-Mazzucchelli obtained a further generic result in 2025 [CM]. If a closed manifold of dimension at least two has nonzero first Betti number, then an open dense set of metrics in every $C^{k}$ topology, $2 \leqslant k \leqslant \infty$, has infinitely many closed geodesics of arbitrarily large length. Their mechanism is dynamical: an Aubry-Mather minimizing closed geodesic can be made hyperbolic with a transverse homoclinic by an arbitrarily $C^{\infty}$-small perturbation, producing a horseshoe; if no such minimizer exists, a theorem of Mañé already supplies infinitely many closed geodesics. This is strong generic progress, but it does not prove the conjecture for every metric.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Prove infinitely many geometrically distinct closed geodesics on every closed Riemannian sphere $\mathbb{S}^{n}$, $n \geqslant 3$.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Prove the conjecture for compact rank-one symmetric spaces, especially complex, quaternionic, and Cayley projective spaces.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Find sharp growth lower bounds for the number of closed geodesics of length at most $L$ under generic and nongeneric metrics.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Clarify which Finsler analogues remain true without reversibility assumptions.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend the Rademacher-Taimanov growth results from connected sums and 3-manifold prime decomposition to broader classes of manifolds with large fundamental group [RT].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether the Aubry-Mather/homoclinic mechanism of Contreras-Mazzucchelli can be made nonperturbative, or extended to manifolds with $b_{1}=0$ through finite covers or other minimizing classes [CM].
\item[7.] AI-generated formulation. Develop a quantitative Morse-theoretic criterion which detects when all high iterates of finitely many prime closed geodesics cannot account for the loop-space homology.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 82}

Let $M$ be a compact manifold without conjugate point. If $M$ is homotopically equivalent to the torus, prove that $M$ is flat.
This was conjectured by E. Hopf and proved by him for two-dimensional $M$. L. Green [G] has proved that the total scalar curvature of $M$ must be nonpositive, and is zero only if $M$ is flat. It is believable that the fundamental group of a compact manifold without conjugate point has exponential growth unless the manifold is flat.

Status. Solved. Burago and Ivanov proved in 1994 that every Riemannian metric without conjugate points on a torus is flat [BI].

The two-dimensional case was proved earlier by E. Hopf [H], following the Morse-Hedlund study of manifolds without conjugate points [MH]. Green [G] proved that in the compact case without conjugate points the total scalar curvature is nonpositive, with equality only in the flat case. Burago-Ivanov's proof introduced a higher-dimensional rigidity mechanism using minimal geodesics and stable norms for periodic metrics, and it settled the torus version of the Hopf problem completely.

The broader expectation, mentioned in the original problem, remains a major theme: compact manifolds without conjugate points should behave like nonpositively curved manifolds in many algebraic and dynamical ways, even though no curvature sign is assumed. Ivanov-Kapovitch [IK] proved structural restrictions on fundamental groups of closed manifolds without conjugate points, for instance virtual splitting properties of centralizers. Related rigidity questions also appear for cylinders, noncompact quotients, and Lorentzian analogues, where the Riemannian rigidity can fail [BE, Me].

Recent work on manifolds without conjugate points is less about the already solved torus theorem and more about the dynamical and group theoretic environment around it. For uniform visibility manifolds without conjugate points, Wu studied Patterson-Sullivan measures, measures of maximal entropy, equilibrium states, counting of free-homotopy classes containing closed geodesics, and rigidity features of the Margulis function [Wu]. Liu studied Myrberg limit sets and Bowen-Margulis-Sullivan measures in the same visibility setting [Li]. These results reinforce the idea that the correct extension of the Hopf-Burago-Ivanov theorem is not merely "flatness versus nonflatness," but a package of entropy, counting, boundary dynamics, and group-growth rigidity for manifolds without conjugate points.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Classify closed manifolds without conjugate points whose fundamental group has polynomial growth.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether a closed manifold without conjugate points and virtually abelian fundamental group must be finitely covered by a flat torus.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Prove entropy or marked-length-spectrum rigidity results for metrics without conjugate points under hypotheses weaker than nonpositive curvature.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Understand which group-theoretic properties of nonpositively curved manifolds extend to the no-conjugate-points setting.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Relate polynomial or subexponential group growth to entropy, Patterson-Sullivan theory, and closed-geodesic counting in visibility manifolds without conjugate points [Wu, Li].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Formulate a quantitative almost-flat theorem: if a torus has no conjugate points after a small controlled perturbation of the Jacobi equation, must the metric be close to flat?
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 83}

Prove the Blaschke conjecture for other symmetric spaces of rank one besides $\mathbb{S}^{N}$. For the sphere, this was established through the efforts of Green, Weinstein, Berger, Kazdan, and Yang (see [B] for the precise history of the problem).

Status. Open for the complex, quaternionic, and Cayley projective-space models. The metric Blaschke conjecture is solved for homology spheres and homology real projective spaces, but not for the other compact rank-one symmetric spaces [M].

The Blaschke problem asks for rigidity of compact manifolds whose injectivity radius equals their diameter, or equivalently in the classical setting, manifolds whose geodesics have the same strong cut-locus behavior as compact rank-one symmetric spaces. Besse's monograph [B] remains the standard source for the history and for the connection with manifolds all of whose geodesics are closed. The sphere and real-projective-space branches were proved through the sequence of works of Berger, Kazdan, Weinstein, and Yang. Yang's paper [Y] belongs to this sphere/Wiedersehen branch; it does not prove the complex, quaternionic, or Cayley projective cases asked for in the present problem.

In modern terminology, the broader Blaschke-manifold program is often stated as the classification of compact Riemannian manifolds with injectivity radius equal to diameter as compact rank-one symmetric spaces, up to scale. McKay's corrected survey [M] gives the essential status audit. A Blaschke manifold modelled on $\mathbb{C} P^{2}, \mathbb{H} P^{2}$, or $\mathbb{O} P^{2}$ is known to be diffeomorphic to its model, and in the $\mathbb{H} P^{n}$ case it is known to be homotopy equivalent to the model, but these topological conclusions do not identify the metric. The survey also records flaws in earlier claimed proofs for great sphere fibrations, so those claims cannot be used to mark the projective cases solved. Recent work studies nearby Besse and diameter-rigidity hypotheses, for example Besse projective spaces with many diameter-realizing directions [AV].

The recent Besse-manifold perspective gives a useful way to report progress on the problem. Blaschke manifolds are Besse, but the converse is false without additional diameter information. Adelstein and Vargas Pallete prove that Besse projective spaces with sufficiently many diameter-realizing directions are Blaschke [AV]. Thus one current direction is to replace the exact injectivity-radius-equals- diameter hypothesis by measurable or incidence-type assumptions on diameter-realizing geodesics, and then recover the Blaschke conclusion.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Prove that a Blaschke manifold modelled on $\mathbb{C} P^{n}, \mathbb{H} P^{n}$, or $\mathbb{O} P^{2}$ is isometric, after rescaling, to its compact rank-one symmetric model.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Upgrade the known diffeomorphism or homotopy classifications in the projective cases to metric rigidity without relying on the flawed great-sphere-fibration arguments identified by McKay [M].
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Develop quantitative stability: if $\operatorname{inj}(M, g)$ is close to $\operatorname{diam}(M, g)$, is $(M, g)$ close to a compact rank-one symmetric space?
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Study Finsler and low-regularity analogues of the Blaschke conjecture.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine the minimal "many diameters" condition under which a Besse projective space must be Blaschke [AV].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Classify Besse manifolds which satisfy a weakened "many diameters" condition, interpolating between Zoll metrics and Blaschke metrics.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 84}

Prove that a compact harmonic manifold is symmetric.
A manifold is defined to be harmonic if geodesic spheres of small radius have constant mean curvature (see [B]).

Status. Partially resolved in the formulation stated. Szabó proved the Lichnerowicz conjecture when the universal cover is compact, in particular for compact harmonic manifolds with finite fundamental group [Sz]. For a compact harmonic manifold whose universal cover is noncompact, symmetry is known under additional hypotheses such as no focal points or Gromov-hyperbolicity of the fundamental group [K], but the unrestricted compact-quotient case remains open [KP].

Harmonicity means that the volume density in geodesic polar coordinates, equivalently the mean curvature of sufficiently small geodesic spheres, depends only on the radius. Flat spaces and rank-one symmetric spaces are the basic examples. Lichnerowicz conjectured that harmonic manifolds should be flat or rank-one locally symmetric. Szabó's spectral and "nice embedding" argument establishes this for compact simply connected harmonic spaces and hence for compact universal covers. Compactness of the base manifold must not be confused with compactness of its universal cover.

The noncompact theory is subtler. Damek-Ricci spaces give nonsymmetric homogeneous harmonic manifolds, showing that the unrestricted noncompact Lichnerowicz conjecture is false [DR]. However, the classification of noncompact simply connected harmonic manifolds remains a major topic. Knieper proves that rank one, Anosov geodesic flow, purely exponential volume growth, and Gromov hyperbolicity are equivalent in the relevant noncompact harmonic setting; when such a space admits a compact quotient, dynamical and entropy rigidity identify it with a rank-one symmetric space [K]. He also proves rank one under the no-focal-points assumption. The missing bridge in the general compact-quotient case is to derive one of these equivalent rank-one conditions from harmonicity alone.

Two nearby developments help clarify the frontier. Zimmer proved rigidity results for compact asymptotically harmonic manifolds under additional assumptions, including nonpositive curvature or Gromov hyperbolic fundamental group [Z]. Kim-Park-Shah revisited the three-dimensional asymptotically harmonic case with minimal horospheres and supplied a corrected proof completing the classification in dimension three: the model is flat or real hyperbolic [KPS]. These asymptotic variants are not identical to the compact-universal-cover problem solved by Szabó, but they identify which horosphere and boundary-at-infinity hypotheses can force symmetric geometry.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Prove that a noncompact simply connected harmonic manifold admitting a compact quotient is flat or a rank-one symmetric space.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Prove that every nonflat noncompact harmonic universal cover of a compact manifold has purely exponential volume growth, equivalently rank one, an Anosov geodesic flow, or Gromov hyperbolicity [K, KP].
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Understand the boundary between rank-one symmetric spaces and nonsymmetric Damek-Ricci examples through asymptotic geometry.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Develop stability versions of Szabó's compact theorem.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend the three-dimensional classification of asymptotically harmonic manifolds to higher dimensions under minimal horosphere, visibility, or entropy hypotheses [KPS, Z].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Formulate a compactness theorem for harmonic metrics near a fixed locally symmetric metric in terms of the radial volume-density function.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 85}

Let $M$ be a compact manifold with finite fundamental group. Can one find a non-hyperbolic closed geodesic? For the details of this problem see [Kl], and the paper of Ballmann-Thorbergsson-Ziller [BTZ].

Status. Open in general. Important partial results are known under additional curvature, homotopy, or low-dimensional hypotheses, but there is no general theorem proving that every compact Riemannian manifold with finite fundamental group has a non-hyperbolic closed geodesic.

The problem belongs to the stability theory of closed geodesics. A closed geodesic is hyperbolic when the linearized Poincaré return map has no eigenvalue on the unit circle. Thus a non-hyperbolic closed geodesic is a closed geodesic with elliptic or degenerate linearized behavior. Klingenberg's book [Kl] gives the classical background. Ballmann-Thorbergsson-Ziller [BTZ, BTZ2, BTZ3] proved strong existence and stability results for closed geodesics, in particular under positive curvature or homotopy-theoretic assumptions. These results show that topology and index iteration can force special closed geodesics, but they do not settle the full finite-fundamental-group problem.

For surfaces, there is stronger evidence. Contreras-Oliveira [CO] proved that every Riemannian metric on $\mathbb{S}^{2}$ or $\mathbb{R} P^{2}$ can be $C^{2}$-approximated by a smooth metric with an elliptic closed geodesic. Later, Contreras-Mazzucchelli [CM] proved a $C^{2}$-generic dichotomy for closed surfaces: a generic geodesic flow has either an elliptic closed geodesic or is Anosov. Since Anosov geodesic flows cannot occur on a closed surface of genus at most one, this gives a $C^{2}$-open dense elliptic-geodesic result on $\mathbb{S}^{2}$ and $\mathbb{R} P^{2}$. It remains a generic or approximation theorem, not an existence theorem for every metric, so it does not settle the exact finite-fundamental-group question.

The generic and Finsler viewpoints help sharpen what is missing. Rademacher-Taimanov explain how generic Riemannian statements about closed geodesics can be transferred to Finsler metrics and prove second-closed-geodesic results under topo-logical or hyperbolicity assumptions [RT]. Their discussion also recalls the classical generic alternative: for sufficiently regular generic metrics, either all closed geodesics are hyperbolic or a non-hyperbolic geodesic of twist type appears, in which case Birkhoff-Lewis type arguments produce many closed geodesics. Thus the hard case of the present problem is the possibility of a metric on a finite-fundamental-group manifold whose entire closed-geodesic dynamics is hyperbolic in a way compatible with the topology.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Prove the existence of a non-hyperbolic closed geodesic on every Riemannian sphere $\mathbb{S}^{n}, n \geqslant 2$.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Decide whether a compact Riemannian manifold with finite fundamental group can have all closed geodesics hyperbolic.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Understand whether bumpy metrics on simply connected compact manifolds can force elliptic closed geodesics through index-growth or resonance identities.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend the surface dichotomy between elliptic closed geodesics and Anosov dynamics to higher-dimensional geodesic flows under topological restrictions.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Compare the Riemannian finite-fundamental-group question with generic Finsler alternatives and second-closed-geodesic criteria [RT].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Develop a quantitative criterion which detects non-hyperbolicity from the failure of the closed-geodesic index spectrum to match an Anosov-type model.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 86}

If $M$ is diffeomorphic to the $N$-sphere, give a lower estimate on the number of embedded closed geodesics.
It is well known that Lusternik-Schnirelmann proved the existence of three distinct embedded closed geodesics if $N=2$ (See Lusternik-Schnirelmann [LS].). For contributions to this type of problem, see [Kl].

Status. Partially resolved. For $\mathbb{S}^{2}$, the Lusternik-Schnirelmann theorem gives at least three embedded closed geodesics for every metric [LS]. For $\mathbb{S}^{N}, N \geqslant 3$, generic metrics have infinitely many embedded closed geodesics, but no positive every-metric lower bound for embedded closed geodesics is known in comparable generality [R].

The two-dimensional case is special because closed geodesics can be studied through curve-shortening and planar topology. Bangert [B], Franks [F], and Hingston [H] imply that every Riemannian 2-sphere has infinitely many geometrically distinct closed geodesics, but this does not mean infinitely many embedded ones. The classical Lusternik-Schnirelmann lower bound of three simple closed geodesics is the robust embedded statement on $\mathbb{S}^{2}$.

In higher dimensions the variational theory on the free loop space gives closed geodesics, but controlling embeddedness is much harder. Hingston-Rademacher [HR] obtained resonance results for loop homology of spheres. More recently, Rademacher proved a sharp generic embeddedness statement in dimensions at least three: for a $C^{r}$-generic Riemannian metric, $r \geqslant 2$, every prime closed geodesic is simple and distinct prime geodesics are disjoint [R]. Combining this with generic existence and hyperbolic-dynamics results, a $C^{2}$-generic metric on any compact simply connected manifold of dimension at least three has exponentially many simple closed geodesics. The proof perturbs metrics locally near intersection segments and then uses the bumpy-metric theorem and a Baire argument. This is a strong generic solution, but it does not provide an every-metric lower estimate.

Recent work mainly strengthens the two-dimensional and Finsler versions rather than solving the higher-dimensional embedded problem. Liokumovich-Nabutovsky-Rotman proved that every Riemannian 2-sphere has three simple periodic geodesics whose lengths are bounded by a universal multiple of the diameter, and obtained sharper area-diameter estimates in thin cases [LNR]. De Philippis-Marini- Mazzucchelli-Suhr extended both the Lusternik-Schnirelmann three-simple-geodesics theorem and the Bangert-Franks-Hingston infinite-geodesics theorem to reversible Finsler 2-spheres [DMMS]. These results show that the 2-dimensional theory now has both quantitative and Finsler refinements, while the higher-dimensional embeddedness issue remains the main gap. For bumpy Riemannian 2-spheres, Ko further shows that there are simple closed geodesics of Morse indices 1, 2, and 3, and develops strong Morse inequalities directly on the space of simple closed curves [Ko].

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Prove that every Riemannian $\mathbb{S}^{N}, N \geqslant 3$, has at least one embedded closed geodesic, and then seek a nontrivial dimension-dependent lower bound.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Upgrade Rademacher's generic infinite and exponential-growth theorem to arbitrary metrics, or identify the precise nongeneric obstruction [R].
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Separate the problem of simple embedded closed geodesics from the general closedgeodesic problem in loop-space Morse theory.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend the three-geodesics theorem to natural higher-dimensional notions such as embedded closed geodesics with prescribed index or minimax origin.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Seek diameter, volume, or curvature-controlled length bounds for embedded closed geodesics in higher-dimensional spheres, modeled on the 2-sphere estimates [LNR].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Develop a minimax procedure for sweepouts by embedded loops in $\mathbb{S}^{N}$ which prevents self-intersection in the limiting geodesic.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 87}

Generalize Loewner and Pu's inequality $[\mathrm{P}]$ to higher dimensions. The Loewner inequality says that for the two-torus,
$$\frac{A}{l^{2}} \geqslant C$$
where $l$ is the length of the shortest closed homotopically nontrivial loop and $C$ is a universal constant. In this regard, consult the work of Berger [B] and Gromov [G1].

Status. Partially resolved. Gromov's systolic inequality for essential manifolds gives a fundamental higher-dimensional generalization of Loewner's and Pu's inequalities, but sharp constants, equality cases, and the relations among distinct higher systoles remain open in many natural classes [G2].

Loewner's inequality for $\mathbb{T}^{2}$ and Pu's inequality for $\mathbb{R} P^{2}$ are optimal two-dimensional estimates relating area to the square of the shortest noncontractible loop. Gromov introduced the filling radius and proved that if $M^{n}$ is essential, then
$$\operatorname{sys}_{\pi_{1}}(M, g)^{n} \leqslant C_{n} \operatorname{Vol}(M, g),$$
where $C_{n}$ depends only on the dimension [G2]. This places the Loewner-Pu phenomenon in a topological framework: the fundamental class must remain nontrivial in the homology of the classifying space of the fundamental group. The proof and its later expositions are a central part of modern systolic geometry [G1, K].

The general theorem is not usually sharp. Even for natural families such as projective spaces, lens spaces, complex projective spaces, and manifolds with nontrivial cup products, the search for optimal constants and equality cases is delicate. Stable systolic inequalities, filling-radius inequalities, and higher-dimensional systoles give several different extensions of the Loewner-Pu picture. Recent work continues this direction by extending sharp Gromov-type inequalities from $\mathbb{C} P^{n}$ to broader cohomological settings [GHK].

The point of the Goodwillie-Hebda-Katz extension is that some cohomological hypotheses do more than produce a nonsharp systolic inequality: when the fundamental class is a cup product of two-dimensional classes, one obtains a natural extension of Gromov's optimal inequality for complex projective space [GHK]. This suggests that one should organize higher-dimensional Loewner-Pu problems by the structure of the cohomology ring and by whether the resulting systolic inequality is merely qualitative, quantitatively sharp, or sharp with a rigid equality case.

Tsiamis obtained a complementary sharp development in 2026 [T]. Under positive scalar-curvature hypotheses he proves sharp bounds for the 2-systole, spherical 2-systole, and stable 2-systole in Kähler and spin ${ }^{c}$ settings. Equality identifies Fubini-Study $\mathbb{C} P^{n}$ in the principal Kähler theorem, with further sharp refinements distinguishing complex quadrics and other Fano models. These results concern higher-dimensional two-systoles rather than the homotopy one-systole in Loewner's inequality, and so they enlarge rather than close the original program.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Determine the optimal constants in Gromov's systolic inequality for important essential manifolds beyond $\mathbb{T}^{2}$ and $\mathbb{R} P^{2}$.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify equality cases for sharp systolic inequalities in higher dimensions.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Understand when cup products, Massey products, or other cohomological structures force optimal systolic inequalities.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Relate homotopy systoles, stable systoles, and filling radius in a way that preserves sharp constants.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend the Goodwillie-Hebda-Katz optimal inequality to cohomology rings involving mixed degrees, torsion, or Massey products [GHK].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine which of the sharp scalar-curvature two-systolic inequalities extend beyond the Kähler or spin ${ }^{c}$ hypotheses, and whether their equality cases remain rigid [T].
\item[7.] AI-generated formulation. Develop a quantitative stability theorem for Gromov's essential-manifold inequality: if the ratio $\mathrm{Vol} / \mathrm{sys}_{\pi_{1}}^{n}$ is nearly minimal, must the metric be close to a canonical extremal metric?
\end{itemize}

\section*{VI. Minimal Submanifolds}

\section*{Problem 88}

Prove that any three-dimensional manifold must contain an infinite number of immersed minimal surfaces. Sacks and Uhlenbeck [SU] proved the existence of a minimal sphere in any compact manifold which is not covered by a contractible space. Sacks-Uhlenbeck and Schoen-Yau [SY] independently proved that any incompressible surface can be deformed into a minimal surface. When the ambient manifold is three-dimensional, an argument of Osserman [O] shows that they are immersed. In most cases, they are in fact embedded by the results of Meeks and Yau [MY] and more recent work of Freedman, Hass and Scott [FHS]. The work of Meeks-Simon-Yau [MSY] also showed that, starting from any compact surface in a compact three-dimensional manifold, one can minimize its area "within its isotopy class."

For a general three-dimensional manifold, Pitts [P] proved the existence of one such minimal surface. However, one does not know the genus of the surface from his method.

Status. Solved for closed three-dimensional Riemannian manifolds. Song proved Yau's conjecture in full generality: every closed Riemannian manifold of dimension between 3 and 7 contains infinitely many smooth closed embedded minimal hypersurfaces [So]. In dimension three this gives infinitely many embedded minimal surfaces, hence also immersed minimal surfaces.

The early theory provided several different sources of minimal surfaces. Sacks-Uhlenbeck [SU] proved existence of minimal 2-spheres in many compact manifolds. Schoen-Yau [SY] and Sacks-Uhlenbeck proved that incompressible surfaces can be represented by minimal surfaces. In dimension three, regularity and embeddedness were developed through the work of Osserman [O], Meeks-Yau [MY], Freedman-Hass-Scott [FHS], and Meeks-Simon-Yau [MSY]. Pitts [P] used Almgren-Pitts min-max theory to prove the existence of at least one minimal surface in a closed three-manifold.

The modern solution proceeds through min-max theory for the area functional. Marques-Neves proved the conjecture under positive Ricci curvature, in dimensions at most seven [MN]. Irie-Marques-Neves proved the conjecture for $C^{\infty}$-generic metrics by showing that minimal hypersurfaces are dense [IMN]. Song then removed the genericity and curvature hypotheses [So]. Thus the original existence question is now settled, while quantitative questions about genus, index, area growth, multiplicity, and distribution remain very active.

Song's proof also supplies a reusable mechanism [So]. Assuming finitely many minimal hypersurfaces, one cuts maximally along stable ones to obtain a compact core with stable boundary and attaches cylindrical ends. Min-max widths on this completion localize closed minimal hypersurfaces inside the core and satisfy a linear "cylindrical Weyl law." The resulting arithmetic behavior of the widths contradicts the assumed finite list. This isolates the main ideas that remove both the Frankel and genericity hypotheses.

The post-solution literature has turned the conjecture into a quantitative theory of the area spectrum. Liokumovich-Marques-Neves proved the Weyl law for the volume spectrum [LMN], giving the correct asymptotic scale for the Almgren-Pitts widths. Marques-Neves-Song proved equidistribution of minimal hypersurfaces for generic metrics [MNS], and Chodosh-Mantoulidis and Zhou supplied multiplicity-one and Allen-Cahn/min-max regularity inputs which clarify why the generic minimal hypersurfaces counted by the theory should behave like genuine Morse critical points of area [CM], [Z]. For Yau's original three-dimensional problem, the main frontier is therefore no longer existence but the structure of the infinite family: how the areas obey the Weyl law, whether genus and index have predictable asymptotics, and how much of the generic picture survives for degenerate metrics.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Give sharp genus and index bounds, or asymptotic laws, for the minimal surfaces produced by the min-max widths in a closed three-manifold.
\item[2.] Original direction. Extend abundance results to noncompact three-manifolds under natural bounded-geometry or finitevolume hypotheses.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Prove non-generic versions of the Weyl-law and equidistribution picture: determine when the min-max minimal surfaces are equidistributed and how their areas grow with the width parameter [LMN], [MNS].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify possible multiplicities and bubbling behavior in degenerate metrics, comparing Almgren-Pitts, Simon-Smith, and Allen-Cahn constructions [CM], [Z].
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether the generic density theorem can be strengthened to prescribed topology or bounded index in dimension three, rather than only producing an uncontrolled infinite set [IMN], [MNS].
\end{itemize}
6. AI-generated formulation. Develop a canonical "minimal-surface spectrum" for closed three-manifolds which records area, genus, index, and multiplicity together, analogous to the Laplace spectrum.

References.

\section*{Problem 89}

Prove that there are four distinct embedded minimal spheres in any manifold diffeomorphic to $\mathbb{S}^{3}$. One should study the work of Sacks and Uhlenbeck [SU] in this regard.

Status. Open in full generality, but solved in two major cases: for bumpy metrics and for metrics with positive Ricci curvature.
The harmonic-map min-max theory of Sacks-Uhlenbeck produced minimal immersions of two-spheres. In the embedded category, the first basic existence theorem for an arbitrary Riemannian three-sphere is the Simon-Smith theorem, which gives at least one embedded minimal two-sphere by a one-parameter sweepout. Jost developed related embedded minimal-sphere existence results for manifolds diffeomorphic to a ball or a sphere.

The number four is topologically natural: the space of embedded two-spheres in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ is related, through the Smale conjecture and Hatcher's work, to the space of great spheres, which is homotopy equivalent to $\mathbb{R} \mathbb{P}^{3}$. Thus one expects four critical points of the area functional on this space. The main analytic obstruction is that different min-max families may produce the same minimal sphere, possibly with multiplicity.

Important partial results appeared before the recent solution in generic and positively curved cases. White proved multiplicity results under positive Ricci curvature and near the round metric. Haslhofer and Ketover proved that a three-sphere with a generic metric contains at least two embedded minimal two-spheres. Wang and Zhou then proved that a bumpy metric on $\mathbb{S}^{3}$, or a metric with positive Ricci curvature, contains at least four distinct embedded minimal two-spheres. Their proof uses a multiplicity-one theorem for the Simon-Smith min-max theory: unstable two-sided components occur with multiplicity one, preventing the four natural sweepout classes from recycling a single unstable sphere [WZ]. Talipov obtained a quantitative refinement in 2026 [T]. If Ric $>0$ and $R_{g} \geqslant \Lambda_{0}>0$, the four spheres may be chosen with
$$\operatorname{area}\left(\Sigma_{i}\right) \leqslant \frac{12 \pi(i+1)}{\Lambda_{0}}, \quad 1 \leqslant i \leqslant 4 .$$
His proof combines Simon-Smith widths, Ricci flow, and a sharp eigenvalue inequality on $\mathbb{S}^{2}$; it also yields at least three nonplanar minimal spheres in sufficiently elongated ellipsoids. The remaining case is the arbitrary non-bumpy metric without a positivity assumption.

Recent work also shows that the four-sphere theorem is part of a broader Morse-theoretic program for spaces of embedded surfaces in three-manifolds. Li-Wang proved strong Morse inequalities for the area functional on embedded tori and spheres in a three-sphere with positive Ricci curvature, giving at least four embedded minimal tori and, for bumpy metrics, at least nine [LW]. Li-Wang-Yao developed analogous equivariant Simon-Smith arguments in lens spaces and $\mathbb{R P}^{3}$, where projective planes, Klein bottles, spheres, and tori interact [LWY]. These results do not close the arbitrary metric case of Yau's Problem 89, but they strongly support the view that the correct proof should be a robust Morse theory for the area functional on embedded surface spaces, with multiplicity-one and degenerate-critical-set issues under control. In the same positive-Ricci setting, Chu proved in 2025 that for every $g \geqslant 0$ there is an embedded minimal surface of genus $g$, with area at most twice the first Simon-Smith width [C]. This topological abundance does not by itself produce four genus-zero surfaces for a completely arbitrary metric, but it strengthens the Morse-theoretic picture behind the conjecture.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Prove the four-sphere conclusion for every smooth Riemannian metric on $\mathbb{S}^{3}$, without assuming bumpiness or positive Ricci curvature.
\item[2.] Original direction. Develop a non-generic Morse theory for the area functional on the space of embedded two-spheres in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ which can count degenerate critical points with the correct multiplicity.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether every Riemannian three-sphere contains quantitatively controlled embedded minimal two-spheres, with area or index bounds depending only on natural curvature, volume, or diameter data; compare Talipov's positive-Ricci scalar-curvature bounds [T].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend the Wang-Zhou multiplicity-one Simon-Smith method from bumpy and positive-Ricci metrics to arbitrary metrics by a controlled degeneration or perturbation argument [WZ].
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Build the analogous counting theory for embedded minimal tori, projective planes, and Klein bottles in spherical space forms, continuing the programs of Li-Wang and Li-Wang-Yao [LW], [LWY].
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Construct a "sphere-width spectrum" for embedded two-spheres in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ whose first four critical values always correspond to four geometrically distinct embedded minimal spheres.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 90}

Is it true that every compact differentiable manifold can be minimally embedded into $\mathbb{S}^{N}$ for some $N$ ?
Recently, W.-T. Hsiang and W.-Y. Hsiang studied the problem of minimally embedding some exotic spheres in $\mathbb{S}^{M}$.

Status. Partially resolved. Lawson proved that every closed orientable surface admits a minimal embedding into the round $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ [L]. Important further families are known, but no general theorem covers all compact differentiable manifolds, and even the analogous universal statement for closed nonorientable surfaces remains open [KKMS].

The problem should be separated from the Whitney and Nash embedding theorems. Those theorems give smooth embeddings, or isometric embeddings after a Riemannian metric has been chosen, but they do not force the image to be minimal in a round sphere. Takahashi's theorem gives the basic analytic characterization: an isometric immersion into a sphere is minimal exactly when its coordinate functions are eigenfunctions of the Laplacian with the same eigenvalue. Thus the problem asks whether every compact smooth manifold admits some Riemannian metric and some finite eigenspace which gives an embedding into a round sphere with constant length.

Classical examples come from homogeneous spaces and from eigenmap constructions. Lawson's conjugate-surface and reflection construction settled the topological realization problem for closed orientable surfaces in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$. The work of W.-T. Hsiang, W.-Y. Hsiang and Sterling shows that even exotic spheres can sometimes be realized by minimal immersions or by related constant-mean-curvature embeddings in spheres. These results show that the smooth structure itself is not an obvious obstruction. However, the known constructions still depend on special symmetry, special eigenfunctions, or explicit topology.

The present form of the problem remains a broad existence problem at the interface of differential topology, spectral geometry, and the theory of minimal submanifolds. One also has to distinguish immersion from embedding: Takahashi-type spectral constructions often produce minimal immersions first, while embeddedness may require additional separation properties of the chosen eigenfunctions.

Recent progress has mostly clarified adjacent spectral problems rather than solved Yau's universal embedding question. El Soufi-Ilias connected minimal immersions by eigenfunctions with extremal metrics for Laplace eigenvalues [ESI], and this point of view has become a productive source of examples. On surfaces, Petrides proved broad existence theorems for optimal metrics and Laplace minimal eigenmaps, giving branched minimal immersions into spheres or ellipsoids in many cases [Pe]. Karpukhin-Metras formulated higher-dimensional extremal Laplace and Steklov problems using $n$-harmonic maps [KM], showing that the natural variational framework in dimensions greater than two is subtler than the classical surface theory. Kislitsyn's recent work on complex-valued eigenfunctions gives another construction mechanism for minimal submanifolds in spheres [Ki]. These results suggest that the original problem may be attacked by first producing a metric with a sufficiently rich eigenspace and then proving a separation theorem turning the corresponding eigenmap into an embedding.

The surface theory has recently become much more quantitative. Karpukhin-Kusner-McGrath-Stern use equivariant Laplace and Steklov eigenvalue optimization to construct many prescribed-topology minimal embeddings in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ with area below $8 \pi$ [KKMS]. Their paper also formulates the still-open nonorientable-surface realization problem. In a higher-dimensional direction, Wang constructed in 2026 infinitely many embedded minimal hypersurfaces of pairwise distinct 3-manifold types in $\mathbb{S}^{4}$, including principal $S^{1}$-bundles and Seifert fibered manifolds; in particular, $S^{1} \times \Sigma_{g}$ is obtained for every odd genus $g$ [W]. The method uses equivariant min-max theory for the suspended Hopf action. This is substantial new evidence in dimension three, but far from a universal construction for arbitrary compact manifolds.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Determine whether every compact smooth manifold admits a Riemannian metric whose eigenfunctions for a single eigenvalue give a minimal embedding into some round sphere.
\item[2.] Original direction. Decide whether the weaker statement with "minimal immersion" in place of "minimal embedding" is true for every compact smooth manifold.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Prove that every closed nonorientable surface admits a minimal embedding into some round sphere, as conjectured in [KKMS].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Find purely topological or smooth-structure obstructions, if any, to minimal embedding into a sphere.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify which exotic spheres admit minimal embeddings, rather than merely minimal immersions, into round spheres.
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether extremal-eigenvalue metrics on surfaces, and their higher-dimensional $n$-harmonic analogues, can be desingularized or perturbed from branched eigenmaps to genuine minimal embeddings [Pe], [KM].
\item[7.] Reference-driven formulation. Characterize when a finite eigenspace of a chosen metric separates points and tangent directions strongly enough to convert Takahashi's minimal immersion criterion into an embedding criterion.
\item[8.] AI-generated formulation. Develop a flexible Takahashi-Nash principle: characterize when a smooth embedding can be deformed, after changing the intrinsic metric, into an eigenmap embedding with minimal image in a sphere.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 91}

Is there any complete minimal surface of $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ which is a subset of the unit ball?
This was asked by Calabi [C]. There is an example of a complete minimally immersed surface between two planes due to Jorge and Xavier [JX]. Calabi has also shown that such an example exists in $\mathbb{R}^{4}$. (One takes an algebraic curve in a compact complex surface covered by the ball and lifts it up.)

Status. Solved affirmatively for immersed minimal surfaces. Nadirashvili constructed a complete bounded immersed minimal surface in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ in 1996. The embedded versions are much more rigid and have different answers under standard hypotheses.

The first step toward the bounded problem was the Jorge-Xavier construction of a complete minimally immersed surface between two parallel planes. This showed that completeness did not force extrinsic properness even in a slab. Nadirashvili then used Runge approximation methods to construct a complete bounded minimal disk in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$, thereby answering Calabi's question in the immersed category.

After Nadirashvili's construction, the problem split into several more precise versions. Martin and Morales constructed complete properly immersed minimal surfaces in bounded convex bodies, showing that properness in a bounded domain is possible when the boundary of the domain is included as the limiting boundary. In contrast, Colding and Minicozzi proved the embedded Calabi-Yau conjectures for embedded surfaces, showing that embeddedness imposes strong properness and unboundedness phenomena. The later work of Meeks, Pérez and Ros refined the finite-genus and finite-topology embedded theory. Thus the original immersed question is solved, while the modern subject concerns topology, embeddedness, properness, and quantitative control of complete minimal surfaces in bounded or convex regions.

The conformal version has also developed strongly. Alarcón and collaborators showed that many prescribed open Riemann surfaces occur as complete bounded minimal surfaces, and recent work of Alarcón extends this flexibility to complete proper minimal surfaces with Cantor ends in minimally convex domains [A]. More precisely, for every compact Riemann surface $R$ and every minimally convex domain $\Omega \subset \mathbb{R}^{3}$, he constructs a Cantor set $C \subset R$ such that $R \backslash C$ is the conformal type of a complete proper minimal surface in $\Omega$. Thus, in the immersed category, boundedness is compatible not only with completeness but also with very complicated conformal type and end structure.

At the same time, recent higher-dimensional work shows that the embedded Calabi-Yau picture is special to surfaces. Aryan-McWeeney construct complete improperly embedded minimal hypersurfaces in $\mathbb{R}^{n+1}$ for every $n \geqslant 3$ by iteratively gluing catenoidal necks [AM]. Their examples have unbounded curvature, infinite genus, infinitely many ends, and are unbounded; hence they disprove a general properness principle but not the bounded embedded version of Calabi's question. They also prove that every complete embedded minimal hypersurface with bounded second fundamental form is unbounded, and obtain polynomial chord-arc control for bounded-curvature embedded minimal disks when $3 \leqslant n \leqslant 5$. This sharpens the modern formulation of Problem 91: the immersed surface question is solved, the finite-topology embedded surface question is rigid, and the right general problem is to identify exactly which extra hypotheses force properness or unboundedness.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Classify the conformal types and topological types which can occur for complete bounded immersed minimal surfaces in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$.
\item[2.] Original direction. Determine sharp conditions under which a complete embedded minimal surface in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ must be proper.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Understand which bounded domains in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ admit complete properly immersed minimal surfaces with prescribed topology.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Develop quantitative versions of the embedded Calabi-Yau phenomenon: for example, relate intrinsic completeness, curvature blow-up, and extrinsic escape in embedded minimal surfaces.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine the exact complex structures and end spaces, beyond Cantor complements of compact Riemann surfaces, that can occur for complete proper minimal surfaces in minimally convex bounded domains [A].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Compare the surface Calabi-Yau theorem of Colding-Minicozzi with higher-dimensional counterexamples to properness; identify the dimension-dependent hypotheses, such as bounded curvature or disk topology, under which a properness theorem survives [AM], [CM].
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[7.] Reference-driven formulation. Decide whether every complete embedded finite-genus minimal surface in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ is proper when its end space is uncountable; countable end space is covered by Meeks-Pérez-Ros [MPR].
\item[8.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether a complete bounded embedded minimal hypersurface can exist in $\mathbb{R}^{n+1}, n \geqslant 3$, when curvature is allowed to be unbounded; the examples in [AM] are nonproper but unbounded.
\item[9.] AI-generated formulation. Find a moduli-theoretic compactness theory for complete bounded immersed minimal surfaces which records the Runge-type approximation data responsible for nonproperness.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 92}

What are the complete, embedded, minimal surfaces (with finite genus) in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ ?
The only known examples are the catenoid and the helicoid. It is possible to prove that any such surface is standardly embedded, in the topological sense.

Status. Open as a full finite-genus classification problem, but many fundamental subcases have been solved. The simplyconnected case was solved by Meeks and Rosenberg: the only complete embedded simply-connected minimal surfaces in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ are the plane and the helicoid.

The historical picture changed after the discovery of the Costa surface. The older expectation that plane, catenoid and helicoid exhaust the embedded finite-topology examples became false: Costa's genus-one finite-total-curvature surface, and the subsequent Hoffman-Meeks families, showed that there are many embedded examples with finite topology. Thus the problem is not merely to prove uniqueness of the classical examples, but to organize the full moduli space.

Several decisive classification theorems now structure the subject. Collin proved that a properly embedded minimal surface in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ with finite topology and more than one end has finite total curvature. Meeks and Rosenberg proved the uniqueness of the helicoid in the simply-connected case. Meeks, Pérez and Ros completed the classification of properly embedded minimal planar domains, showing that the infinite-topology genus-zero examples are the Riemann minimal examples. Later finite-topology and finite-genus results describe conformal type, asymptotics, ends, flux, and properness under broad hypotheses.

The remaining finite-genus classification is best viewed as a moduli problem. One wants to understand all properly embedded minimal surfaces of fixed finite genus, with arbitrary end structure, and to describe their possible limits, degenerations, and Weierstrass data.

The current organizing framework is the Meeks-Pérez-Ros program, which combines lamination theory, limit-leaf stability, local removable-singularity theorems, dynamics of moduli spaces, and flux control. Pérez's recent survey emphasizes that the genus-zero infinite-topology classification is now a four-step theorem ending with the Riemann minimal examples, while finite positive genus remains the natural next frontier [P]. The same program relates properness to the number of limit ends and proves that finite genus with countably many ends falls under strong properness and structural constraints [MPR2], [P]. Thus the old phrase "finite genus" now hides several separate problems: finite total curvature, finitely many ends, one or two limit ends, uncountably many ends, and the compactification of moduli spaces under curvature blow-up.

Two precise frontiers emerge from this program [P]. For finite topology, the Hoffman-Meeks conjecture predicts that a connected embedded minimal surface of genus $g$ with $r>2$ ends exists only if $r \leqslant g+2$. The best general theorem currently gives some finite bound $r \leqslant r(g)$, without a sharp formula; the conjecture remains open even for $g=1$. For finite genus with possibly infinitely many ends, Meeks-Pérez-Ros prove properness when the end space is countable, equivalently when there are at most two limit ends [P]. Whether a complete embedded finite-genus surface with an uncountable end space can be nonproper remains open. These statements locate the surviving classification problem more sharply than the older phrase "finite genus."

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Classify properly embedded minimal surfaces in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ of a fixed finite genus and arbitrary end space.
\item[2.] Original direction. Describe the moduli spaces of finite-total-curvature embedded minimal surfaces with prescribed genus and number of ends.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine sharp compactness and degeneration theorems for finite-genus embedded minimal surfaces with curvature concentration.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify possible flux data and asymptotic configurations for finite-genus properly embedded minimal surfaces.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Complete the Meeks-Pérez-Ros program for positive finite genus with one or two limit ends, including properness, dynamics, and lamination limits [P].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Decide whether every finite-genus properly embedded minimal surface with countably many ends is controlled by a finite collection of model ends and flux parameters [MPR2].
\item[7.] Reference-driven formulation. Prove the Hoffman-Meeks bound $r \leqslant g+2$ for finite-topology embedded minimal surfaces, beginning with the still-open genus-one case [P].
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[8.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify one-ended genus- $g$ helicoids and prove or disprove the expected uniqueness for each $g \geqslant 1[\mathrm{P}]$.
\item[9.] AI-generated formulation. Develop a dictionary between the topology of the end space and the dynamical/lamination limits of finite-genus embedded minimal surfaces in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 93}

Prove that every smooth, regular Jordan curve in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ can bound only a finite number of stable minimal surfaces.
If the Jordan curve is real analytic, Tomi [T] proved that it can bound only a finite collection of locally minimal disks. Tomi's argument is quite general, and the basic point that he requires to generalize the theorem to the smooth case is the proof of the absence of boundary branch points for stable minimal surfaces whose boundary is a smooth regular curve. To date, this last assertion is unestablished.

If we assume that the minimal surface has least area in the strong sense, Hardt-Simon [HS] established the absence of boundary branch points, thus proving the finiteness in this case.

There are various uniqueness theorems after suitable perturbation of the boundary. These were due to Böhme, Morgan, Tomi, Tromba and others.

Status. Open in the stated smooth-boundary form. Important special cases and generic variants are known, but there is no general finiteness theorem for all stable minimal surfaces bounded by an arbitrary smooth regular Jordan curve.

The classical finiteness statements require careful separation [J]. Tomi's 1973 theorem says that the global area minimizers are finite provided every such minimizer is immersed; real analyticity of the boundary, or a total-curvature bound below $4 \pi$, supplies this premise through the classical branch-point theory [T]. His later stable-disk theorem assumes the absence of boundary branch points and allows only controlled interior branching [T2]. Hardt-Simon proved the boundary regularity needed for the strongly area-minimizing oriented Plateau problem [HS]. None of these theorems removes the branch-point hypothesis for every stable disk spanning an arbitrary smooth Jordan curve.

There is also a useful nonsmooth positive result. Jakob proved that a simple closed extreme polygon bounds only finitely many immersed stable minimal disks [J]. Each such disk is isolated in the $C^{0}$ Plateau space, while the collection of stable immersed disks is closed in the compact space of all Plateau solutions. The decisive boundary input is that an extreme polygon prevents boundary branch points. This theorem does not follow by smoothing the polygon: its corner expansions are part of the isolation argument, so it highlights rather than removes the missing smooth-boundary step.

The problem is subtle because existence and uniqueness behave non-generically. Morgan constructed a smooth curve in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ bounding a continuum of minimal manifolds, showing that some finiteness statements fail if the stability or minimizing hypotheses are weakened. Morgan's separate fixed-boundary theory proves finiteness for stable or area-minimizing hypersurfaces under several extremality and analyticity hypotheses [M2]. On the other hand, later work of White and Hoffman-White gives strong generic finiteness and parity theorems for embedded minimal surfaces with prescribed boundary in mean-convex threemanifolds. In the Hoffman-White framework a curve is bumpy when no spanning minimal surface carries a nonzero normal Jacobi field vanishing on the boundary; compactness plus bumpiness gives finiteness, and degree theory gives the mod-two count [HW]. These results support the expectation that the finite-stable-surface statement should be true under suitable nondegeneracy hypotheses, while the fixed smooth-boundary problem remains unresolved.

Two nearby developments clarify what is missing. White's generic transversality theorem shows that, after generic perturbation of the ambient metric, simple minimal immersions are transverse and self-transverse, and it gives generic regularity for two-dimensional area-minimizing currents [W2]. This is the kind of Fredholm nondegeneracy one would want for a boundary "bumpy curve" theory. White's later work on boundary singularities in mean curvature flow also gives new information about total-curvature thresholds for minimal surfaces with a smooth simple closed boundary [W3]. These results do not remove boundary branch points for arbitrary stable Plateau solutions, but they show that the obstruction is now quite focused: one needs a fixed-boundary regularity and compactness theorem strong enough to rule out hidden branching or accumulation of stable solutions.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Prove absence of boundary branch points for stable minimal surfaces with arbitrary smooth regular Jordan boundary.
\item[2.] Original direction. Determine whether every smooth regular Jordan curve bounds only finitely many stable minimal disks.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify the degenerations by which a sequence of stable minimal surfaces with the same smooth boundary could fail to have a finite subsequence.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend generic parity and finiteness theorems to less generic fixed curves under local analytic or geometric conditions on the boundary.
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Formulate a fixed-boundary analogue of White's generic transversality theorem: for a generic smooth Jordan curve, prove that every stable Plateau solution is nondegenerate and has no boundary branch points [W2].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Relate total curvature of the boundary curve to topological type and stability of spanning minimal surfaces, extending White's mean-curvature-flow estimates [W3].
\item[7.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend Jakob's compact-plus-isolation proof from extreme polygons to smooth curves, or identify exactly which corner expansion must be replaced by a smooth-boundary estimate [J].
\item[8.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether a bumpy smooth Jordan curve in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$, without first placing it on the boundary of a mean-convex three-ball, bounds only finitely many stable immersed disks [HW].
\item[9.] AI-generated formulation. Define a boundary bumpy curve condition for Plateau critical points and prove that finiteness of stable solutions holds exactly under this nondegeneracy condition.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 94}

Given a single smooth, regular, Jordan curve, can one find a nontrivial, continuous family of minimal disks bounded by this curve?

There is a classical example due to Lévy [L] and Courant [C] of a rectifiable Jordan curve which is smooth except at one point and which bounds an uncountable number of minimal disks. (A proof of the validity of this example depends on the "bridge principle" which was first established by Kruskal [K]. A more rigorous proof of the bridge principle was independently established by Almgren-Solomon [AS], and Meeks-Yau [MY].) Morgan [M] has found an example of continuous family of minimal surfaces whose boundary consists of four disjoint circles.

Status. Open for a single smooth regular Jordan curve bounding a nontrivial continuous family of minimal disks. Known examples either allow a nonsmooth boundary point, use more than one boundary component, or produce continua of minimal surfaces rather than the precise disk-with-one-smooth-Jordan-boundary configuration.

The classical Lévy-Courant example shows that a rectifiable Jordan curve, smooth except at one point, can bound uncountably many minimal disks. The singular boundary point is essential in the known construction and prevents it from answering the smooth regular problem. The bridge principle, developed by Kruskal and later by Almgren-Solomon and Meeks-Yau, explains how new minimal surfaces can be created by joining boundary components with thin bridges. Morgan's example gives a continuous family of minimal surfaces whose boundary consists of four disjoint circles, showing that continuum phenomena are real in Plateau theory.

For a single smooth Jordan curve, however, the disk case remains much more rigid. Generic finiteness and parity theorems for minimal surfaces with prescribed boundary suggest that continuous families should be nongeneric. The unresolved issue is whether one can create such a family while keeping the boundary a single smooth embedded curve and keeping every spanning surface a disk.

Recent work around Plateau theory gives useful comparison cases but does not settle the Euclidean smooth-Jordan-curve question. In the asymptotic Plateau problem in hyperbolic three-space, Huang-Lowe-Seppi proved both uniqueness criteria and nonuniqueness phenomena; in particular they construct quasicircles at infinity which bound uncountably many stable minimal disks [HLS]. This is not an example in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ with a compact smooth boundary, but it shows that continuum families of stable disks can persist in a geometrically natural Plateau problem with a single boundary curve. Their construction uses a self-similar quasicircle surrounding nested pairs of round circles; asymptotic Plateau compactness produces stable disks on alternating sides, and an infinite sequence of binary choices yields the uncountable family. The ideal boundary and this nested separation mechanism are precisely the features that would have to be replaced in a compact Euclidean construction. On the other side, Hoffman-White type parity theorems and White's generic regularity results suggest that any Euclidean example should come from a genuinely degenerate fixed-boundary Fredholm problem, not from a generic boundary curve [W], [Wh]. Thus the modern version of Problem 94 is best phrased as a bifurcation problem for Plateau disks at a smooth curve where the Jacobi operator has kernel.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Construct, or rule out, a smooth regular Jordan curve in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ which bounds a nontrivial continuous family of minimal disks.
\item[2.] Original direction. Determine whether any such family must contain degenerate minimal disks with nontrivial Jacobi fields satisfying the fixed boundary condition.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Decide whether the Lévy-Courant nonsmooth example can be smoothed without destroying the continuum of disk solutions.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Understand whether bridge-principle constructions can be adapted from multiple boundary components to one smooth Jordan boundary component.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Compare compact Plateau families in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ with asymptotic Plateau families in $\mathbb{H}^{3}$ : which features of the Huang-Lowe-Seppi quasicircle examples can survive when the boundary is a compact smooth Jordan curve [HLS]?
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Develop a boundary Fredholm theory in which Hoffman-White parity can be continued through degenerate curves and used to detect possible continua [W].
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[7.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether a nonzero fixed-boundary Jacobi field on a Plateau disk can always be integrated to a local family, or identify the higher-order obstruction to such a bifurcation.
\item[8.] Reference-driven formulation. Decide whether a continuum is possible when all spanning disks are embedded, stable, and satisfy a uniform curvature bound.
\item[9.] AI-generated formulation. Define a local bifurcation model for Plateau disks at a degenerate boundary curve and classify which bifurcations can persist under smoothing of the boundary.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 95}

Let $\sigma$ be a smooth Jordan curve in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ which bounds an embedded disk in the unit ball of $\mathbb{R}^{4}$. Prove that there is a curve $\sigma^{\prime}$ isotopic to $\sigma$ in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ which bounds an embedded minimal disk in the unit ball of $\mathbb{R}^{4}$. An application of this would be the proof that the slice knot is a ribbon knot.

Status. Open in the stated geometric form. The problem should be separated from the slice-ribbon conjecture itself: sliceribbon asks whether every smoothly slice knot is ribbon, and that problem remains open. Yau's formulation proposes a stronger minimal-surface route toward such a conclusion, but no general theorem is known which replaces an arbitrary slice disk, after isotopy of the boundary knot in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$, by an embedded minimal disk in the Euclidean four-ball.

The geometric difficulty is twofold. First, Plateau theory in higher codimension naturally produces area-minimizing currents or branched minimal disks, while the problem asks for a smooth embedded disk with controlled boundary. Boundary regularity, branch points, and embeddedness are therefore central issues. Second, the disk must lie in the correct boundary-knot isotopy class. Once such a minimal disk $X: D \rightarrow B^{4}$ exists, however, the radial part of Yau's strategy is automatic. Since the coordinate functions of a minimal immersion are harmonic,
$$\Delta_{D}|X|^{2}=2|d X|^{2}=4 .$$
Thus $|X|^{2}$ is strictly subharmonic and has no interior local maximum. After the standard generic Morse adjustment, this is precisely the no-maxima radial movie associated with a ribbon disk. The central unsolved step is therefore existence of a smooth embedded minimal representative, not exclusion of radial maxima after it has been found.

Gauge-theoretic and Floer-theoretic invariants show why any positive answer must be very delicate. Donaldson theory and the work of Kronheimer-Mrowka on embedded surfaces in four-manifolds give sharp constraints on smoothly embedded surfaces and on slice genus in many settings. These constraints do not by themselves settle the minimal-disk question above, but they show that a successful theorem would have to interact with modern smooth concordance invariants, not only with classical Plateau theory.

Recent knot-concordance work has sharpened the surrounding slice-ribbon landscape. Dai-Kang-Mallick-Park-Stoffregen proved that the (2, 1)-cable of the figure-eight knot is not smoothly slice [DKMPS], removing a long-standing potential counterexample to the slice-ribbon conjecture. Kang-Park-Taniguchi extended this direction to further figure-eight cables using real Seiberg-Witten Frøyshov invariants [KPT]. Hom-Park used bordered Heegaard Floer theory and the $\gamma_{0}$ invariant to show strong restrictions on when connected sums of certain fibered knots can be ribbon [HP]. Computationally, Dunfield-Gong studied knots through 19 crossings and found large-scale evidence consistent with slice-ribbon phenomena, while isolating a comparatively small list of unresolved sliceness cases [DG]. One of their most interesting knots has since become a sharper test. Oliveira-Smith proved in 2026 that the Dunfield-Gong homotopy four-sphere is standard and hence that the knot $18_{\text {nh00000601 }}$ is smoothly slice in the standard $B^{4}$ [OSm]. The knot is not known to be ribbon, although the constructed disk is fibered handleribbon, so it is now an explicit potential counterexample to slice-ribbon and a natural test for Yau's minimal disk proposal. In another direction, Lobb recasts ribbon concordance as a partial-order problem and proves that the $(4,5)$ torus knot is a global minimum in its concordance class for reduced rational Khovanov homology [Lob]. None of these results produces Yau's desired minimal disk in $B^{4}$, but they show that any geometric proof of the slice-ribbon implication must be compatible with a very rich and computable concordance theory.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Determine which slice knots can be isotoped to bound embedded minimal disks in the Euclidean four-ball.
\item[2.] Original direction. Identify geometric conditions on a slice disk which force the boundary knot to be ribbon.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Relate the radial Morse theory of embedded disks in $B^{4}$ to gauge-theoretic and Floertheoretic concordance invariants.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Decide whether special classes of slice knots admit minimal-disk representatives under additional geometric hypotheses.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Test Yau's minimal-disk strategy on families where slice-ribbon is known or strongly constrained, such as 3-stranded pretzel knots, figure-eight cables, and $\gamma_{0}$-sharp fibered knots [GJ], [DKMPS], [KPT], [HP].
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Develop obstructions to minimal-disk representatives from modern concordance invariants: determine whether knot Floer, Khovanov, instanton, or equivariant Seiberg-Witten data can detect unavoidable branching, self-intersection, or failure of compactness in a codimension-two Plateau construction.
\item[7.] Reference-driven formulation. Decide whether the slice knot $18_{\mathrm{nh} 00000601}$ is ribbon and whether its fibered handle-ribbon disk can be deformed to an embedded minimal disk [OSm].
\item[8.] Reference-driven formulation. Formulate the no-interior-maximum identity $\Delta_{D}|X|^{2}=4$ as a quantitative Morse theory for almost-minimal disks, stable under the limiting process used to solve Plateau's problem.
\item[9.] AI-generated formulation. Define a "minimal ribbon complexity" of a slice knot by minimizing the number of radial critical points among geometrically controlled embedded disks in $B^{4}$.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 96}

What is the structure of the space of minimal surfaces of a fixed genus in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ ? Lawson [L] has proved that, besides $\mathbb{R P}^{2}$, any closed surface can be minimally embedded in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$. Which conformal structures can be realized in such a way? What happens if we replace $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ by $\mathbb{S}^{N}$ with $N>3$ ?

Status. Open in general. There is a complete answer in genus zero and a striking classification in genus one, but the fixed-genus moduli problem in higher genus, and the question of which conformal structures occur, remain largely open.

Lawson's 1970 construction produced embedded minimal surfaces in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ of many topological types and showed that minimal surface theory in the round three-sphere is far richer than the Euclidean compact case. In genus zero, the only embedded minimal two-sphere in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ is the totally geodesic equator. In genus one, Lawson conjectured that the Clifford torus is the only embedded minimal torus in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$; this was proved by Brendle. Thus the conformal structure in the embedded genus-one case is rigid: it is the conformal structure of the Clifford torus.

For higher genus there are many constructions but no classification. The Lawson surfaces give an infinite sequence of embedded examples, and later constructions of Karcher-Pinkall-Sterling, Kapouleas-Yang, Choe-Soret and others produced further families. If $\mathcal{M}_{g}$ denotes the space, modulo ambient isometries, of minimal embeddings of a genus- $g$ surface into $\mathbb{S}^{3}$, then Choi-Schoen proved that $\mathcal{M}_{g}$ is compact in the smooth topology [ChS]. This is an important positive result, but it neither classifies $\mathcal{M}_{g}$ nor determines whether it is finite. The broader local theory of Colding-Minicozzi describes the two basic curvature-concentration mechanisms for fixed-genus sequences: double spiral staircases in the uniformly locally simply connected regime, and short-neck or pair-of-pants decompositions in the complementary regime [CM1, CM2].

The replacement of $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ by $\mathbb{S}^{N}, N>3$, changes the problem substantially. Higher codimension allows many more harmonicmap and algebraic-geometric constructions, and the conformal type is no longer controlled by the same embedded hypersurface restrictions. Thus the higher-codimension version should be treated as a problem about minimal immersions, eigenmaps, and special projective or algebraic models, rather than only as a hypersurface moduli problem.

There are now several complementary modern approaches to this moduli problem. Integrable-systems methods, especially DPW and spectral data, give concrete access to conformal structures in low genus; Heller's work on genus two surfaces and Lawson's genus-two example shows how stable holomorphic bundles and meromorphic connections enter the description [He1], [He2]. From a different direction, Karpukhin-Kusner-McGrath-Stern used equivariant eigenvalue optimization to produce embedded minimal surfaces in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ with prescribed topology and area below $8 \pi$, proving $\left|\mathcal{M}_{g, 8 \pi}\right| \geqslant\lfloor(g-1) / 4\rfloor+1[\mathrm{KKMS}]$. Kapouleas-McGrath then constructed Clifford-torus doublings with catenoidal bridges along parallel torus knots and obtained the stronger quadratic count $\left|\mathcal{M}_{g}\right| \geqslant C g^{2}$ for large $g[\mathrm{KMg}]$. This is particularly relevant to Yau's question because it links the topological realization problem, the Laplace spectrum, and the image of the moduli space in Teichmüller space. The current picture is therefore not just construction versus classification: one wants to organize the fixed-genus space simultaneously by topology, conformal structure, spectrum, area, index, symmetry, and degeneration.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Classify embedded minimal surfaces of each fixed genus $g \geqslant 2$ in the round three-sphere.
\item[2.] Original direction. Determine the subset of the moduli space of Riemann surfaces of genus $g$ which can be realized by embedded minimal surfaces in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Describe the compactification of the fixed-genus moduli space: which degenerations, bubbling phenomena, or limiting laminations can occur when genus, ambient metric, or embeddedness is allowed to vary, and how do the Colding-Minicozzi neck and double-spiral models appear at the boundary [CM1, CM2]?
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether the Lawson surfaces are unique within natural symmetry, index, or area classes.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Use integrable-systems data to characterize which genus-two and higher-genus conformal structures can occur for embedded minimal surfaces in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ [He1], [He2].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Relate equivariant eigenvalue-optimization constructions to the classical Lawson and Karcher-Pinkall-Sterling families: determine whether the new low-area examples exhaust any natural part of the fixedgenus moduli space [KKMS].
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[7.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine the asymptotic growth of $\left|\mathcal{M}_{g}\right|$ : is the quadratic lower bound of Kapouleas-McGrath sharp, and is $\mathcal{M}_{g}$ finite for every fixed $g \geqslant 2[\mathrm{KMg}]$ ?
\item[8.] AI-generated formulation. Construct a period-map-like invariant from embedded minimal surfaces in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ to Teichmüller space and describe its image, fibers, and boundary behavior.
\end{itemize}
