\section*{Problem 97}
(Lawson). Is the only embedded minimal torus in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ the Clifford torus?
There are many minimal tori in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ which are not Clifford tori, but they are not embedded.

Status. Solved. Brendle proved in 2013 that the Clifford torus is the only embedded minimal torus in the round three-sphere.
Lawson's construction produced many compact minimal surfaces in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ and led naturally to the conjecture that the embedded torus case is rigid. There are many immersed minimal tori in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$, and the spectral-curve theory of Pinkall-Sterling shows that the immersed theory has rich finite-type structure. The difficulty of Lawson's conjecture was therefore not minimality alone, but the combination of minimality and embeddedness.

Brendle's proof introduced a two-point function and an Alexandrov reflection argument adapted to the nonlocal geometry of an embedded minimal torus. The method shows that the surface must have the symmetries of the Clifford torus and hence must be congruent to it. The result gives the complete embedded genus-one classification in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ and forms one of the central solved cases in the broader fixed-genus moduli problem.

After Brendle's theorem, the Clifford torus became not only the unique embedded torus but also a central local model for higher-genus constructions. Choe-Soret constructed new minimal surfaces desingularizing Clifford tori [CS]. Kapouleas-Wiygul constructed low-genus desingularizations of three Clifford tori and proved related characterization results for the generating disks [KW]. Kapouleas-McGrath later produced new Clifford-torus doublings and used them to obtain a quadratic lower bound for the number of embedded minimal surfaces in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ of prescribed genus, as well as new examples relevant to Yau's first-eigenvalue question [KMg]. These developments do not reopen the genus-one problem, but they show that the Clifford torus remains the organizing singular model for many higher-genus moduli questions.

There is also a quantitative counterpart to the rigidity picture. Bi-Zhou proved that a unit-density integral two-varifold in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$, with support of genus at least one and Willmore energy at most $2 \pi^{2}+\delta^{2}$, is, after a conformal transformation, $W^{2,2}$-close to the Clifford torus with error $O(\delta)$ [BZ]. This is a stability theorem for the Willmore minimum rather than a new proof of the Lawson conjecture: conformal transformations need not preserve minimality, and the theorem applies to a larger varifold class. Its importance here is that it supplies a precise topology and rate in which near-equality forces geometric closeness.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Find analogous uniqueness theorems for embedded minimal surfaces of higher genus under natural symmetry, index, or area assumptions.
\item[2.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify immersed minimal tori in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ with additional constraints such as embedded fundamental domain, low spectral genus, or low Morse index.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether Brendle's two-point-function method extends to other ambient threemanifolds or to free-boundary minimal tori.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Relate the Lawson conjecture to quantitative stability: compare the $W^{2,2}$ Willmore stability of Bi-Zhou with stronger smooth, spectral, or Morse-index stability statements inside the minimal-surface class [BZ].
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify Clifford-torus doubling and desingularization families, and decide when gluing, min-max, and eigenvalue-optimization constructions produce the same embedded minimal surfaces [CS, KW, KMg].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Develop an Alexandrov-type nonlocal maximum principle for higher-genus embedded minimal surfaces in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ which detects hidden rotational symmetry.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 98}
(Lawson). Let $M$ be an embedded minimal surface in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$. Prove that the two domains in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ divided by $M$ have equal volume.
This is a delicate form of the Gauss-Bonnet theorem. Indeed, if $M^{2 N-1} \subseteq \mathbb{S}^{2 N}$ is a compact connected hypersurface such that all the odd elementary symmetric functions of the second fundamental form are zero, then the general Gauss-Bonnet theorem proves that the two components of $\mathbb{S}^{2 N} \backslash M^{2 N-1}$ have equal volume provided they have the same Euler characteristic. For a minimal surface in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$, these two components are always diffeomorphic (cf. Lawson [L]).

In the general case of $\mathbb{S}^{N}$ for $N>3$, the conjecture fails. C.-L. Terng, for example, shows that $\mathbb{S}^{P}(\sqrt{P / N}) \times \mathbb{S}^{N-P}(\sqrt{(N-P) / N})$ does not divide $\mathbb{S}^{N+1}$ into two equal-volume pieces unless $P=N-P$.

Status. False. Karcher-Pinkall-Sterling constructed embedded minimal surfaces in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ for which the two complementary domains do not have equal volume.

The conjecture was natural because of Lawson's unknottedness theorem and the Gauss-Bonnet-type calculation recalled above. In odd hypersurface dimension inside an even-dimensional sphere, the vanishing of the odd elementary symmetric functions can force equal volumes when the two complementary domains have the same Euler characteristic. For embedded minimal surfaces in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$, the two regions are diffeomorphic, so it was reasonable to expect a similar volume balance. The Karcher-Pinkall-Sterling paper proves more than the bare existence of a counterexample. It constructs a minimal patch inside a Coxeter tetrahedron, extends it by reflections, proves embeddedness by controlling the polar surface, and then estimates the patch inside a tube about an equatorial reflection sphere. For the surface generated by their $(\pi / 3, \pi / 2, \pi / 5)$ patch, the tube contains less than half of $\mathbb{S}^{3}$, which forces unequal complementary volumes [KPS, §5]. By contrast, their self-dual examples contain great circles whose symmetries exchange the two sides, so those examples are balanced. Thus minimality and embeddedness alone do not impose volume balance; side-exchanging symmetry is the decisive extra feature in the balanced examples.

The higher-dimensional product examples also show that the failure is not accidental. Clifford-type products $\mathbb{S}^{P}(\sqrt{P / N}) \times$ $\mathbb{S}^{N-P}(\sqrt{(N-P) / N})$ are minimal hypersurfaces in spheres, but they divide the ambient sphere into equal-volume pieces only in the symmetric case $P=N-P$. Thus any true equal-volume theorem must use stronger symmetry, parity, or curvature hypotheses than minimality alone.

Recent existence results make the false problem useful as a moduli question. New gluing, desingularization, and equivariant eigenvalue-optimization constructions produce large families of embedded minimal surfaces in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ [CS, KMg, KKMS]. On such families one can ask whether the volume imbalance varies nontrivially, whether symmetry forces zeros of the imbalance, and what happens near singular limits built from Clifford tori or other highly symmetric configurations. Thus the modern replacement is not an equal-volume theorem for all embedded minimal surfaces, but a study of enclosed volume as an invariant on concrete moduli spaces.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Characterize embedded minimal surfaces in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ which do divide the sphere into two equal-volume domains.
\item[2.] Original direction. Determine whether equality of the two volumes follows from additional assumptions such as antipodal symmetry, special isoparametric structure, or prescribed symmetry group.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. For a symmetry group preserving $M$, determine from its action on the two components of $\mathbb{S}^{3} \backslash M$ whether volume balance is forced; in particular, separate symmetries preserving each side from those exchanging the sides.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Study the volume imbalance as a functional on moduli spaces of embedded minimal surfaces in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Find sharp geometric bounds for the possible volume imbalance in terms of genus, area, index, or symmetry.
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Compute the imbalance on the Karcher-Pinkall-Sterling, Choe-Soret, Kapouleas-McGrath, and equivariant eigenvalue-optimization families, and decide whether balanced members occur by symmetry or bifurcation [KPS, CS, KMg, KKMS].
\item[7.] AI-generated formulation. Define an "enclosed-volume map" on the fixed-genus minimal-surface moduli space in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ and describe its image and critical points.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 99}
(Chern). Prove that the only embedded minimal hypersurface in $\mathbb{S}^{N+1}$ which is diffeomorphic to $\mathbb{S}^{N}$ is the totally geodesic sphere.

An affirmative answer will be interesting even for a special case where we assume the cone over the hypersurface is stable in $\mathbb{R}^{N+2}$. Hopefully this would mean that an area-minimizing hypersurface which is a topological manifold is smooth. Under the assumption of stability of the cone the conjecture is true for $N=2,3,4,5$, see [S]. For higher codimension the conjecture is false; see [LO].

Status. False in the stated generality, already for $N=3$. Hsiang constructed infinitely many noncongruent, non-equatorial embedded minimal three-spheres in $\mathbb{S}^{4}[\mathrm{H}]$.

The problem is a natural strengthening of the classical rigidity of the equatorial sphere. In low dimensions, and especially for embedded minimal two-spheres in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$, the totally geodesic sphere is indeed rigid. Simons' work on minimal varieties and stable cones also gives strong positive results for the stable-cone version in low dimensions. This explains why the conjecture was plausible from the viewpoint of regularity theory for area-minimizing hypersurfaces.

The higher-dimensional situation is different. In the notation of the present problem, Hsiang's embedded examples occur for $N \in\{3,4,5,6,7,9,11,13\}$, and Tomter obtained a non-equatorial embedded minimal hypersphere for every odd $N \geqslant 3$. By contrast, Hsiang-Tomter's main theorem for every $N \geqslant 3$ is an immersed existence theorem; it should not be quoted as embedded in all dimensions [HT]. The even-dimensional embedded case $N \geqslant 8$ remains the unresolved parity range of the spherical Bernstein problem. In higher codimension, Lawson-Osserman showed that analogous minimal-surface-system uniqueness and regularity statements fail even more dramatically.

Recent work supplies new constructions rather than restoring rigidity. Carlotto-Schulz gave a self-contained dynamicalsystems construction of infinitely many pairwise non-isometric embedded minimal hyperspheres in $\mathbb{S}^{4}$ and $\mathbb{S}^{6}$ [CaS]. In 2026, T. Wang-Z. Wang-Zhou developed an equivariant min-max theory with topological control and constructed another embedded non-equatorial minimal $\mathbb{S}^{3} \subset \mathbb{S}^{4}$ [WwZ]. Unlike the classical ODE shooting construction, their variational method persists for a class of symmetric nonround metrics. This does not enlarge the known dimension range, but it changes the available mechanism and opens a route toward robust or multiplicity results.

Modern rigidity progress is therefore best read as a collection of replacement theorems. Lei-Xu-Xu obtain small-gap results for minimal hypersurfaces with constant scalar curvature [LXX]. Cheng-Wei-Yamashiro prove, under the additional constancy of the third power sum $f_{3}$, that $S>n$ forces $S>1.8252 n-0.712898$ [CWY]. Magliaro-Mari-Roing-Savas-Halilaj prove the sharp complete pinching alternative $|A|^{2} \leqslant n$ : the hypersurface is totally geodesic or covers a Clifford torus [MMRS]. C. Wang-P. Wang show that, under reflection symmetry in the $n+2$ coordinate hyperspheres, the average inequality $\bar{S} \geqslant n$ is rigid at Clifford tori [WW]. None of these theorems uses the topology $M \cong \mathbb{S}^{N}$ alone, so none revives the false original conjecture.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Classify embedded minimal hyperspheres in $\mathbb{S}^{N+1}$ under additional assumptions such as low index, curvature pinching, or prescribed symmetry.
\item[2.] Original direction. Construct, or rule out, a non-equatorial embedded minimal $\mathbb{S}^{N} \subset \mathbb{S}^{N+1}$ for every even $N \geqslant 8$.
\item[3.] Original direction. Determine sharp dimension bounds for stable-cone rigidity when the link is a smooth topological sphere.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Understand whether area-minimizing hypersurfaces which are topological manifolds must be smooth under natural extra hypotheses.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify Hsiang-Tomter type minimal hyperspheres and their deformations in higherdimensional spheres.
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend the equivariant min-max construction of [WwZ] to new group actions and dimensions, especially the unresolved even- $N$ range, while retaining the hypersphere topology.
\item[7.] Reference-driven formulation. Prove sharp pinching and isoparametric rigidity theorems for minimal hyperspheres under constant scalar curvature or small $|A|^{2}$-gap assumptions, sharpening [LXX] and [CWY].
\item[8.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend symmetry-based Clifford-torus and totally-geodesic rigidity theorems to weaker symmetry, average-curvature, or low-index hypotheses [WW].
\end{itemize}
9. AI-generated formulation. Define a "Chern-defect" measuring the failure of a minimal hypersphere in $\mathbb{S}^{N+1}$ to be totally geodesic, and seek pinching theorems forcing this defect to vanish.

\section*{Problem 100}

Is it true that the first eigenvalue for the Laplace-Beltrami operator on an embedded minimal hypersurface of $\mathbb{S}^{N+1}$ is $N$ ?
This is not known even for $N=2$. An affirmative answer will imply that the area of embedded minimal surfaces in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ will have an upper bound depending only on the genus. This is a consequence of the theorem of Yang-Yau [YY].

Status. Open in the stated generality as of July 2026. There are strong lower bounds and many special cases. A 2025 preprint claims a proof, but the argument as posted has decisive gaps and does not justify a status change; later 2026 work continues to treat Yau's statement as a conjecture [Zh2, Yu].

The coordinate functions of an embedded minimal hypersurface $M^{N} \subset \mathbb{S}^{N+1}$ are eigenfunctions with eigenvalue $N$, so the problem is to prove that no smaller positive eigenvalue exists. Choi and Wang proved the fundamental lower bound $\lambda_{1}(M) \geqslant N / 2$. In dimension two, the conjecture would combine with the Yang-Yau eigenvalue estimate to give area bounds for embedded minimal surfaces in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ depending only on genus.

Many structured examples satisfy the conjecture. Choe and Soret proved $\lambda_{1}=2$ for reflection-generated surfaces whose simply connected fundamental patch has fewer than six edges; this covers the Lawson and Karcher-Pinkall-Sterling examples [CS]. Tang and Yan proved the conjecture for isoparametric minimal hypersurfaces, connecting it to Chern-type problems for minimal hypersurfaces with constant scalar curvature [TY]. Kapouleas-McGrath proved the stronger first-eigenspace statement for their Clifford-torus doublings and for a broad class of side-symmetric equatorial-sphere doublings [KMg].

The quantitative lower-bound program has also advanced. If $\Lambda=\max _{M}|A|$, Duncan-Sire-Spruck prove
$$\lambda_{1}(M) \geqslant \frac{N}{2}+\frac{a_{N}}{\Lambda^{6}+b_{N}}$$
with explicit dimensional constants [DSS]. Yu improves this to
$$\lambda_{1}(M) \geqslant \frac{N}{2}+\frac{N^{2}}{27\left(\sqrt{\Lambda^{2}+2 N}+\Lambda\right)^{2}}$$
[Yu]. In dimension two, Zhao proves $\lambda_{1} \geqslant 1+\varepsilon_{g}$ for a genus- $g$ embedded minimal surface [Zh1]; his higher-dimensional work gives a positive correction $G\left(N,|A|_{\text {max }},|A|_{\text {min }}\right)$ to $N / 2$ and a dimension-only gap when $|A|$ is constant [Zh2]. These results do not reach $N$, but they identify the loss in the harmonic-extension and Reilly-formula method.

The claimed proof in [Ze] was audited directly. Its trial functions $u_{\beta}$ converge in $H^{1}$ to a coordinate function $x_{i}$, and the calculation $R\left[u_{\beta}\right] \rightarrow N$ therefore recovers only the already known upper bound $\lambda_{1} \leqslant N$; the paper never proves that $\left\{u_{\beta}\right\}$ is a minimizing sequence for $\lambda_{1}$. Moreover, the asserted exact orthogonality $\int_{M} x_{i} e^{-\beta d\left(p, p_{0}\right)^{2}}=0$ is deduced from a local parity argument that is not valid on a general embedded hypersurface. An independent check is provided by its stability corollary, which treats the variable potential $|A|^{2}$ as a constant spectral shift and concludes that every closed embedded minimal hypersurface in a sphere is stable. Thus the posted proof does not establish the main theorem.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Prove $\lambda_{1}=N$ for every closed embedded minimal hypersurface in $\mathbb{S}^{N+1}$.
\item[2.] Original direction. Prove the conjecture for embedded minimal surfaces in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$ under weaker assumptions than symmetry or membership in known families.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether Chern-type constant-scalar-curvature conditions imply the firsteigenvalue conclusion.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Find sharp improvements of the Choi-Wang bound $\lambda_{1} \geqslant N / 2$ in terms of genus, area, index, or bounds for the second fundamental form.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Compare the claimed full solution of Zeng with the quantitative approaches of Duncan-Sire-Spruck, Zhao, and Yu, and isolate which analytic step would upgrade a gap estimate to $\lambda_{1}=N$ [DSS, Ze, Zh2, Yu].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Decide whether a genuine mean-zero correction of the coordinate functions can be combined with harmonic extension and the Reilly formula to produce a lower, rather than merely an upper, estimate for $\lambda_{1}$.
\end{itemize}
7. AI-generated formulation. Develop a spectral compactness theory for sequences of embedded minimal hypersurfaces in spheres which tracks possible collapse of $\lambda_{1}$ below the coordinate eigenvalue.

\section*{Problem 101}

Is there a closed minimal surface in $\mathbb{S}^{N}$ with negative curvature?

Status. Solved affirmatively in high codimension. Ancona, Labourie, Roig-Sanchis and Toulisse proved that for every sufficiently large integer $N$ there is a closed minimal surface in $\mathbb{S}^{N}$ whose induced Gaussian curvature is negative everywhere [ALRT]. In contrast, Lawson's Gauss-Codazzi argument rules out such a surface in $\mathbb{S}^{3}[\mathrm{~L}]$. The smallest possible ambient dimension remains unknown.

The question asks for a compact minimal surface whose intrinsic Gaussian curvature is negative everywhere. For a minimal surface in a sphere this is a strong pointwise condition, not merely a topological condition. By Gauss-Bonnet, such a surface must have genus at least two, but the main difficulty is to realize a negatively curved metric by a minimal immersion into a round sphere.

The recent construction uses closed Riemann surfaces with large automorphism groups and a strategy inspired by Song's high-dimensional random-representation method. More precisely, the surfaces $\Sigma_{N}$ satisfy
$$\left\|K_{\Sigma_{N}}+8\right\|_{C^{k}} \longrightarrow 0 \quad \text { for every fixed } k,$$
and their symmetry quotients can be chosen to be one fixed Riemann surface. Pointwise $C^{k}$ convergence of curvature, rather than an average statement, is what eventually forces $K_{\Sigma_{N}}<0$.

This should be separated from Song's precursor. Song constructed closed branched minimal surfaces in high-dimensional spheres which Benjamini-Schramm converge to a rescaled hyperbolic plane and for which $K+8$ tends to zero only in normalized $L^{1}$ [So]. That average control allows exceptional regions and does not answer the pointwise-sign question. Ancona-Labourie-Roig-Sanchis-Toulisse replace the cusped three-punctured sphere in Song's construction by a rigid compact orbifold, pass to suitable finite covers, and upgrade the convergence to $C^{k}$ control [ALRT]. The method is genuinely high-dimensional. After Lawson's obstruction in $\mathbb{S}^{3}$, the first unresolved low-codimension cases are $\mathbb{S}^{4}$ and $\mathbb{S}^{5}$.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Determine the smallest dimension $N \geqslant 4$ for which there exists a closed minimal surface in $\mathbb{S}^{N}$ with negative curvature everywhere.
\item[2.] Original direction. Decide whether such examples exist in $\mathbb{S}^{4}$ or $\mathbb{S}^{5}$.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Construct embedded, rather than merely immersed, negatively curved closed minimal surfaces in spheres when possible.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Understand which negatively curved metrics on closed surfaces can arise as induced metrics of minimal immersions into round spheres.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Make the threshold " $N$ sufficiently large" effective and quantify genus, symmetry-group order, branching, and the rate in the $C^{k}$ curvature bounds [So, ALRT].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Develop a compactness theory for almost-hyperbolic minimal surfaces in high-dimensional spheres and describe their limiting hyperbolic geometry.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 102}

As a generalization of Bernstein's theorem, Fischer-Colbrie-Schoen [FCS] and do Carmo-Peng [DP] proved that any complete stable minimal surface in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ is a plane. Can one generalize this statement to the case of a complete stable hypersurface in $\mathbb{R}^{N}$ for $N \leqslant$ ?

Status. False as stated, with exactly one intermediate dimension still open. For a complete, connected, two-sided stable minimal immersion $M^{n} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}^{n+1}$, flatness is known for $n \leqslant 5$, is open for $n=6$, and is false for every $n \geqslant 7$. Equivalently, among the ambient dimensions asked for here, the theorem holds in $\mathbb{R}^{3}, \ldots, \mathbb{R}^{6}$, remains open in $\mathbb{R}^{7}$, and fails in $\mathbb{R}^{8}$.

The surface case in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ was proved independently by do Carmo-Peng and by Fischer-Colbrie-Schoen. Their result says that a complete stable minimal surface in Euclidean three-space must be a plane. Schoen-Simon-Yau developed curvature estimates for stable minimal hypersurfaces, giving the basic analytic framework for higher-dimensional Bernstein-type rigidity. These estimates explain why the natural critical range is tied to the regularity threshold for minimal hypersurfaces and to the classical Bernstein theorem.

The modern positive progress is recent. Chodosh and Li proved that a complete two-sided stable minimal immersed hypersurface in $\mathbb{R}^{4}$ is flat. Chodosh, Li, Minter and Stryker then proved the analogous result in $\mathbb{R}^{5}$, and Mazet proved the $\mathbb{R}^{6}$ case using the strategy of Chodosh-Li-Minter-Stryker together with volume estimates [M]. Thus the only unresolved positive case is a six-dimensional hypersurface in $\mathbb{R}^{7}$.

The obstruction at the next dimension is concrete. The Simons cone in $\mathbb{R}^{8}$ is area-minimizing [BDG], and the Hardt-Simon foliation produces smooth complete nonflat area-minimizing hypersurfaces asymptotic to it [HS]. Area minimization implies two-sided stability, so these hypersurfaces disprove the requested statement in ambient dimension 8. This parametric stable counterexample occurs one dimension earlier than the non-affine entire-graph counterexamples, which begin with graphs over $\mathbb{R}^{8}$ in ambient $\mathbb{R}^{9}$.

One important feature of the recent work is methodological. The Chodosh-Li proof in $\mathbb{R}^{4}$ has a positive-scalar-curvature flavor, and their anisotropic version gives an alternative proof which is closer to scalar-curvature techniques [CL2]. This suggests that the remaining $\mathbb{R}^{7}$ case should be studied not only through classical curvature estimates, but also through stability inequalities, volume growth, blow-down cones, and scalar-curvature comparison ideas. Bellettini proves the required curvature estimate for six-dimensional stable immersions under extrinsic Euclidean volume growth [B]. Antonelli-Xu isolate an algebraic obstruction to extending the current $\mu$-bubble parameter choices to $n=6$ [AX], while a 2026 synthesis confirms that this is still the sole missing dimension [CCMMR].

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Decide whether every complete two-sided stable minimal hypersurface in $\mathbb{R}^{7}$ is a hyperplane.
\item[2.] Original direction. Remove the extrinsic Euclidean-volume-growth hypothesis from the known $\mathbb{R}^{7}$ curvature estimate, or construct a counterexample.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify the blow-downs of complete stable $M^{6} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}^{7}$ and determine whether stability alone forces Euclidean volume growth.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend the recent scalar-curvature and positive-mass methods used in low dimensions to the full stable Bernstein range.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Find a replacement for the matrix inequalities in the present $\mu$-bubble arguments, which Antonelli-Xu show cannot close in hypersurface dimension 6 with the existing parameter family [AX].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Develop a quantitative stable Bernstein theorem: if the stability inequality is almost sharp at all scales, then the hypersurface is quantitatively close to a hyperplane.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 103}

If $u$ is an entire solution of the minimal surface equation on $\mathbb{R}^{N}$, does $u$ have polynomial growth?
Status. Open in this form. For $N \leqslant 7$ the Bernstein theorem implies that every entire solution is affine, hence has linear growth. For $N \geqslant 8$ non-affine entire solutions exist, but the general growth question remains a separate asymptotic problem.

Bombieri and Giusti [BG] gave a central analytic tool for this circle of questions. Bombieri also suggested a connection with the first eigenvalue of minimal hypersurfaces in $\mathbb{S}^{N}$; the blow-down analysis of Allard-Almgren [AA] is another important point of contact.

The background is the classical Bernstein problem. Bernstein proved the two-dimensional case; De Giorgi, Almgren, and Simons extended the flatness theorem up to graphs over $\mathbb{R}^{7}$. Bombieri-De Giorgi-Giusti then constructed non-affine entire solutions in dimension $N \geqslant 8$ using the minimizing property of the Simons cone. Thus polynomial growth is automatic in the affine range, while in the non-affine range the question asks for a much finer description of the asymptotic behavior of entire minimal graphs.

Moser's theorem shows that bounded gradient forces an entire solution to be affine. Bombieri-Giusti proved Harnack inequalities on minimal surfaces and generalized this rigidity under boundedness assumptions on many derivatives. More recently, Farina sharpened this direction: for $N \geqslant 8$, if $N-7$ partial derivatives are bounded on one side, then the solution is affine; this is sharp with respect to the Bombieri-De Giorgi-Giusti examples. These results show that asymptotic control of slopes is powerful, but they do not by themselves settle the unrestricted polynomial-growth question.

The connection with Allard-Almgren is through blow-downs and tangent cones at infinity. If one could prove sufficient uniqueness or quantitative convergence of blow-downs for entire minimal graphs, the growth problem would become closer to the spectral and stability questions for the corresponding minimal cones in spheres. This is the reason the problem is naturally related to first-eigenvalue questions for minimal hypersurfaces in $\mathbb{S}^{N}$.

Recent work around the question suggests two nearby but distinct directions. Guo studies the much stronger ansatz that $u$ itself is a polynomial [G]. No nonlinear real polynomial solution is currently known. Guo proves that there are no quadratic or cubic polynomial solutions and obtains a factorization theorem for the leading homogeneous term $P_{m}=p^{k} Q$, where $k$ is odd, $Q$ is nonnegative, and the zero set of the irreducible factor $p$ is an area-minimizing cone. The tangent cone cannot be isoparametric. In domain dimension 8, existence of a nonlinear polynomial solution would force a non-strictly-minimizing cubic cone. These are strong algebraic restrictions, but polynomiality of $u$ is far stronger than the polynomial-growth assertion in Yau's question.

Nonlocal and higher-codimension minimal graph problems have Bernstein-type analogues, but they do not settle the classical codimension-one problem. Weitsman proved an exponential upper growth estimate for positive minimal graphs over planar simply connected domains with zero boundary values [W]. This is a two-dimensional boundary-value theorem, not an estimate for arbitrary entire graphs on $\mathbb{R}^{N}$; it therefore supplies a comparison mechanism rather than direct progress on the conjecture.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Prove or disprove that every entire solution of the minimal surface equation on $\mathbb{R}^{N}$ has polynomial growth.
\item[2.] Original direction. Classify the possible growth rates of non-affine entire minimal graphs in the Bombieri-De Giorgi-Giusti range $N \geqslant 8$.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine whether uniqueness of blow-downs for entire minimal graphs implies polynomial asymptotics.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Relate polynomial growth of an entire minimal graph to the first eigenvalue, stability, and Jacobi fields of its tangent cone at infinity.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Compare polynomial growth with weaker a priori bounds, such as exponential growth estimates for minimal graphs under boundary hypotheses, and identify which extra entire-graph assumptions force an algebraic rate [W].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Decide whether any non-affine real polynomial can solve the codimension-one minimal surface equation; if so, classify the possible tangent-cone factors allowed by Guo's structure theorem [G].
\item[7.] AI-generated formulation. Develop a frequency-function or monotonicity theory for the minimal surface equation which detects polynomial growth directly from blow-down data.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 104}

Classify the topological type of the seven-dimensional area minimizing cones in $\mathbb{R}^{8}$.
Status. Open. The regularity theory reduces the problem to the study of smooth stable minimal hypersurfaces $M^{6} \subset \mathbb{S}^{7}$ which arise as links of area-minimizing hypercones in $\mathbb{R}^{8}$, but no complete topological classification of such links is known. As of 2026, the only known area-minimizing hypercones in $\mathbb{R}^{8}$ are the quadratic cones over $\mathbb{S}^{2} \times \mathbb{S}^{4}$ and $\mathbb{S}^{3} \times \mathbb{S}^{3}$; it is not known whether this list is complete [E, C].

Lawson observed that one expects only finitely many diffeomorphism classes and that effective bounds should be obtainable. Merely from stability, Simons [Ss] obtains an explicit $L^{2}$ estimate on the second fundamental form of the link $M^{6} \subseteq \mathbb{S}^{7}$. Comparable $L^{p}$ estimates through the top dimension would give topological control, but the required effective classification of links has not been carried out.

The two known models are the quadratic cones over the minimal products $\mathbb{S}^{2}(\sqrt{2 / 6}) \times \mathbb{S}^{4}(\sqrt{4 / 6})$ and $\mathbb{S}^{3}(1 / \sqrt{2}) \times \mathbb{S}^{3}(1 / \sqrt{2})$ in $\mathbb{S}^{7}$. The symmetric second example is the usual Simons cone, whose area-minimizing property was proved by Bombieri-De Giorgi-Giusti. The third stable quadratic cone over $\mathbb{S}^{1} \times \mathbb{S}^{5}$ is minimizing only on one side and is not area-minimizing [E]. More generally, an area-minimizing hypersurface cone in $\mathbb{R}^{8}$ has an isolated singularity at the origin; away from the origin its link is a smooth minimal hypersurface in the unit sphere. Thus the classification problem is equivalent to a classification of those smooth minimal hypersurfaces in $\mathbb{S}^{7}$ whose cones are area-minimizing.

Simons' work gives the fundamental stability identity and $L^{2}$ control for stable minimal hypersurfaces. Schoen-Simon-Yau and Schoen-Simon developed curvature estimates and regularity theory for stable minimal hypersurfaces, while Leon Simon obtained further curvature estimates and compactness tools relevant to possible links. These results explain why one expects finiteness of topological types, but they do not identify all possible types.

There are many explicit and semi-explicit constructions of minimizing cones in other dimensions and codimensions. Calibrated geometry gives important classes, and Lawlor's curvature criterion provides a powerful sufficient condition. Cui's 2026 survey and construction paper emphasizes that all currently known regular area-minimizing hypercones are isoparametric, while also recording Yau's $\mathbb{R}^{8}$ classification question as open [C]. This empirical isoparametric pattern is not a classification theorem.

Two more recent directions sharpen the surrounding analytic picture. Zhang revisited Lawson's homogeneous areaminimizing hypercones and showed that the full homogeneous list classified by Lawlor is area-minimizing, with coflat calibrations singular only at the origin [Z]. Székelyhidi constructed minimal hypersurfaces with cylindrical tangent cones and proved a strong unique-continuation theorem for such tangent cones; many of the examples are area-minimizing [Sz]. These works do not classify seven-dimensional minimizing hypercones in $\mathbb{R}^{8}$, but they clarify which analytic and calibration mechanisms can produce or rigidify minimizing cones. Edelen proves a cone-decomposition and bi-Lipschitz compactness theory for stable or bounded-index seven-dimensional minimal hypersurfaces and obtains finiteness of diffeomorphism types under global area and index bounds in a closed eight-manifold [E]. He explicitly does not classify the tangent cones; rather, his theorem shows what controlled degeneration can be proved without such a classification.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Classify all smooth stable minimal hypersurfaces $M^{6} \subset \mathbb{S}^{7}$ whose cones are area-minimizing.
\item[2.] Original direction. Decide whether the two known quadratic minimizing cones, with links $\mathbb{S}^{2} \times \mathbb{S}^{4}$ and $\mathbb{S}^{3} \times \mathbb{S}^{3}$, exhaust all area-minimizing hypercones in $\mathbb{R}^{8}$.
\item[3.] Original direction. Obtain effective bounds on the Betti numbers and diffeomorphism types of the links of sevendimensional area-minimizing cones in $\mathbb{R}^{8}$.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Decide whether every such cone is forced by a known symmetry, calibration, or Lawlor-type minimizing criterion.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify the possible singular tangent cones of area-minimizing hypersurfaces in dimension 8.
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Relate Lawson-Lawlor homogeneous minimizing cones and cylindrical tangent-cone constructions to the topology of links in $\mathbb{S}^{7}[\mathrm{~L}, \mathrm{Sz}, \mathrm{Z}]$, and determine whether Edelen's cone-decomposition invariants can be made effective for the two known cone models [E].
\end{itemize}
7. AI-generated formulation. Construct computable invariants of stable minimal links in $\mathbb{S}^{7}$ which distinguish the finite list of possible topological types, if Lawson's finiteness expectation is made effective.

\section*{Problem 105}
(Chern). Consider the set of all compact minimal hypersurfaces in $\mathbb{S}^{N}$ with constant scalar curvature. Think of the scalar curvature as a function on this set. Is the image of this function a discrete set of positive numbers?

Status. Open in general. For a minimal hypersurface $M^{n} \subset \mathbb{S}^{n+1}$, constant scalar curvature is equivalent to constant $S=|A|^{2}$, since
$$R=n(n-1)-S .$$
Thus Chern's question is usually formulated as the discreteness of the possible constant values of $|A|^{2}$.
There have been the works of Simons [S], Chern-do Carmo-Kobayashi [CDK], Lawson [L] and Yau [Y]. More recently, Terng and Peng [TP] made a breakthrough on this problem.

The first decisive results are gap theorems. Simons' identity gives a strong integral inequality for minimal submanifolds in spheres. Chern-do Carmo-Kobayashi and Lawson used this circle of ideas to show rigidity in the first pinching range: in the hypersurface case, if $S \leqslant n$, then the constant values force either the totally geodesic sphere or the Clifford examples. These results identify the first possible values but do not prove discreteness of the whole image.

Peng-Terng introduced higher-order integral identities and invariants which gave important progress toward the next gaps. Their work became the standard route to the stronger form of the conjecture: a compact minimal hypersurface in $\mathbb{S}^{n+1}$ with constant scalar curvature should be isoparametric. If this stronger statement were true, discreteness would follow from the classification and structure theory of isoparametric hypersurfaces.

Modern work has obtained sharper partial gap theorems. For example, Lei-Xu-Xu proved a rigidity theorem near the first Clifford value: under $0 \leqslant S-n \leqslant n / 18$, one has $S \equiv n$ and the hypersurface is a Clifford torus. Cheng-Wei-Yamashiro proved further rigidity under additional constancy assumptions, such as constancy of the third mean curvature invariant $f_{3}$, in an improved numerical range. These results support Chern's prediction but still leave the full discreteness problem open.

Very recent papers continue to attack special curvature-invariant regimes without settling the unrestricted conjecture. Ge-Tan-Yan-Zhang proved that a closed minimal Willmore hypersurface of dimension $n \geqslant 5$ with constant scalar curvature and $S>n$ satisfies an explicit lower bound tending to $2 n-3$ as $n \rightarrow \infty$; under either of two pointwise lower bounds on $f_{4}$ they obtain the expected conclusion $S \geqslant 2 n$ [GTYZ]. Their proof refines Yang-Cheng's Peng-Terng integral estimates and closes them with a pointwise upper estimate for $f_{4}$.

He-Xu-Zhao proved a classification theorem for closed minimal hypersurfaces $M^{4} \subset \mathbb{S}^{5}$ with constant scalar curvature and constant third mean curvature: the examples are the equatorial sphere, Clifford products, or Cartan's minimal hypersurface, with $S=0,4,12[\mathrm{HXZ}]$. Their argument studies the characteristic polynomial of the shape operator and the image of the Gauss-Kronecker curvature, then invokes the low-dimensional isoparametric classification.

Ge-Liu-Luo-Yan proved a 2026 rigidity theorem in $\mathbb{S}^{5}$ under constant Gauss-Kronecker curvature, replacing the usual consecutive trace hypotheses by the invariant set $\{H, S, K\}$ [GLLY]. These authors construct weighted 3-forms adapted to $S$ and $K$ and prove the Euler characteristic vanishes in order to close the global curvature estimates across the repeated-principalcurvature locus. These preprints reinforce the trend that Chern's discreteness problem is being approached through increasingly precise finite-dimensional curvature invariants, but each still assumes an invariant beyond constant scalar curvature.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Prove that the set of constant values of $S=|A|^{2}$ for compact minimal hypersurfaces in $\mathbb{S}^{n+1}$ is discrete.
\item[2.] Original direction. Prove the stronger Chern conjecture: compact minimal hypersurfaces in $\mathbb{S}^{n+1}$ with constant scalar curvature are isoparametric.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Establish the second and higher gap theorems predicted by the isoparametric examples.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Relate Chern's conjecture to Yau's first eigenvalue conjecture for embedded minimal hypersurfaces in spheres.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine which additional constant curvature invariants, such as $f_{3}$, fourth mean curvature, or Gauss-Kronecker curvature, force isoparametricity in low dimensions and then in general [CWY, GTYZ, HXZ, GLLY].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. In $M^{4} \subset \mathbb{S}^{5}$, remove first the constant- $f_{3}$ or constant- $K$ hypothesis from the 2026 classifications, and identify which part of the characteristic-polynomial or weighted-form arguments genuinely requires it [HXZ, GLLY].
\end{itemize}
7. AI-generated formulation. Develop an effective "Chern spectrum" for constant-scalar-curvature minimal hypersurfaces, including numerical invariants which would isolate each possible value of $S$.

\section*{Problem 106}

Let $M$ be a compact, three-dimensional manifold with curvature -1. Let $\Sigma$ be a surface of genus $g$ such that there is some continuous $f: \Sigma \rightarrow M$ with $f_{*}: \pi_{1}(\Sigma) \rightarrow \pi_{1}(M)$ an injection. It was known ([SY], [SU]) that such a map can be deformed to be a minimal immersion. Is it true that for most $M$, the resulting immersion would be unique?

Status. Open as stated, and not yet a precise genericity question. Almost-Fuchsian surface-group covers have a unique closed incompressible minimal surface, whereas quasi-Fuchsian manifolds can have arbitrarily many. The latter manifolds are noncompact, however, so their multiplicity does not by itself disprove the original question for compact hyperbolic 3-manifolds.

The existence part belongs to the classical interaction between minimal surface theory and 3-manifold topology. Schoen-Yau and Sacks-Uhlenbeck gave analytic existence theorems for least-area or harmonic representatives of incompressible surface classes. The topological theory of Freedman-Hass-Scott further shows that least-area incompressible surfaces behave optimally when their homotopy classes contain embedded representatives [FHS].

For a surface subgroup $\Gamma<\pi_{1}(M)$, a minimal immersion lifts to the associated cover $\mathbb{H}^{3} / \Gamma$. When this cover is almost-Fuchsian-that is, it contains a closed incompressible minimal surface with principal curvatures in (-1, 1)-the surface is unique [U, HW1]. The proof uses the normal exponential flow and the strict principal-curvature bound to obtain barriers and rule out a second closed minimal surface.

Outside the almost-Fuchsian region, Biao Wang constructed quasi-Fuchsian manifolds with at least two closed incompressible minimal surfaces [W]. Huang-Wang then produced, for every $N>0$, a quasi-Fuchsian manifold containing at least $2^{N}$ such surfaces [HW2]. Their min-max and barrier construction proves that the curvature condition is genuinely restrictive, but these examples have topology $\Sigma \times \mathbb{R}$ and infinite volume. No source checked in this audit supplies a compact hyperbolic 3-manifold with two minimal immersions in the same prescribed surface-subgroup homotopy class.

Agol's virtual Haken theorem [A] greatly enlarges the supply of embedded incompressible surfaces in finite covers of closed hyperbolic 3-manifolds, but it does not compare minimal representatives in one fixed homotopy class. Recent work of Coskunuzer-Huang-Lowe-Vargas Pallete gives Schottky manifolds with infinitely many closed minimal surfaces [CHLV], again in a noncompact setting. These results clarify that abundance across homotopy classes and uniqueness within one class are different questions.

Finally, Mostow rigidity prevents one from varying the hyperbolic metric on a fixed closed 3-manifold. Thus "most $M$ " must mean a probability model or density on closed hyperbolic manifolds, or else a genericity notion for surface subgroups inside a fixed manifold. Until one of these is specified, the original question has no single formal yes-or-no interpretation.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Decide whether a fixed $\pi_{1}$-injective homotopy class in a closed hyperbolic 3-manifold can contain two noncongruent minimal immersions.
\item[2.] Original direction. Choose a precise model of "most," for example random Heegaard splittings or a complexity-ordered family of closed hyperbolic manifolds, and test uniqueness in that model.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Characterize the quasi-Fuchsian manifolds in which the closed incompressible minimal surface is unique, beyond the sufficient almost-Fuchsian condition [U, HW1, W, HW2].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. For a surface subgroup of a closed hyperbolic 3-manifold, relate uniqueness in its subgroup cover to index, least-area properties, and quantitative bounds on the principal curvatures.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine how the number and Morse indices of closed incompressible minimal surfaces change when one crosses the boundary of almost-Fuchsian space [HW1, HW2].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Develop a wall-crossing theory for minimal incompressible surfaces in quasi-Fuchsian 3-manifolds, where new minimal surfaces appear or disappear at degenerate critical points of the area functional.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 107}

Let $M$ be a complete minimal surface in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$. Osserman [O] has proved that the Gauss map of $M$ cannot omit a set of positive capacity in $\mathbb{S}^{2}$ and he conjectured that it in fact could not omit more than four points in $\mathbb{S}^{2}$. Recently, Xavier [X] proved that it cannot omit more than eleven points. Based on the method of Xavier, Bombieri [B] improved the number to seven. Can one improve it to four? Can one generalize these assertions to three-dimensional minimal hypersurfaces?

Status. Partially resolved. The surface case in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ is solved. Fujimoto proved that the Gauss map of a complete nonflat minimal surface in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ can omit at most four points of $\mathbb{S}^{2}$, and this bound is sharp. Higher-dimensional and higher codimension analogues remain separate questions and are only partially understood in general.

The historical line is now quite clear. Osserman's theorem first showed that the Gauss image of a complete nonflat minimal surface cannot omit a set of positive capacity. Xavier then proved that the Gauss map cannot omit seven points; the older attribution to an unpublished Bombieri improvement should not be used as a separate reference here. Fujimoto completed the Osserman-Xavier program by proving the sharp bound four. The sharpness comes from classical examples of Voss: complete minimal surfaces whose Gauss maps omit four prescribed points.

For complete minimal surfaces with finite total curvature the situation is more rigid. Jorge-Mercuri proved the sharp bound that the Gauss map of a complete nonflat minimal surface of finite total curvature can omit at most two points [JM], with equality realized by the catenoid. This uses the meromorphic extension of the Gauss map to the compactified surface and topology of the covering, so it is a different mechanism from Fujimoto's general completeness argument.

For higher-dimensional minimal hypersurfaces the Gauss map takes values in a sphere but is no longer a meromorphic function on a Riemann surface. The complex-analytic Nevanlinna theory behind Fujimoto's proof is therefore not directly available. This is the main reason the three-dimensional hypersurface analogue remains a different and harder problem.

There are nevertheless higher-dimensional Bernstein-type substitutes. Ding proved that a complete oriented embedded minimal hypersurface in $\mathbb{R}^{n+1}$ with Euclidean volume growth is an affine hyperplane if its Gauss image avoids a neighborhood of a half-equator [D]. The proof treats the Gauss map as a harmonic map and combines image avoidance with curvature estimates and a Liouville argument. It is a strong rigidity theorem, but not a finite omitted-point theorem. Jost-Xin-Yang obtain related Bernstein conclusions when the normal Gauss image lies in suitable regions of a Grassmannian [JXY].

For two-dimensional domains, complex analysis still gives sharper analogues in higher codimension. Aiyama-Akutagawa-Imagawa-Kawakami study complete minimal surfaces in $\mathbb{R}^{4}$ [AAIK], while Dethloff-Ha treat ramification on annular ends in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ and $\mathbb{R}^{4}[\mathrm{DH}]$. Huynh proves that a nondegenerate generalized Gauss map of a complete minimal surface in $\mathbb{R}^{n}$ cannot omit a generic projective hypersurface of an explicit bounded degree [Hu]. These results show which parts of Nevanlinna theory survive a change of codimension, but they do not answer Yau's higher-dimensional-domain question.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Find the optimal omitted-value theorem for the Gauss map of complete minimal hypersurfaces in $\mathbb{R}^{n+1}, n \geqslant 3$.
\item[2.] Original direction. Determine which omitted subsets of $\mathbb{S}^{n}$ can occur as the complement of the Gauss image of a complete nonflat minimal hypersurface.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Remove or weaken the Euclidean-volume-growth and half-equator-neighborhood hypotheses in Ding's higher-dimensional rigidity theorem [D].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify complete minimal surfaces in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ whose Gauss map omits exactly four points.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine the optimal projective-hypersurface defect relation for generalized Gauss maps of complete minimal surfaces in $\mathbb{R}^{n}$ [AAIK, DH, Hu].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Relate omitted-value bounds for higher-dimensional Gauss maps to quantitative stability or Liouville theorems for the harmonic Gauss map.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 108}

Suppose $H$ is an area-minimizing hypersurface in a manifold $M$. Prove that by a perturbation of the metric on $M$, the singularities of $H$ may be eliminated, retaining the $N-1$ dimensional homology class represented by $H$. For some related problems, see B. White [W].

Status. Partially resolved. For integral codimension-one homology minimizers, the requested arbitrarily small metric perturbation is now known through ambient dimension 11. Above dimension 11, perturbation gives a strictly improved bound on the singular-set dimension, but full smoothness remains open. The PL question is also open and is logically separate.

Is it true that the support of a codimension-one area-minimizing current has a PL structure? For a general high-codimension minimal current, it is not true that the support is a real analytic variety (see Milani [M]). For the present state of the problem, see Almgren [A].

The starting point is the Federer-Fleming and De Giorgi regularity theory for mass-minimizing hypersurfaces. In codimension one, the singular set has codimension at least 7 in the hypersurface. This explains why the problem is invisible in low dimensions and why dimension 8 is the first critical case, with the Simons cone as the basic model singularity.

White's work initiated a systematic study of how singularities and tangent cones behave under perturbations. Later regularity theorems of Schoen-Simon and Wickramasekera gave powerful structural control for stable and almost minimizing hypersurfaces. In high codimension, Almgren's regularity theorem and the De Lellis-Spadaro program show that the singular set is much smaller than the whole current, but the local structure can be highly nonclassical; Milani's examples warn against expecting real analyticity of supports in general.

The metric-perturbation problem has a sharp recent chronology. Smale proved it for homologically area-minimizing hypersurfaces in ambient dimension 8 [Sma]. Chodosh-Mantoulidis-Schulze extended the homology and Plateau results to ambient dimensions 9 and 10 [CMS] and then obtained improved generic partial regularity in all higher dimensions [CMS2]. Finally, Chodosh-Mantoulidis-Schulze-Wang proved the following precise form through dimension 11: for a closed oriented $(n+1)$-manifold, a nonzero class in $H_{n}(M ; \mathbb{Z})$, and any metric $g$, there are arbitrarily $C^{\infty}$-small perturbations $g^{\prime}$ such that every $g^{\prime}$-minimizing current in that class is a sum of disjoint smooth oriented hypersurfaces when $n+1 \leqslant 11$ [CMSW]. When $n+1 \geqslant 12$, the same theorem gives
$$\operatorname{dim}_{\mathcal{H}} \text { Sing } \leqslant n-10-\varepsilon_{n}$$
for a dimensional $\varepsilon_{n}>0$. Thus Yau's perturbation request is solved through ambient dimension 11, not merely supported by examples there.

Li-Wang's generic regularity and nondegeneracy theorem for locally stable and min-max hypersurfaces in ambient dimension 8 [LW] belongs to the same program, but it should not replace Smale's earlier theorem for homology minimizers. The proof mechanisms in the recent homology results combine a one-parameter family of minimizers, cone splitting, quantitative separation of leaves by positive Jacobi fields, and, in dimension 11, a second blow-up analysis using an Almgren frequency for Jacobi fields on cylindrical minimizing cones.

The codimension-one hypothesis is essential. Liu proved that in many higher-codimension ranges there are open sets of metrics for which every minimizer in a smooth-representable homology class remains singular; for dimensions $d, c \geqslant 3$ the forced lower bound is $\max \{d-5, d-c\}$ [Liu]. This negative theorem does not contradict [CMSW], which concerns hypersurfaces. It also reinforces that the PL question is a different structural problem: rectifiability and stratification of the singular set do not automatically triangulate the entire support.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Extend the full metric-perturbation theorem for homologically area-minimizing hypersurfaces from ambient dimension 11 to all dimensions [CMSW].
\item[2.] Original direction. Determine whether one can perturb a specified singular minimizer itself to a smooth minimizer, rather than only obtain smoothness of minimizers after perturbing the metric in the fixed homology class.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Decide whether the support of a codimension-one area-minimizing current always has a natural PL structure.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify which tangent cones to minimizing hypersurfaces persist under generic perturbations of the metric.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Improve the $n-10-\varepsilon_{n}$ singular-set estimate in ambient dimensions at least 12, and identify the tangent cones that obstruct another dimension jump [CMS2, CMSW].
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Resolve the remaining higher-codimension pairs not covered by Liu's persistence theorem, especially codimension 2 and the low-dimensional cases $d=3,4<c$ [Liu].
\item[7.] AI-generated formulation. Construct a deformation complex for singular minimizing hypersurfaces whose obstruction classes measure whether a metric perturbation can remove each singular stratum.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 109}

Let $\Omega$ be a $k$-dimensional, compact, minimal submanifold of $\mathbb{R}^{N}$. Prove the isoperimetric inequality
$$\operatorname{Vol}(\Omega)^{k-1} \leqslant c_{k} \operatorname{Vol}(\partial \Omega)^{k}$$
where $c_{k}$ is given by
$$c_{k}=\frac{\operatorname{Vol}(B(1))^{k-1}}{\operatorname{Vol}(\partial B(1))^{k}}$$
with $B(1)$ signifying the unit ball in $\mathbb{R}^{k}$. It is true if $k=N$, that is, domains in $\mathbb{R}^{N}$.
Status. Partially resolved. The sharp inequality is classical for domains in $\mathbb{R}^{k}$ and for several classes of minimal surfaces. Brendle proved the sharp isoperimetric inequality for compact minimal submanifolds of codimension at most 2 in Euclidean space, with equality only for a flat round ball. The fully general sharp statement in codimension at least 3 remains open.

This inequality with a constant larger than the sharp Euclidean constant above is known to be true; see [FF], [Alm], [All], [MS], and [BDG].

If $k=2$ and $\Omega$ is simply connected, this result is classical and is due to Carleman (see Osserman [O]). If $k=2$ and $\Omega$ is doubly connected, it is again true, and due to Osserman and Schiffer [OS] and Feinberg [F].

An approach is to show that the extremal case for the inequality can be realized as a stationary integral varifold which one might be able to show is a flat $k$-disk. That flat $k$-disks are indeed extreme among nearby nonparametric surfaces has been studied by B. White [W].

The early history begins with the classical Euclidean isoperimetric inequality and its two-dimensional minimal-surface analogues. Carleman proved the sharp inequality for simply connected minimal surfaces. Osserman-Schiffer and Feinberg proved the doubly connected case. These results show that minimality supplies enough extra structure to recover the Euclidean sharp constant in important surface cases.

For higher-dimensional submanifolds, the Michael-Simon Sobolev inequality gives a powerful isoperimetric inequality, but not with the sharp Euclidean constant. Almgren's optimal filling inequality gives sharp results for area-minimizing currents, which is stronger than mere stationarity but applies to a different class of objects.

Brendle's work introduced an ABP-type method for submanifolds and proved a sharp Sobolev inequality in arbitrary dimension and codimension. In codimension 2, and hence also in codimension 1 by embedding a hypersurface into one higher Euclidean space, this yields exactly the sharp isoperimetric inequality stated here. Equality is rigid: the extremal submanifold must be a flat round ball. The arbitrary-codimension case remains the natural remaining form of the problem, so the safest status is partial resolution rather than a complete solution of Yau's full statement.

The ABP method has continued to generate related sharp inequalities. Brendle also proved a sharp logarithmic Sobolev inequality for submanifolds in Euclidean space, including the mean-curvature term in the Michael-Simon spirit [B2]. Balogh-Kristály later obtained sharp and rigidity-enhanced logarithmic-Sobolev inequalities on noncompact Euclidean submanifolds, with codimension-free constants in the minimal case [BK]. Lu extended the ABP approach to sharp logarithmic-Sobolev inequalities in manifolds with nonnegative curvature [Lu]. These results do not settle the sharp isoperimetric constant in arbitrary codimension, but they make clear that the remaining problem is part of a larger sharp-constant program for Sobolev, logarithmic Sobolev, and isoperimetric inequalities on submanifolds.

Two recent expositions make the remaining gap especially transparent. Brendle-Eichmair give both the ABP and unequaldimensional optimal transport proofs in the hypersurface case [BE], while Brendle's 2026 survey isolates the normal-bundle Jacobian estimate that produces the sharp codimension-2 constant [B3]. Balogh-Kristály-Mester obtain explicit $L^{p}$ Sobolev inequalities for minimal submanifolds of every codimension; for $p \geqslant 2$ their constants are codimension-free and asymptotically sharp as the dimension grows, but they explicitly remain nonsharp in codimension at least 3 [BKM]. Hence these advances do not close Yau's $p=1$ sharp-constant problem.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Prove the sharp Euclidean isoperimetric inequality for compact minimal submanifolds in arbitrary codimension.
\item[2.] Original direction. Determine whether Brendle's ABP method can be sharpened to give the Euclidean constant beyond codimension 2.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify equality and near-equality cases for sharp isoperimetric inequalities on minimal submanifolds.
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend the sharp inequality to singular stationary integral varifolds under natural boundary hypotheses.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Find a codimension-independent $p \downarrow 1$ limit of the transport inequalities in [BKM] that retains the Euclidean sharp constant.
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Develop a quantitative stability version: if a compact minimal submanifold nearly attains the Euclidean isoperimetric constant, then it is close, in a suitable varifold or graphical sense, to a flat round disk.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 110}

Let $\Sigma$ be a compact surface, and let $f: \Sigma \rightarrow M$ be a minimal immersion into a three-dimensional manifold such that $f$ has least area among all the maps homotopic to $f$. (If $\Sigma$ has boundary, we consider only immersions which are embeddings on $\partial \Sigma$ and we fix the image of $f(\partial \Sigma)$ also.) If $\Sigma$ is $\mathbb{S}^{2}$ or a planar domain, Meeks-Yau [MY] prove that $f$ is an embedding. When $\Sigma$ has higher genus, Freedman-Hass-Scott [FHS] prove that $f$ is an embedding assuming that $f$ is homotopic to an embedding. Without this latter assumption, $f$ need not be an embedding. However, it is believed that $f$ tends to minimize the complexity of the self-intersection set. It is of basic interest for the topologists to estimate the number of triple points of $f$.

Status. Open for effective triple-point estimates, but the motivating claim that least area should minimize the number of triple points is false in general. Gulliver-Scott constructed a least-area incompressible torus with triple points that is homotopic to a general-position immersion with no triple points [GS].

The foundational results separate the problem into two regimes. For spheres and planar domains, Meeks-Yau proved strong embeddedness results for least-area maps. For higher genus incompressible surfaces, Freedman-Hass-Scott proved that a two-sided least-area representative homotopic to an embedding is embedded, apart from the explicit possibility that it double covers a one-sided embedded surface [FHS]. Their proof is based on area swapping, cut-and-paste, and rounding: an inessential product region or removable intersection would yield an area-decreasing competitor.

Their slogan that least-area surfaces "intersect least" requires the right invariant. For a two-sided incompressible immersion $f$, they define a self-intersection invariant $D(f)$ that remains meaningful when the immersion is not in general position, and prove that a least-area map minimizes $D$ in its homotopy class. For tori and Klein bottles this controls the ordinary number of double curves. For higher-genus surfaces, they give an example showing that a least-area map need not minimize the number of double curves.

Gulliver-Scott show that triple points are even less well behaved [GS]. Their main example is a Riemannian 3-manifold and a least-area incompressible map of a torus having triple points, although the same homotopy class contains a general-position immersion without triple points. Consequently neither triple-point count nor the lexicographic complexity ( $t, d$ ) can be the universal invariant minimized by area. The remaining problem is to bound triple points or to relate them to $D(f)$ under additional transversality, analyticity, or topological hypotheses.

There are purely topological constraints. Banchoff proved that for a self-transverse immersion of a closed surface in $\mathbb{R}^{3}$, the number of triple points is congruent modulo 2 to the Euler characteristic of the surface [B]. Carter-Ko generalized the parity formula to immersions in 3-manifolds, adding the Euler characteristic of the image [CK]. These are obstructions, not upper bounds.

There are also useful existence results in noncompact hyperbolic settings. Collin-Hauswirth-Mazet-Rosenberg constructed compact embedded and least-area incompressible minimal surfaces in complete finite-volume hyperbolic 3-manifolds [CHMR]. Huang-Wang proved that closed immersed incompressible surfaces in cusped hyperbolic 3-manifolds can be deformed to closed immersed least-area surfaces in their homotopy classes [HW]. These results do not directly estimate triple points, but they broaden the supply of least-area representatives on which the refined questions can be tested.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Give an effective upper or lower bound for the number of triple points of a least-area representative in terms of topological data of the homotopy class.
\item[2.] Original direction. Relate the number of triple points to the Freedman-Hass-Scott invariant $D(f)$ under a generic or analytic metric [FHS, GS].
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Determine hypotheses under which a least-area representative minimizes the lexicographic complexity $(t, d)$, despite the Gulliver-Scott counterexample [GS].
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Decide whether a least-area immersion for a bumpy or real-analytic metric has finitely many triple points and admits an effective bound.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Combine the parity formula with normal-surface or subgroup complexity to obtain nontrivial lower bounds for unavoidable triple points [B, CK].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Define a "minimal self-intersection norm" on surface homotopy classes in a 3-manifold and compare it with the number of triple points of least-area representatives.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 111}

Let $f: M_{1} \rightarrow M_{2}$ be a diffeomorphism between two compact manifolds with negative curvature. If $h: M_{1} \rightarrow M_{2}$ is a harmonic map which is homotopic to $f$, is $h$ a univalent map? For $n=2$, this was proved in [SY] and [S].

Status. True in dimension 2 and false in every dimension $n \geqslant 6$; the cited counterexample technology does not settle dimensions 3, 4 and 5. Schoen-Yau and Sampson proved univalence for harmonic maps between negatively curved surfaces [SY, S]. Farrell-Ontaneda-Raghunathan proved that for every $n \geqslant 6$ there are diffeomorphisms between closed negatively curved $n$-manifolds whose harmonic representatives are not one-to-one [FOR, FOs].

For $n>2$, Calabi has a counterexample if we do not impose conditions on $M_{1}$ and $M_{2}$. (In Calabi's example, $M_{2}$ is a torus.)
The distinctions among non-univalence, failure to be a diffeomorphism, and non-cellularity are essential. The maps in [FOR] answer Yau's question negatively by failing to be one-to-one. Farrell-Ontaneda also constructed harmonic cellular maps which are limits of diffeomorphisms but are not diffeomorphisms [FO]. Other exotic structure constructions produce harmonic homotopy equivalences that are not cellular [FJO2, FOs]. These are related phenomena, but they are not interchangeable conclusions about one example.

The positive two-dimensional result is a consequence of the special structure of harmonic maps between surfaces: the Hopf differential, negative curvature, and degree-one topology combine to rule out folding. In higher dimensions the analytic uniqueness of harmonic representatives does not force pointwise injectivity.

The high-dimensional counterexamples are topological as much as analytic. Farrell-Jones rigidity and exotic smooth or triangulated structures make it possible to construct negatively curved manifolds which are homeomorphic or homotopy equivalent in controlled ways but whose harmonic representatives fail to be diffeomorphisms or even limits of homeomorphisms. Thus the original question, without further geometric or dimensional hypotheses, has a negative answer.

The remaining meaningful directions are therefore to settle dimensions 3-5 and to isolate hypotheses which restore univalence: curvature pinching, control of the Jacobian, or assumptions on the smooth structure and the harmonic representative.

The contrast with the general existence and uniqueness theory for harmonic maps is important. Eells-Sampson proved existence for targets of nonpositive curvature, and Hartman proved uniqueness in a homotopy class under suitable nonpositivecurvature hypotheses [ES, H]. These theorems explain why there is a canonical harmonic representative in the homotopy class; the Farrell-Ontaneda type examples show that canonicity and uniqueness of the harmonic representative are still much weaker than being a homeomorphism or a diffeomorphism.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Decide the original question in dimensions 3, 4 and 5.
\item[2.] Original direction. Find curvature pinching or Jacobian hypotheses which imply univalence in dimensions greater than 2.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify the possible singular or non-injective behavior of harmonic cellular maps in the Farrell-Ontaneda examples.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Study whether stronger target curvature assumptions, such as locally symmetric negative curvature, force better regularity or injectivity of the harmonic representative.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Separate the roles of existence, uniqueness, cellularity, and injectivity in the nonpositivecurvature harmonic-map theory, especially for maps homotopic to homeomorphisms or diffeomorphisms [ES, H, FO, FOR].
\item[6.] AI-generated formulation. Define a quantitative "harmonic folding defect" for degree-one harmonic maps and determine whether it is controlled by exotic smooth-structure invariants.
\end{itemize}

\section*{Problem 112}

Prove that $\pi_{i}\left(\mathbb{S}^{N}\right)$ can be represented by harmonic maps. What happens if we replace $\mathbb{S}^{N}$ by a compact manifold with finite fundamental group?

Status. Open for the standard round metrics. No theorem is known which realizes every element of every $\pi_{i}\left(\mathbb{S}^{N}\right)$ by a single harmonic map $\mathbb{S}^{i} \rightarrow \mathbb{S}^{N}$. Smith's join construction does prove that every class in $\pi_{n}\left(\mathbb{S}^{n}\right)$ has a harmonic representative for $1 \leqslant n \leqslant 7[\mathrm{~S}]$. Peng-Tang and later Clifford-system constructions realize further specific unstable homotopy classes [PT, QTY]. These results are substantial but do not solve the uniform problem.

One should refer to the paper of R. T. Smith [S].
Peng-Tang developed additional special cases [PT]. If one allows deformation of the domain metric, more examples can be obtained [R].

The problem sits between homotopy theory and the variational theory of the energy functional. Some basic representatives, including totally geodesic maps, holomorphic maps between round 2-spheres, Hopf fibrations, and maps built from harmonic polynomials or orthogonal multiplications, are harmonic for geometric reasons. Smith's construction joins such polynomial maps by solving an equivariant nonlinear ordinary differential equation [S]. Peng-Tang use isoparametric and equivariant geometry to construct additional representatives [PT].

The difficulty is that the target sphere has positive curvature, so the direct Eells-Sampson convexity theory for nonpositively curved targets does not apply. Minimizing or flowing the energy in a prescribed homotopy class can lose energy through bubbling, and the limiting harmonic maps need not preserve the original homotopy class in a simple way. This is why the question is much subtler than merely knowing the homotopy group.

Ratto's work shows that deforming the domain metric produces additional harmonic representatives [R]. This suggests a useful separation between two problems: fixed round domain and target metrics, versus the flexible problem where the domain metric is part of the unknown.

The first Hopf case already displays the gap. For every class in $\left[\mathbb{S}^{3}, \mathbb{S}^{2}\right]$ one can choose metrics for which a harmonic representative exists, while for the standard round metrics the only presently known examples are compositions of a Hopf fibration with conformal maps of $\mathbb{S}^{2}$. Recent rigidity results recover this form under extra hypotheses such as horizontal weak conformality or constant energy density [Geo].

The modern analytic obstruction is best understood through bubbling. Sacks-Uhlenbeck compactness produces harmonic spheres after perturbing the energy, but in positive curvature the limiting object may be a bubble tree rather than a single harmonic representative of the original class [SU]. Parker-Wolfson gave a systematic bubble-tree compactification for harmonic maps $[\mathrm{PW}]$. Thus a current form of the problem is to decide when a homotopy class has an honest smooth harmonic representative, rather than only a limiting bubble configuration whose components represent pieces of the class.

\section*{Related open problems.}
\begin{itemize}
\item[1.] Original direction. Determine exactly which elements of $\pi_{i}\left(\mathbb{S}^{N}\right)$ admit harmonic representatives for the standard round metrics.
\item[2.] Original direction. Determine whether every element of $\pi_{i}\left(\mathbb{S}^{N}\right)$ can be represented by a harmonic map after a suitable deformation of the domain metric.
\item[3.] Reference-driven formulation. Extend the representation problem from spheres to compact manifolds with finite fundamental group, using their universal covers.
\item[4.] Reference-driven formulation. Understand how bubbling in harmonic map heat flow changes the homotopy class of maps between spheres.
\item[5.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify which homotopy classes of spheres are represented by a single harmonic sphere and which are represented only after bubble-tree decomposition [SU, PW].
\item[6.] Reference-driven formulation. Classify harmonic maps $\mathbb{S}^{3} \rightarrow \mathbb{S}^{2}$ for the round metrics without the extra horizontalconformality or constant-energy hypotheses [Geo].
\item[7.] AI-generated formulation. Construct an "energy spectrum" for each element of $\pi_{i}\left(\mathbb{S}^{N}\right)$ recording whether the class has a smooth harmonic critical representative, only a bubble-tree representative, or no representative for the fixed metrics.
\end{itemize}
